<meta>This page transitions from the Table of Contents into the author's Preface, providing historical context for the philosopher Mozi (Motse) and his school of thought, Mohism, in relation to the dominant Confucian tradition.</meta>

# PREFACE

Every civilization has its shapers and its spokesmen. While most recognized representatives of a culture have not been without their proper merit, many masterly and mighty souls have been allowed to sink into oblivion. In the case of China, Fate played her usual but tragic trick. While Confucius symbolizes to us the blooming flower of Chinese thought, Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> suggests only a faded blade of grass. Yet what is currently true has not always been the case. At one time, Mohism <note>original: "Moism"</note>, with its forcefully stated doctrines on ethics, politics, economics, and religion, seriously threatened to overtake Confucianism as the representative Chinese view of life and way of living. As a matter of fact, Mencius <note>A famous Confucian philosopher (c. 372–289 BC) who defended Confucianism against rival schools.</note> tells us in his own words:

> “I am alarmed by these things and address myself to the defense of the doctrines of the former sages, and to oppose Yang <note>original: "Yang"; referring to Yang Zhu, a philosopher who advocated for a form of ethical egoism or "everyone for himself."</note> and Mo <note>Referring to Mozi.</note>.”

Unfortunately for the intellectual world, Confucianism finally won out through the suppression of its rival systems, including Mohism. As a formal discipline, Mohism has been little noticed through all these centuries, but as an habitual way of life, it has taken deep root in the soil of the nation and the fiber of the people. The vitality of the philosophy is further evidenced by the fact that "Young China" <note>A term for the intellectual and social reform movement in the early 20th century (the May Fourth era), which sought to modernize China by revisiting or rejecting ancient traditions.</note> in her present period of unrest is again eagerly turning to her old teacher, who taught under rather similar conditions over two millennia ago.

By way of introduction, we must be contented with these few words just to highlight the historical position and

<summary>This preface introduces Mozi as a once-powerful rival to Confucius whose influence was suppressed but persisted in the Chinese character, now seeing a revival in the early 20th century.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Motse, Mohism, Confucius, Mencius, Yang Zhu, Young China, Chinese Philosophy, history of Moism</keywords>

--- Page 12 ---
<meta>This page continues the author's preface, moving from the historical rivalry between Mozi and Confucius to the specific methodology and scope of this translation.</meta>

...the enduring relevance of our author. The life of the teacher and the development of his school form a fascinating story, while the structure of his doctrines creates a beautiful system. We have attempted this exciting task and pleasant duty, and the results are contained in a companion volume, *Mozi, the Neglected Rival of Confucius* <note>original: "Motse"; Y.P. Mei published this companion study in 1934 to provide the biographical and historical context omitted here.</note>. Therefore, any further remarks here would risk boring the reader with repetition of what we have already stated simply in that volume, and might distract the reader from following the philosopher’s own words.

However, a few explanations regarding the translation may be helpful. Sun Yirang’s <note>original: "Sun Yi-Jang"; Sun Yirang (1848–1908) was a famous Qing dynasty scholar. His work *Mozi Jiangu* (The Works of Mozi with Commentaries) restored the corrupted text to a readable state.</note> *The Works of Mozi with Commentaries* is universally considered the best among the Chinese versions of the text. We have based our translation on his version and have followed his commentaries for our interpretation. The few instances where we felt it necessary to differ from his authority have been noted in the footnotes.

The decision to exclude chapters from the *Works* that do not deal with ethical or political questions was made by both choice and necessity. As a look at the "Table of Contents" will show, the excluded chapters belong to two groups: the canonical chapters <note>These are the "Mohist Canons" (chapters 40–45), which focus on logic, epistemology, and science.</note> and their supplements, and the chapters on military tactics <note>Chapters 52–71, which detail the defense of city walls and various siege engines.</note>. The latter group contains many obsolete technical terms and contributes little to clarifying Mozi’s <note>original: "Motse's"</note> core thought. Regarding the former group, besides the unresolved question of who actually wrote them, these few pages are perhaps the most difficult reading in all of Chinese literature. Even if one is sure of the...

<summary>The author explains that this translation relies on Sun Yirang's scholarship and justifies the exclusion of Mozi's technical chapters on logic and military defense to focus on his ethical and political philosophy.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Sun Yirang, translation methodology, Mohist Canons, military tactics, Chinese literature, ethics, politics</keywords>

--- Page 13 ---
<meta>This page continues the translator's preface, detailing the methodology of the translation and acknowledging the influence of previous scholars like Hu Shih, Alfred Forke, and James Legge.</meta>

# PREFACE

...meaning of their contents <note>This refers to the difficult technical chapters on logic and science mentioned on the previous page.</note>. Since I do not claim to be an expert in those fields, a method other than direct translation is necessary to convey their meaning clearly. As an excellent example, we might cite Professor Hu Shih <note>Hu Shih (1891–1961) was a key figure in China's New Culture Movement and a pioneer in applying modern logical analysis to ancient Chinese texts.</note>, who, in his work *The Development of the Logical Method in Ancient China*, attempted an explanation of some of the Mohist canons <note>The "canons" are the highly technical and often corrupted chapters of the Mozi (chapters 40–45) dealing with logic and science.</note>.

This translation was written entirely independently. Afterward, the result was compared with the German translation of the complete *Works* by Professor Alfred Forke <note>Alfred Forke (1867–1944) was a German sinologist; his 1922 translation was the first complete version of the Mozi in a European language.</note>, which is the only other extensive translation of Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> in a European language. However, on certain points, especially in his introductory essay, I must disagree with his conclusions. At one stage in my research, I tried to document these differences of opinion in their proper context, but later these notes appeared so tedious and argumentative that I decided to omit them altogether.

Regarding the English used in this translation, I feel compelled to repeat the explanation so often given by translators. During the course of this work, I have often faced a dilemma: should I preserve the original flavor and expression of the ancient Chinese author, or should I use modern, idiomatic English? For obvious reasons, I have usually decided on the former, sometimes perhaps at the expense of the latter. Nevertheless, I hope I have succeeded in presenting the work in intelligible English.

The quotations from other works found in this volume are all translated directly from their respective Chinese texts. In the case of the numerous quotations from the Chinese Classics, I acknowledge the great effort taken by James Legge <note>James Legge (1815–1897) was a Scottish missionary whose translations of the Confucian Classics became the standard English versions for decades.</note> by providing references to his translation of each passage, although I have

<summary>In this section of the preface, Y.P. Mei explains his translation methodology, his decision to prioritize the original Chinese style over modern English idioms, and his relationship to the work of other scholars like Alfred Forke and James Legge.</summary>
<keywords>translation methodology, Hu Shih, Alfred Forke, Mohist canons, James Legge, Chinese Classics, linguistic style</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the translator's preface, focusing on the scholarly collaboration and institutional support that made this English translation of Mozi possible. It follows a discussion of the challenges in translating technical Mohist chapters and comparisons with Alfred Forke's German version.</meta>

<page-num>xiv</page-num>

# PREFACE

...I have very rarely been able to use his <note>Referring to Alfred Forke, whose German translation was mentioned on the previous page.</note> version without making changes. This was done so that readers can compare different interpretations and understand the context and significance of passages when the footnotes in this book are not enough to explain them.

In addition to my debt to Sun Yirang <note>original: "Sun Yi-Jang" (1848–1908), the preeminent Qing dynasty scholar whose commentary, the *Mozi Jiangu*, is the standard reference for studying Mozi.</note> as mentioned previously, I want to take this opportunity to thank Professor Lewis Hodous of Columbia University. He patiently read through the initial drafts of the manuscripts <note>original: "MSS."</note> and offered many suggestions, many of which I have incorporated here. Dr. Berthold Laufer of the Field Museum of Natural History in Chicago <note>original: "Chicago Field Museum"</note> also dedicated his time and provided advice on the translation. 

I owe more than I can say to Professor James Hayden Tufts <note>original: "J. H. Tufts" (1862–1942), an American philosopher and colleague of John Dewey.</note> of the University of Chicago. He was a noble and kind teacher who offered constant encouragement throughout this project and even spent his hard-earned vacation last spring reading the manuscripts. Finally, I wish to express my appreciation for the hospitality of the Library of Congress in Washington, D.C., where I completed much of this translation under excellent conditions.

->Y. P. MEI<-

LONDON.
July, 1927.

<margin>2</margin>

<meta>This page begins the main text of the *Mozi* (Motse). It transitions from the translator's preface to the first chapter of Book I, which discusses the vital role that virtuous and learned scholars play in the stability of a state.</meta>

<page-num>1</page-num>

# BOOK I

## CHAPTER I <note>Most scholars almost unanimously consider this chapter and the two following it to be spurious (not written by Mozi himself). This chapter acts as an appendix to the "Exaltation of the Virtuous" chapters (Chapters 8–10) and likely should not have been placed at the very beginning of the book.</note>

->BEFRIENDING THE LEARNED <note>The term "learned" translates the Chinese word *shih* (士). A *shih* was a philosopher-gentleman who combined virtue, talent, and courage with their education; they were often members of the lower nobility who served as advisors or warriors.</note><-

If a ruler does not support the learned in his state, he will harm the state. If a ruler is not eager to recommend virtuous people when he sees them, he is neglecting his duty to the country. Enthusiasm should only be shown toward the virtuous, and plans for the country should only be shared with the learned. There have been very few rulers who could maintain their countries while neglecting the virtuous and looking down on the learned.

In the past, Lord Wen (of Chin, who reigned 780–746 B.C.) was once in exile, yet he later became the leading feudal lord. Lord Huan (of Ch'i, who reigned 685–643 B.C.) was once forced to leave his state, yet he later became a "tyrant" <note>original: 霸 (ba). "Tyrant" is used here in the ancient Greek sense, meaning a "hegemon" or an absolute ruler who rose to power through strength and influence rather than through traditional inheritance.</note> among the feudal lords. Lord Kou Chien of Yüeh (who reigned 496–465 B.C.) was once humiliated by the King of Wu <note>Referring to Lord Fu Ch'a of Wu, who reigned 495–473 B.C.</note>, yet he was later...

<summary>This page introduces the Mohist principle that a state's survival depends on the ruler's ability to respect and utilize virtuous, learned advisors. It cites historical examples of famous Chinese rulers who overcame adversity by valuing such men.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Book I, Befriending the Learned, shih (士), Lord Wen, Lord Huan, Lord Kou Chien, virtue, statecraft, hegemon (霸)</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the discussion of great historical leaders who overcame adversity. It moves from specific historical examples to a broader philosophical discussion on the character of the virtuous leader and the importance of honest, even harsh, advisors.</meta>

<page-num>2</page-num>

# THE WORKS OF MOZI <note>original: Motse</note>

...looked upon with awe by the princes of China.<sup>1</sup> The reason that these three men became famous and successful in the world is that they were able to endure shame and humiliation within their own states.

The greatest men do not recognize defeat. The next greatest turn failure into success, and they achieve this by employing the talents of the people.

I have heard it said: "It is not that there is no peaceful home, but that my heart is not at peace while others are homeless; it is not that my wealth is insufficient, but that I long to improve the conditions of others." Therefore, the superior man <note>original: chün-tzu (君子). This refers to a person of high moral character and cultivation, often translated as "gentleman" or "exemplary person."</note> is strict with himself but lenient with others in matters of conduct, while the common people are lenient with themselves but strict with others. The superior man carries out his ambitions through action and studies his situation when he is at leisure. Even when he is treated as an average person, he feels no resentment. This is because he has self-confidence. Therefore, those who attempt what seems difficult will obtain what they desire, but few who only chase what they desire can avoid what they dislike.<sup>2</sup>

Therefore, crafty ministers are harmful to the lord, and flattering subordinates are damaging to the ruler. The lord should have ministers who do not compromise their principles; the ruler should have subordinates who are willing to be stern. Only when counsel is given with foresight and advice is offered with honesty...

---

<sup>1</sup> Yüeh was on the southern border of China. It was also considered barbarian in origin. See the Sketch Map included in *Motse, the Neglected Rival of Confucius*.

<sup>2</sup> This is almost an anticipation of the modern philosophical criticism known as the Hedonistic Paradox <note>The Hedonistic Paradox is the idea that constant pleasure-seeking actually prevents one from finding true happiness, which is often found instead through duty or difficult work.</note>.

<summary>This page concludes the historical examples of leaders who rose from humiliation to power and introduces the moral characteristics of the "superior man." It emphasizes that true success comes from self-discipline, self-confidence, and the willingness to accept stern, honest advice from ministers.</summary>

<page-num>3</page-num>

# BEFRIENDING THE LEARNED

...can the life of the state be secure and permanent. If, on the contrary, subordinates only value their positions and remain silent, the ministers close to the ruler will say nothing, those far away will only sigh, and the people will become resentful. When a ruler is surrounded by praise and flattery and cut off from sound advice, the country is in danger. Was it not because they refused to employ wise scholars that King Chieh <note>original: Chieh, the last king of the Hsia Dynasty, traditionally dated 1818–1766 BCE.</note> and King Chow <note>original: Chow, the last king of the Shang Dynasty, traditionally dated 1154–1122 BCE.</note> lost both their empires and their lives? Therefore, it is said: offering the country's greatest treasures to a ruler is not as praiseworthy as recommending virtuous people and introducing learned scholars.

Among the five types of weapons, the sharpest is the first to be broken. Among the five types of swords, the keenest is the first to wear out. Sweet-water wells go dry sooner, and beautiful trees are chopped down more often. The tortoises that provide the clearest divinations <note>original: "more responsive"; referring to the ancient practice of burning tortoise shells to predict the future.</note> are burned more often, and snakes that show supernatural power are sacrificed more frequently. In this way, Pi Kan <note>original: Pi Kan, a royal uncle of King Chow who was tortured for his honest advice.</note> died because of his integrity; Meng Fen <note>original: Meng Fen, a man of legendary strength.</note> perished because of his strength; Hsi Shih <note>original: Hsi Shih, a famous beauty of the 5th century BCE used as a political pawn between rival states.</note>...

---

<margin>1. Chieh was the last king of the Hsia Dynasty, 1818–1766 BCE.</margin>
<margin>2. Chow was the last king of the Shang Dynasty, 1154–1122 BCE.</margin>
<margin>3. Pi Kan was a royal uncle of the wicked King Chow. He was so bold in his advice that Chow tortured him to death.</margin>
<margin>4. Meng Fen was a man of great strength. He was said to have pulled the horns out of oxen’s heads. However, chronologically, he belongs to the end of the fourth century BCE, and Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> could not have known him. The likely explanation is that this incident was inserted by a later writer. But, as noted at the beginning of this chapter, the text of this and the next two chapters has been so heavily altered that it is hardly worth the effort to try to explain every inconsistency.</margin>
<margin>5. Hsi Shih was a famous beauty of the fifth century BCE. Lord Goujian <note>original: Kou Chien</note> of Yue <note>original: Yüeh</note> gave her as a gift to Lord Fucha <note>original: Fu Ch'a</note> of Wu, who were military rivals. Fucha could not resist her charms, and the state of Wu fell into ruin. Wu was soon after absorbed by Yue.</margin>

<summary>Mozi warns that rulers who prioritize flattery over the honest counsel of scholars lead their states to ruin, citing the downfall of Kings Chieh and Chow. He also observes the irony that exceptional qualities—like sharpness, strength, or beauty—often lead to early destruction or sacrifice.</summary>

This shows that there are but few who excel beyond others and do not perish because of it. Hence the saying: "The position of the supreme is hard to maintain."

Even a kind ruler will not show favor to ministers who lack merit. Even an affectionate father will not love his useless sons. He who occupies a position but is not equal to the task is not the proper person for that position. He who draws a salary <note>original: "emoluments"</note> but does not deserve the rank is not the proper owner of those earnings. Strong bows may be hard to pull, but they can reach great heights and pierce deeply. Good horses may be hard to ride, but they can carry heavy burdens and make long journeys. Real talents may be hard to command, but they can be trusted to serve as ambassadors <note>original: "envoys"</note> to the court of the emperor and to meet with the nobility.

Therefore, the big rivers do not look down on the little brooks that serve as tributaries. And great men do not neglect any humble <note>original: "menial"</note> task or reject any trifle; in this way, they become useful instruments <note>original: "vessels"</note> for the world. The water in a river does not come from a single source, nor is a fur coat that is worth a thousand pieces of silver <margin>A *yi* (鎰) is an ancient measure of silver. It is sometimes given as equivalent to 20 *taels* (a weight measurement) and sometimes as 24.</margin>...

<summary>This page discusses how exceptional individuals often face tragic ends and argues that true value in a person or official comes from their capability and merit rather than their mere presence or status.</summary>

...is not made from the white fur <note>original: "white fur ^1"; this refers to the *hu-p'u*, the fine, soft fur from under a fox's legs. A high-quality robe required many of these small pieces, symbolizing that greatness is achieved by gathering many small, valuable things.</note> of a single fox. Now, to reject those who are right but hire only those who agree with oneself is not the way to be a great ruler. Just as heaven and earth do not seek to dazzle us, great bodies of water do not merely boil and foam, and great fires <note>original: "conflagrations"</note> do not just sparkle <note>original: "coruscate"</note>, so the character of a true leader <note>original: "imperial character"</note> does not place itself out of reach.

As for a minor leader <note>original: "chieftain"</note> of only a thousand people, he is as rigid as an arrow and as smooth as a sharpening stone <note>original: "hone"</note>, unable to tolerate different ways of life. Narrow gorges dry up quickly, shallow streams are soon empty, and barren land does not produce fruit. When a ruler keeps his favors <note>original: "favours"</note> only within his palace, they cannot be shared with the entire country.

## CHAPTER II

### SELF-CULTIVATION

Even though there must be tactics in war, courage is the foundation. Even though there must be ceremonies for mourning, genuine grief is essential. Although a scholar should be well-educated, he must first of all demonstrate good character <note>original: "conduct"</note>. If the seeds are not sown well, there is no point in laboring for a good harvest. If you have not befriended those nearby, there is no point in trying to attract those who are far away. If your own relatives are not in harmony <note>original: "submissive"</note> with you, there is no point in trying to establish connections with the outside world. If you cannot complete a single task from start to finish, there is no point in attempting many things. And if you remain ignorant of an obvious truth that is pointed out to you, there is no point in seeking broad knowledge.

Therefore, when the ancient kings governed the empire, they would examine what was within reach and attract those at a distance. When superior men <note>original: "superior men"; the Chinese term *junzi* refers to a person of high moral character and social status, often translated as "gentleman"</note> investigate a region, it leads to orderly government. When they discovered misconduct or corruption, they corrected their own behavior first. Because of this, all complaints vanished and behavior became naturally disciplined. When virtuous leaders refuse to listen to deceitful words, utter threats, or think of harming others, then even underhanded people lose their influence. Therefore, superior men grow more energetic in their duties every day, while their selfish desires grow weaker and their bearing becomes more dignified.

<vocab>Self-Cultivation, character <note>original: "conduct"</note>, ancient kings <note>original: "early kings"</note>, empire, superior men, government</vocab>

<summary>Mozi explains that self-cultivation and moral integrity are the necessary foundations for any larger achievement, whether in scholarship, family life, or national government. He emphasizes that a leader must first correct their own behavior and manage immediate responsibilities before they can effectively rule a distant population.</summary>
<keywords>self-cultivation, superior men, junzi, moral foundation, leadership, ancient kings, social order</keywords>

--- Page 21 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on "Self-Cultivation" from Chapter II, moving from the impact of leadership on society to the specific internal qualities and lifelong commitment required of a "superior man."</meta>

# SELF-CULTIVATION

The way of the superior man <note>original: "superior man"; the Chinese term *junzi* refers to a person of high moral character</note> makes a person incorruptible in poverty and righteous when wealthy; it leads them to love the living and mourn the dead. These four qualities of character cannot be faked; they must be a genuine part of one’s personality. In his mind, there is nothing but love; in his behavior, there is nothing but respect; and from his mouth, there is nothing but gentleness. When someone follows this path until it fills their very being—pervading their limbs and skin—and continues until they are old and gray without ever stopping, that person is truly a sage <note>original: "sage"; a person of supreme wisdom and virtue</note>.

The wisdom of a person whose purpose is not firm will never reach very far. The actions of a person who does not keep their promises will never be effective. Anyone unwilling to share what they have with others is not worthy of being a friend. And one who lacks firm principles, broad knowledge, and keen judgment is not fit to be a companion. Just as a weak tree trunk can only support small branches, courage without moral development <note>original: "cultivation"</note> leads only to wasted energy <note>original: "dissipation"</note>. And just as a polluted spring results in a muddy stream, untrustworthy behavior will ruin a person’s reputation <note>original: "fame"</note>. For reputation does not appear from nowhere, nor does praise grow on its own. A good reputation follows real achievement; it cannot be gained through hypocrisy.

Even a person with great insight will not be listened to if they talk too much but are slow to act. Even a hard-working, capable person will accomplish nothing if they constantly boast of their achievements. The wise person understands everything in their heart but speaks with simplicity; they are capable but do not brag about their deeds. Consequently, their reputation is honored throughout the world...

<vocab>superior man, sage, wisdom, self-cultivation, fame, hypocrisy, conduct, gentility</vocab>

<summary>This page outlines the essential internal qualities of a "superior man" and a "sage," emphasizing that true virtue must be genuine, consistent, and demonstrated through action rather than empty words or boasting.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Self-Cultivation, Junzi, Sagehood, Integrity, Reputation vs. Reality, Moral Philosophy</keywords>

<page-num>8</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...over. In speaking, one should value cleverness <note>original: "ingenuity"</note> over the quantity of words, and deep understanding <note>original: "insight"</note> over mere flowery language <note>original: "eloquence"</note>. If a person lacks wisdom and insight, and instead allows their character to waste away through lack of discipline <note>original: "dissipation"</note>, they are doing the exact opposite of what should be cultivated in a person.

Any virtue that does not spring sincerely from the heart will not last, and any result of an action that was not genuinely intended by the individual will not endure. There are no shortcuts to a good reputation <note>original: "fame"</note>, and there are no tricks to earning genuine praise. The superior man <note>original: "superior man"; the Chinese term *junzi* refers to a person of high moral character</note> views his physical body merely as a vessel for his character. No one who prioritizes personal profit while easily discarding their reputation has ever been recognized as a true gentleman <note>original: "gentleman"; here, a person of noble character and integrity</note> in this world.

## CHAPTER III
### ON DYEING <note>The title of this chapter is a metaphor for the way environment and associates shape character and governance. Many historical figures cited here lived long after Motse's time, suggesting that later followers may have expanded upon his original observation about the dyer.</note>

Watching a dyer of silk at work, Motse <note>original: "Motse"; also known as Mozi (c. 470–391 BC), the philosopher who founded Mohism</note> sighed and said: "What is dyed in blue becomes blue; what is dyed in yellow becomes yellow. When the silk is put in a different dye, its color also changes. Having been dipped five times, it has changed its color five times. Therefore, dyeing must be done with great care. This is true not only for silk; even a country changes its 'color' in response to its influences." <note>original: "jan" 染; in Chinese, this word means both 'to dye' and 'to influence' or 'to be contaminated.'</note>

Thus, the ancient ruler Shun came under the influence of the virtuous men Hsü Yu and Po Yang; the Great Yü <note>the legendary founder of the Xia Dynasty</note> was influenced by Kao T'ao and Po Yi; T'ang <note>founder of the Shang Dynasty</note> was influenced by Yi Yin and Chung Huei; and King Wu <note>founder of the Zhou Dynasty</note> was influenced by the Grand Duke and Duke Chou. These four kings were surrounded by good influences. Because of this, they came to possess the empire and were appointed Sons of Heaven. <note>original: "T'ien Tse" 天子; this title for the Emperor implies he is the 'Son of Heaven,' a ruler who holds a divine mandate to govern on behalf of the heavens.</note> Their achievements and great fame extended from Heaven to earth. Whenever the most exceptionally kind and righteous figures in the world are mentioned, these are always the ones people refer to.

<summary>Motse uses the metaphor of silk dyeing to explain how individuals and nations are shaped by their environment and the people who influence them. He illustrates this by showing how the Great Kings of Chinese history achieved success because they sought guidance from virtuous advisors.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, dyeing, influence (jan), ethics, Shun, Yü, T'ang, King Wu, Son of Heaven (T'ien Tse), leadership, environment</keywords>

<page-num>11</page-num>

# ON DYEING

[...were under the influence of] <note>The text continues a list of failed rulers from the previous page.</note> Chih Kuo and Chang Wu; Shang of Chung Shan was under the influence of Wei Yi and Yen Ch’ang; and Lord K’ang of Sung was under the influence of T’ang Yang and T’ien Pu Li. <margin>These were the wicked helpers who led unsuccessful rulers to ruin.</margin> Now, these six rulers had been under bad influences. Consequently, their states were ruined and they were executed; their ancestral temples were destroyed and their descendants were wiped out. <note>In ancient China, the destruction of an ancestral temple symbolized the total end of a family line and its political legitimacy.</note> The leaders and their subjects were scattered, and the common people were left homeless. Today, the whole world points to these six rulers as the most greedy and disruptive of people.

Now, how can rulers find security? They can find it by following the proper path. And a person will naturally follow the right path when they are under good influence. Therefore, capable rulers focus their efforts on choosing the right men to advise them, even if they are not as careful in managing every detail of the administration themselves. In contrast, incompetent rulers exhaust their bodies and waste their energy; they strain their minds and obsess over every thought, yet their states only fall into greater danger and they themselves suffer more humiliation. 

It is not that these six rulers did not value their states or that they held their own lives cheap. It is simply that they did not understand the relative importance of things. Because of the bad influences surrounding them, their sense of what truly matters became distorted.

This principle applies not only to states but also to individuals. If a person chooses friends who love magnanimity <note>original: "magnanimity" (Ren)—the Confucian/Mohist virtue of human-heartedness or benevolence.</note> and righteousness <note>original: "righteousness" (Yi)—doing what is morally right and just.</note>, and who are careful and respectful, then that person's family will naturally become more prosperous, their own life will be more peaceful, and their reputation will become more honorable every day. In their career as an official, they will be properly qualified for their duties.

<summary>This page concludes the "Dyeing" metaphor by listing rulers ruined by bad advisors and arguing that a leader's primary duty is the careful selection of virtuous companions, as influence shapes both political fate and personal character.</summary>

<keywords>Influence, Dyeing, Mentorship, Chih Kuo, Lord K'ang of Sung, Statecraft, Magnanimity, Righteousness, Virtue</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the chapter "On Dyeing," finishing the list of historical figures who represent good and bad influences and summarizing the core metaphor of the essay.</meta>

<page-num>12</page-num>

# ON DYEING

Examples of such men are Tuan Kan Mu <note>original: Tuan Kan Mu was a pupil of Tse Hsia (Zixia), who was a noted disciple of Confucius.</note>, Ch’intse <note>original: Ch'intse was a disciple of Motse.</note>, and Fu Yüeh <note>original: Fu Yüeh is a great virtuous man who rose from a laborer to become a high-ranking minister under the Shang dynasty.</note>.

On the contrary, if a person chooses friends who are nothing but arrogant and quarrelsome, and who only pretend to be close, that person’s family will naturally fall into poverty, their life will be in greater danger, and their reputation will become more dishonorable every day. Such a person will not be fit to hold public office. Examples of these negative influences are Tse Hsi, Yi Ya, and Shu Tiao <note>These three were historical figures famous for their manipulative or corrupt behavior in the courts of ancient China.</note>.

An ancient Ode says: "One must choose what to be dyed with." <note>This likely refers to a lost poem from the *Classic of Poetry*.</note> To be careful about what influences you are "dyed" by—or "tinged" with—is the central theme of this essay.

<summary>This page concludes the argument that a person's character and success are determined by the company they keep, using historical figures as examples and quoting an ancient poem to reinforce the metaphor of dyeing.</summary>

# CHAPTER 4 <note>original: IV</note>

## ON THE NECESSITY OF STANDARDS

Motse <note>also known as Mozi, a philosopher of the 5th century BCE</note> said: To accomplish anything whatsoever, one must have standards. No one has ever accomplished anything without them. The leaders fulfilling their duties as generals and government advisors have their standards. Even the craftsmen performing their tasks also have their standards. Artisans make square objects according to the carpenter's square and circular objects according to the compasses; they draw straight lines with the carpenter's chalk-line and find the vertical perpendicular by using a plumb weight <note>original: pendulum</note>. All artisans, whether skilled or unskilled, use these standards. The only difference is that skilled workers are accurate. Though unskilled laborers may not achieve total precision, they still do much better by following these standards than they would without them. Thus, all artisans follow standards in their work.

Now, the government of the world <note>original: empire</note> and the governments of the large states do not observe their standards. This shows that the rulers are even less wise than the craftsmen.

What, then, should be taken as the proper standard for government? How will it do for everyone to imitate <note>The text ends here mid-sentence, continuing on the next page. In the original Chinese, the word for "imitate" is the same word used for "standard" (法, *fǎ*).</note>

***

<note>original: This chapter seems to be an appendix or summary of the central themes in chapters 14–16 on "Universal Love" and chapters 26–28 on the "Will of Heaven."</note>

<note>original: Only four standards are mentioned in the text above. In the "Record of Trades" (*K'ao Kung Chi*) section of the *Rites of Zhou* (*Chou Li*), a fifth standard is specified which is omitted here: "finding the level with water."</note>

<note>original: In Chinese, the word for "standard" (法, *fǎ*) is the same word used for "imitate" or "model after." To avoid being repetitive, the translator has used "imitate" in the final sentence.</note>

<summary>Motse argues that just as craftsmen require tools like the square and compass to produce quality work, government requires objective standards to function effectively—standards which he claims the rulers of his time lacked.</summary>

<keywords>Motse, Mozi, standards, Fa, craftsmanship, governance, Rites of Zhou, political philosophy, logic</keywords>

--- Page 28 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that human models—parents, teachers, and rulers—are often flawed and therefore unsuitable as absolute standards for governance, proposing instead the "Will of Heaven" as the only impartial guide.</meta>

<page-num>14</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...his parents? There are numerous parents in the world, but few are truly benevolent <note>original: "magnanimous." The Chinese term is *rén*, referring to the core Confucian and Mohist virtue of humaneness or benevolence.</note>. For everyone to imitate their parents is to imitate the unbenevolent. Imitating the unbenevolent cannot be said to be following the proper standard.

How would it work for everyone to follow their teacher? There are numerous teachers in the world, but few are benevolent. For everyone to imitate their teacher is to imitate the unbenevolent. Imitating the unbenevolent cannot be taken as following the proper standard.

How would it work for everyone to imitate their ruler? There are many rulers in the world, but few are benevolent. For everyone to imitate the ruler is to imitate the unbenevolent. Imitating the unbenevolent cannot be taken as following the right standard. So then, neither the parents, nor the teacher, nor the ruler should be accepted as the ultimate standard in government.

What, then, should be taken as the standard in government? Nothing is better than following Heaven <note>original: *Tiān*, referring to the supreme moral force or divine sky-authority.</note>. Heaven is all-inclusive and impartial in its activities, abundant and unceasing in its blessings, and lasting and untiring in its guidance. And so, when the sage-kings <note>The legendary virtuous rulers of ancient China, such as Yao, Shun, and Yu.</note> had accepted Heaven as their standard, they measured every action and enterprise by Heaven. What Heaven desired, they would carry out; what Heaven detested <note>original: "abominated"</note>, they refrained from.

Now, what is it that Heaven desires, and what is it that it detests? Certainly, Heaven desires to have people benefit and love one another, and it detests having them hate and harm one another. How do we know that Heaven desires to have people love and benefit one another and detests having them hate and harm one another? Because it... <note>The sentence ends mid-thought and continues on the following page.</note>

<summary>Motse argues that because parents, teachers, and rulers are often morally flawed, they cannot serve as the ultimate standard for society; instead, one must look to Heaven, which provides a model of impartial love and universal benefit.</summary>
<keywords>standards (fǎ), Heaven (Tiān), benevolence (rén), sage-kings, impartial love, governance, Mohism</keywords>

<page-num>15</page-num>
<header>ON THE NECESSITY OF STANDARDS</header>

Heaven loves and benefits all people everywhere <note>original: "universally"</note>. How do we know that Heaven loves and benefits all people everywhere? Because it claims all as its own and accepts offerings from all. All states in the world, large or small, are cities of Heaven, and all people, young or old, noble <note>original: "honourable"</note> or humble, are its subjects; for they all graze oxen and sheep, feed dogs and pigs, and prepare clean wine and cakes <note>These sacrificial cakes were of a specific type, made of millet and glutinous rice.</note> to sacrifice to Heaven. Does this not mean that Heaven claims all and accepts offerings from all? Since Heaven does claim all and accepts offerings from all, what then can make us say that it does not desire for people to love and benefit one another? Therefore, Heaven will bless those who love and benefit others. Heaven will curse those who hate and harm others, for it is said that he who murders the innocent will be struck by misfortune. How else can we explain the fact that people, murdering each other, will be cursed by Heaven? Thus we are certain that Heaven desires to have people love and benefit one another and detests <note>original: "abominates"</note> having them hate and harm one another.

The ancient sage-kings—Yü, T'ang, Wen, and Wu <note>These are the famous sage-kings of the Three Dynasties: the Hsia, Shang, and Chou. See the Chronological Table in the companion volume, *Motse, the Neglected Rival of Confucius*, for their dates and historical setting.</note>—loved the people of the world everywhere, leading them to reverence Heaven and worship the spirits. Their benefits to the people were many. Consequently, Heaven blessed them, establishing them as emperors; and all the feudal lords of the empire showed them respect. (On the other hand) the wicked...

<summary>This page argues that Heaven is a universal authority that desires mutual love among humans, evidenced by the fact that it accepts sacrifices from all and historically rewarded the benevolent sage-kings.</summary>

kings Chieh, Chow, Yu, and Li <note>These four kings are traditional Chinese archetypes of tyranny whose moral failures were said to have caused the collapse of their respective dynasties.</note>, hated everyone in the world, leading the people to curse Heaven and mock the spirits. Consequently, Heaven brought disaster <note>original: "calamity"</note> upon them, taking away their empires and their lives; and future generations <note>original: "posterity"</note> have condemned them to this day. Chieh, Chow, Yu, and Li, then, are those who committed evil and were struck by disasters.

## CHAPTER 5 <note>This and the following chapters appear to be supplements to Chapters 20–22, which discuss the "Economy of Expenditure."</note>

### THE SEVEN CAUSES OF ANXIETY

Motse <note>The philosopher Mozi, founder of Mohism.</note> said: There are seven causes of worry to a state, and they are: 

1. When the outer and the inner city walls cannot be defended; 
2. When an enemy state is approaching and yet one’s neighbors do not come to the rescue; 
3. When the resources of the people have all been spent on useless projects and gifts are all squandered upon incompetent men; when the people’s resources are exhausted without producing any profit and the treasury is emptied by supporting idle hangers-on; 
4. When the officials care only for their salaries, and the traveling scholars <note>original: "sophists"</note> value only their personal connections; and when subordinates do not dare to remonstrate <note>To "remonstrate" means to formally protest or point out the mistakes of a superior, particularly a ruler.</note> against the oppressive laws the ruler has made; 
5. When the ruler is overconfident in his own wisdom and consults no one; when he feels he is secure and makes no preparations against attack; and when he does not realize that he must be vigilant while neighbors are plotting against him; 
6. When those who are trusted are not loyal, and those who are loyal are not trusted; 
7. and when the crops are not sufficient for food and the ministers cannot be held accountable; and when rewards fail to make the people happy and punishments fail to make them afraid. 

With these seven causes present in the maintenance of the state, the

---

<note>2. There is a remarkable similarity between the Greek *sophist* and the Chinese *yu k’o* (游客, literally "itinerant scholars" or "traveling persuaders"). In both cases, they began as traveling teachers of wisdom, including the art of rhetoric and statecraft, but degenerated into mere word-players or even worse. See *Motse, the Neglected Rival of Confucius*, chapter 1.</note>

<summary>Motse outlines seven specific political, economic, and social failures—ranging from poor defenses and wasteful spending to government corruption—that lead to the downfall of a state.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, Mozi, Seven Causes of Anxiety, political theory, statecraft, economy, sophists, yu k'o, defense, governance</keywords>

the state will perish, and, in the defense of a city, the city will be reduced to ruin by the approaching enemy. Wherever these seven causes are found, the country will face disaster.

Now, the five grains <gloss>1</gloss> are the people's mainstay and the primary source of the ruler's revenue. When the people lose their means of support, the ruler cannot collect any revenue either. And without food, the people will not maintain order. Therefore, food supplies should be secured, land should be cultivated, and government spending must be cut down. When all five grains are harvested, all five tastes <gloss>2</gloss> will be served to the ruler; when they are not all harvested, the five tastes will not be fully provided.

The failure of one grain is called **dearth** <note>original: 饉 - *jǐn*</note>; the failure of two grains is called **scarcity** <note>original: 旱 - *hàn*</note>; the failure of three grains is called **calamity** <note>original: 凶 - *xiōng*</note>; the failure of four grains is called **extreme want** <note>original: 饋 - *kuì*</note>; and the failure of all five grains is called **famine** <note>original: 饑 - *jī*</note>. 

When the country is in a state of dearth, all the salaries of the officials below the rank of minister will be reduced by one-fifth. In a state of scarcity, they will be reduced by two-fifths. In a time of calamity, they will be reduced by three-fifths. In a state of extreme want, they will be reduced by four-fifths. Finally, when famine is in the country, there will be no salaries paid beyond basic food rations. 

Therefore, when famine and dearth strike a country, the ruler will omit three of the five traditional items of sacrifice <note>Ceremonial offerings made to ancestors or spirits.</note>. The officials will suspend their court sessions, the scholars will not go to school, and the lord will not put on his formal robes to give audience to his subjects. Even envoys from other...

<gloss>1</gloss> The five grains are traditionally: (1) rice, (2) millet, (3) panicled millet <note>original: "millet of a different variety"</note>, (4) wheat, and (5) soybeans.
<gloss>2</gloss> The five tastes include bitter, spicy <note>original: "hot"</note>, sour, sweet, and salty. Ordinarily, this term is used figuratively to mean elaborate cooking and sumptuous, high-quality food.

# THE SEVEN CAUSES OF ANXIETY

Feudal lords and messengers from neighboring states are entertained with basic cooked food only, and it is not a lavish feast. The extra side-horses for carriage teams are removed, and the walkways in the palace are not weeded. Horses are no longer fed with grain, nor are the concubines and maids dressed in silk. This is the sign of extreme scarcity.

Now, if a woman carrying her child while drawing water from a well were to drop the child in, she would naturally try her hardest to save it. Yet, famine and food shortages are a much greater disaster than the loss of one child. Should there not be an even greater effort to prevent them? People are gentle and kind when the harvest is good, but they become selfish and cruel when it is bad. How can they be blamed for this? When many people produce but few consume, there will never be a bad year; conversely, when few produce but many consume, there can never be a good year. Therefore, it is said: a scarcity of supplies should encourage the study of the agricultural seasons, and a lack of food requires strict economy in spending.

The ancients produced wealth according to the seasons. They identified the sources of wealth before they harvested the products, and so they had plenty. Could even the ancient sage-kings guarantee that the <term>five grains</term> <note>The five staple crops of ancient China, traditionally listed as hemp, millet, rice, wheat, and beans.</note> would always ripen and be harvested, or that floods and droughts would never occur? Yet, under their rule, no one froze or starved. Why was this? It was because they made full use of the natural seasons and were frugal in their own living expenses. The *History of Hsia* says that a great flood <note>original: "the Deluge"</note> lasted seven years during the time of King Yü, and the *History of Yin* <margin>Yin is another name for the Shang Dynasty (c. 1600–1046 BCE).</margin> tells that a drought occurred...

<summary>Motse argues that famine is a preventable disaster caused by poor economic management rather than just nature, urging rulers to practice frugality and seasonal planning. He illustrates this by describing how even the legendary sage-kings faced natural disasters but avoided mass starvation through wise resource management.</summary>

...the drought during the reign of King T'ang lasted for five years. These are the extremes of disasters. Yet the people were not frozen or starved. Why was this so? The reason lies in diligent production and thrifty consumption. Therefore, famine and scarcity cannot be prepared for unless there are stored grains in the granaries, and justice cannot be maintained against the unjust unless there are ready weapons in the armory. One cannot defend oneself unless the inner and outer city walls are in good repair, and one cannot meet emergencies unless his plans are well thought out. 

Thus, Ch'ing Chi <note>Ch'ing Chi was a powerful man in the state of Wu. He was induced to take the journey mentioned here and was murdered on the way.</note> was unprepared, and he should not have set out on his journey. Chieh made no preparations against T'ang and was sent into exile. And Chow made no preparations against Wu and was executed. Now, Chieh and Chow were both emperors in rank and possessed the whole empire, yet they both perished at the hands of rulers of states that were only a hundred <term>li</term> <note>A li is equal to approximately 1/3 mile.</note> square. What is the reason for this? It is because they depended on their rank and wealth and made no preparations. 

Therefore, preparation is what a country should emphasize. Supply is the treasure of a country, armament is its claws, and the city walls are the stronghold of its self-defense. These three items are the essentials for the existence of a state.

(The present rulers) squander great amounts of wealth to reward the undeserving, empty the treasury to acquire carriages and horses, and exhaust the laborers to build palaces and provide amusements. Upon their death, they even demand thick coffins and many layers of clothing and fur coats for their burial.

<summary>This page emphasizes that national survival depends on diligent preparation, including stored food and military readiness, rather than wealth or rank. Rulers build elaborate porches and pavilions for themselves while they are alive, and magnificent tombs for when they are dead. Even before the rulers are fully satisfied with their amusements, the hardship has already become unbearable for their subjects. Furthermore, food is what the sages <note>The "sages" refer to the idealized wise rulers of ancient Chinese tradition whom Motse frequently cites as examples of virtue.</note> valued most. The history of the Zhou <note>original: "Chou"</note> Dynasty says: “Without a three-year supply of food in storage, a state cannot truly be a state, as it is in danger of losing its sovereignty. If a family does not have enough food stored to last for three years, its children can no longer be its children, for they are in danger of being abandoned or sold to others.” This, then, is what constitutes the necessary preparation of a country.

# CHAPTER VI

## INDULGENCE IN EXCESS

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: Before the art of building houses and palaces was known, early people stayed near mounds and lived in caves. This environment was damp and harmful to their health. Consequently, the sage-kings <note>The "sage-kings" here refer to the legendary "Nest Builders" (Youchaoshi), who are traditionally considered the first teachers of architecture in the fourth millennium BCE. See *Mozi, the Neglected Rival of Confucius*, chapter 1.</note> built houses and palaces. The guiding principles for these buildings were as follows: The house shall be built high enough to avoid dampness and moisture; the walls shall be thick enough to keep out the wind and cold; the roof shall be strong enough to withstand snow, frost, rain, and dew; and the walls within the palace shall be high enough to maintain the proper separation between men and women <note>original: "propriety of the sexes"</note>. These standards are sufficient; any expenditure of money or energy that does not provide additional usefulness <term>utility</term> shall not be allowed. When city walls are repaired using regular labor, the people may feel tired, but they are not driven to exhaustion. When taxes are collected according to established custom, the people may lose some of their money, but they feel no bitterness. The real suffering of the people does not come from these necessary works; it comes from heavy taxes levied for extravagance.

The sage-kings built houses and palaces to improve living conditions, not for visual pleasure. They made clothes, hats, belts, and shoes to protect the body, not for the sake of novelty. They practiced frugality <note>original: "thrifty"</note> themselves and taught the people to do the same. As a result, the people of the entire world became orderly, and there was enough wealth to go around.

<summary>Mozi argues that the ancient sage-kings established architecture and clothing solely for protection and social order, rather than for luxury. He concludes that state spending is only justified when it provides practical utility, and that true social order stems from such frugality.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, sage-kings, Nest Builders, architecture, utility, taxation, frugality, social order</keywords>

--- Page 37 ---
<meta>This page continues the critique of extravagant rulers, contrasting their wasteful construction of palaces with the utilitarian standards of the ancient sage-kings. It then transitions to the history and proper purpose of clothing.</meta>

for use. When the present rulers build their residences, they act quite differently from this. They tax the people heavily, robbing them of their means of livelihood, in order to have their palaces covered with porches and pavilions in various designs and adorned with paintings and sculpture. When the ruler builds his palaces in this fashion, his assistants naturally imitate him. Consequently, there is not sufficient wealth to prepare against famine and scarcity <note>original: "dearth"</note> or to relieve orphans and widows. As a result, the people become more unruly as the state becomes poorer. If the rulers sincerely desire to have the empire orderly and hate to see it in disorder, they must not indulge in building such houses and palaces.

Before clothing was known, primitive people wore coats of furs and belts of straw. These were neither light and warm in winter nor light and cool in summer. The sage-king <note>This refers to Huang Ti, the "Yellow Emperor" (traditionally ruled 2697–2597 BCE), whose wife is credited with teaching women the art of raising silkworms. See *Mozi, the Neglected Rival of Confucius*, chapter 1.</note> thought this did not satisfy the needs of humanity. Therefore, he taught the women to produce silk and flax and to weave cloth and linen, so as to make clothing for the people. 

The guiding principles for clothing were these: In winter, the underwear shall be made of spun silk so as to be light and warm. In summer, it shall be made of coarse flax <term>linen</term> so as to be light and cool. This is sufficient. Therefore, the sages made their clothes just to fit their stature and size, and not for the purpose of pleasing the senses or to dazzle the common people. In that age, durable carts and gentle horses were not valued, nor were sculpture and adornments prized. What is the reason for this? The reason lies in...

This was the nature of their leadership. The people had enough to live on at home to meet the challenges of either drought or flood, scarcity <note>original: "dearth"</note> or famine. Why? Because they understood the needs of self-support and paid little attention to external appearance. So, the people were frugal and orderly, and the ruler was thrifty and easily supported. The storehouses and treasuries were full, prepared against misfortunes. Armor and weapons were not left to rust from disuse, and the soldiers and the people were not exhausted; they were ready to punish those who would not submit. In this way, the ruler could become a supreme leader <note>original: "tyrant." In this context, the term refers to a "Hegemon" (Ba), a ruler who holds undisputed authority over other states, rather than a cruel dictator.</note> over the whole world.

The present rulers are quite different from this when they make their clothes. Even though they have what is warm and light in winter and what is light and cool in summer, they still tax the people heavily. They rob them of their means of livelihood in order to have elaborately embroidered and magnificent garments. Hooks are made of gold, and ornaments on the belts <term>girdles</term> consist of pearls and jade. Women are employed to do the embroidery and men to do the carving. All these are merely for the decoration of the body. They really add little to its warmth. Wealth is squandered and energy is wasted for nothing <note>original: "naught"</note>. Therefore, when clothing is made for a brilliant appearance rather than for the body, the people will become unprincipled <note>original: "wicked"</note> and rebellious, and the ruler will become extravagant and deaf to good counsel. It will be impossible to keep the country out of disorder. If the rulers sincerely desire the world to be orderly and hate to see it in chaos, they must not indulge in making excessive clothing.

[The sage-king] taught the people to attend to farming and to plant trees to provide them with food. The sole purpose of securing food is to increase energy, satisfy hunger, strengthen the body, and fill <note>original: "appease"</note> the stomach. He was frugal in spending wealth and simple in his living habits; as a result, the people became wealthy and the country became orderly.

With the present rulers, everything is different. They tax the people heavily in order to enjoy elaborate meals of various meats, fish, and turtles cooked in many different ways. The lord of a large state is served a hundred courses, and the lord of a small state is served dozens. These dishes cover a table space of ten square feet. One's eyes cannot even see all the dishes, one's hands cannot handle them all, and one's mouth cannot taste them all. In winter, the food freezes, and in summer, it spoils <note>original: "sour"</note>. Because the ruler serves himself in this way, his assistants naturally imitate him. Thus, the rich and those of high rank are wasteful and extravagant, while the lonely and destitute <note>original: "solitary and miserable"</note> are left hungry and cold. It is impossible to keep such a state from falling into chaos. If the rulers sincerely desire the empire to be orderly and hate to see it in disorder, they must not indulge in excessive eating and drinking.

Before primitive people knew how to make boats and carts, they could neither carry heavy loads nor travel great distances. Therefore, the sage-king¹ created boats and carts to assist the people. These boats and carts were made to be durable and convenient so that they could carry a great deal and travel far. Such an undertaking requires little wealth but produces many benefits. ...it agreeable and convenient. The people were not exhausted, yet the ruler’s needs were all supplied. Consequently, the people were drawn to him.

When the rulers of today build boats and carts, it is quite different. Even after making them durable and convenient, they still tax the people heavily to decorate them. The carts are decorated with intricate embroidery, and the boats are adorned with carvings. Women are forced to stop weaving to produce the embroidery, leaving the people without enough clothing to stay warm. Meanwhile, men must abandon their farming to do the carving, which leads to the people going hungry. When a ruler builds such extravagant boats and carts for himself, his officials naturally imitate him. Therefore, the people suffer from both hunger and cold, which drives them to turn to crime <note>original: "wickedness"</note>. Widespread crime is followed by harsh punishment, and harsh punishment throws the country into chaos. If rulers sincerely want the empire to be orderly and hate to see it in disorder, they must not indulge themselves by building excessive boats and carts.

Every creature living between Heaven and earth and within the "four seas" <margin>This refers to the early Chinese conception of the world's boundaries. These are the two complementary forces in Chinese philosophy—Yin represents the passive, cool, and feminine, while Yang represents the active, warm, and masculine.</note> Even the greatest sages cannot change this. ---

<note>original: "This harmony is one of two principles in constant strife against each other. Compare this metaphysical concept of reality with the Pythagorean ἁρμονίη (harmony) and the Heraclitean 'opposite tension'."</note>

<summary>Motse critiques modern rulers for taxing the poor to decorate their transport, arguing that such extravagance leads to poverty and crime. He then begins to explain that all life is governed by the unchanging harmony of Heaven, Earth, Yin, and Yang.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, Sage-kings, extravagance, Yin and Yang, Four Seas, social order, ancient Chinese philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page concludes the argument regarding the natural order of the sexes and transitions into a warning against excess in five specific areas of life: shelter, clothing, food, transportation, and family structure. It contrasts the moderate practices of the ancient sage-kings with the destructive indulgences of modern rulers.</meta>

<page-num>27</page-num>
<header>INDULGENCE IN EXCESS</header>

...and women, birds and animals, and the natural division between the sexes. Even the early kings could not escape these basic laws of nature. Even the great sages of ancient times had to maintain a household. <note>The traditional Chinese household was a large unit. Aside from relatives and dependents, the core household consisted of the master, the mistress, and a group of concubines and maids; their number increased according to the lord's rank and wealth.</note> However, because their behavior did not cause resentment, there was no dissatisfaction among the people. Since women were not kept as unmarried servants within the palace, there were few bachelors in the empire. Because women were not hoarded in the palace and there were few bachelors in the countryside, the empire’s population was large.

In contrast, the current rulers of large states keep as many as a thousand women in their households, and those of small states keep as many as a hundred. Consequently, most men in the empire are without wives, and many women are without husbands. The natural roles of men and women are disrupted <note>original: "prevented"</note>, and the population decreases. If rulers truly desire the population to be large and hate to see it shrink, they must not indulge in keeping too many women.

Now, in these five areas <note>These five areas include (1) shelter, (2) clothing, (3) food, (4) boats and vehicles, and (5) sexual relations or household size.</note>, the sages are moderate and frugal, while wicked men are indulgent and excessive. Moderation and economy bring prosperity, while indulgence and excess lead to destruction. One must not overindulge in these five things. When husband and wife do not indulge in excess, Heaven and Earth will remain in harmony. When wind and rain are not excessive, the <term>five grains</term> <note>original: "five grains." These are the five staple crops of ancient China, usually identified as rice, wheat, beans, and two types of millet.</note> will ripen. And when people do not indulge in excessive clothing, their bodies will be comfortable.

<summary>Mozi argues that rulers hoarding women in large harems leads to a declining population and social instability. He concludes that moderation in five key areas—shelter, clothing, food, transport, and family—is essential for natural harmony and the prosperity of the state.</summary>

# CHAPTER VII
## THE THREEFOLD ARGUMENT <note>This chapter serves as a response to objections regarding Motse's "Condemnation of Music" (found in Chapters 32–34). It functions like a philosophical appendix where the author addresses criticisms of his core doctrine.</note>

Ch'eng Fan <note>Ch'eng Fan was a scholar who studied both Confucianism and Moism, the philosophy of Motse.</note> asked Motse: "Sir, you say the sage-kings did not have music. But in ancient times, when the feudal lords were tired of attending to government business, they found relaxation in the music of bells and drums. When the ministers and officials were tired of their duties, they found recreation in the music of the *yü* <term>yü: a Chinese hand organ consisting of thirty-six reed pipes</term> and the *shê* <term>shê: a horizontal stringed instrument similar to a psaltery, with twenty-five strings</term>. And the farmers plowed the fields in spring, weeded them in summer, reaped the harvest in autumn, and stored the grain in winter; afterward, they would enjoy the music of jars and vases <note>original: "music of jars and vases." These earthenware items were a recognized category of ancient Chinese musical instruments.</note>.

"Sir, you say the sage-kings did not have music. To deny them this would be like keeping a horse under its yoke without ever releasing it, or keeping a bow drawn and never unstringing it. Is this not impossible for an ordinary human being?"

Motse replied: "In ancient times, the sage-kings Yao and Shun lived in simple huts, and yet they established codes of social conduct <note>original: "propriety"</note> and composed music <note>This indicates that these works were produced under their authority and sponsorship, not necessarily that they wrote them personally.</note>. King T'ang sent the tyrant Chieh into exile on the ocean and established himself as the ruler of the empire. Having achieved success and having no cause for anxiety, he expanded upon the music of the former kings..."

<summary>Ch'eng Fan challenges Motse’s rejection of music, arguing that all people—from rulers to farmers—require music as a necessary form of rest and recreation. Motse begins his rebuttal by acknowledging that the ancient sage-kings did indeed establish codes of music and ritual.</summary>

<keywords>Motse, Ch'eng Fan, Moism, music, sage-kings, Yao, Shun, T'ang, Chieh, yü, shê, recreation, social conduct</keywords>

--- Page 43 ---
<meta>This page continues the dialogue between Ch'eng Fan and Motse regarding the necessity of music. Motse is responding to the idea that ancient sage-kings utilized music for relaxation and governance, arguing instead that there is an inverse relationship between musical complexity and political effectiveness.</meta>

# THREEFOLD ARGUMENT

Tang the Successful added his own composition, called "The Salvation,"¹ to the music of the former kings, and also established the "Chiu Chao" <note>original: "Chiu Chao." This refers to the "Nine Summonings," a series of musical movements traditionally associated with the ancient Emperor Shun.</note>. King Wu conquered the Yin dynasty, executed the tyrant Zhou, and established himself as the ruler of the empire. Having achieved success and having no cause for anxiety, he added his own composition, called "Hsiang" <note>original: "Hsiang." A musical dance commemorating King Wu’s military victory over the Shang/Yin dynasty.</note>, to the music of the former kings. King Ch'eng of Zhou again added his own composition, named "Tsou Yü" <note>original: "Tsou Yü." Named after a mythical creature of virtue, this music celebrated the peace of King Ch'eng’s reign.</note>, to the music of the previous kings. 

The reign of King Ch'eng was not as effective as that of King Wu; King Wu's was not as effective as that of Tang the Successful; and Tang the Successful's was not as effective as those of Yao and Shun. Therefore, the ruler who has the more elaborate music has the less efficient government. Judging from this, music is not a tool used to govern the empire.

Ch'eng Fan objected: "Sir, you have said the sage-kings did not have music. But these records show they did. How then can you claim the sage-kings had no music?"

Motse said: The desire of the sage-kings² was to eliminate excess. Eating, of course, is beneficial, but it takes so little intelligence to eat when one is hungry that the effort may be considered negligible. In the same way, the sage-kings had music, but it was so minimal that it may also be considered negligible.

---

¹ This name was given to the composition to commemorate his campaign against Chieh the wicked king, the success of which freed the people from tyranny.
² Certain phrases, clauses, or even passages seem to be missing from the original text here. <note>This indicates a corruption or gap in the ancient Chinese manuscripts available to the translator.</note>

<summary>Motse argues that historical records show a decline in the quality of governance as royal music became more complex and elaborate. He clarifies that while sage-kings had music, it was so minimal and focused on avoiding excess that it was practically nonexistent compared to the luxuries of his own time.</summary>

# BOOK II

## CHAPTER 8

### PROMOTING THE VIRTUOUS (PART 1) <note>original: "Exaltation of the Virtuous." The Chinese word *hsien* (賢) includes more than just moral goodness; it also implies wisdom and talent. It is similar to the Socratic idea that "knowledge is virtue."</note>

> Motse said: Today, all rulers desire their provinces to be wealthy, their people to be numerous, and their government to maintain order. But what they actually get is not wealth but poverty, not a large population but scarcity, and not order but chaos—this is to lose what they desire and obtain what they try to avoid. Why is this? <note>In the text of Motse, a question is often used to transition between two thoughts or to introduce an explanation. This is similar to how listeners provide prompts in Plato’s *Dialogues*.</note>

> Motse said: This is because the rulers have failed to promote the virtuous and to employ capable people in their government. When virtuous people are numerous in the state, order will be stable; when virtuous people are scarce, order will be unstable. Therefore, the primary task of the lords is nothing other than increasing the number of virtuous people.

But how can we increase the number of virtuous people?

> Motse said: Suppose it is desired to increase the number of good archers and chariot drivers <note>original: "drivers." Ancient Chinese education consisted of the "Six Arts": Ritual, Music, Archery, Chariot Driving, Literacy/Composition, and Mathematics. Confucius was a strong supporter of this curriculum, which is comparable to the educational program in Plato’s *Republic*.</note> in the country. It will only...

<summary>Motse argues that the primary cause of a state's failure—poverty and chaos—is the ruler's inability to recruit and empower virtuous and capable leaders. He identifies the "multiplication of the virtuous" as the most essential task for maintaining a stable and prosperous society.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, meritocracy, virtue, hsien, 賢, governance, statecraft, Six Arts, political stability, recruitment</keywords>

# PROMOTING THE VIRTUOUS (PART 1)

It is only natural to enrich, honor, respect, and praise <note>original: "commend"</note> these people; then good archers and good chariot drivers can be expected to flourish in the country. This should be done even more for the virtuous and the excellent—those who are firm in their morality, skilled in speaking, and experienced in governance <note>original: "statecraft"</note>. Since these people are the treasures of the nation and the pillars <note>original: "props"</note> of the state, they should also be enriched, honored, respected, and praised so that they will increase in number.

When the ancient sage-kings <gloss>1</gloss> governed the country, they declared: 
> "The unjust <note>original: "unrighteous"</note> will not be enriched; the unjust will not be honored; the unjust will not be shown favor <gloss>2</gloss>; and the unjust will not be kept close to the ruler." 

Upon hearing this, the wealthy and the high-ranking people of the country all began to think it over, saying: 
> "What I have been relying on was my wealth and high status. Now the Lord <note>In this context, "the Lord" refers to the sovereign ruler.</note> promotes the righteous without discriminating against the poor or the lowly. Therefore, I must not act unjustly." 

Upon hearing this, those who were favored by the ruler also began to think it over, saying: 
> "What I have been relying on was personal favor. Now the Lord promotes the righteous without discriminating against those who have been, until now..."

---

<gloss>1</gloss> Later sections of the text show that Motse is referring specifically to the legendary kings Yao, Shun, Yü, T'ang, Wen, and Wu. (See page 38, note 1).

<gloss>2</gloss> The Chinese word used here is *Ch'in* (親). The common meaning of this word is "relatives" or "to be related to." Based on that meaning, this clause would read: "relatives, if they are unjust, will not be recognized as family." Similarly, the sentence that follows would be: "Upon hearing this, the relatives also began to think, saying, 'What I relied on was my family relationship. Now the Lord promotes the righteous without discriminating against those who are unrelated. Therefore, I must not act unjustly.'" However, that phrasing is awkward and ruins the balanced rhythm of the sentences, which is a key feature of Motse’s writing. The translation used here—using "favor"—captures the general meaning while keeping the text readable.

<summary>Motse argues that rulers must incentivize virtue and talent just as they do technical skills like archery, asserting that even the wealthy and well-connected will turn toward righteousness if they see the ruler favors merit over status.</summary>

...neglected. Therefore, I must not act unjustly <note>original: "do unrighteousness"</note>.” Upon hearing this, those close to the ruler began to deliberate, saying: “I used to depend on my personal intimacy with the ruler. Now the Lord <note>The sovereign ruler.</note> promotes the righteous without discriminating against those who are distant. Now, what is the reason for all this?

It is only through material rewards that a superior can employ his subordinates, and it is only through the art of governance <note>original: "statecraft"</note> that subordinates can serve their lord. Take, for example, a rich man who built his walls high and left only one gate. When a burglar entered, the man closed the gate and searched for him, and the burglar had no way to escape. Why? Because the man held the position of advantage <gloss>1</gloss>.

Therefore, in administering the government, the ancient sage-kings <note>The legendary wise rulers of China’s past, such as Yao and Shun.</note> gave high rank to those of moral excellence and honored the virtuous. If a person was capable, even a farmer or a craftsman <note>original: "artisan"</note> would be employed. They were appointed to high rank, paid a generous salary <note>original: "liberal emoluments"</note>, trusted with important responsibilities, and empowered to issue final orders. For, if their rank were not...

To bestow these three honors upon the virtuous is not so much to reward virtue as it is to ensure the success of the government <gloss>1</gloss>. Therefore, ranks should be standardized based on virtue, tasks assigned according to one's office, and rewards given according to the labor performed. When pay is distributed in proportion to achievements, officials cannot remain in constant honor, and the common people need not remain in permanent low status <note>original: "eternal humility"</note>. If a person is capable, promote him; if incapable, demote him. Give prominence to public approval and set aside personal grievances <note>original: "private grudges"</note> in the matter of selecting men. This, then, is the principle.
