### CHAPTER IX

### EXALTATION OF THE VIRTUOUS (II) <note>original: "Exaltation of the Virtuous" (Shang Xian). This refers to the Mohist principle of meritocracy, where individuals are promoted based on their character and ability rather than their social status or family ties.</note>

Motse <note>Also known as Mozi, a philosopher who lived during the Warring States period of ancient China and founded the school of Mohism.</note> said: Now, in caring for the people, ruling the state, and governing the country, rulers desire permanency and stability. But why do they not learn that the exaltation of the virtuous is the foundation of government?

How do we know that the exaltation of the virtuous is the foundation of government?

When the honorable and wise run the government, the ignorant and humble remain orderly; but when the ignorant and humble run the government, the honorable and wise become rebellious. Therefore, we know that the exaltation of the virtuous is the foundation of government.

The ancient sage-kings <note>Legendary moral leaders of China's past, such as Yao, Shun, and Yu, whom Motse held up as ideal rulers.</note> greatly emphasized the exaltation of the virtuous and the employment of the capable. Without showing special favor to relatives, the rich and honored, or the attractive, they exalted and promoted the virtuous, enriched and honored them, and made them governors and leaders. The vicious <note>original: "vicious". In this context, it refers to those who are wicked or lack moral character, rather than simply being aggressive.</note> they held back and banished, dispossessed and degraded, and made them laborers and servants. 

Because of this, everyone was encouraged by rewards and deterred by punishments, and they competed with one another to attain virtue. Thus, the virtuous multiplied and the wicked decreased in number. This is what it means to exalt the virtuous. Then the sage-kings watched their words and observed their conduct, discovered their capabilities, and carefully assigned them to their offices. This is what it means to employ the capable. Accordingly, those who were capable of governing—

<summary>Motse argues that promoting the virtuous and employing the capable is the essential foundation of stable government, as seen in the practices of the ancient sage-kings who prioritized merit over family ties or appearance.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, meritocracy, sage-kings, virtue, governance, social order, employment of the capable</keywords>

--- Page 51 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument from Chapter IX, detailing how appointing virtuous people to different levels of government—from local districts to the national court—leads to economic prosperity, social order, and successful diplomacy.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>EXALTATION OF THE VIRTUOUS (II)</header>
<page-num>37</page-num>

...those capable of governing the country were appointed to govern the country; those capable of administering the court were appointed to administer the court; and those capable of managing the districts were appointed to manage the districts. Consequently, all those in charge of the country, the court, and the districts were the most virtuous in the land.

When a virtuous person rules the country, they start the day early and stay up late, hearing legal cases and attending to government business. As a result, the country is well-governed and the laws are administered fairly. When a virtuous person administers the court, they wake up early and stay up late, collecting taxes from border passes and markets, and on products from the mountains, forests, rivers, and fields to fill the state treasury <note>original: "court"</note>. Consequently, the treasury is full and resources are not wasted. When a virtuous person manages the districts, they go out before sunrise and return after sunset, plowing, sowing, planting, cultivating, and gathering the grain harvests. As a result, grain is plentiful and the people have an ample food supply. 

Therefore, when the country is well-governed, the laws are well-administered; and when the treasury is full, the people are wealthy. For spiritual matters <note>original: "the higher sphere"</note>, the rulers had the means <note>original: "wherewith"</note> to produce wine and cakes for sacrifices and drink offerings <note>original: "libation"</note> to Heaven and the spirits. For relations with foreign countries, they had the means to provide furs and money to maintain friendships with neighboring feudal lords. For the people within their borders, they had the means to feed the hungry and provide rest for the weary. Most importantly, they had the resources to support and honor <note>original: "cherish"</note> the virtuous. Therefore, from above, Heaven and the spirits enrich them; from the outside, the feudal lords submit to them; from within, the people feel affection for them; and the virtuous...

<summary>This page describes the practical benefits of meritocracy, explaining how virtuous officials at all levels of government ensure legal justice, a full treasury, and agricultural abundance.</summary>

--- Page 52 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the "Exaltation of the Virtuous," explaining how the legendary sage-kings applied this principle through specific administrative rules regarding rank and compensation to ensure effective governance.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>38</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...become loyal to them. Because of this, they could find satisfaction in planning and success in execution. In defense they are strong, and in attack they are victorious. Now, the method that enabled the sage-kings of the Three Dynasties—namely Yao, Shun, Yü, T’ang, Wen, and Wu <note>The "Three Dynasties" (the Xia, Shang, and Zhou) and these specific rulers were considered symbols of perfect virtue in ancient China. Yao and Shun were legendary emperors who predated these dynasties.</note>—to rule the empire and lead the feudal lords was nothing other than this principle of the exaltation of the virtuous.

However, if there is only the principle while the technique of its application is not known, then it would still seem to be incomplete. Therefore, three rules should be established. What are these three rules? They are: 

1. When their rank <note>The social and political standing of the virtuous officials.</note> is not high, the people will not show them respect; 
2. When their salaries <note>original: "emoluments"</note> are not generous, the people will not place confidence in them; 
3. When their orders are not final, the people will not stand in awe of them. 

So the ancient sage-kings placed them high in rank, gave them generous salaries, trusted them with important responsibilities, and decreed their orders to be final. All of this was done not merely to reward their subordinates; it was to empower them to fulfill their duties <note>original: "fulfil their trust"</note>.

Thus runs an Ode <note>A reference to the *Classic of Poetry* (Shijing), an ancient collection of Chinese poems often quoted by philosophers to provide traditional authority to their arguments.</note>: 
> "I am instructing you to take the worries and cares of the world as your own; I am teaching the order of ranks for the virtuous and talented. Who can handle..."

***

# EXALTATION OF THE VIRTUOUS (II)

...heat without rinsing his hands (in cold water)?” <gloss>1</gloss> This illustrates how, in the past, rulers could not function without befriending subordinates and assistants. It was as necessary as rinsing one's hands when handling hot objects to protect them.

The ancient sage-kings focused on finding and hiring virtuous men—honoring them with high ranks and granting them land—working tirelessly toward this goal throughout their lives. In return, these virtuous men hoped only to find an enlightened ruler to serve. They exhausted their physical strength <note>original: "powers of the four limbs"—a Chinese idiom for total physical effort</note> to attend to the king’s business, remaining untired to the end of their lives. When there were successes and merits, they were attributed to the emperor. Thus, credit for virtue belonged to the emperor, while complaints and criticisms were directed toward his subordinates. Peace and joy remained with the king, while worries and sorrows were the responsibility of the officials. This was how the ancient sage-kings managed their government. Today's rulers, trying to imitate the ancients, also claim they want to employ the virtuous by promoting them. They give them high ranks, but their salaries <note>original: "emoluments"</note> do not increase proportionally. Now,

To hold a high rank but receive a small salary <note>original: "emoluments"</note> will not inspire the people's confidence. The virtuous would think to themselves: "This is not real respect for me, but only an attempt to use me as a tool." Now, how can people be loyal or affectionate toward their superiors when they are treated only as a means to an end? Therefore, an ancient king said: "He who is too power-hungry in government will not share his responsibilities with others. He who hoards wealth will not offer generous salaries to others." When tasks are not delegated and fair salaries are not paid, one must ask: how would virtuous men ever be drawn to the side of such rulers?

And when the virtuous are not by the ruler’s side, the wicked <note>original: "vicious"</note> will surround them on both sides. When the wicked are in positions of influence, then praise will not be given to the virtuous, and punishment will not be dealt to the evil. If the rulers follow these men in governing their states, rewards will fail to reach the virtuous, and punishment will fail to reach the wicked. When rewards do not reach the virtuous and punishments do not reach the wicked, the good will find no encouragement, and the bad will face no obstacles. 

At home, these wicked people fail in their duty <note>original: "are not filial"</note> to their parents; once they leave their hometown, they show no respect to their elders. They behave without restraint and ignore the social rules regarding interactions between men and women <note>original: "rules of propriety about sexes"</note>. If trusted with the administration of the court, they would steal; if trusted to defend a city, they would start a rebellion. If their lord dies, they would not follow him in death; if the lord is forced to flee the country, they would not join him in exile <margin>1. These were the standard requirements for loyalty under ancient Chinese law and tradition.</margin>. In judging...

<page-num>41</page-num>
<header>EXALTATION OF THE VIRTUOUS (II)</header>

In legal cases, they are not just, and in dividing property, they show favoritism <note>original: "partial"</note>. They are unhelpful in planning and inefficient in execution. They are neither strong in defense nor victorious in attack. The reason the wicked kings of the Three Dynasties—specifically Jie, Zhou, You, and Li <note>original: "Chieh, Chow, Yu, and Li," referring to the legendary "bad" last rulers of early Chinese dynasties</note>—misruled the country and brought ruin to their states was simply because they employed wicked people.

Why is this? It is because they understood trivial matters <note>original: "petty affairs"</note> but were ignorant about things of great importance. When rulers cannot make a coat themselves, they will hire skilled tailors. When they cannot slaughter an ox or a sheep, they will hire skilled butchers. In these two instances, they know they should promote the virtuous and hire capable people for the task. 

But when it comes to the disorder of the country and the danger to the state, they do not realize they should promote the virtuous and hire capable people for government. Instead, they hire their relatives, wealthy people without merit, and those who are simply attractive. But will hiring the unmerited wealthy and the good-looking necessarily result in wise and intelligent leadership? To let such people rule the country is to let the unwise and unintelligent rule the country—and disorder can then be predicted. Furthermore, the rulers are distracted by attractive appearances and show favor to people without actually testing their knowledge. As a result, those who are not capable of leading a hundred men are put in charge of a thousand, and those unable to lead a thousand are put in charge of ten thousand. What is

<page-num>42</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

What is the reason for this? It is because these positions are high in rank and offer great wealth <note>original: "emoluments," meaning the salaries or profits derived from an office</note>. Therefore, the ruler’s personal favorites are chosen for them. However, to make someone who is incapable of ruling a thousand men rule ten thousand is to increase their duty tenfold. The business of government arises daily. It must be attended to every day, yet the day cannot be lengthened ten times over. 

To govern, furthermore, requires knowledge. When a person's knowledge is not increased tenfold, but a tenfold task is assigned to them, the obvious result is that they will attend to one matter and neglect nine others. Even if the task is worked on day and night, it still cannot be executed well. The reason for this is that rulers do not understand that they should promote <note>original: "exalt"</note> the virtuous and employ the capable in government.

Thus, the promotion of the virtuous and the employment of the capable—with the resulting success in government—is presented above in the earlier paragraphs. And the neglect <note>original: "depreciation"</note> of the virtuous—with the resulting confusion in government—is presented here in these paragraphs. If rulers now want to govern their states so that they will be permanent and unshakeable, why do they not learn that promoting the virtuous is the foundation of government?

Furthermore, is this principle merely an idea of Motse? It is the way of the sage-kings and a tenet of the *Chu Nien* <note>original: "Chü Nien"</note>, a book by an ancient king. It is recorded there: "[The King] sought out wise men to protect and aid you." And thus—

...and handed him the government of the realm <note>original: "empire"</note> and the rule over the people.

Why is it that these people, starting in humble positions, arrived at honor, and starting in poverty, arrived at wealth? It is because these rulers understood the importance of promoting <note>original: "exalting"</note> the virtuous and employing the capable in government. Therefore, none of the people were hungry without having food, cold without clothing, tired without rest, or disturbed without peace. Furthermore, the ancient sage-kings, in promoting the virtuous and employing the capable in government, were following the ways of Heaven. Even Heaven does not discriminate between the poor and the rich, the honored and the humble, the distant and the near, or those related and unrelated to those in power. The virtuous were promoted and honored; the wicked <note>original: "vicious"</note> were held back and banished.

Now, who were those who, possessing wealth and position, still strove after virtue and were rewarded? They were the sage-kings of the Three Dynasties, namely, Yao, Shun, Yü, T’ang, Wen, and Wu. <note>These are legendary and historical rulers of the Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties, cited by Motse as perfect examples of leadership.</note> How were they rewarded? When they governed the realm, they loved all the people universally and benefited them, leading them in honoring Heaven and serving the spirits. As they loved and benefited the people, Heaven and the spirits rewarded them, appointing them to be Sons of Heaven <term>T'ien-tzu: a title for the Emperor, implying he rules by divine right or mandate.</term> and parents of the people. Consequently, people praised them, calling them sage-kings even to this day. These, then, were those who, possessing wealth and position, still strove after virtue and were rewarded.

Now, who were those who, possessing wealth and position...

<header>EXALTATION OF THE VIRTUOUS (II) 45</header>

Who were those that held wealth and position, yet practiced evil and were punished? They were the wicked kings of the Three Dynasties,¹ namely, Chieh, Chow, Yu, and Li. <note>original: "Three Dynasties" refers to the Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties. Chieh and Chow are traditionally cited as the tyrannical final rulers of the first two dynasties.</note> How do we know they were the ones? When they governed the realm <note>original: "empire"</note>, they hated all the people without exception <note>original: "inclusively"</note> and oppressed them, leading them to curse Heaven and the spirits. Because they oppressed and destroyed the people, they were punished by Heaven and the spirits; their bodies were mutilated and torn, their children and grandchildren were scattered and driven away, their family lines were ended, and their descendants were wiped out. Consequently, the people spoke out against them, calling them wicked kings even to this day. These, then, are the examples of those who possessed wealth and position, yet practiced evil and were punished.

Now, who were those related to the ruler but lacked virtue and were punished? Count Kun² was of direct royal descent, but he had fallen away from royal virtues. Therefore, he was banished to the wilderness of Yü Yü, where light could not reach him in prison, and the emperor showed him no favor. This is the example of someone who was a relative but lacked virtue and was punished.

Now, who were those employed by Heaven because of their capability? They were Yü, Chi, and Kao T’ao. <note>Yü was the founder of the Xia dynasty; Chi was the legendary ancestor of the Zhou dynasty; Kao T'ao was a famous judge under the sage-kings.</note> How do we know this? It is found in the "Penal Code of Lü," a book of an early king, which states: "The Emperor Yao inquired among his subjects, and complaints were made against the Miaos." <note>The Miaos were an ethnic group often characterized in early Chinese texts as rebellious or "barbarian" outsiders.</note> It says again: "Because the feudal lords were appointed without wisdom, even the widows and widowers are not protected. Dignity is respected only when it is..."

² Kun <note>also spelled Gun</note> is the father of the virtuous Emperor and Great Engineer Yü. He was traditionally punished for failing to control the Great Flood.

Thereupon three chiefs were commissioned to care for and comfort the people: (1) Po Yi delivered the laws and statutes and taught the people with them; (2) Yü subdued the Flood and reclaimed the land, giving names to hills and rivers; (3) Chi stepped down from his rank to sow seeds and encourage good farming <term>husbandry</term>. The people received all the benefits from the achievements of these three chiefs." ¹ This means that the three sages were careful in their speech, vigilant in their conduct, and deep in their thoughts, studying and planning every detail for the benefit of the world. By doing so, they served Heaven on high, and Heaven blessed their virtue. By bestowing these benefits on the people below, the people enjoyed the results long after the sages' lifetimes.

<meta>This page concludes the specific historical examples of the three sage-ministers and transitions into a philosophical defense of their methods, contrasting the lasting virtue of the ancients with the failed, forceful tactics of contemporary rulers.</meta>

<page-num>47</page-num>
<header>EXALTATION OF THE VIRTUOUS (II)</header>

Thus said the ancient kings: "Now, this principle, when applied broadly to govern the world, will not be found lacking <note>original: "slender"</note>; when applied narrowly, it will not be too cumbersome <note>original: "unwieldly"</note>; when practiced with wisdom, it will benefit the people far beyond their own lifetimes." Referring to this principle, the "Eulogy of Chou" <note>original: "Eulogy of Chou" refers to the *Zhou Song*, a section of the *Shing Ching* or Classic of Poetry, used for ancestral temple rituals.</note> sings: "The virtue of the sage shining upon the world is as lofty as Heaven, as wide as the earth, and as high as a mountain. It is unbreakable and infallible; it is as illuminating as the sun and as brilliant as the moon, lasting as long as heaven and earth." This describes how enlightening, all-embracing, deep-rooted, and permanent is the virtue of a sage. Therefore, the virtue of a sage truly encompasses all of heaven and earth.

Now, contemporary rulers want to be lords over the empire and heads of the feudal lords. But how can this be achieved without virtue and righteousness? Their current methods rely on overpowering and intimidating others. But why do they choose this path? It simply drives the people toward death. Life is what people most eagerly desire, and death is what they most greatly dread. When what they desire is denied and what they dread happens so often, rebellion follows. From ancient times to the present, no one has ever been able to rule the empire and lead the feudal lords using such force. Rulers desire to lead the world, to have their ideas prevail everywhere, and to establish their names for future generations <term>posterity</term>. Why, then, do they not realize that the exaltation of the virtuous <note>This refers to the core Mo-ist doctrine of promoting people based on merit and character rather than birth or favoritism.</note> is the very foundation of government? This was the actual practice of the sages.

<summary>This page argues that a ruler's power must be built on virtue rather than force, as history proves that intimidation only leads to death and failure, whereas the "exaltation of the virtuous" creates a lasting, heaven-like governance.</summary>
<keywords>Eulogy of Chou, virtue, sage-kings, righteousness, rule by force, meritocracy, exaltation of the virtuous, political philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page transitions from the general principle of virtue to a practical analogy regarding how rulers should incentivize good behavior, specifically introducing Mozi’s argument for promoting the worthy as the foundation of a stable state.</meta>

# CHAPTER X

## PROMOTING THE VIRTUOUS (PART III)

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: Every ruler in the world desires for their state to be wealthy, for their population to grow, and for their government and legal system to be orderly. However, they do not understand how to govern their states and people by promoting the virtuous. In this, they have truly missed the very foundation of government. 

Can we not demonstrate this point to them using analogies? Suppose that, in governing his state, a feudal lord were to proclaim: "I shall reward and honor everyone who is a skilled archer or chariot driver; I shall punish and shame everyone who is not." If we were then to ask the people of that state who would be happy and who would be afraid, we can naturally assume that the skilled archers and drivers would be happy, while those without those skills would be afraid. 

I have followed this same logic and proposed that rulers should proclaim: "I shall reward and honor all who are loyal and faithful; I shall punish and shame all who are disloyal and unfaithful." If we were then to ask the people who would be happy and who would be afraid, we can naturally assume the loyal and faithful would be happy, while the disloyal and unfaithful would be afraid. Therefore, a state and its people should be governed by promoting the virtuous. In this way, those in the state who do good will be encouraged, and those who do evil will be stopped.

Governing the world, then, seems to consist of encouraging good and preventing evil. But why is it that I hold the examples of Yao, Shun, Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu in such high regard? <note>These names refer to the legendary Sage Kings of Chinese antiquity, whom Mozi viewed as the ultimate examples of moral leadership.</note> ¹

— — —

¹ See above <note>original: "Supra"</note>, p. 38, note 1.

<summary>Mozi argues that the foundation of a successful state is the promotion of virtuous people, using the analogy that people will strive for virtue if it is rewarded just as they would strive for technical skills like archery.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Promoting the Virtuous, governance, Sage Kings, meritocracy, incentives, social order</keywords>

--- Page 63 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for "Promoting the Virtuous" as the foundation of good government. It transitions from the historical example of the Sage Kings to a critique of contemporary rulers who fail to apply the same common-sense meritocracy to statecraft that they apply to simple domestic tasks.</meta>

# EXALTATION OF THE VIRTUOUS (PART III)

Because they managed the government in such a way that those in the empire who did good were encouraged and those who did evil were stopped. Thus, the principle of promoting the virtuous <note>original: "exaltation of the virtuous"</note> is identical to the path followed by the Sage Kings: Yao, Shun, Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu.

The gentlemen <note>In this context, "gentlemen" refers to the educated class of officials and advisors to the rulers.</note> of today all praise the virtuous in their private speech and personal conduct. But when it comes to the actual administration of government for the public, they fail to promote the virtuous and employ the capable. Therefore, I know that these gentlemen understand only minor trifles and not matters of real significance.

How do I know this is true?

Suppose a ruler had a cow or a sheep that he could not slaughter himself; he would surely look for a skilled butcher. Or if he wanted a garment that he could not make himself, he would surely look for a skilled tailor. For these tasks, the ruler would not employ his relatives, the wealthy who have no talent, or those who are merely good-looking, because he clearly knows they are incapable. He would be afraid they would ruin the tasks at hand. So, in these matters, rulers do not fail to promote the virtuous and employ the capable. 

Again, if the ruler had a sick horse that he could not cure himself, he would surely look for an experienced veterinarian. Or if he had a stiff bow that he could not pull, he would surely look for a skilled craftsman. For these tasks, the ruler would not employ his relatives, the wealthy who have no talent, or those who are merely good-looking, because he clearly knows they are incapable. He would be afraid they would ruin the tasks...

<summary>Mozi argues that while rulers hire skilled professionals for domestic tasks like tailoring or animal care, they inconsistently ignore merit when appointing government officials, choosing relatives or favorites instead.</summary>
<keywords>meritocracy, Sage Kings, government administration, Mozi, promoting the virtuous, social critique</keywords>

...needs to be attended to. So, in these personal matters, the rulers do not fail to promote the virtuous and employ the capable. But when it comes to the affairs of the state, everything is different. The ruler's relatives, the wealthy who lack merit, and those who are merely attractive are the ones promoted. Does it not seem, then, that these rulers love their states even less than they love a stiff bow, a sick horse, a garment, or a cow or a sheep? Therefore, I know that the "gentlemen" of the world understand only minor trifles and not matters of true significance. This is like trying to use the mute as messengers or the deaf as music directors.

In contrast, when the sage-kings <note>Idealized legendary rulers of China's golden age, such as Yao and Shun, who were cited as models of perfect virtue.</note> of ancient times governed the empire, they brought wealth and honor to people who were not necessarily their relatives, or wealthy without merit, or merely attractive. At one time, Shun <note>A legendary ruler (c. 2200 BCE) who rose from a peasant background.</note> farmed the land at Mount Li,¹ made pottery by the river, fished in Lake Lei,¹ and worked as a traveling peddler in Chang Yang.¹ Yao <note>Shun’s predecessor, who chose Shun to succeed him based on his character rather than family ties.</note> discovered him at Fu Tse,¹ made him emperor, and turned over to him the government of the empire and the leadership over the people. 

Yi Yin² <note>A famous minister and strategist of the Shang dynasty.</note> once served in the bridal party for the daughter of the Prince of Hsin and was then employed as a cook. Tang <note>The founder of the Shang dynasty.</note> discovered him and elevated him to the rank of <term>High Duke</term> <note>original: "High Duke" (Shang-fu). One of the highest official positions in ancient China.</note>, entrusting him with the government of the empire and the leadership over the people. Once, Fu Yueh³ <note>An advisor to King Wu Ding of the Shang Dynasty.</note> lived in the District of Pe Hai¹ and worked building prison walls. His clothing was made of coarse cloth and tied with ropes. Wu Ting <note>A king of the Shang Dynasty (c. 1200 BCE).</note> discovered him and elevated him to be a High Duke, handing him the—

<header>EXALTATION OF THE VIRTUOUS (III)</header>

...the governance of the empire and leadership over the people. Now, when Yao exalted Shun, T'ang exalted Yi Yin, and Wu Ting exalted Fu Yüeh, was it because they were their relatives, or wealthy without merit, or merely attractive? It was only because by adopting their views, carrying out their plans, and following their ways, Heaven above would be blessed, the spirits in the middle world would be blessed, and the people below would be blessed. Therefore, they were promoted and exalted.

Having understood the principle of exalting the virtuous in government, the ancient sage-kings inscribed it on bamboo and silk <note>original: "bamboos and silk"; these were the primary materials for writing in ancient China before the invention of paper.</note> and engraved it on bronze dishes and vases, <note>Mottoes and maxims were often inscribed on metal or stone vessels as personal reminders.</note> to hand it down to their descendants. Thus, we find in the "Penal Code of Lü," <note>Previously mentioned on page 46, note 1.</note> a book belonging to an ancient king, <note>This refers to King Mu of Chou, who reigned around 951 BCE, during whose time the Code was created.</note> the following:

> "The King said: 'Listen! Come, you rulers of states and territories, I will tell you how to make punishments a blessing. Now it is your duty to give peace to the people—what should you be most concerned about when choosing? Should it not be the right men? What should you handle with the greatest reverence? Should it not be punishments? What should you calculate most carefully? Should it not be who they should affect?'" <note>This passage is taken from James Legge’s translation of the *Classic of History* (Shujing).</note>

This means that with insight in choosing men and consideration in applying punishments, you can follow the ways...

The gentlemen of the world desire wealth and honor, and they dislike poverty and low status. Now, how can you obtain the former and avoid the latter? There is no better way than to practice virtue. What, then, is the way to practice virtue? Let those who have physical strength be alert to help others; let those who have wealth strive to share it with others; and let those who possess the <term>Tao</term> (the way of nature and life) teach others persuasively. Through this, the hungry will be fed, the cold will be clothed, and the restless will have order. When the hungry are fed, the cold are clothed, and the restless have order—this is how an abundant life is secured. 

However, those whom current rulers are enriching and honoring are merely their own relatives, the wealthy who have no merit, and those who are physically attractive. What guarantee is there that these people are wise? When the unwise are put in charge of the government of the country, national disorder can be predicted. ...no other way to gain these rewards than to be the rulers' relatives, to be wealthy without merit, or to be physically attractive. Clearly, one cannot become these things through learning or effort.

So, when the art of judging character is not understood, even if virtuous people are as great as the sage-kings Yü, T’ang, Wen, and Wu, they will receive no praise. And, although a relative of the ruler may be physically or mentally unfit, or as evil as the tyrants Chieh and Chow <note>Chieh and Chow were the legendary final rulers of the Xia and Shang dynasties, respectively, cited in Chinese history as the ultimate examples of cruelty and failed leadership.</note>, they will face no condemnation. Therefore, rewards are not given to the virtuous, and punishments are not given to the wicked. Because those who are rewarded have no merit, those who are punished are naturally innocent. As a result, the people become broken in spirit and neglected in body, despairing of ever doing good. With their strength left unused, they refuse to help one another; with their surplus supplies rotting and fermenting, they refuse to share with one another; and by hiding the excellent <term>Tao</term> <note>original: "Tao"; the Way, referring to the path of moral truth and social harmony.</note>, they refuse to show it to others. Consequently, the hungry are not fed, the cold are not clothed, and the disorganized are not given order.

In the days of old, the sage-king Yao had Shun, Shun had Yü, Yü had Kao T’ao, T’ang had Yi Yin, and King Wu had Hung Yao, T’ai Tien, Nan Kung K’uo, and San Yi Sheng—because of these wise appointments, the world was harmonious and the people were prosperous. Those nearby felt contented, and those far away were attracted to their rule. Wherever the sun and the moon shone, wherever boats and vehicles could reach, wherever rain and dew fell, and wherever life depended on farming grain <margin>This marks the distinction between the civilized world and those considered barbarians. In modern terms, this refers to areas where civilization has reached the agricultural stage.</margin>; almost everyone was converted to goodness by this example. Therefore, if the rulers of the world today truly desire to practice benevolence and righteousness...

# BOOK III

## CHAPTER XI

### IDENTIFICATION WITH THE SUPERIOR <note>In Chinese, the title of these three essays is *shang tong* (尚同). While it is literally "Superior Identification," it is generally interpreted as "Identification with the Superior." Motse argues for a process of social alignment where the people's standards match the ruler's, and the ruler's standards match Heaven's. This creates social solidarity based on the will of the common people and the divine. Some older translations used "Solidarity," but this is based on a likely misreading of the character *shang* (尚), which here means "superior" (上).</note> (I)

Motse said: In the beginning of human life, before there were laws and government, the custom was "everyone according to his own idea." <note>The Chinese word here is *yi* (義). While it often means "righteousness," in this context it refers to an individual's own standards, notions, or viewpoints. Because the meaning changes slightly throughout the text, it is translated variously as "notion," "idea," "view," "purpose," or "standard."</note> 

Consequently, every person had their own idea; two people had two different ideas, and ten people had ten different ideas—the more people there were, the more different notions existed. Everyone approved of their own view and disapproved of the views of others, leading to mutual resentment. 

As a result, fathers and sons and older and younger brothers became enemies and grew distant from one another, unable to reach any agreement. People worked to the detriment of others, using water, fire, and poison against each other. Extra energy was not used for mutual aid; surplus goods were allowed to rot rather than being shared; and the best teachings <note>original: "Tao" (道), referring to the "Way" or the essential moral path.</note> were kept secret and not revealed. The disorder in the human world was as great as...

<summary>This page introduces the concept of "Identification with the Superior" by describing a chaotic "state of nature" where the lack of a unified moral standard led to social conflict and the breakdown of families.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, Identification with the Superior, shang tong, yi, state of nature, social disorder, Tao, governance, moral standards</keywords>

<page-num>56</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...compared to that among birds and beasts. Yet all this disorder was due to the lack of a ruler.

Therefore, (Heaven) <note>The translator, Y.P. Mei, added "Heaven" here to provide a subject for the sentence. In the original Chinese text, the subject is often omitted, but parallel passages on page 71 suggest that Motse believed Heaven was the authority that chose the first ruler. This clarifies that Motse viewed government as divinely established rather than a purely democratic election.</note> chose the most virtuous person in the world and established him as Emperor. Feeling that his own abilities were not enough, the Emperor chose other virtuous people in the world and installed them as the <term>three ministers</term> <note>original: *san gong*; these were the highest-ranking officials who advised the Emperor.</note>. Seeing the vastness of the empire and the difficulty of personally attending to matters of right and wrong, or profit and harm, among people in distant countries, the three ministers divided the empire into feudal states and assigned them to feudal lords. Feeling that their own abilities were also not enough, the feudal lords, in turn, chose the most virtuous people in their states and appointed them as their officials. 

When the rulers were all installed, the Emperor issued a decree to all the people, saying: "Upon hearing of good or evil, one shall report it to a superior. What the superior considers to be right, everyone shall consider to be right; what the superior considers to be wrong, everyone shall consider to be wrong. When the superior makes a mistake, the subordinates shall offer good advice; when the subordinates show virtue, the superior shall recommend them to the public. To align oneself with the superior and not to side with the subordinates—this is what deserves encouragement from above and praise from below. On the other hand, if upon hearing of good or evil one does not report it to a superior; if one does not consider right what the superior considers right; if one does not consider wrong what the superior considers wrong..."

<header>IDENTIFICATION WITH SUPERIOR (I) 57</header>

...one should not consider to be wrong. If, when the superior was at fault, no one offered good advice; if, when subordinates showed virtue, no one recommended them for recognition; if someone sided with subordinates instead of aligning themselves with the superior—this behavior deserved punishment from the authorities and condemnation from the people.” The ruler made these principles the basis for rewards and punishments. He was perceptive and won the people’s trust.

The head of the village was the most noble <note>original: "high-minded"</note> and compassionate <note>original: "tender-hearted"</note> man in the village. He instructed the people of the village, saying: “Whenever you hear of anything good or evil, you must report it to the head of the district. Whatever the head of the district considers to be right, everyone must consider to be right. Whatever he considers to be wrong, everyone must consider to be wrong. Remove from your speech anything that is not good and adopt his good way of speaking. Remove from your conduct anything that is not good and adopt his good behavior. If we do this, how can there be disorder in the district?”

Now, how was order established in the district? There was order in the district because the leader was able to unify the standards of the district. The head of the district was the most noble and compassionate man in the area. He instructed the people of the district, saying: “Whenever you hear of anything good or evil, you must report it to the lord <note>The "lord" refers to the feudal ruler of the state.</note>. Whatever the lord considers to be right, everyone must consider to be right; whatever he considers to be wrong, everyone must consider to be wrong. Remove from your speech anything that is not good and adopt his good way of speaking. Remove from your conduct anything that is not good and adopt his good behavior. If we do this, how can there be disorder in the state?”

Now, how was order brought about in the feudal state? There was order in the state because the feudal lord was able to unify the standards within his borders. The lord of the state was the most noble <note>original: "high-minded"</note> and compassionate <note>original: "tender-hearted"</note> man of the state. Whatever the emperor considers to be right, everyone must consider to be right; whatever the emperor considers to be wrong, everyone must consider to be wrong. Eliminate from your speech anything that is not good and learn from his good speech. Remove from your conduct anything that is not good and learn from his good conduct. If this is done, how can there be disorder in the empire?"

Now, how is order brought about in the empire? There was order in the empire because the emperor was able to unify the standards of the entire realm. If, however, the people all align themselves <note>original: "identify themselves"</note> with the Son of Heaven <note>The Emperor</note> but not with Heaven itself, then chaos <note>original: "the jungle"; used here as a metaphor for disorder</note> still remains. Currently, the frequent occurrences of hurricanes and floods are simply the punishments from Heaven upon the people for failing to align their standards with the Will of Heaven. Therefore, Motse said: The wise sage-kings of ancient times devised the five punishments <note>These were traditional Chinese legal penalties: (1) tattooing the face, (2) cutting off the nose, (3) cutting off the feet, (4) castration, and (5) death. Most have long been out of use.</note> to rule the people. This allowed them to restrain those who did not align themselves with their superiors—a system similar to how threads are organized into bundles or how a fishing net is controlled by a single main rope.

## CHAPTER XII
### IDENTIFICATION WITH THE SUPERIOR (II)

Motse said: When we look back to the time before there were rulers, it seems the custom was that "everyone in the world followed their own standard." Because of this, every man had his own standard; ten men had ten different standards, and a hundred men had a hundred different standards—the more people there were, the more standards there were. Everyone approved of their own views and rejected the views of others, which led to mutual conflict. Even fathers, sons, and brothers became enemies because they were unable to reach any agreement. Extra energy was not used to help others; the best teachings <note>Tao: The Way or essential principles</note> were kept secret; and surplus goods were left to rot instead of being shared. The disorder in the human world could be compared to the chaos among wild birds and beasts. This disorder resulted from a lack of regulations governing the relationships between ruler and subject, superior and subordinate, and elder and younger, as well as the absence of rules between fathers and sons and between older and younger brothers.

Realizing that this confusion was caused by the absence of a ruler who could unify the standards of the world, Heaven <note>The original text implies "Heaven" as the actor here; see p. 56, note 1</note> chose the most virtuous, sagacious <note>meaning: profoundly wise</note>, and intelligent person in the world and crowned him Emperor, charging him with the duty of unifying the wills of everyone in the empire. After being crowned, the Emperor realized it was impossible to unify the world using only his own eyes and ears. Therefore, he chose the most understanding...

<meta>This page continues Chapter XII, "Identification with the Superior (II)," detailing the hierarchical structure established by the Emperor to manage a vast population and the specific behavioral mandates issued to unify the people's moral standards.</meta>

<page-num>60</page-num>

->THE WORKS OF MOTSE<-

...virtuous, <term>sagacious</term> <note>meaning: wise and showing good judgment</note>, and wise people of the world and appointed them as the three ministers, sharing with them the duty of unifying the standards <note>original: "unifying the standards"—this refers to the process of aligning everyone’s moral and social judgments with those of their leaders to ensure social harmony</note> throughout the empire. Once the Emperor and the three ministers were in office, they realized the vastness of the empire and the difficulty of unifying all the people living in mountains and forests or in far distant lands. Therefore, they systematically divided the empire and appointed numerous feudal lords, charging them with the duty of unifying the standards within each of their states. The feudal lords, in turn, felt it was difficult to unify the standards in their states using only their own senses of hearing and sight. Therefore, they chose the most virtuous people in the state to be their ministers and secretaries—and so on, down to the heads of districts and villages—sharing with them the duty of unifying the standards across the state.

When the lords of the country and the leaders of the people had been appointed, the Emperor issued mandates, instructing the people: “When you discover something good, you must report it to your superior; when you discover something evil, you must report it to your superior. Whatever the superior considers to be right, everyone shall consider right; whatever the superior considers to be wrong, everyone shall consider wrong. When there is virtue among the people, it should be publicly recommended; when the superior is at fault, they should be given good counsel. You shall <term>identify yourself with the superior</term> <note>This is the core Mohoist concept of "Shangtong," or "Uniformity with the Superior," where social order is maintained by everyone following the moral lead of those above them</note> and not merely side with your subordinates <note>This warns against forming factions or cliques that might undermine the ruler's authority</note>. By doing this, a person deserves encouragement from their superiors and praise from the people. On the other hand, if you discover something good and do not report it, or discover something evil and do not report it; if you do not consider right what the superior considers right, or wrong what the superior considers wrong; if there

<header>IDENTIFICATION WITH THE SUPERIOR (II)</header>

...should be no recommendation when there is virtue among the people and no good counsel when the superior is at fault; and if you should unite with your subordinates but disagree with the superior—you deserve heavy punishment from your superiors and condemnation from the people.” Therefore, the <term>sage-kings</term> <note>idealized rulers of ancient Chinese history, such as Yao, Shun, and Yu, whom Mozi presents as models of perfect moral leadership</note> of old were very judicious and consistent in their punishments and rewards. Because of this, all the people aspired to earn rewards and praise from the superior and dreaded his condemnation and punishment.

Thereupon, in accordance with the policy of the Emperor, the village head proceeded to unify the purposes within the village. Having accomplished this, he led the people of the village to <term>identify themselves</term> <note>original: "identify themselves with"—this refers to the Moist doctrine of *Shangtong*, or "Upward Conformity," where people align their moral judgments with those of their leaders</note> with the head of the district, saying: “All you people of the village are to identify yourselves with the head of the district, and are not to side with subordinates. What the head of the district thinks to be right, everyone shall think to be right; what he thinks to be wrong, everyone shall think to be wrong. Put away your evil speech and learn his good speech; put away your evil conduct and learn his good conduct. For the head of the district is naturally the most virtuous person in the district. If all the people in the district follow the example of their head, how then can the district be disorderly?”

Now, how is it that the head of the district was so successful in governing the district? It was simply because he could unify the purposes of the whole district that the district was so orderly. Having accomplished this, he in turn led the people of his district to identify themselves with the <term>feudal lord</term> <note>the ruler of a specific state within the larger empire</note>, saying: “All you people of the district shall identify yourselves with the lord of the state, and shall not...”

<summary>This page describes the hierarchical process of "Identifying with the Superior," where village and district heads align the people's moral standards with the leaders directly above them. It emphasizes that social order is achieved through a unified sense of right and wrong, starting from the smallest local unit and moving up toward the state level.</summary>

<keywords>Identification with the Superior, Shangtong, social hierarchy, village head, district head, sage-kings, Mozi, moral unity, rewards and punishments</keywords>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...side with those below you. What the lord judges to be right, everyone shall judge to be right; what he judges to be wrong, everyone shall judge to be wrong. Discard your harmful speech and learn his good speech; discard your harmful conduct and learn his good conduct. For the lord of the state is naturally the most virtuous person in the state. If all the people in the state follow the example of their lord, how then can the state be in disorder?”

Now, why was the feudal lord so successful in governing the state? It was simply because he could unify the purposes within the state, which ensured that the state was orderly. Having accomplished this, he in turn led the people of his state to <term>identify themselves</term> <note>original: "identify themselves with"—this refers to the Moist doctrine of *Shangtong*, or "Upward Conformity," where people align their moral judgments with those of their leaders</note> with the Emperor, saying: “All you people of the state shall identify yourselves with the Emperor and shall not side with those below you. What the Emperor judges to be right, everyone shall judge to be right; what he judges to be wrong, everyone shall judge to be wrong. Discard your harmful speech and learn his good speech; discard your harmful conduct and learn his good conduct. For the Emperor is naturally the most noble and compassionate man in the empire. If all the people of the empire follow his example, how then can the state be disorderly?”

Now, why was the Emperor so successful in governing the empire? It was simply because he could unify the purposes in the empire, which ensured that the empire was orderly.

But to carry the process of identifying with the superior up to the <term>Son of Heaven</term> <note>a traditional title for the Emperor of China, representing his role as the mediator between the divine and the earthly realm</note> and not further up to Heaven itself—then the "jungle" <note>original footnote: "Figure for disorder. See p. 58, note 1." This metaphor suggests that without divine alignment, society remains a wild, chaotic wilderness</note> from Heaven is not yet cleared away. If that were the case, Heaven would send down cold and heat without moderation, and...

<summary>This page explains how social order is maintained by aligning the moral standards of the people with their local lords, and the lords with the Emperor. It concludes by warning that this alignment must ultimately extend to Heaven itself to avoid natural and social chaos.</summary>

With purification and ritual baths <note>This purification is a preparation to make the person fit to perform the sacrifice. It lasts sometimes three days, and sometimes even seven days. Clean clothes are put on after bathing. Wine and meat are not consumed. It is intended to produce an intense concentration of attention to enable the worshiper to come into contact with the spirits. They did not dare miss the proper times for the spring and autumn sacrifices. In judging lawsuits, they did not dare to be unjust. In distributing property, they did not dare to be unfair. Even when at leisure, they did not dare to be disrespectful. Because the sage-kings acted as such good rulers, Heaven and the spirits commended their leadership from above, and the people cherished it from below. To work under the hearty approval of Heaven and the spirits is...

People might then ask: "Since there are rulers in the world today, why is there still disorder in the empire?" 

Motse said: The political leaders of today are quite different from those of the past. <note>This is a very explicit reflection on the political conditions of Motse's own time, roughly the 5th century BC.</note> The situation is similar to the story of the **Five Punishments** <note>These traditionally included tattooing, cutting off the nose, amputation of the feet, castration, and death.</note> and the **Prince of Miao** <note>The name of an ancient ethnic group or tribe, often depicted as a rebellious people who resisted the early Chinese sage-kings.</note>. But when the Prince of Miao established the Five Punishments, they threw his empire into chaos. Is it possible that the punishments themselves are to blame? In reality, the fault lies in how they were applied.

They created a code of five tortures and called it law.” <note>Compare with the translation by James Legge, vol. 3, p. 591.</note> This means that those who know how to apply punishments can use them to govern the people effectively. But those who do not know how to apply them turn them into five types of torture. Is it the punishments that are at fault? No, it is only when their application is not appropriate that—

The lords of the present day, however, do exactly the opposite. Administration is conducted merely to seek flattery. Fathers, brothers, and other relatives and friends are placed in high positions and appointed as rulers over the people. Realizing that the leader <note>original: "superior"</note> did not appoint these officials for the welfare of the people, the people all keep their distance and refuse to align <note>original: "identify"</note> themselves with their leader. Consequently, the goals of the leader and the subjects <note>original: "subordinates"</note> are not unified. Because of this, rewards and praises fail to encourage the people to do good, and punishments and fines fail to stop them from doing evil.

How do we know this is the case?

In governing the country, the ruler announces: "I will reward whoever deserves a reward." Suppose the goals of the leader and the subjects are different; anyone rewarded by the leader would be condemned by the public. And in the life of a community, public condemnation is the ultimate authority. Even if there is a reward from the leader, it will provide no encouragement. Again, in governing the country, the ruler announces: "I will punish whoever deserves punishment." Suppose the goals of the leader and the subjects are different; anyone punished by the leader would be praised by the public. And in the life of a community, public approval is the ultimate authority. Even if there is punishment from—

Therefore, if there was a virtuous man thousands of <term>li</term> <note>A traditional Chinese unit of distance, roughly one-third of a mile or 500 meters</note> away, even if his own clansmen or the people in his district did not know of his virtue, the emperor was able to reward him. Because of this, all the people in the world were astonished and carefully avoided doing evil, saying:

> "The emperor has the hearing and sight of a spirit!"

> "It was no spirit, but only the ability to make use of others' ears and eyes to help one's own hearing and sight; to make use of others' lips to help one's own speech; to make use of others' minds to help one's own thought; and to use others' limbs to help one's own actions."

When there are many people to help one's hearing and sight, then of course one can hear and see far; when there are many to help one's speech, then one's...

Good advice can comfort many people. When there are many people to contribute to one's thoughts, then plans can be shaped quickly. They trot and gallop. The Emperor's considerations are all-inclusive." ^1 Further: "My team consists of dark grey horses. The six reins look like silk. They trot and gallop. <meta>This page begins Chapter XIII, the third part of Mozi’s discourse on "Identification with the Superior." It argues that a ruler must align his administration with the people's collective sense of right and wrong to ensure order through consistent rewards and punishments.</meta>

## CHAPTER XIII

### IDENTIFICATION WITH THE SUPERIOR (III)

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: The goal of a wise ruler is to implement what creates order among the people and to avoid what causes chaos.

But what is it that creates order among the people? When the ruler’s administration aligns with the desires of the people, there will be order; otherwise, there will be chaos.

How do we know this is true?

When the ruler’s administration aligns with the desires of the subjects, it demonstrates an understanding of what the people approve and disapprove of. When there is such an understanding, good deeds will be discovered and rewarded, and bad deeds will be discovered and punished; consequently, the country will certainly have order. 

However, when the ruler’s administration does not align with the desires of the subjects, it shows a lack of understanding regarding what the subjects approve or disapprove of. When there is no such understanding, then good behavior will not be recognized and rewarded, and bad behavior will not be discovered and punished. With virtue unrewarded and evil unpunished, such a government will surely throw the country into chaos. Therefore, when rewards and punishments do not match the people’s sense of justice <note>original: "desires of the people"</note>, the matter must be examined very carefully.

But how can the desires of the people—which are so many and various—be satisfied?

To this, Mozi said: It can be achieved only by adopting the principle of <term>Identification with the Superior</term> <note>original: "Shang Tong," the Mohist concept of administrative and moral unification where everyone aligns their judgment with those above them in the social hierarchy</note> in government.

<vocab>Mozi, Identification with the Superior, ruler, administration, order, confusion, rewards, punishments</vocab>

<summary>Mozi explains that social order depends on a ruler's administration aligning with the people's collective values, ensuring that rewards and punishments are applied correctly. He argues that the only way to harmonize these diverse public desires is through the principle of "Identification with the Superior."</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Identification with the Superior, Shang Tong, social order, administration, rewards and punishments, political philosophy, Mohism</keywords>

--- Page 85 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter XIII of the "Identification with the Superior" discourse. It explains the Mohist theory of the state of nature—a time of chaos before government existed—and the subsequent divine appointment of a hierarchy to unify moral standards.</meta>

# IDENTIFICATION WITH THE SUPERIOR (III)

How do we know the principle of Identification with the Superior can govern the empire?

Why not then examine the administration and the theory of government from ancient times? In the beginning, there was no ruler and everyone was independent. Since everyone was independent, there was one purpose when there was one man, ten purposes when there were ten men, a hundred purposes when there were a hundred men, a thousand purposes when there were a thousand men, and so on until the number of men became innumerable and the number of different purposes became innumerable along with them. And all of them approved of their own ideas and disapproved of the ideas of others. Thus, there was strife among the strong and struggle among the weak.

Thereupon Heaven <gloss>1</gloss> wished to unify the standards in the world. The most virtuous person was selected and made emperor. Recognizing that his power alone was insufficient to govern the empire, the emperor chose the next best men (in virtue and wisdom) and honored them by making them the three ministers. Recognizing that their powers alone were insufficient to assist the emperor, the three ministers in turn divided the empire into feudal states and assigned them to feudal lords. Recognizing that his power alone was insufficient to govern all that were within his four borders, the feudal lord in turn selected his next best men and commissioned them as ministers and secretaries. Recognizing that their power alone was insufficient to assist their feudal lord, the ministers and secretaries again selected their next best and appointed them district heads and clan patriarchs <note>original: "clan patriarchs," refers to the heads of large family units or local lineages</note>.

<note>1 The subject (Heaven) becomes explicit here, while it was omitted in the last two chapters. See above, p. 56, note 1; p. 59, note 1.</note>

<vocab>Identification with Superior, Heaven, emperor, three ministers, feudal states, feudal lords, district heads, clan patriarchs</vocab>

<summary>This page describes the transition from a chaotic "state of nature," where every individual had their own conflicting standards of right and wrong, to an organized hierarchy established by Heaven. It outlines the delegation of power from the Emperor down through ministers and feudal lords to local clan patriarchs to ensure unified governance.</summary>
<keywords>state of nature, hierarchy, moral unification, Heaven (Tian), appointment of rulers, social contract, Mohist political theory</keywords>

--- Page 86 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of the hierarchy of government, moving from the appointment of officials by Heaven and the Emperor to the problem of conflicting moral standards between rulers and their subjects.</meta>

<page-num>72</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

Therefore, in appointing the three ministers, the feudal lords, the ministers and secretaries, and the district heads and clan patriarchs <note>original: "clan patriarchs," leaders of local kinship groups</note>, the emperor was not selecting them for wealth and honor, leisure and ease. He chose them to assist in administration and the legal system. Hence, when Heaven established the empire, located the capital, and commissioned the sovereign, kings, lords, and dukes, and appointed secretaries, scholars, professors, and elders—it was not to give them an easy life. It was only to divide the responsibilities and let them help manifest the light of Heaven.

Why are the superiors now unable to govern their subordinates, and the subordinates unwilling to serve their superiors? It is because of a mutual disregard for one another.

What is the reason for this? The reason is a difference in standards. Whenever standards of right and wrong differ, there will be opposition. The ruler may think a man is good and reward him. Yet that same man, though rewarded by the ruler, may provoke the condemnation of the people for the very same act. Therefore, those who do what the ruler considers "good" are not necessarily encouraged by rewards because they fear social disapproval. Conversely, the ruler may think a man is evil and punish him. This man, though punished by the ruler, might simultaneously receive the approval of the people. Therefore, those who do what the ruler considers "evil" are not necessarily stopped by punishments. Thus, rewards and honors from the ruler cannot encourage good behavior, and his denunciation and punishment cannot prevent evil behavior. What is the reason for this? The reason is a difference in standards.

But how can the standards in the world be unified?

Motse <note>original: "Motse," also known as Mozi, the founder of Mohism</note> said: "Why not let each member of the clan organize his purposes and identify them with those of the patriarch?" <note>1 The text here is faulty. This reading is suggested internally by the next two parallel paragraphs.</note>

<vocab>Motse, Heaven, feudal lords, clan patriarchs, jurisdiction, administration, standards, rewards, punishments</vocab>

<summary>This page explains that government officials were appointed by Heaven to serve the public good rather than for personal gain, and it identifies conflicting moral standards between rulers and the people as the primary cause of social disorder.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Mohism, governance, social order, moral standards, divine mandate, hierarchy, rewards and punishments</keywords>

--- Page 87 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of Mozi’s political theory, "Identifying Upward" (Identification with the Superior), explaining how local clan order is established through a system of mandatory reporting and rewards, which must then be scaled up to the state level.</meta>

<page-num>73</page-num>
<header>IDENTIFICATION WITH THE SUPERIOR (III)</header>

The patriarch <note>original: "patriarch," the head of a local kinship group or clan</note> should establish laws and announce to the clan:

> “Anyone who discovers someone helping the clan must report it; anyone who discovers someone harming the clan must report it. Reporting a benefactor of the clan is the same as helping the clan yourself. Upon learning of this, the leader will reward you, and upon hearing of it, the community will praise you. However, failing to report someone harming the clan is the same as harming the clan yourself. Upon learning of this, the leader will punish you, and upon hearing of it, the community will condemn you.”

Consequently, every member of the clan will desire rewards and honor while seeking to avoid the leader's public condemnation and punishment. When they see good deeds, they will report them; when they see evil deeds, they will report them. In this way, the patriarch can reward the good and punish the evil. When good is rewarded and evil is punished, the clan will certainly achieve order. Now, why is it that the clan becomes orderly? It is simply because the administration is based on the principle of Identifying with the Superior <note>Mozi’s concept of "Shang Tong," which requires subordinates to align their moral judgments with those of their immediate superiors</note>.

Now that the clan is in order, does that mean the entire feudal state is properly governed?

Not at all. A state is composed of many clans. Each person favors their own clan and dislikes others, leading to conflict between the strong and struggles among the weak. Therefore, the clan patriarchs must once again align the goals of their clans with those of the feudal lord. The feudal lord should also establish laws and announce to the state:

> “Anyone who discovers someone helping the state must report it; anyone who discovers someone harming the state must report it. Reporting a benefactor of the state is the same as helping the state yourself.

<summary>Mozi explains how social order is created by requiring clan members to report good and bad behavior to their patriarch, a system that must then be mirrored by aligning patriarchs with the feudal lord.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Identification with the Superior, social order, patriarch, feudal lord, reward and punishment, reporting system, governance</keywords>

--- Page 88 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of Mozi’s political theory of "Identifying Upward." It scales the system of mandatory reporting and moral alignment from the level of the individual feudal state up to the level of the entire empire.</meta>

<page-num>74</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

“When the leader <note>original: "superior"</note> knows of him, he will reward him; when the people hear of him, they will praise him. Anyone who sees someone harming the state <note>original: "malefactor of the state"</note> and fails to report it is treated as if they have harmed the state themselves. When the leader knows of such a person, he will punish him; when the people hear of him, they will condemn him.”

Consequently, everyone in the state desires to receive rewards and honor while avoiding the leader's public condemnation and punishment. When they see good deeds, they will report them; when they see evil deeds, they will report them. Thus, the feudal lord can reward the good and punish the evil. When good is rewarded and evil is punished, the feudal state will certainly achieve order. Why is it that the state becomes orderly? It is simply because the administration is based on the principle of Identifying with the Superior <note>Mozi’s concept of "Shang Tong," which requires subordinates to align their moral judgments with those of their immediate superiors</note>.

Now that the feudal state is in order, is that all there is to governing the empire?

Not at all. The empire is composed of many states. Each state favors its own interests and dislikes other states. This leads to conflict between the strong and struggles among the weak. Therefore, the feudal lord must once again align the goals of his state and identify them with those of the emperor. The emperor should also establish laws and proclaim to the empire:

> “Anyone who discovers someone helping the empire <note>original: "benefactor of the empire"</note> must report it; anyone who discovers someone harming the empire <note>original: "malefactor of the empire"</note> must report it. Reporting someone who helps the empire is the same as helping the state yourself. When the leader knows of this, he will reward you; when the people hear of it, they will praise you. However, failing to report someone who harms the empire is the same as harming the empire yourself. When the leader knows of this, he will punish you; when the people hear of it, they will condemn you.”

<vocab>Motse, Identification with the Superior, feudal lord, emperor, malefactor, benefactor</vocab>

<summary>Mozi explains that order is achieved in a state when citizens report good and bad behavior to their lord, and argues that this same principle of "Identifying Upward" must be applied to the whole empire under the emperor.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Identifying Upward, political order, rewards and punishments, social surveillance, feudalism, imperial governance</keywords>

--- Page 89 ---
<meta>This page continues the explanation of Mozi’s political theory of "Identifying Upward," extending the hierarchy of moral alignment from the emperor all the way to the Will of Heaven, while providing historical justification for the system.</meta>

<page-num>75</page-num>
<header>IDENTIFICATION WITH THE SUPERIOR (III)</header>

As a result, everyone in the empire will want to receive rewards and honor and avoid public condemnation and punishment from their emperor. When they see good or evil deeds, they will report them. Then the emperor can reward the good and punish the evil. With good rewarded and evil punished, the empire will certainly be orderly. Now, why does the empire become orderly? It is simply because the administration is based on the principle of Identifying with the Superior <note>Mozi’s concept of "Shang Tong," which requires subordinates to align their moral judgments with those of their immediate superiors</note>.

Once the empire is orderly, the emperor will further organize the goals of the empire and align them with the Will of Heaven <note>Tian Zhi: Mozi's belief that the ultimate standard of morality and governance is established by Heaven</note>.

Therefore, the principle of Identifying with the Superior can govern the empire when used by the emperor; it can govern the state when used by a feudal lord; and it can govern the clan when used by a clan patriarch. Whether used on a large scale to govern the entire empire or on a small scale to govern a single clan, this principle is never found lacking or useless. Hence the proverb: “To govern the world-empire is the same as ruling a single family clan; to command all the people in the world is the same as managing a single individual.”

Does anyone think that all this is merely an invention of Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> and that this teaching did not exist among the sage-kings of old? In truth, they shared the same opinion. All the sage-kings managed their governments by the principle of Identifying with the Superior; therefore, the world became orderly. How do we know this is true? It is recorded in the “Great Oath” <note>original: "Grand Oath," likely referring to the *Tai Shi* section of the Classic of History</note> among the books of the ancient kings: “If an unscrupulous man discovers a case of intrigue and deception...”

<summary>Mozi explains that the ultimate stage of "Identifying Upward" is aligning the emperor's will with the Will of Heaven, arguing that this universal principle works for organizations of any size and was used by ancient sage-kings.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Identification with the Superior, Will of Heaven, Sage-kings, Great Oath, political philosophy, social order</keywords>

--- Page 90 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the administrative efficiency and justice achieved through the principle of "Identifying Upward." It explains how the ancient sage-kings used a network of virtuous officials to act as their eyes and ears across the empire.</meta>

<page-num>76</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI <note>original: "Motse"</note></header>

"...and fails to make it known, he shall be punished equally." $^1$

This means that whoever discovers any crime and does not report it will be considered as having committed a crime of the same magnitude.

Therefore, in governing the empire, the ancient sage-kings chose only virtuous people for both the remote outposts and the offices near them. Because there were many people to help the king see and hear, he succeeded in planning and completed his tasks before others could. His good reputation spread quickly. Simply because he could trust his staff in the administration, the benefits were as we have stated. There is an ancient proverb that says:

> "The sight of one eye cannot compare with that of two; the hearing of one ear cannot compare with that of two; the grasp of one hand cannot compare with that of two."

Now, because the sage-king could trust his staff in the administration, he received these benefits. Therefore, during the reign of the ancient sage-kings over the empire, if there was a virtuous man more than a thousand <term>li</term> <note>A Chinese unit of distance, traditionally about one-third of a mile or 500 meters</note> away, the king could reward him before the people in that man's own district and village even knew about it. And if there was a wicked man about a thousand <term>li</term> away, the king could punish him before the people in that same district and village even knew about it. 

Though one might suppose the sage-king had extraordinarily keen hearing and sight, how could he see everything beyond a thousand <term>li</term> at a single glance? How could he hear everything beyond a thousand <term>li</term> in a single moment? In fact, the sage-king could see without traveling there and hear without being physically present.

***

$^1$ In the current text of the *Book of Documents* <note>original: "Shu Ching"</note>, this quotation does not appear. Only the last clause, with some modification, is found in the "Great Declaration" <note>original: "Grand Oath"</note>. See James Legge, volume 3, page 287.

<summary>This page explains that the legendary sage-kings governed effectively not through supernatural powers, but by employing a vast network of virtuous officials who reported good and evil deeds from across the empire.</summary>

--- Page 91 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument that "Identifying Upward" is the key to maintaining law and order, stressing that this alignment must be rooted in the ruler's love for the people.</meta>

<page-num>77</page-num>
<header>IDENTIFICATION WITH THE SUPERIOR (III)</header>

...near. Yet what kept the thieves, robbers, bandits, and highwaymen moving all over the empire without being able to find refuge anywhere? This is the beauty <note>meaning the effectiveness or excellence</note> of adopting the principle of Identification with the Superior <note>Also translated as "Identifying Upward," this is the Moarist concept that social order is achieved when subordinates align their moral judgments with those of their virtuous superiors</note> in government.

Therefore Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: Whoever orders his people to identify themselves with their superior must love them dearly. For the people will not obey orders except when they are commanded with love and held in confidence. Lead them with wealth and honor ahead, and push them with just punishments from behind. When government is carried on like this, even if I wanted someone *not* to identify himself with me, it would be impossible.

Therefore Mozi said: If the kings, dukes, and important personages of the world now sincerely want to practice benevolence <note>original: "magnanimity"</note> and righteousness and be superior men, and if they want to follow the way of the sage-kings on the one hand and contribute toward the benefit of the people on the other, they cannot leave the principle of Identification with the Superior unexamined and misunderstood. Identification with the Superior is, indeed, the foundation of government and the essence of orderliness.

<summary>Mozi concludes that the principle of Identifying Upward creates a society where crime cannot hide, but stresses that this system only works if the ruler truly loves the people. He asserts that this alignment is the fundamental basis for all orderly and righteous government.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Identification with the Superior, Identifying Upward, governance, social order, benevolence, rewards and punishments, sage-kings</keywords>
