<meta>This page begins Book IV, introducing the core Moarist doctrine of "Universal Love" (Jian'ai) as the fundamental solution to social chaos. It establishes the "physician analogy," arguing that a ruler cannot fix society without first diagnosing the root cause of its problems.</meta>

# BOOK IV

## CHAPTER XIV

### Universal Love (Part I) <note>original: "(I)"</note>¹

The wise man who is responsible for governing the empire should understand the cause of disorder before he can bring about order. Unless he knows its cause, he cannot regulate it. This is similar to the situation of a physician who is attending to a patient. He must know the cause of the ailment before he can cure it. Unless he knows its cause, he cannot cure it. How is the situation any different for one who is meant to resolve social disorder? He, too, must know the cause of the disorder before he can regulate it. Unless he knows its cause, he cannot regulate it. The wise man who is in charge of governing the empire must, therefore, investigate the cause of disorder.

Suppose we try to locate the cause of disorder; we shall find it lies in the lack of mutual love. What is called "disorder" is simply the lack of filial piety <note>The traditional Chinese virtue of respect, obedience, and care for one's parents and elderly family members</note> on the part of the minister and the son toward the ruler and the father. Because he loves himself and not his father, the son seeks his own benefit at the expense of his father. Because he loves himself and not his elder brother, the younger brother seeks his own benefit at the expense of his elder brother. Because he loves himself and not his ruler, the minister seeks his own benefit at the...

---

¹ This chapter has been translated by James Legge in the introduction <note>original: "Prolegomena"</note> to *Mencius* <note>original: "孟子"</note>, *Chinese Classics*, volume 2. His translation is based on the Pi Yuan edition of the text, which is inferior to the Sun Yirang <note>original: "Sun Yi Jang"</note> edition, and his own reading of that edition is also incorrect on several points.

<summary>Mozi argues that social disorder is like a disease that cannot be cured without diagnosing its cause, which he identifies as a lack of mutual love. He explains that individuals currently prioritize their own interests over the well-being of their family members and rulers.</summary>

->UNIVERSAL LOVE (I)<-

detriment <note>original: "disadvantage"</note> of his emperor. These actions are what we call social disorder. When a father shows no affection for his son, when an elder brother shows no affection for his younger brother, and when an emperor shows no affection for his minister, this is also called disorder. When a father loves only himself and not his son, he benefits himself at the expense of his son. When an elder brother loves only himself and not his younger brother, he benefits himself at the expense of his younger brother. When an emperor loves only himself and not his minister, he benefits himself at the expense of his minister. The reason for all of this is a lack <note>original: "want"</note> of mutual love.

This same principle applies to thieves and robbers. Because a thief loves only his own family and not other families, he steals from other families to profit his own. Because a robber loves only himself and not others, he commits violence against others to profit himself. The reason for all of this is a lack of love. This is also true regarding the conflicts between the households of great ministers and the invasions between the states of feudal lords. Because a minister loves only his own household and not others, he disrupts other households to profit his own. Because a feudal lord loves only his own state and not others, he attacks other states to profit his own. These instances represent the height of confusion in the world. When we investigate the causes, we find they all arise from a lack of mutual love.

Suppose everyone in the world practiced universal love <note>Jian'ai: Mozi's core concept of impartial care for all people regardless of their relationship to oneself</note>, loving others as they love themselves. Would there still be any disrespectful <note>original: "unfilial"</note> individuals? If everyone regarded their father, elder brother, and emperor as they regard themselves, how could they harbor any disrespectful feelings? Would...

<summary>Mozi argues that all social ills—from family disputes and theft to civil unrest and international warfare—are caused by a lack of mutual love. He proposes that "Universal Love," where one cares for others as much as oneself, is the only way to eliminate "unfilial" behavior and global disorder.</summary>

--- Page 94 ---
<meta>This page concludes the initial argument for Universal Love by illustrating how it eliminates specific social ills—such as theft, family strife, and war—and leads to a peaceful, orderly world.</meta>

<page-num>80</page-num>
<header>THE [WORKS OF MOTSE]</header>

Could there still be any unloving <note>original: "unaffectionate"</note> person? When everyone regards their younger brother, son, and minister as themselves, where could they direct any ill will? Therefore, there will be no lack of filial devotion <note>original: "unfilial feeling"</note> or mutual care. 

Will there then be any thieves and robbers? When everyone regards other families as their own family, who will steal? When everyone regards other people as themselves, who will rob? Therefore, there will be no more thieves or robbers. 

Will there be conflict among the <term>houses of the ministers</term> <note>These were the powerful noble families and clans that served the ruler but often fought each other for power.</note> or invasions among the states of the <term>feudal lords</term> <note>Regional rulers during the Zhou Dynasty who governed territories granted by the Emperor.</note>? When everyone regards the houses of others as their own, who will cause disturbances? When everyone regards the states of others as their own, who will invade? Therefore, there will be neither conflict among the noble houses nor invasions among the states of the feudal lords.

If everyone in the world practiced universal love—states not attacking one another, noble houses not disturbing one another, thieves and robbers disappearing, and emperors, ministers, fathers, and sons all being affectionate and devoted—if all this comes to pass, the world will be orderly. 

Therefore, how can a wise ruler <note>original: "the wise man who has charge of governing the empire"</note> fail to restrain hatred and encourage love? So, when there is universal love in the world, it will be orderly; and when there is mutual hatred in the world, it will be disorderly. This is why Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> insisted on persuading people to love one another.

<summary>This page argues that universal love is the ultimate solution to crime, civil unrest, and international warfare, concluding that a wise ruler must prioritize love over hatred to ensure social order.</summary>

# CHAPTER XV

## UNIVERSAL LOVE (II)

Motse <note>The philosopher Mozi, active approximately 470–391 BCE.</note> said: The purpose of the <term>magnanimous</term> <note>original: "magnanimous"; refers to a "humane person" or one of high moral character (ren zhe).</note> is to be found in providing benefits for the world and eliminating its calamities.

But what are the benefits of the world, and what are its calamities?

Motse said: Mutual attacks between states, the seizure of power among noble families <note>original: "houses"</note>, and people hurting one another; the lack of kindness and loyalty between a ruler and his subjects, the lack of affection and <term>filial piety</term> <note>The traditional virtue of respect, obedience, and care for one's parents and ancestors.</note> between father and son, and the lack of harmony between older and younger brothers—these are the major calamities in the world.

But where did these calamities come from? Did they arise out of mutual love?

Motse said: They arise out of a lack of mutual love. At present, <term>feudal lords</term> <note>Regional rulers who governed territories during the Zhou Dynasty.</note> have learned only to love their own states and not the states of others. Therefore, they have no moral hesitation about attacking other states. The heads of noble families have learned only to love their own families and not the families of others. Therefore, they have no hesitation about seizing power from other families. And individuals have learned only to love themselves and not others. Therefore, they have no hesitation about hurting others. When feudal lords do not love one another, there will be war in the fields. When heads of families do not love one another, they will seize one another’s power. When individuals do not love one another, they will injure one another. When the ruler and the ruled do not love one another...

<summary>Motse argues that the world's major disasters—such as war, family conflict, and social injustice—stem from a lack of "universal love," as people prioritize their own interests over those of others.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, universal love, social order, filial piety, feudal lords, noble houses, ethics</keywords>

--- Page 96 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the negative consequences of a lack of universal love, specifically regarding the breakdown of social hierarchies and the oppression of the weak by the strong, before introducing Motse's solution.</meta>

<page-num>82</page-num>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

<note>This continues the thought from the previous page regarding the relationship between ruler and subjects.</note> they will not be gracious and loyal. When father and son do not love each other, they will not be affectionate and <term>filial</term> <note>Respectful and dutiful toward one’s parents and ancestors.</note>. When elder and younger brothers do not love each other, they will not be harmonious. When nobody in the world loves anyone else, naturally the strong will overpower the weak, the many will oppress the few, the wealthy will mock the poor, the honored will disdain the humble, and the cunning will deceive the simple. Therefore, all the calamities, strifes, complaints, and hatred in the world have arisen out of a lack of mutual love. Therefore, the benevolent disapproved of this lack.

Now that there is disapproval, how can we have this condition altered?

Motse <note>The philosopher Mozi (c. 470–391 BCE).</note> said it is to be altered by the way of <term>universal love</term> <note>original: "universal love"; the Mohist concept of *jian ai*, meaning impartial care or concern for everyone equally.</note> and mutual aid.

But what is the way of universal love and mutual aid?

Motse said: It is to regard the state of others as one's own, the houses of others as one's own, and the persons of others as one's self. When <term>feudal lords</term> <note>Regional rulers who governed territories during the Zhou Dynasty.</note> love one another, there will be no more war; when heads of houses love one another, there will be no more mutual <term>usurpation</term> <note>The act of seizing someone else's power or property by force.</note>; when individuals love one another, there will be no more mutual injury. When the ruler and the ruled love each other, they will be gracious and loyal; when father and son love each other, they will be affectionate and filial; when elder and younger brothers love each other, they will be harmonious. When all the people in the world love one another, then the strong will not overpower the weak, the many will not oppress the few, the wealthy will not mock the poor, the honored will not disdain the humble, and the cunning will not deceive the simple. And it is all due to mutual love that calamities,

<summary>Motse argues that all social disorder stems from a lack of mutual love and proposes "universal love" and "mutual aid" as the solution. He defines this as treating the interests and persons of others with the same care one gives to oneself, which would naturally end war, oppression, and family strife.</summary>

...conflicts, complaints, and hatred are prevented from arising. Therefore, those who are benevolent <term>exalt</term> <note>original: "exalt"; to praise or promote something highly.</note> it.

But the gentlemen of the world would say: "That is all well and good. It is, of course, very excellent when love becomes universal. But it is merely a difficult and distant ideal."

Motse said: This is simply because the gentlemen of the world do not recognize what benefits the world, nor do they understand what causes its disasters. Now, to besiege a city, to fight in the fields, or to achieve a reputation at the cost of one's life—these are things that men find difficult. Yet, when a superior encourages them, the <term>multitude</term> <note>The common people or the masses.</note> can do them. Furthermore, universal love and mutual aid are quite different from these difficult tasks. Whoever loves others is loved by others; whoever benefits others is benefited by others; whoever hates others is hated by others; whoever injures others is injured by others. Then, what difficulty is there with universal love? It is only that the ruler fails to embody it in his government and the ordinary man fails to embody it in his conduct.

Formerly, Lord Wen of the state of Chin <note>Lord Wen of Jin (reigned 636–628 BCE), a famous ruler of the Spring and Autumn period.</note> liked the rough, simple uniforms of soldiers. And so, all his ministers and officers wore sheepskin jackets, carried their swords in leather belts, and put on hats made of spun silk. <gloss>1</gloss> Thus attired, they attended the Lord when they went in to the palace and paced the court when they stayed outside. What was the reason for this? It was because the ruled will carry out whatever the ruler encourages. And Lord Ling of the state of Ch'u <note>Lord Ling of Chu (reigned 540–529 BCE).</note> favored slender waists. And so all his ministers and...

Officers restricted themselves to a single meal a day. They tightened their belts only after exhaling, and they became so weak they could not stand up without leaning against the wall. Again, Lord Kou Chien <note>original: "Kou Chien"; modern Pinyin: Goujian (reigned 496–465 B.C.), the King of Yue, famous for his perseverance and eventual victory over the state of Wu.</note> of the state of Yüeh admired a warrior’s courage and trained his subjects accordingly. To test his soldiers, he had his palace boat set on fire. He proclaimed that all the treasures of the state were inside it. He personally beat the drum to urge them forward. Hearing the drum, the soldiers rushed into the flames in total disorder. More than a hundred strong men perished in the fire. Only then did the Lord beat the gong to signal their retreat.

Therefore, Motse said: Now, things like wearing scantily dyed, coarse clothing, or achieving fame at the cost of one's life, are things people find very difficult. Yet, when the ruler encourages them, the <term>multitude</term> <note>The common people or the masses.</note> can endure them. Furthermore, universal love and mutual aid are different from these. Whoever loves others is loved by others; whoever benefits others is benefited by others; whoever hates others is hated by others; and whoever injures others is injured by others. Then, what is so difficult about universal love? It is only that the ruler fails to embody it in his government and the ordinary man fails to embody it in his conduct.

Nevertheless, the gentlemen of the empire think that, although it would be an excellent thing if love could be universalized, it is something quite <term>impracticable</term> <note>Impossible to do or carry out in practice.</note>. They say it is like picking up Mount Tai and leaping over the Chi River <note>Mount Tai is one of the Five Sacred Mountains of China; the Chi (Qi) River was a major waterway in ancient China. # UNIVERSAL LOVE (II)

<margin>¹ This referred to the heart of Chinese territory at that time.</margin>

...to be able to carry Mount Tai and leap over the Chi River would be an extreme feat of strength. Such a feat has never been performed from ancient times to the present. But universal love and mutual aid are quite different from this. The ancient sage-kings actually practiced them. 

How do we know they did? When Yu <note>original: "Yü"; Yu the Great, legendary founder of the Xia dynasty, celebrated for his dedication to flood control.</note> was working to bring the Great Flood <note>original: "the Deluge"</note> under control, he dug the West River and the Yu Tou River in the west to drain the water from the Qu, Sun, and Huang Rivers. In the north, he built a dam across the Yuan and Ku Rivers to fill the Hou Zhi Ti (a basin) and the Hu Chi River. He used Mount Di Zhu as a water divide and dug a tunnel through Mount Lung Men. All these things were done to benefit the people west of the Yellow River and various northern tribes, including the Yen, Tai, Hu, and Ho. 

In the east, he drained the Great Plain and built levees <note>original: "dykes"</note> along the Meng Chu River. The watercourse was divided into nine canals to regulate the water in the east and to benefit the people of the District of Chi.¹ In the south, he completed the work on the Yangtze, Han, Huai, and Ju Rivers. These ran eastward and emptied into the Five Lakes. This was done to benefit the people of Jing, Chu, Kan, Yue, and the southern tribes. All these were the deeds of Yu. We can see, then, that universal love can be put into practice.

When King Wen <note>The founder of the Zhou Dynasty, held up as a model of a virtuous and just ruler.</note> was ruling the Western land, his influence shone like the sun and the moon across all four corners of the earth. He did not allow large states to oppress small states; he did not allow the many <note>original: "multitude"</note> to oppress the vulnerable <note>original: "single-handed"</note>; he did not allow the influential...

<summary>Motse argues that universal love is a practical goal by citing the historical examples of Yu the Great’s massive flood-control projects and King Wen’s fair governance. These rulers prioritized the welfare of all people, proving that such a virtue can be successfully implemented.</summary>

...[ruling, he did not allow the wealthy] and strong to take away the grain and livestock from the farmers. Heaven visited him with blessings. Consequently, the elderly and childless had the means to support themselves in their old age, those who were alone or without brothers had the opportunity to join in the social life of others, and orphans had the support they needed to grow up. This was what King Wen <note>The first king of the Zhou Dynasty, revered as a model of wisdom and benevolence.</note> accomplished. We can, then, put universal love into practice.

When King Wu <note>The son of King Wen, who overthrew the corrupt Shang Dynasty to establish the Zhou Dynasty.</note> was about to perform a ritual service at Mount Tai <note>One of the most sacred mountains in China, often used for imperial sacrifices.</note>, it was recorded: "Blessed is Mount Tai. The Duke of Zhou, by long descent, is about to perform his duty. As I have obtained the approval of Heaven, the virtuous <note>original: "magnanimous"</note> arise to save the people of China¹ as well as the surrounding tribes from the tyranny of Emperor Zhou <note>original: "Chow"; the last, decadent ruler of the Shang Dynasty</note>. Though Emperor Zhou has many near relatives, they cannot compare with the virtuous. If there is sin anywhere, I alone am responsible."² This describes the deeds of King Wu. We can, then, put universal love into practice.

Therefore, Motse said: If the rulers sincerely desire the world to be wealthy and dislike poverty, and if they desire order and dislike chaos, they should promote universal love and mutual aid. This is the way of the ancient sage-kings and the way to bring order to the world; it should not be neglected.

---

¹ Hsia <note>modern: "Xia"</note> is one of the honorary names for the Chinese people or China. *Shang Hsia*, then, means China under the Shang Dynasty.

² This quotation as a whole is not found in any of the existing versions of the classics. The last two sentences are found in the "Great Declaration" in the *Shu Ching* <note>The *Classic of History* or *Book of Documents*</note>. James Legge <note>A famous 19th-century Scottish sinologist and translator</note> gives a different meaning to the last sentence. A passage in "The Successful Completion of the War" shows some resemblance to the first part of the quotation. However, this specific essay is one of the most well-known forgeries in the entire *Shu Ching* collection. The references to Legge are vol. iii, part ii, p. 292 and pp. 312-13.

<summary>Motse cites the examples of King Wen and King Wu to demonstrate that universal love is a practical and historical reality that brings social welfare and justice, urging modern rulers to adopt these principles for a stable society.</summary>

<meta>This page begins Chapter XVI, "Universal Love (III)," where Motse shifts from historical examples to a logical analysis of the world's problems. He identifies "partiality"—favoring one's own interests over others—as the root cause of all social and political conflict.</meta>

# CHAPTER XVI

## UNIVERSAL LOVE (III)

Motse <note>also known as Mozi, a Chinese philosopher of the 5th century BCE</note> said: The purpose of the virtuous <note>original: "magnanimous"</note> person lies in procuring benefits for the world and eliminating its misfortunes <note>original: "calamities"</note>. Now, among all the current misfortunes, which are the most important? The attack on small states by large ones, the disruption of small houses by large ones, the oppression of the weak by the strong, the misuse of the few by the many, the deception of the simple by the cunning, and the disdain shown toward the humble by the honored—these are the misfortunes in the world <note>original: "empire"</note>. 

Again, the lack of grace on the part of the ruler, the lack of loyalty on the part of the subjects, the lack of affection on the part of the father, and the lack of filial piety <note>the virtue of respect for one's parents and ancestors</note> on the part of the son—these are further misfortunes in the world. Also, the mutual injury and harm which unscrupulous people do to one another with weapons, poison, water, and fire is still another misfortune in the world.

When we come to think about the cause of all these misfortunes, how have they arisen? Have they arisen out of love for others and benefiting others? Of course, we should say no. We should say they have arisen out of hate for others and injuring others. If we should classify one by one all those who hate others and injure others, should we find them to be universal in their love or partial? Of course, we should say they are partial. Now, since partiality against one another is the cause of the major misfortunes in the world, then partiality is wrong.

Motse continued: Whoever criticizes others must have

<summary>Motse identifies the various misfortunes of the world—such as war, oppression, and family strife—and argues that they all stem from "partiality" rather than universal love. He concludes that because partiality causes these evils, it is inherently wrong.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, Mozi, Universal Love, partiality, social order, ethics, filial piety, calamities</keywords>

--- Page 102 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical argument from the beginning of Chapter XVI. Having identified "partiality" (favoring oneself and one's own group) as the source of the world's harms, Motse now proposes "universality" (universal love) as the necessary replacement and solution.</meta>

<page-num>88</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...[there must be] something to replace them. Criticism without a positive suggestion is like trying to stop a flood with more water or put out a fire with more fire. It will certainly be worthless.

Motse said: Partiality <note>favoring one's own interests or group over others</note> is to be replaced by universality <note>an inclusive, equal concern for all people</note>. But how can partiality be replaced by universality? Now, when everyone regards the states of others as they regard their own, who would attack another's state? Others would be treated like oneself. When everyone regards the capital cities of others as they regard their own, who would seize another's capital? Others would be treated like oneself. When everyone regards the houses of others as they regard their own, who would disturb another's house? Others would be treated like oneself. 

Now, when states and cities do not attack and seize each other, and when families <note>original: "clans"</note> and individuals do not disturb and harm one another—is this a misfortune <note>original: "calamity"</note> or a benefit to the world? Of course, it is a benefit. When we consider the cause of these various benefits, how have they arisen? Have they arisen out of hating and injuring others? Of course not. We must say they have arisen out of loving and benefiting others. If we were to categorize all those who love and benefit others, would we find them to be partial or universal? We would certainly say they are universal. Now, since universal love is the cause of the greatest benefits in the world, Motse proclaims that universal love is right.

And, as has already been said, the interest of the virtuous <note>original: "magnanimous"</note> person lies in securing benefits for the world and eliminating its disasters. Now that we have discovered that the consequences of universal love are the greatest benefits to the world, and...

<summary>Motse argues that partiality must be replaced by universal love, demonstrating that if people treated others' property and states as their own, social and political conflict would cease. He concludes that because universal love produces the greatest benefits for the world, it is the morally correct path.</summary>

--- Page 103 ---
<meta>This page continues the logical argument from page 88, concluding that partiality is the source of world calamities and moving into a practical defense of universal love against critics who doubt its usefulness.</meta>

<page-num>89</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL LOVE (III)</header>

...[the consequences of] partiality are the major calamities <note>disasters or great misfortunes</note> in the world. This is the reason why Motse said partiality is wrong and universality is right. When we try to develop and provide benefits for the world using universal love as our standard, then attentive ears and keen eyes will respond in service to one another, limbs will be strengthened to work for one another, and those who know the Way <note>original: "Tao"; the path of virtue or natural order</note> will untiringly instruct others. Thus, the elderly and those who have neither spouse nor children will have the support and supplies to live out their old age, and the young, the weak, and orphans will have the care and guidance <note>original: "admonition"</note> they need to grow up. When universal love is adopted as the standard, these are the resulting benefits. It is incomprehensible, then, why people should object to universal love when they hear of it.

Yet the objections are not yet exhausted. It is asked: "It may be a good thing, but can it be of any use?"

Motse replied: If it were not useful, then even I would disapprove of it. But how can there be anything that is good but not useful? Let us consider the matter from both sides. Suppose there are two men. Let one of them hold to partiality and the other to universality. Then the advocate of partiality would say to himself, "How can I take care of my friend as I do of myself? How can I take care of his parents as if they were my own?" Therefore, when he finds his friend hungry, he would not feed him, and when he finds him cold, he would not clothe him. In his friend's illness, he would not nurse <note>original: "minister to"</note> him, and when he is dead, he would not bury him. Such is the word and such is the deed of the advocate of partiality. The advocate of universality is quite unlike this, both in word [and deed...]

<summary>Motse argues that universal love creates a mutually supportive society that cares for the vulnerable and begins a thought experiment to prove that universal love is more practical and useful than partiality.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, universal love, partiality, social welfare, Tao, utility, ethics, mutual aid</keywords>

--- Page 104 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the "partial" person and the "universal" person, illustrating how their differing philosophies translate into specific actions toward their friends and family.</meta>

<page-num>90</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...and in action. He would say to himself, "I have heard that to be an honorable person <note>original: "superior man"; a person of high moral character</note>, one should care for his friend as he cares for himself, and care for his friend’s parents as if they were his own." Therefore, when he finds his friend hungry, he feeds him; when he finds him cold, he clothes him. During his friend's illness, he serves him, and when he dies, he buries him. Such are the words and such are the actions of the advocate of universal love <note>original: "universality"</note>.

These two types of people, then, are opposite to each other in both word and deed. Suppose they are both sincere in their words and decisive in their actions, so that their speech and behavior match perfectly—like two halves of a matching tally stick <note>a "tally" refers to a piece of wood or bone split in two to prove an agreement when the halves are rejoined</note>—and that every word they speak is realized in their deeds. Let us then consider further: 

Suppose a war breaks out, and a man is dressed in his armor and helmet, ready to join the army, where life and death are unpredictable. Or suppose a man is commissioned as a deputy by the ruler to travel to distant countries like Ba, Yue, Qi, and Jing <note>ancient Chinese states; original spelling: Pa, Yüeh, Ch’i, and Ching</note>, and his arrival and return are entirely uncertain. Under such circumstances, let us ask: whom would a person trust with the care of his family and parents? Would it be the friend who practices universal love or the friend who shows partiality? 

It seems to me that on occasions like these, there are no fools in the world. Even if a person is someone who objects to universal love, he will still choose to trust the universal friend. This is a verbal objection to the principle while actually choosing to live by it—a self-contradiction between one’s words and one's deeds. It is baffling, then, why people should object to universal love when they hear of it.

Yet the objections are not yet exhausted. It is further argued:

This demonstrates that people instinctively recognize the practical value of universal love despite their theoretical objections.</summary>

<meta>This page transitions the argument from the behavior of ordinary individuals to the responsibilities of rulers, comparing how a "partial" ruler and a "universal" ruler respond to the needs of their people.</meta>

<page-num>91</page-num>
<header>UNIVERSAL LOVE (III)</header>

Perhaps this is a good standard <note>original: "criterion"</note> for choosing between ordinary men, but it might not apply to rulers.

Let us consider the matter from both sides once more. Suppose there are two rulers: let one of them practice favoritism <note>original: "partiality"</note> and the other practice universal love <note>original: "universality"</note>. The partial ruler would say to himself, "How can I take care of my people as well as I take care of myself? That would be completely against common sense." He believes a person’s life on earth is brief, like a galloping horse glimpsed through a crack in a wall <note>a traditional Chinese metaphor for the fleeting nature of time</note>. Therefore, when he finds his people hungry, he does not feed them; when he finds them cold, he does not clothe them. When they are sick, he does not tend to them, and when they die, he does not bury them. Such is the speech and such is the conduct of the partial ruler. 

The universal ruler is the opposite in both word and deed. He would say to himself, "I have heard that to be a righteous <note>original: "upright"</note> ruler of the world, one should first look after his people and only then look after himself." Therefore, when he finds his people hungry, he feeds them; when he finds them cold, he clothes them. In their sickness, he tends to them, and when they die, he buries them. Such is the speech and such is the conduct of the universal ruler.

These two rulers, then, are in direct opposition in both word and deed. Suppose they are both sincere in their words and decisive in their actions, so that their speech and behavior match perfectly—like two halves of a matching tally stick <note>a piece of wood or bone split in two to prove an agreement when the halves are rejoined</note>—and that every word they speak is realized in their deeds. Let us then consider further: Suppose a disastrous plague <note>original: "pestilence"</note> breaks out, leaving most people in misery and extreme poverty <note>original: "privation"</note>, and many lie dead...

<summary>Motse compares the "partial ruler," who prioritizes himself over his subjects, with the "universal ruler," who believes a leader's duty is to provide for his people's basic needs and survival.</summary>
<keywords>partiality, universal love, leadership, social welfare, Mozi, ancient Chinese philosophy, duty of rulers</keywords>

<margin>¹ This refers to those who lack the means to be buried in proper tombs. Properly burying the dead was considered a primary duty for both a son and a ruler.</margin>
...leaving them to lie in ditches.¹ Under such circumstances, let us ask: if a person had to choose between these two rulers, which would he prefer? It seems to me that in such a situation, there are no fools in the world. Even a person who objects to the principle of universal love would choose the universal ruler. This is a verbal objection to the principle while actually choosing it in practice—a clear contradiction between one's words and one's deeds. It is hard to understand, then, why people object to universal love when they hear of it.

Yet the objections are not yet exhausted. Critics point out that while universal love may be noble <note>original: "magnanimous"</note> and righteous, how can it actually be realized? They argue that universal love is as impossible as carrying Mount Tai <note>original: "Mt. T’ai," one of the sacred mountains of China</note> under one's arm and leaping over rivers. Therefore, they say universal love is nothing more than a pious wish—how can it ever be made real?

Motse replied: Carrying Mount Tai and leaping over rivers are things that have never been accomplished since the beginning of man. But universal love and mutual aid were personally practiced by the six ancient sage-kings <note>The legendary ideal rulers of China’s past, such as Yao, Shun, and Yu</note>.

How do we know they actually did this?

Motse said: I was not a contemporary of theirs; I have never heard their voices or seen their faces. However, the sources of our knowledge are found in what was written on bamboo and silk, what was engraved in metal and stone, and what was inscribed on the ritual vessels handed down to future generations.²

The *Great Declaration* <note>A chapter from the *Book of History*</note> proclaims: “King Wen was like the sun and the moon, shedding glorious and shining light in the four directions <note>original: "four quarters"</note> as well as over the Western land.”

<page-num>93</page-num>

# UNIVERSAL LOVE (III)

This is to say that the love of King Wen <note>The founder of the Zhou Dynasty, regarded as a model of virtue and wisdom.</note> was so broad and universal that it was like the sun and the moon shining upon the world without partiality. This is an example of universal love on the part of King Wen; what Motse has been discussing is actually derived from the example of King Wen.

Moreover, this is true not only in the "Great Declaration" <note>A chapter in the *Shujing*, or *Book of Documents*.</note>, but also in the "Oath of Yu." <gloss>1</gloss> Yu <note>Yu the Great, the legendary founder of the Xia Dynasty, famous for controlling the Great Flood.</note> said in that text: "Come, all you many people, listen and pay attention to my words. It is not that I, a single person, would willingly stir up this conflict. The Prince of Miao is becoming more and more unreasonable; he deserves punishment from Heaven. Therefore, I lead you to appoint the lords of the states and go to punish the Prince of Miao." Yu went to war against the Prince of Miao not for the sake of increasing his wealth, multiplying his blessings <note>original: "felicitations"</note>, or indulging his senses <note>original: "indulging his ears and eyes"</note>, but for the sake of securing benefits for the world and removing its troubles. This is universal love on the part of Yu, and what Motse has been talking about is really derived from the example of Yu.

Again, it is true not only in the "Oath of Yu" but also in the "Oath of Tang." Tang <note>King Tang, the founder of the Shang Dynasty.</note> said: "I, the unworthy Lü <note>Tang’s personal name.</note>, venture to offer a sacrifice of a first-born male animal to Heaven on high and Mother Earth, saying, 'Now there is a great drought sent from heaven. It happens right in my, Lü's, time. I do not know whether I have wronged Heaven or men. Goodness I dare not hide; guilt I dare not excuse—this is clearly seen in the mind of God <note>The original term is *Shangdi*, the High Sovereign or Supreme Deity.</note>. If there is sin...'"

---

<gloss>1</gloss> This title does not appear in the current text of the *Shujing* (*Book of Documents*). Something similar to the content of the following quotation is found in the "Counsels of Yu." Compare <note>original: "Cf."</note> Legge, vol. iii, pp. 64, 65.

<summary>Motse cites historical "oaths" and declarations from the sage-kings Wen, Yu, and Tang to demonstrate that their military actions and sacrifices were motivated by universal love and the welfare of the people rather than personal desire.</summary>

anywhere, hold me responsible for it; if I myself am guilty, may the rest be spared."<sup>1</sup> This means that although he held the honor of being an emperor and the wealth of possessing the whole world, Tang did not shrink from offering himself as a sacrifice to plead with God <note>original: "God" - here referring to *Shangdi*, the High Deity in ancient Chinese belief</note> and the spirits. This is universal love on Tang's part, and what Motse has been discussing is truly derived from Tang's example.

Furthermore, this is true not only in the "Oath of Yu" and the "Oath of Tang," but also in the "Poems of Chou."<sup>2</sup> To quote:

> "The way of the [good] emperor is wide and straight, without partiality and without favoritism. The way of the [good] emperor is even and smooth, without favoritism and without partiality. It is straight like an arrow and just like a balance. The superior man follows it, [and even] the unprincipled man looks on [without resentment]."

Thus, the principle I have been explaining should not be regarded as a mere theoretical <note>original: "doctrinaire"</note> notion. In the past, when Wen and Wu administered the government, both of them rewarded the virtuous and punished the wicked without showing partiality to their own relatives or brothers. This is precisely the universal love of Wen and Wu. And what Motse has been talking about is truly derived from the examples of Wen and Wu.<sup>3</sup> It is incomprehensible, then, why people should object to universal love when they hear of it.

<page-num>95</page-num>

# UNIVERSAL LOVE (III)

Yet the objection is still not fully addressed. It raises the question: if one does not think specifically in terms of benefits and harms to one's own parents, can it truly be considered filial piety?

Motse replied: "Now let us examine the plans that filial sons make for their parents. I ask you, when they make these plans, do they desire for others to love their parents or to hate them? Judging from the whole doctrine of filial piety, it is certain that they desire others to love their parents. Now, what should I do first in order to achieve this? Should I first love the parents of others so that they will love my parents in return, or should I first hate the parents of others so that they will love my parents in return? Clearly, I should first love the parents of others so that they will love my parents in return.

Therefore, those who desire to be filial toward one another's parents, if they must choose whether to love or hate the parents of others, had best first love and benefit them. Would anyone suggest that all filial sons are so foolish and stubborn <note>original: "stupid and incorrigible"</note> that they cannot understand this? Let us inquire further into the matter. It is said in the *Ta Ya*<sup>1</sup> among the books of the ancient kings: 'No thought is without its value; no virtue goes unrewarded. When a peach is thrown to us, we return a plum <note>original: "prune" — in the original Chinese idiom, this refers to a plum (li), representing a fair exchange</note>.' This is to say that whoever loves others will be loved, and whoever hates others will be hated. It is then quite difficult to understand why people should object to universal love when they hear of it."

Is it because they believe it is difficult and impossible to put into practice? There are—

James Legge <note>a famous 19th-century translator of Chinese classics</note>, in volume 4, page 514, translates it as:
> "Every word finds its answer;
> Every good deed has its reward."

<meta>This page continues the argument that universal love is practical and achievable. Motse uses historical examples of three different rulers who successfully influenced their subjects to adopt extremely difficult habits—starvation, martyrdom, and poverty—to demonstrate that people will follow the lead of their superiors even in the most challenging circumstances.</meta>

<note>Ching and Ch’u are names for the same state during this period. In chapter 15, page 83, the name "Ch’u" is used.</note>

...instances of even much harder tasks being accomplished. In the past, Lord Ling of the state of Ching <note>original: "Ching ¹"</note> favored slender waists. During his reign, the people of the state of Ching ate no more than one meal a day. They became so weak that they could not stand up without support and could not walk without leaning against a wall. Now, a restricted diet is very difficult to endure, yet they did so. Because Lord Ling encouraged it, his people were able to change within a single generation to match the preferences of their superior. 

Lord Kou-chien of the state of Yue admired courage and trained his ministers and soldiers in it for three years. Concerned that their training had not yet made them proficient, he ordered a boat to be set on fire and beat the drum to signal an advance. The soldiers at the front of the ranks were even pushed forward into the danger. Those who perished in the flames and the water were countless. Yet, even then, they would not retreat without a signal. Ordinarily, the soldiers of Yue would have been terrified. To be burned alive is a difficult task, and yet it was achieved. Because the Lord of Yue encouraged it, his people were able to change within a single generation to match the preferences of their superior. 

Lord Wen of the state of Chin liked simple, coarse clothing. Consequently, during his reign, the people of Chin wore suits of plain cloth, sheepskin jackets, hats of spun silk, and large, rough shoes. Dressed in this way, they would go in to see the Lord and walk about the court. Wearing such coarse clothing is difficult for some to accept, yet it was done. Because Lord Wen encouraged it, his people were able to change within a single generation to match the preferences of their superior.

<summary>Motse provides historical examples of rulers who persuaded their subjects to adopt extreme behaviors—starvation, sacrificial courage, and plain dress—to argue that society can be transformed if a leader encourages the principle of universal love.</summary>

--- Page 111 ---
<meta>This page concludes the argument that social change is driven by the example of leaders. Motse (Mozi) argues that if subjects could be persuaded by their rulers to starve themselves or die in battle, they can certainly be persuaded to practice universal love, which is far more beneficial and natural.</meta>

<page-num>97</page-num>

### UNIVERSAL LOVE (III)

...wearing coarse clothing are among the hardest things in the world, yet when the leaders <note>original: "superiors"</note> encouraged these things, the people were able to change within a single generation. Why was this so? It was due to the people's desire to conform to their superiors. Now, as for **universal love** and **mutual aid**, they are undoubtedly beneficial and easy to practice. It seems to me that the only trouble is that there is no leader who encourages them. If there were a leader who encouraged these principles—promoting them with rewards and praise, and discouraging their opposite with punishments—I believe people would turn toward universal love and mutual aid as naturally as fire moves upward and water flows downward. It would be an unstoppable force in the world.

Therefore, **universal love** is truly the way of the **sage-kings** <note>The legendary, virtuous rulers of China's ancient past, such as Yao and Shun.</note>. It is what brings peace to rulers and provides for the well-being of the people. The gentleman <note>original: "Chün-tzu," referring to a person of high moral character.</note> would do well to understand and practice universal love; then he would be gracious as a ruler, loyal as a minister, affectionate as a father, filial <note>original: "filial," meaning showing respect and devotion to one's parents.</note> as a son, courteous as an elder brother, and respectful as a younger brother. So, if the gentleman desires to be a gracious ruler, a loyal minister, an affectionate father, a filial son, a courteous elder brother, and a respectful younger brother, universal love must be practiced. It is the way of the sage-kings and the greatest blessing for the people.

---

<note>¹ Throughout this entire discussion of universal love, and especially in this summary at the end, the Western reader might notice the absence of the relationship between husband and wife, or mother and child. A "mother" is not specifically mentioned because, in the context of parental affection, she is considered completely equal to the father. The relationship between husband and wife is another of the **five cardinal relations** <note>The traditional Five Relationships of Confucian and Mohist ethics: ruler and subject, father and son, husband and wife, elder and younger brother, and friend and friend.</note> in Chinese society. However, this relationship was rarely discussed in historical literature because Chinese culture shared a sense of privacy and restraint regarding natural and domestic matters. The author also likely felt that this relationship would naturally improve once all other social roles followed the principle of universal love.</note>

<summary>Motse concludes that universal love is easier to implement than the difficult sacrifices rulers often demand of their subjects. He argues that if leaders model and reward universal love, it will become as natural as fire rising or water falling, fulfilling all essential social roles.</summary>

<keywords>universal love, mutual aid, sage-kings, social modeling, five cardinal relations, filial piety, social harmony</keywords>

<meta>This page begins the fifth book of the works of Mozi, transitioning from the concept of universal love to a direct moral argument against offensive warfare. The author uses a series of analogies involving theft and violence to establish a scale of criminal behavior.</meta>

# BOOK V

## CHAPTER XVII

### CONDEMNATION OF OFFENSIVE WAR (I) <note>This chapter is quoted in its entirety in a translation by Professor Hu Shih in his *The Development of the Logical Method in Ancient China*, pp. 69–71. There is almost no difference between that translation and this one, except for a few choices of words and expressions. The reader may refer to that work for a more detailed comparison.</note>

Suppose a man enters someone else’s orchard and steals their peaches and plums. Upon hearing of this, the public will condemn it; when the authorities catch him, they will punish him. Why? Because he injures others to profit himself. 

As for stealing <note>original: "seizing"</note> dogs, pigs, chickens, and piglets from another person, this is even more unjust <note>original: "unrighteous"</note> than stealing peaches and plums from an orchard. Why? Because it causes others to suffer more <note>A clause seems to have been lost here when comparing this sentence with the following sentences that express the same idea. The correct text here likely should be: "Because others are caused to suffer more; when others are caused to suffer more, it is more inhumane and criminal."</note>, and it is more inhumane and criminal. 

When it comes to entering another person's stable and taking their horses and oxen, it is even more inhumane than stealing their dogs, pigs, chickens, and piglets. Why? Because others are made to suffer more; and when others are made to suffer more, the act is more inhumane and criminal. 

Finally, as for murdering an innocent person, stripping him of his clothing, and seizing his spear and sword, this is even more unjust than entering another's stable and taking his horses and oxen. Why? Because it...

<summary>Mozi begins a logical argument against offensive warfare by comparing it to increasingly severe acts of theft, arguing that the more harm an act causes to others for personal gain, the more criminal and inhumane it is.</summary>
<keywords>offensive war, injustice, Mozi, moral philosophy, analogies of theft, crime and punishment, Hu Shih</keywords>

<page-num>99</page-num>

...causes others to suffer more; when others are caused to suffer more, then the act is more inhumane and criminal.

All the gentlemen <note>In this context, "gentlemen" refers to the educated ruling class or scholars (the *junzi*) who were expected to uphold moral standards.</note> of the world know that they should condemn these things, calling them unjust <note>original: "unrighteous"</note>. But when it comes to the massive attack on other states, <margin>The great injustice of attacking states.</margin> they do not know that they should condemn it. On the contrary, they applaud it, calling it righteous. Can this be said to be knowing the difference between justice and injustice?

The murder of one person is called unjust and incurs one death penalty. Following this argument, the murder of ten persons will be ten times as unjust and there should be ten death penalties; the murder of a hundred persons will be a hundred times as unjust and there should be a hundred death penalties. All the gentlemen of the world know that they should condemn these things, calling them unjust. But when it comes to the great injustice of attacking states, they do not know that they should condemn it. On the contrary, they applaud it, calling it righteous. 

And they are really ignorant of its being unjust. Hence they have recorded their judgment to leave to their descendants <note>original: "posterity"</note>. If they did know that it is unjust, then why would they record their false judgment to leave to future generations?

Now, if there were a man who, upon seeing a little blackness, should say it is black, but, upon seeing much, should say it is white; then we should think he could not tell the difference between black and white. If, upon tasting a little bitterness one should say it is bitter, but, upon tasting much, should...

# CHAPTER XVIII
## CONDEMNATION OF OFFENSIVE WAR (II)

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: If the rulers of today sincerely wish to be careful in their criticism and praise, wise in their rewards and punishments, and moderate in their government and legal administration; <note>The original text of this paragraph is incomplete. The lost clause likely read: “They must condemn offensive wars.”</note>

Therefore Mozi said: There is an ancient saying that when one is not successful in making plans, one should use the past to predict the future <note>original: "predict the future by the past." This idea is also explored in a passage in the *Confucian Analects* (論語; *Lun Yu*). See Legge, vol. i, book i, p. 8.</note> and learn about what is absent by looking at what is present. When one plans in this way, one can be enlightened. <note>This paragraph serves as an introduction to the historical argument which forms the main body of this chapter.</note>

Now, consider a country going to war. If it is in winter, it will be too cold; if it is in summer, it will be too hot. Therefore, war should be waged in neither winter nor summer. If it is in spring, it will take people away from sowing and planting; if it is in autumn, it will take people away from reaping and harvesting. <note>The text appears to be missing a sentence here: “So it should be neither in spring nor in autumn.”</note> Should the people be taken away from their work in any of these seasons, countless numbers would die of hunger and cold. 

Furthermore, when the army sets out, the bamboo arrows, the feathered flags, the tents, the armor, the shields, and the sword hilts—countless quantities of these will break and rot and never be recovered. The spears, the lances, the swords,

<summary>Mozi begins a practical critique of offensive war, arguing that it is physically and economically ruinous because it disrupts the agricultural seasons and destroys vast amounts of military equipment.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, offensive war, military logistics, agricultural cycle, statecraft, Confucian Analects, economic waste</keywords>

the daggers <note>original: "poniards"</note>, the chariots, the carts—countless quantities of these will break and rot and never be returned. Then countless horses and oxen will start out fat and come back lean, or will not return at all. And countless people will die because their food supplies will be cut off and cannot be delivered due to the great distances of the roads. And countless people will fall sick and die from the constant danger, the irregularity of eating and drinking, and the extremes of hunger and overeating. Then, the army will be lost in large numbers or entirely; in either case, the losses will be countless. This means the spirits of the ancestors will lose those who offer them sacrifices, and the number of these lost worshippers will also be countless.

Why then does the government deprive the people of their opportunities and benefits to such a great extent? It has been answered: "I desire the fame of a victor and the possessions obtained through conquest. That is why I do it."

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: But when we consider victory in itself, there is nothing useful about it. When we consider the possessions obtained through it, they do not even make up for the loss. Now, regarding the siege of a city of three *li* <term>li: a Chinese unit of distance, approximately one-third of a mile or 500 meters</term> or an outer wall <term>kuo</term> <gloss>1</gloss> of seven *li*—if these could be captured without using weapons or taking lives, it would be acceptable. But in reality, those killed must be counted by the ten thousand, and those widowed or left solitary must be counted by the thousand, before a city of three *li* or an outer wall <term>kuo</term> of seven *li*

<summary>Mozi details the catastrophic logistical and human costs of offensive warfare, arguing that the meager territorial gains of a siege never justify the massive loss of life, resources, and religious traditions.</summary>

# CONDEMNATION OF OFFENSIVE WAR (II)

...thousands of lives before a city could be captured. Moreover, the great powers <term>states of ten thousand chariots: major states whose military strength was measured by the size of their chariot force</term> now have thousands of empty towns that could be occupied without conquest, and vast territories totaling millions of *mu* <term>mu: a Chinese unit of land area, approximately one-sixth of an acre</term> which could be cultivated without conquest. In these states, land is abundant but people are few. To drive the people to their deaths and increase the danger felt by both rulers and subjects just to capture an empty city is to throw away what is needed (people) and treasure what is already held in abundance (land). Such an undertaking does not serve the interests of the country.

Those who try to justify <note>original: "gloss over"</note> offensive wars argue: "In the south, there are the lords of Chu <note>original: "Ching"</note> and Yueh, and in the north, there are the lords of Qi <note>original: "Ch’i"</note> and Jin <note>original: "Chin"</note>. When their states were first assigned to them, they were only about thirty miles <note>original: "a hundred li"</note> square in area, with only a few tens of thousands of people. Through wars and attacks, their territories have increased to over a thousand miles square and their populations to several million. Therefore, offensive wars should not be condemned."

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: Although these four or five states may have reaped benefits, their conduct does not follow the Way <term>Way (Tao): the correct, universal principle of conduct</term>. It is like a physician giving medicine to his patients. If a physician were to give every sick person in the world the exact same medicine, out of the ten thousand who took it, there might be four or five who were cured. Nevertheless, it could not be said to be...

<summary>Mozi argues that offensive war is irrational because states already have more land than people to work it, and he dismisses the success of a few large empires as a rare exception rather than a universal justification for conflict.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, offensive war, Tao, mu, chariot force, Qi, Jin, Chu, Yueh, land vs population</keywords>

--- Page 118 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy of a "medicine" that benefits a few but kills many, applying it to offensive wars. Mozi provides historical examples of states destroyed by such warfare and addresses counter-arguments regarding military strength.</meta>

...be a common medicine <note>original: "commonly beneficial"</note>. Thus, a filial son would not give it to his parent, and a loyal minister would not give it to his king. <note>This illustration reflects the early stages of medical practice in China. Legend says that Shen Nung (the Divine Farmer), in the 28th century BCE, tasted various herbs himself to determine their properties. He remains the patron god of Chinese medicine.</note> 

Since the world was divided into states in ancient times, a great many of them have been destroyed by attacks. We have heard of older cases through history, and we have seen recent cases with our own eyes. How do we know this is true? In the east, there was the state of Ju <note>original: "Chü"</note>. It was a small state situated in the midst of large states. It did not show respect or obedience to the big states, and therefore the latter did not like or favor it. Consequently, from the east, Yue <note>original: "Yüeh"</note> seized its land by force, and from the west, Qi <note>original: "Ch'i"</note> swallowed it up entirely. Thus, Ju was destroyed between two large states because of offensive wars. 

It was also due to offensive wars that, in the south, the states of Chen <note>original: "Ch'en"</note> and Cai <note>original: "Ts'ai"</note> were wiped out by Wu and Yue. It was also due to offensive wars that, in the north, Butuho <note>original: "Pu T'u Ho"</note> perished among the states of Yan <note>original: "Yen"</note>, Dai <note>original: "Tai"</note>, Hu, and Mo.

Therefore Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: If the rulers of today truly desire gain and wish to avoid loss, and if they desire security and wish to avoid danger, they must condemn offensive wars.

Those who try to justify <note>original: "gloss over"</note> offensive wars would say: "Those states were destroyed because they could not organize and lead their populations. I can organize and lead my people and wage war with them; who, then, would dare to be disobedient?"

Mozi said: You might be able to organize and lead your...

<summary>Mozi lists several historical states that were destroyed by their larger neighbors to prove that offensive war is a "medicine" that kills more often than it cures. He concludes that rulers seeking security must condemn such warfare, even if they believe their own military organization is superior.</summary>

### CONDEMNATION OF OFFENSIVE WAR (II) 105

...multitudes, but can you compare yourself with the ancient Helü of Wu <note>original: "Ho Lü"</note>? Helü of Wu (around 510 <term>BCE</term>) in ancient days drilled his soldiers for seven years. Wearing armor and carrying weapons, they could cover three hundred <term>li</term> <note>A "li" is a traditional Chinese unit of distance, often called a "Chinese mile"; 300 li is roughly 100 miles.</note> in a single day before setting up camp for the night. Passing through Zhulin <note>original: "Chu Lin"</note>, they emerged at the narrow Min Pass. They engaged in battle with the state of Chu <note>original: "Ch'u"</note> at Boju <note>original: "Po Chü"</note>. After subduing Chu, Helü received the rulers of Song and Lu in his court. 

By the time of Fuchai <note>original: "Fu Ch'ai"; Fuchai was Helü's son and successor, reigning from approximately 495 to 473 BCE.</note>, he attacked Qi <note>original: "Ch'i"</note> in the north, camped on the Wen River, fought at Ailing, and soundly defeated Qi, forcing them to swear an oath of alliance at Mount Tai. In the east, he attacked Yue <note>original: "Yüeh"</note>, crossing the Three Rivers and the Five Lakes, and compelled them to submit at Kuaiji <note>original: "Kuei Chi"</note>. None of the nine tribes dared to show him disrespect. 

When he returned home, however, he failed to provide for the orphans of war or support the many commoners. He relied entirely on his own might, gloated over his success, praised his own cleverness, and neglected the instruction and training of his people. He built the Gusu Terrace <note>original: "Monument of Ku Su"; Gusu was the capital of the state of Wu, located in modern-day Suzhou.</note> which remained unfinished even after seven years of work. By this time, the people of Wu felt exhausted and disheartened. Observing the internal conflict between the rulers and the subjects in Wu, Goujian <note>original: "Kou Chien"</note> of Yue gathered his forces to seek revenge. He broke into the <term>kuo</term> <note>The fortified inner city or capital.</note> from the north, seized the royal ceremonial boat, and surrounded the palace. And so, the state of Wu was destroyed.

Some time ago, the state of Jin <note>original: "Chin"</note> was governed by six ministerial families, and Zhi Bo <note>original: "Chih Po"</note> (around 455 <term>BCE</term>) was the most powerful among them. He looked at the vast size of his lands and the great number of his people, and he desired to attack the other feudal lords in order to achieve a rapid...

<summary>Mozi recounts the rise and fall of King Helü and King Fuchai of Wu to illustrate how excessive military success, coupled with the neglect of domestic welfare and the arrogance of leaders, leads to a state's ruin. He then introduces the case of Zhi Bo of Jin as another example of overreaching power.</summary>

...the spread of his courageous reputation through war and battle. So he organized his brave warriors and arranged his naval and chariot forces. He attacked the house of Zhongxing <note>original: "Chung Hsing"</note> and seized it. This convinced him that his plans were successful. Then he attacked Fan and totally defeated him. In this way, he absorbed three families into one. He did not stop even there, but surrounded Minister Xiang <note>original: "Hsiang"</note> of Zhao <note>original: "Chao"</note> at Jinyang <note>original: "Chin Yang"</note>. 

By this time, the leaders of Han and Wei came together and deliberated, saying: "An ancient proverb says: 'When the lips are removed, the teeth will become cold' <note>This famous idiom implies that neighboring states are interdependent; if one is destroyed, the others are exposed to danger.</note>. If the House of Zhao falls in the morning, we would follow it in the evening; if the House of Zhao falls in the evening, we would follow it by morning. A poet sings: 'If the fish does not act while it is still in the water, what can it do once it is already placed on land?'" 

Thereupon, the three ministers worked with united strength and a single mind, opening mountain passes and blazing trails, putting on armor and rallying their warriors. With Han and Wei attacking from the outside and Zhao from within, they battled Zhi Bo <note>original: "Chih Po"</note> and completely defeated him.

Therefore, Mozi said: An ancient proverb says: "The gentleman <note>original: "superior man"</note> should not look to water for a mirror, but to other people." In water used as a mirror, one sees only their own face; but by using people as a mirror, one can foresee good or bad fortune. Have those who currently believe that offensive wars are beneficial considered the story of Zhi Bo? It is clearly evident that such wars are not favorable, but disastrous.

# CHAPTER XIX

## CONDEMNATION OF OFFENSIVE WAR (III)

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: What does the world now praise as good? Is an act not praised because it is useful to Heaven on high, to the spirits in the middle sphere, and to the people below? Certainly, no other reason is needed for praise than for an act to be useful to Heaven on high, to the spirits in the middle, and to the people below. Even the ignorant would say an action is praiseworthy when it helps Heaven on high, the spirits in the middle, and the people below. And what the world agrees upon as good is simply the way of the sage-kings <note>The "sage-kings" refer to the legendary ideal rulers of China’s golden age, such as Yao, Shun, and Yu, who Mozi frequently cites as moral examples.</note>.

Now, the feudal lords <note>The hereditary rulers of the various Chinese states during the Warring States period.</note> in the empire still attack and assault each other. This is to praise a principle without understanding its real meaning. They resemble a blind person who uses the names of "black" and "white" just like everyone else, but cannot actually distinguish between such objects. Can this be said to be real discrimination?

Therefore, when deliberating for the empire, the ancient wise men always considered the real meaning of these principles and acted accordingly. Consequently, they were no longer uncertain in their actions. All desires, whether for those far away or near at hand, were satisfied, and Heaven, the spirits, and the people were all blessed. Such was the way of the wise men. Governing the empire, the ancient magnanimous men always worked for mutual satisfaction with the large states, brought the empire into harmony, and united all that was within the four seas <note>A traditional term for the boundaries of the known world surrounding China.</note>. Then they led the people in the empire to diligently serve God <note>In this context, the text refers to *Shangdi*, the supreme deity or "High Lord" in ancient Chinese theology.</note>, the spirits of the hills and rivers, and the...

<summary>Mozi defines "goodness" through a tripartite utility—benefiting Heaven, spirits, and the people—arguing that rulers who wage war are hypocritical "blind men" who ignore these principles.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, utility, Heaven, spirits, sage-kings, feudal lords, offensive war, four seas, Shangdi</keywords>

--- Page 122 ---
<meta>This page continues the comparison between the ancient sage-kings, who acted for the benefit of all, and the destructive rulers of Mozi’s contemporary era who pursue offensive warfare.</meta>

<page-num>108</page-num>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI <note>original: "MOTSE"</note></header>

spirits and ghosts. Many were the benefits they brought to the people and great was their success. As a result, Heaven rewarded them, the spirits enriched them, and the people praised them. The supreme honor of being called the **Son of Heaven** <note>The traditional title for the Emperor of China, signifying a divine right to rule.</note> was conferred upon them, and the great wealth of the entire world was given to them. Their names share in the permanence and greatness of Heaven and Earth and are not forgotten even to this day. Such is the way of the wise, and such is the way by which the ancient kings came to possess the world.

The rulers and lords of today are quite different. They all organize their warriors and prepare their boat and chariot forces; they make their armor strong and their weapons sharp in order to attack some innocent state. Upon entering the state, they cut down the grain fields and fell the trees and woods; they tear down the inner and outer walls of the city and fill up the ditches and ponds; they seize and kill the sacrificial animals and burn down the ancestral temple; they kill and murder the people and exterminate the elderly and the weak; they loot the treasures and valuables. The soldiers are encouraged to advance by being told: “To die in battle is the highest service you can render; to kill many is the next best; to be wounded is the lowest. But if you should drop out from your rank and attempt to sneak away, the penalty will be death without mercy.” Thus, the soldiers are driven by fear.

Now, to capture a state and destroy an army, to disturb and torture the people, and to disregard the aspirations of the sages through such chaos—is this intended to bless Heaven? In doing so, the people of Heaven are gathered together to besiege the towns belonging to Heaven. This is to murder men...

<summary>Mozi contrasts the ancient sage-kings, who were rewarded by Heaven for benefiting the people, with contemporary rulers who bring ruin and death through offensive warfare. He argues that modern military aggression destroys civilization and defies the will of Heaven.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, Sage-Kings, Son of Heaven, offensive warfare, military ethics, Heaven, ancestral temples, Warring States period</keywords>

--- Page 123 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument against offensive warfare, shifting from the physical destruction of cities to the spiritual and economic consequences. It concludes that war fails to benefit Heaven, the spirits, or the common people.</meta>

<page-num>109</page-num>
<header>CONDEMNATION OF OFFENSIVE WAR (III)</header>

...[disregarding the will] of Heaven, stripping the spirits of their altars, ruining the state, and killing the sacrificial animals. This, then, is not a blessing to Heaven above. 

Is it intended to bless the spirits? But the people of Heaven are murdered, the spirits are deprived of their sacrifices, the earlier kings <note>original: "the earlier kings" — meaning the ancestral spirits of the state</note> are neglected, the masses are tortured, and the people are scattered. Therefore, it is not a blessing to the spirits in the middle realm. 

Is it intended to bless the people? But any "blessing" given to the people by killing them off must be very meager indeed. And when we calculate the expense—which is the root of the hardships of the living <note>this is an explicit expression of the common Chinese attitude towards the government as primarily a tax-collecting agency</note>—we find that the property of countless people is exhausted. Thus, it is not a blessing to the people below either.

Now that armies are intended for mutual destruction, certain things become evident. If the general is not courageous, if the soldiers are not brave, if the weapons are not sharp, if drills are not frequent, if the force is not large, if the generals are not in harmony, if the ruler's power is not commanding <note>original: "august"</note>, if a siege is not sustained, if an assault is not swift, if the people are not strongly united, and if their determination is not firm—if these things are lacking, the other feudal lords will grow suspicious. When feudal lords become suspicious, enemies will be incited and cause anxiety, and morale will be weakened. 

On the other hand, even if every preparation is in good order and the state goes out to engage in war, the state will still lose its men and the people will neglect their livelihoods <note>original: "vocations"</note>. Have we not heard it said that when a warring state goes on an expedition, of the officers there...

<summary>Mozi argues that offensive war is a triple failure: it offends Heaven, neglects the ancestral spirits, and impoverishes the people through taxation and death. Even a "well-prepared" army causes the state to lose its manpower and disrupts the daily labor of its citizens.</summary>

...must there be several hundred [officials], of the common people there must be several thousand, and of the soldiers and prisoners there must be ten thousand, before the army can set out? It may last for several years, or, at the shortest, several months. So, the ruler <note>original: "superior"</note> will have no time to attend to government, the officials will have no time to attend to their offices, the farmers will have no time to sow or reap, and the women will have no time to weave or spin. That is, the state will lose its men and the people will neglect their occupations. 

Besides, the chariots will break and the horses will be exhausted. As for tents, army supplies, and soldiers' equipment—if one-fifth of these remain after the war, it would already be beyond expectation. Moreover, innumerable men will go missing and be lost on the way; they will become sick from the long distances, meager rations, hunger, and cold, and die in the ditches. Now, the calamity brought upon the people and the world is tremendous. Yet the rulers enjoy doing it. This means they enjoy injuring and exterminating the people; is this not a perverse <note>original: "perversity" — an irrational or wicked departure from what is right</note> act?

The most warring states in the empire today are Qi, Jin, Chu, and Yue <note>original: "Ch'i, Chin, Ch'u, and Yüeh" — the dominant powers during the Warring States period of Chinese history</note>. These four states are all successful in the world. Even if their populations were increased tenfold, they still could not consume all that their land could produce. This means they are in need of men while they have a surplus of land. Still, they strive against each other to possess more land. This is to neglect what is actually needed and to value what is already held in plenty.

The warring lords would excuse <note>original: "gloss over"</note> their conduct with arguments to refute Motse <note>Mozi, the philosopher and founder of Mohism</note>, saying: "Do you condemn attack and assault as unrighteous and not beneficial? But..."

<meta>This page continues Mozi's argument against offensive warfare by addressing a common counter-argument: if war is wrong, why did the ancient sage-kings go to war? Mozi distinguishes between "offensive attack" (which is wrong) and "divine punishment" (which is righteous), using the legendary war between Yu the Great and the Miao tribes as his primary example of the latter.</meta>

<page-num>111</page-num>

# CONDEMNATION OF OFFENSIVE WAR (III)

"In ancient times, Yu <note>Yu the Great, legendary founder of the Xia dynasty</note> made war on the Prince of Miao, Tang on Jie, and King Wu on Zhou. Yet these men are regarded as sages. What is your explanation for this?" 

Mozi said: "You have not examined the terminology of my teaching, and you do not understand its motive. What they did is not to be called 'attack' <note>original: 攻 (gōng) — meaning unprovoked aggression</note>, but 'punishment' <note>original: 誅 (zhū) — meaning a righteous execution or disciplinary war</note>."

In ancient times, the three Miao tribes were in great confusion. Heaven ordered their destruction. The sun rose at night. It rained blood for three days. Dragons emerged in the temple, and dogs cried in the marketplace. Ice formed in summer, and the earth cracked until water gushed forth. The five grains <note>The staple crops of ancient China: usually rice, two types of millet, wheat, and beans</note> appeared in mutated forms. At these signs, the people were greatly shocked. 

Kao Yang <note>Also known as Zhuanxu, a legendary monarch and an ancestor of Shun</note> then gave the command to Yu <margin>Kao Yang is an ancestor of Shun of the sixth generation. The insertion "(to Yu)" is made because this paragraph is intended to cite the circumstances under which Yu made war on the three Miao tribes. However, if this is a story about Yu, then Kao Yang cannot appear in this connection, as Yu lived much later. The details of the whole story sound mythical. More explanatory sentences seem to have been lost from the original text.</margin> in the Yuan Palace. Yu held the imperial jade order <note>A symbol of divine and political authority</note> in hand and set forth to conquer the Miao. Amidst thunder and lightning, a god with the face of a man and the body of a bird was revealed to be waiting upon Yu with a jade tablet <margin>A *kuei* (圭) is a tablet of jade or ivory held by a minister when meeting the emperor.</margin> in hand. The general of the Miao was brought down by an arrow <margin>The original text here contains two peculiar words (撛矢) whose meaning cannot be fully determined. 'Brought down by an arrow' is the translator’s best interpretation.</margin>, and the Miao army fell into great confusion. The Miao tribes became less and less significant ever after. 

Having conquered the Miao, Yu designated the names of the hills and rivers and established order between the high and the low <note>Setting social and geographical hierarchies</note>. With sacrifices he...

<summary>Mozi defends the sage-kings' use of force by redefining it as "divine punishment" rather than "offensive war," citing supernatural omens that justified Yu the Great's campaign against the Miao tribes.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, Yu the Great, Miao tribes, Sage Kings, Divine Punishment, Mandate of Heaven, Ancient China, Mythology, Omens, Just War</keywords>
