...established the four bordering countries, and neither spirits nor men revolted any longer. So there was peace in the world. This was the reason why Yu <note>Yu the Great, founder of the Xia Dynasty</note> made war on the Miao.

When it came to the reign of King Jie of the Xia, Heaven issued a severe decree. The sun and moon did not appear at their proper times. Winter and summer arrived irregularly. The five grains <note>The staple crops of ancient China: rice, two types of millet, wheat, and beans</note> withered and died. Ghosts shrieked throughout the country, and cranes cried out for more than ten nights. Heaven then commissioned Tang <note>The founder of the Shang Dynasty</note> in the Piao Palace to receive the "Great Trust" <note>The Mandate of Heaven to rule</note> that had previously been given to the Xia, because the conduct of the Xia had become deeply corrupt. Only then did Tang dare to lead his army and cross the borders of the Xia. He allowed defectors from the enemy to help destroy the Xia cities. Soon after, a deity came and told him: "The conduct of the Xia is in great confusion. Go and punish him. I will surely allow you to destroy him, as I have received my orders from Heaven." Heaven ordered Chu Yung <gloss>1</gloss> to send down fire on the northwestern corner of the city of Xia. Thus, Tang led the people against Jie and conquered the Xia. He then held an audience with the feudal lords at Po <gloss>2</gloss>. He revealed and made known the will of Heaven and spread it in all four directions, and none of the feudal lords in the empire dared to show disrespect. This was the reason why Tang punished Jie.

When it came to the regime of King Zhou of the Shang <note>The last ruler of the Shang Dynasty, notorious for his cruelty</note>, his conduct was not acceptable to Heaven. Sacrifices were not performed according to the proper seasons. Even in the night <gloss>3</gloss> . . . It rained sand for ten days at Po. The nine caldrons <note>The "Nine Tripods" or caldrons were ritual bronze vessels that symbolized the right to rule; their movement suggested the Mandate of Heaven was shifting.</note> moved on their own.

...from their place. Witches appeared in the darkness and ghosts sighed at night. Some women turned into men. Flesh fell from Heaven like rain. Thorny brambles overran the national highways. Yet the king became even more decadent. A red bird holding a jade scepter <note>original: "kuei"; a ceremonial jade tablet symbolizing authority</note> in its beak landed on Mount Ch’i <note>Mount Ch’i was the homeland of the Zhou people before they overthrew the Shang to become the ruling dynasty of China</note>, proclaiming: “Heaven decrees King Wen of Zhou to punish the Yin <note>Yin is another name for the Shang Dynasty</note> and take possession of its empire.” T’ai Tien then became a minister to King Wen. Mystical charts emerged from the Yellow River, and the *chenghuang* <note>original: "ch'eng-huang"; described as a mythical animal in the fox family with two horns on its back. If it ever existed, it is now extinct.</note> appeared on land. 

Thereupon King Wu ascended the throne. Three gods spoke to him in a dream, saying: “Now that we have drowned King Zhou of Yin in wine, you must go and attack him. We will surely allow you to destroy him.” So, King Wu set out and attacked King Zhou, replacing the Shang Dynasty with the Zhou. Heaven gave King Wu the Yellow Bird Pennant. Having conquered the Yin, he maintained the order established by Tang <note>The founder of the previous dynasty</note> and assigned the duty of ancestral worship for the Zhou ancestors to the various feudal lords. Relationships with the tribes on the four borders were established, and no one in the world dared to show disrespect. This was the reason why King Wu punished King Zhou.

When speaking about the work of these three sages <note>Yu the Great, Tang, and King Wu</note>, their actions should not be called "attacks," but "punishments."

...beneficial? Yet, in ancient times, Hsiung Li was assigned to the state of Chu (around 1100 BC) in the Mountains of Sui. Yi Kuei started at Yu Ju and became the lord of Yue. Uncle Tang and Lu Shang <note>Lü Shang, also known as Jiang Ziya, was the strategist who helped the Zhou overthrow the Shang and was granted the state of Qi</note> were first appointed to rule over the states of Jin (1107 BC) and Qi (around 1120 BC) respectively. All of these rulers started with only a few hundred square *li* <note>original: "li"; a Chinese unit of distance, roughly one-third of a mile or half a kilometer</note> of land. Because they captured other states, each of them now controls a quarter of the empire. How do you explain this?”

Motse said: You have not examined the terminology of my teachings, and you do not understand their underlying principle. In ancient times, the emperor commissioned more than ten thousand feudal lords. Because states were absorbed by one another, all of those ten thousand states have disappeared, leaving only these four remaining. This is like a physician who treats more than ten thousand patients but cures only four. No one would call such a person a good physician.

The warring lords would then try to justify their conduct with arguments, saying: “I do not wage war because I am dissatisfied with my gold and jade, my children, or my land. I want to establish my reputation as a righteous ruler in the world and attract the other feudal lords to me through my virtue.”

Motse said: If there were someone who could establish a reputation for justice in the world and attract the feudal lords to him through his virtue, he would not have to wait long for the whole world to submit to him. For the world has been in turmoil for a long time...

<summary>Motse responds to the argument that conquest is justified by the success of large states like Chu and Qi, comparing a ruler who destroys thousands of states to save four to a doctor who loses thousands of patients to save a few. He argues that true world leadership is gained through virtue and justice, not military expansion.</summary>

<page-num>115</page-num>

# CONDEMNATION OF OFFENSIVE WAR (III)

...[virtue, then the world would soon submit. But now the world is exhausted with offensive campaigns] and war, and it is as exhausting as a boy playing at being a horse. <note>original: "weary like the boy at playing horse"; This refers to a child’s game that causes great fatigue for no practical reward or purpose.</note> If only there were a ruler who would treat the other feudal lords with mutual good faith and seek their benefit first! When a large state acts unjustly, he would join in the victim's sorrow; when a large state attacks a smaller one, he would join in the rescue. When the outer and inner walls of a small state’s city are in ruins, he would demand their repair; when their supplies of cloth and grain are exhausted, he would supply them; when their money and silk <note>In ancient China, silk was frequently used as a form of currency and a measurement of wealth.</note> are insufficient, he would share his own. To befriend a large state in this way will make the large state pleased; to befriend a small state in this way will make the small state pleased. 

With others tired out by conflict while he remains at ease, his own military resources <note>original: "armour and weapon"; here used figuratively to represent all resources for both defense and offense.</note> would be stronger. When we help the people in their need with kindness and mercy, the people will be drawn to us. When good government is substituted for aggressive war, the country will benefit many times over. When the expenses of the army are limited and the harms caused by the feudal lords are removed, then we shall enjoy prosperity. 

Let the control of the army be judicious and the cause be righteous. Be lenient to the people and place confidence in the army—with this approach to meet the forces of the feudal lords, there can be no equal in the world. And the benefits to the world will be innumerable. This is what will truly benefit the world, yet the rulers do not understand how to make use of it. This can be described as ignorance of the most important principle in the world.

***

<summary>Motse argues that a truly great ruler gains power not through conquest, but by acting as a "protector" who provides aid and diplomatic support to smaller states. He concludes that internal good government and righteous leadership are more effective than the exhausting and futile cycle of offensive warfare.</summary>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

Therefore Motse <note>original: "Motse"; also known as Mozi (c. 470 – c. 391 BC), the philosopher who founded Mohism and advocated for universal love and pacifism.</note> said: Now, if the rulers and the leaders <note>original: "gentlemen"; here referring to the *junzi*, the educated and morally upright social class.</note> of the world sincerely desire to secure benefits and avert disasters for the world—if they desire to act with righteousness and be virtuous men, and if they desire to follow the path of the sage-kings <note>The legendary, idealized rulers of China’s high antiquity who were believed to rule by perfect virtue.</note> on one hand while blessing the people on the other—then the doctrine of the **Condemnation of Offensive War** must not be ignored.

<summary>Motse concludes his argument by asserting that any ruler who truly wishes to follow the example of the ancient sage-kings and benefit humanity must adopt the principle of opposing aggressive warfare.</summary>

<meta>This page begins Book VI, shifting the focus from the condemnation of aggressive war to the principle of "Economy of Expenditures." Motse argues that a state's wealth is doubled not by conquering new territory, but by eliminating waste and focusing on utility.</meta>

# BOOK VI

## CHAPTER XX

### ECONOMY OF EXPENDITURES (I)

When a sage <note>original: "sage"; a wise and virtuous ruler who follows the Way.</note> rules a state, the benefits of that state will be doubled. When he governs the empire, the benefits of the empire will also be doubled. This increase is not achieved by seizing land from outside his borders. Instead, it is accomplished by cutting out useless expenditures. Whether issuing an order, starting a project, employing the people, or spending wealth, the sage never does anything without a practical purpose. Therefore, wealth is not wasted and the people’s resources are not exhausted; as a result, many blessings are secured.

In making clothing, what is the purpose? It is to keep out the cold in winter and the heat in summer. The benefit of clothing is measured by the amount of warmth it adds in winter and coolness in summer; anything that is purely decorative and does not contribute to these functions is to be set aside.

In building palaces and houses, what is the purpose? It is to keep out the wind and the cold in winter, the heat and rain in summer, and to protect against thieves. Whatever is merely ornamental and contributes nothing to these goals should be avoided.

In forging armor, shields, and the five weapons <note>original: "five weapons"; in ancient China, these typically included the spear, the lance, the halberd, the sword, and the bow. Commentators disagree on the exact list.</note>, what is the purpose? It is to prepare against invasions, revolts, bandits, and thieves. When these occur, with...

<summary>Motse argues that a wise ruler increases the wealth of his state through frugality and utility rather than conquest. He asserts that clothing, housing, and weaponry should be designed for practical use rather than decoration.</summary>

<keywords>Motse, Mozi, Economy of Expenditures, frugality, sage, statecraft, utility, ancient Chinese philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 132 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion on the principle of "Economy of Expenditures." Having addressed clothing and housing on the previous page, Motse now applies the same logic of utility to military equipment, transportation, and national population growth.</meta>

...armor, shields, and the five weapons <note>original: "five weapons"; in ancient China, these typically included the spear, the lance, the halberd, the sword, and the bow.</note>, one will succeed; without them, one cannot succeed. Therefore, the sages forged armor, shields, and the five weapons. Their quality is measured by how light, sharp, and durable they are. Whatever is merely decorative and contributes nothing to these qualities should be abandoned.

When building boats and vehicles, what is the purpose? A vehicle is for traveling on land, and a boat is for sailing rivers and valleys to transport wealth from the four quarters <note>original: "four quarters"; a traditional term referring to the four cardinal directions or the entire world.</note>. The value of boats and vehicles is measured by the ease and convenience they provide; whatever is merely decorative and contributes nothing to these should be abandoned.

In making all these things, nothing is done without a practical use in mind. Therefore, wealth is not wasted, the people’s resources are not exhausted, and many blessings are gained.

Furthermore, the sages gave up hobbies such as collecting pearls, jade, exotic birds, and animals like dogs and horses, in order to increase the supply of clothing, houses, armor, shields, the five weapons, boats, and vehicles. As a result, these essentials were multiplied several times over. Increasing these things is not difficult. What, then, is difficult to increase? It is the population that is difficult to increase.

In ancient times, the sage-kings <note>original: "sage-kings"; legendary, virtuous rulers of China's past who served as Motse’s moral models.</note> said: "No man of twenty should dare to be without a family; no girl of fifteen should dare to be without a master <note>original: "master"; in this context, this refers to a husband or being settled in a household.</note>." Such were the laws of the sage-kings. Now that the sage-kings have passed away, the people have become lax. Those who choose to marry early sometimes marry at twenty. Those who like...

<summary>Motse explains that tools, weapons, and vehicles should be designed solely for utility rather than decoration to preserve national wealth. He concludes that the most difficult but necessary resource for a ruler to increase is the population, which was formerly managed through strict marriage laws.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument that population growth is hindered by social and political mismanagement. Motse is contrasting the practices of his contemporary rulers with the laws of the ancient sage-kings regarding marriage age and the impact of war on the citizenry.</meta>

# ECONOMY OF EXPENDITURES (I)

...those who start a family late sometimes do not marry until the age of forty. When these late marriages are averaged with the early ones, the resulting average age is still ten years later than the legal requirements set by the sage-kings <note>original: "sage-kings"; legendary, virtuous rulers of China's past who served as Motse’s moral models.</note>. If we assume that children are born on average once every three years, then two or three children would have already been born by the time men today finally marry. This point is not made merely to urge men to establish families early to increase the population, but also... <note>The original text is incomplete at this point.</note>

Those who govern the empire today decrease the population in more ways than one: by overworking the people until they are exhausted, and by imposing heavy taxes. As a result, people fall into poverty, and countless individuals die from hunger and cold. Furthermore, the rulers wage war and attack neighboring states. These conflicts may last an entire year or, at the shortest, several months. Consequently, husbands and wives are separated for long periods. Is this not a way to decrease the population? Living in danger and eating and drinking irregularly, many become sick and die. Through ambushes <note>original: "Hiding in ambush"; the original text is unclear here, and this translation is a scholarly conjecture.</note>, arson, sieges, and battles in open fields, countless men perish. 

Are not the ways of decreasing the population becoming more numerous under the government of today's rulers? These conditions did not exist when the sage-kings administered the government. Under a government led by the wise, many ways to increase the population will emerge.

Therefore, Motse said: Eliminating useless expenditures is the way of the sage-kings and a great blessing to the world.

<summary>Motse argues that late marriage, heavy taxation, and constant warfare are the primary causes of population decline, contrasting these failures with the efficient and life-preserving governance of the ancient sage-kings.</summary>

# CHAPTER XXI
## ECONOMY OF EXPENDITURES (II)

Motse said: The ancient, illustrious kings and sages ruled over the empire and led the feudal lords because they loved the people loyally and blessed them with many benefits. Loyalty inspires confidence. When these blessings were provided, the people did not grow weary during their entire lives and did not feel tired of their rulers until their deaths. That the ancient, illustrious kings and sages could rule the empire and lead the feudal lords is due precisely to this approach.

Furthermore, the ancient sage-kings <note>original: "sage-kings"; legendary, virtuous rulers of China's past who served as Motse’s moral models.</note> authorized the code of laws regarding economy <note>In this context, "economy" refers to thrift and the careful management of resources.</note>, saying: "All you artisans and workers—carpenters and tanners, potters and smiths—do what you can do. Stop when the needs of the people are satisfied." The sage-kings would not undertake anything that caused extra expense but added no benefit to the people.

The ancient sage-kings also authorized the code of laws regarding food and drink, saying: "Stop when hunger is satisfied, breathing becomes strong, limbs are strengthened, and ears and eyes become sharp. There is no need to combine the five tastes <note>The "five tastes" are the traditional Chinese culinary categories: bitter, spicy (hot), sour, sweet, and salty.</note> to an extreme degree or to perfectly harmonize different sweet aromas. No effort should be made to obtain rare delicacies from far-off countries." 

How do we know these were the laws? In ancient times, when Yao <note>A legendary Chinese emperor celebrated for his benevolence and wisdom.</note> was governing the empire, he consolidated Chiao Tse <note>Chiao-Tse is the ancient name for the region now known as West Indo-China.</note> in the south and reached Yu Tu in the north. He expanded the empire from where the sun rises to...

<summary>This page outlines Motse's philosophy on "Economy of Expenditures," citing the ancient sage-kings as models for prioritizing the people's basic needs over luxury and excessive variety in food and crafts.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, sage-kings, Yao, economy, thrift, five tastes, Chiao Tse, ancient laws, governance</keywords>

--- Page 135 ---
<meta>This page continues the description of the ancient sage-kings' moderate lifestyles, contrasting their simple habits with the excessive luxuries of later rulers. It specifically outlines their practical approach to clothing, weapons, and transportation.</meta>

<page-num>121</page-num>
<header>ECONOMY OF EXPENDITURES (II)</header>

His influence reached from the east to where the sun sets in the west, and no one refused to submit or failed to show him respect. Yet, even when he was served with food he enjoyed, he did not eat a double portion of grain <note>original: "double cereal"; likely referring to the luxury of eating two types of grain at once or multiple courses of starch.</note> or both soup and meat together. He ate out of an earthenware *liu* <note>original: "liu 瑠"; an ancient type of ceramic bowl.</note> and drank out of an earthenware *hsing* <note>original: "hsing 刑"; likely referring to a simple clay pitcher or jar.</note>, and he used a simple spoon for his wine. With the elaborate rituals of bowing, stretching, and the formalities of courtly decorum, this sage-king had nothing to do.

The ancient sage-kings established the laws regarding clothing, saying: "Be satisfied with clothes made of blue or grey silk in winter, which are light and warm, and with clothes made of flax-linen in summer, which are light and cool." The sage-kings would not allow any extra spending that did not provide actual benefits to the people.

Because predatory and dangerous animals were destroying men and harming the people, the ancient sage-kings taught the people how to use weapons, saying: "Carry a two-edged sword that pierces when it is thrust and severs when it cuts. It must be made so that it does not break when struck with the flat side; this is the true usefulness of a sword. An armor's purpose is to be light and strong, and to allow for ease of movement."

A vehicle is used to carry heavy loads and to travel great distances. It should be safe to ride and easy to pull—safe so that it does not injure the passenger, and easy to pull so that the destination is reached quickly. This is the true usefulness of a vehicle.

Because wide rivers and broad valleys were once impossible to cross, the sage-kings ordered boats and oars to be built. These were made solely for the purpose of crossing the water. Even when a High Duke <note>original: "High Duke"; a high-ranking member of the nobility or a senior advisor to the emperor.</note> or a feudal lord arrived, the boat and the oars were not replaced with more ornate versions, and the ferryman did not wear any special decorations. Such is the true usefulness of a boat.

<summary>This page details the practical laws of the ancient sage-kings regarding food, clothing, weaponry, and transportation, emphasizing that the value of an object lies in its utility rather than its decoration.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, sage-kings, frugality, utility, clothing, weapons, transportation, ancient China, simplicity</keywords>

--- Page 136 ---
<meta>This page continues the discussion of "Economy of Expenditures," specifically focusing on the ancient sage-kings' practical regulations for funerals and housing. It follows descriptions of their modest approach to food, clothing, and weaponry.</meta>

<page-num>122</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

The ancient sage-kings established laws to limit funeral expenses, saying: "There should only be three layers of burial clothes <note>original: "shrouds"</note>—just enough to hold the decaying body. The coffin shall be three inches thick, which is sufficient to hold the bones. The grave <note>original: "pit"</note> should not be dug deep enough to reach groundwater, but it should be deep enough so that the odors of decomposition <note>original: "gases"</note> do not escape. Once the dead are buried, the living shall not mourn for an excessively long time."

In the earliest days of the human race, before there were palaces or houses, people lived in caves dug into the sides of hills and mounds. The sage-kings were concerned about this, realizing that while caves might protect against the wind and cold in winter, they would be damp on the floor and stifling <note>original: "steaming"</note> overhead during the summer, which could damage the people's health. Therefore, palaces and houses were built and found to be much more useful. 

What, then, is the proper standard for building palaces and houses? Motse <note>also known as Mozi, the philosopher and founder of Mohism</note> said: A building should be designed simply so that the walls block the wind and the cold, and the roof keeps out the snow, frost, rain, and dew. Internally, it must be clean enough for performing religious sacrifices, and the partitions within the palace must be high enough to separate the men's quarters from the women's. The sage-kings would not undertake any project that increased costs without providing a real benefit to the people.

<summary>This page outlines the pragmatic regulations set by ancient Chinese sage-kings regarding burial practices and architecture. It emphasizes their philosophy of "utility over luxury," arguing that both funerals and houses should serve basic human and ritual needs without wasteful excess.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, Mozi, Sage-kings, funeral laws, ancient housing, frugality, ritual purity, architectural standards</keywords>

# CHAPTER XXV

## SIMPLICITY IN FUNERAL (III)

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: The benevolent <note>original: "magnanimous"</note> ruler takes care of the world in the same way that a devoted <note>original: "filial"</note> son takes care of his parents. But how does a devoted son take care of his parents? If his parents are poor, he seeks to enrich them; if the family is small, he seeks to increase the number of descendants; if the family members are in chaos, he seeks to bring them to order. Of course, in doing this, he might find his energy lacking, his resources limited, or his knowledge insufficient. However, he would not dare leave any energy, learning, or resources unused in the service of his parents. These are the three goals <note>original: "interests"</note> of a devoted son in caring for his parents.

The same is true for a benevolent ruler in caring for the world: if the world is poor, he seeks to enrich it; if the population is small, he seeks to increase it; if the people are in chaos, he seeks to bring them to order. Of course, in doing these things, he might find his energy lacking, his resources limited, or his knowledge insufficient, but he would not dare leave any energy, learning, or resources unused in the service of the world. These are the three goals of a benevolent ruler in caring for the world.

After the sage-kings of the Three Dynasties <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties (c. 2070–256 BCE), regarded by Mozi as a golden age of wisdom</note> had passed away and the world had forgotten their principles, some of the scholars <note>original: "gentlemen"</note> of later generations began to regard elaborate funerals and long periods of mourning as noble, righteous, and the duty of a devoted son. Others, however, argued that such practices were neither noble nor righteous, and were not the duty of the 

<summary>Mozi argues that a ruler’s duty to his people mirrors a son’s duty to his parents: to provide wealth, growth, and order. He notes that after the era of the ancient sage-kings, a debate emerged regarding whether elaborate funerals are truly righteous.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, benevolence, filial piety, Three Dynasties, social order, funeral rites, mourning, population growth</keywords>

--- Page 138 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding funeral rites, specifically debating whether elaborate funerals and long mourning periods fulfill the three basic needs of a state: wealth, population growth, and social order.</meta>

<page-num>124</page-num>

->THE WORKS OF MOTSE<-

...devoted <note>original: "filial"</note> son. These two groups criticize each other's words and act in opposing ways, yet both claim to follow the path of the sage-kings: Yao, Shun, Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu <note>Traditional semi-mythical rulers of China's Golden Age, cited by all schools of thought as moral exemplars</note>. Because they disagree in what they say and do, people doubt the claims of both groups. Since the assertions of both are in doubt, it would be best to look at the governance of the nation and its people and see how elaborate funerals and long periods of mourning affect the three primary goals <note>original: "interests"</note>. 

In my view, if adopting these doctrines and practicing these principles could actually enrich the poor, increase the population <note>original: "the few"</note>, remove danger, and bring order to chaos <note>original: "regulate disorder"</note>, then they would indeed be benevolent <note>original: "magnanimous"</note>, righteous, and the duty of a devoted son. Those who give advice would have no choice but to encourage them. A benevolent ruler would seek to have these practices flourish throughout the world and establish them so that the people would praise them and never ignore them throughout their lives. 

On the other hand, if adopting these doctrines and practicing these principles cannot actually enrich the poor, increase the population, remove danger, and bring order to chaos, then they are not benevolent, righteous, or the duty of a devoted son. Those who give advice must discourage them. A benevolent ruler will seek to have them abolished throughout the world and abandoned, so that the people will condemn them and never practice them throughout their lives. From ancient times to the present day, it has never happened that benefits were gained, calamities avoided for the world, or chaos among the people was set right by elaborate funerals and long periods of mourning.

&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;&nbsp;How do we know this?

### SIMPLICITY IN FUNERAL (III)

Even today, the leaders of the world are still uncertain whether elaborate funerals and long periods of mourning are right and beneficial.

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: I have examined the arguments of those who advocate for elaborate funerals and long mourning periods. If their views were taken seriously in this country, it would mean: when a ruler <note>original: "lord"</note> dies, there would be multiple layers of inner and outer coffins. He would be buried deep in the earth. There would be numerous burial shrouds. The embroidery would be intricate. The burial mound would be massive. Consequently, the death of a commoner would exhaust the entire wealth of a family. The death of a regional ruler <note>original: "feudal lord"</note> would empty the state treasury before his body was even surrounded with gold, jade, and pearls, and the grave filled with chariots <note>original: "carts"</note>, horses, and bundles of silk. Furthermore, there would be many canopies and hangings, ritual bronze vessels <note>original: "tings"; these are three-legged vessels used in sacrifices</note>, drums, tables, pots, ice containers, spears, swords, feather banners, and animal hides—all to be carried along in the procession and buried. Only then would the requirements be considered fulfilled. Regarding those who were to be killed to accompany their lord in death <note>This refers to the ancient practice of human sacrifice, or *xun*, where servants and concubines were buried with their masters</note>: for an emperor or a regional ruler, there would be anywhere from several dozen to several hundred; for a minister or secretary, there would be from several to several dozen.

What are the rules the mourner must follow? He must weep without restraint and make sounds as if he is choking. He wears sackcloth on his chest and a hat made of rough flax on his head. He is not permitted to wipe away his tears or his runny nose <note>original: "snivel"</note>. The mourner is required to live in a temporary mourning hut, sleep on a coarse mat of straw, <note>The description of these rites is based on the classic Chinese Books of Rites: the *Rites of Zhou* (Chou Li), the *Etiquette and Ceremonial* (Yi Li), and the *Book of Rites* (Li Chi).</note>

<summary>Mozi critiques the extreme extravagance of traditional funerals, detailing the massive economic drain of burial goods and the horrific practice of human sacrifice. He also describes the severe physical and emotional demands placed on mourners by traditional etiquette.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, burial customs, funeral rites, human sacrifice, mourning rituals, ancient Chinese law, economic waste, Rites of Zhou, Li Chi</keywords>

...and lay his head on a clump of dirt. Then, he is required to fast so as to appear hungry, and to wear thin clothing so as to appear cold. His face and eyes must look sunken, as if he is living in fear, and his complexion must appear dark and haggard. His hearing and vision become dull, and his hands and feet become weak and useless. Furthermore, if the mourner is a high official, he must be assisted just to stand up and must lean on a cane to walk. All of this is expected to last for three years.

If such a doctrine is adopted and such a principle practiced, then rulers cannot come to court early <insert>and stay late; officials cannot attend to the</insert> five offices and six posts <note>original: "five offices and six posts"; these represent the various departments of government, though the specific titles varied by dynasty</note> to encourage farming and forestry or to fill the granaries. Farmers cannot work from dawn until dusk to cultivate the land and plant trees; craftspeople cannot build boats and carriages or manufacture vessels and tools; and women cannot rise early and stay up late to weave and spin. 

Consequently, elaborate funerals result in vast amounts of wealth being buried in the ground, and extended mourning results in long periods of productive work being abandoned. Wealth that has already been produced is carried away into the grave, and the having of children is delayed. To try to seek prosperity in this manner is like trying to seek a harvest while banning farming. This is certainly not the way to find wealth.

Since the practice of elaborate funerals and extended mourning has failed to enrich the nation, perhaps it can at least increase the population? Again, it is powerless to do so. For if elaborate funerals and extended mourning are adopted as...

<header>SIMPLICITY IN FUNERALS (III)</header>

If this were the rule, then upon the death of the emperor there would be three years of mourning. Upon the death of a parent, there would be three years of mourning; upon the death of a wife or the eldest son, there would also be three years of mourning. There would be three years of mourning for all five of these relationships. In addition, there would be one year of mourning for uncles, brothers, and younger sons; five months for near relatives; and several months for aunts, sisters, nephews, and maternal uncles. 

Furthermore, there are established rules that require people to waste away <note>original: "emaciate"</note> their health: the face and eyes must look sunken as if in fear, and the complexion must appear dark. Hearing and sight become dull, and hands and feet become weak and useless. Also, if the mourner is a high official, he must be supported just to stand up and must lean on a cane to walk. 

This lifestyle is expected to last for three years if such a doctrine is adopted and such a principle is practiced. Being so hungry and weak, the people cannot withstand the cold of winter or the heat of summer. Consequently, countless numbers will fall ill and die. Furthermore, sexual relations between husband and wife are forbidden during this time. Trying to increase the population in this way is like seeking a long life by throwing oneself upon a sword. This is not the way to achieve a large population.

Now, since this practice has failed to increase the population, perhaps it can at least regulate social order <note>original: "jurisdiction"</note>? Again, it is powerless. By adopting elaborate funerals and extended mourning as a principle of government, the state will become poor, the people will become few, and the legal system <note>original: "jurisdiction"</note> will fall into disorder. When such a doctrine is adopted and practiced, superiors cannot focus on administration and subordinates cannot attend to their work. When leaders are unable to give their attention to administration, the result will be total disorder.

When subordinates are unable to attend to their work, the supply of food and clothing will become insufficient. An unfilial son will turn to his father for help, and when he does not receive it, he will reject <note>original: "spurn"</note> his father. Likewise, a disloyal minister will turn to his lord for help, and when he does not receive it, he will mock his lord. Furthermore, wicked and immoral people will commit evil and immorality beyond control when they lack clothing to wear abroad and food to eat at home.

Consequently, bandits and thieves will be numerous, while law-abiding people will be few. To seek order by increasing the number of bandits and thieves while decreasing the number of law-abiding people is like demanding that a person not show you his back after forcing him to turn around three times. This is not the way to achieve social order.

However, there are some small states they do not attack. ...subordinates are in harmony. Therefore, the large states would not want to attack them. By adopting elaborate funerals and extended mourning as a principle of government, the state becomes poor, the population decreases, and the administration becomes chaotic. When the state is poor, the ritual offerings of cakes and wine will be impure. When the people are few, the number of worshippers for God and the spirits will be reduced. And when the administration is in disorder, the sacrifices will not be performed at the proper seasons. Furthermore, the worship of God and the spirits is now even being neglected <note>original: "prohibited"</note>. When a government is run in this manner, God and the spirits will look down from on high and deliberate, saying: “Is it better for these people to exist or not to exist? It really makes no difference whether they exist or not.” Therefore, God and the spirits will send judgment upon them, visiting them with calamities, punishing them, and abandoning them. Is this not a fitting outcome?

Therefore, the ancient sage-kings <note>original: "sage-kings"; Here Motse refers mainly to Yu, who of all the sage-kings advocated for the simplest ceremonies.</note> authorized the code of laws regarding the burial of the dead as follows: The coffin shall be three inches thick, which is sufficient to hold the body. As for shrouds, there shall be three pieces of clothing, enough to cover the corpse. It shall not be buried so deep as to reach groundwater, nor so shallow as to allow the odor to rise to the surface. A mound three feet in size shall be large enough. There shall be no extended mourning after burial; instead, there should be a speedy return to work and the pursuit of whatever one can do to achieve mutual benefit. Such are the laws of the sage-kings.

Those who uphold elaborate funerals and extended mourning say:

> "Although elaborate funerals and extended mourning cannot enrich the poor, increase a dwindling population, remove danger, or regulate disorder, they were still a principle of the sage-kings."

Motse replied: Not at all. In ancient times, Yao went north to instruct the eight tribes of Ti <note>The Ti, Jung, and Yi were various aboriginal tribes surrounding China at that time.</note>. He died on the way and was buried on the southern side <note>original: "shade"; meaning the sunny or southern side of the mountain.</note> of Mount Ch'iung. For shrouds, there were only three pieces of clothing. The coffin was made of soft wood and sealed with flax linen. Weeping started only after the burial. There was no mound; the pit was simply filled up. After burial, oxen and horses walked over the site. 

Shun went west to instruct the seven tribes of Jung. He died on the way and was buried in the marketplace of Nan Chi. For shrouds, there were three pieces of clothing. The coffin was made of soft wood and sealed with flax linen. After burial, the people in the marketplace walked over the grave. 

Yü went east to instruct the nine tribes of Yi. He died on the way and was buried at Mount Kuei Chi.

<summary>Motse describes the modest burial laws of the ancient sage-kings Yao, Shun, and Yü to prove that elaborate modern funeral customs are not based on ancient tradition.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the argument against elaborate funerals by contrasting the simplicity of the ancient sage-kings—Yao, Shun, and Yu—with the excessive and wasteful practices of the rulers of Mozi's own time.</meta>

There were only three pieces of clothing for the deceased. The coffin was made of paulownia wood <note>original: "t’ung wood" (桐); a fast-growing tree often used in China for coffins and musical instruments.</note> and sealed with flax linen. It was strong enough not to be crushed when bound with cords, and it did not cave in when under the pressure of the earth. The burial pit was not so deep that it hit groundwater, yet not so shallow that it allowed the odor of decay to rise to the surface. Once the coffin was buried, the leftover earth was gathered over it to create a mound that was exactly three feet high and no more. Judging by the examples of these three sage-kings, elaborate funerals and long periods of mourning were clearly not their way. These three sage-kings held the rank of **Sons of Heaven** <term>Sons of Heaven: A title for the supreme emperor of China, signifying they ruled by divine mandate.</term> and possessed the wealth of the entire empire; yet they chose to be buried in this simple manner. Was this because they lacked the means to do otherwise? Of course not.

The way modern rulers are buried is completely different. They use both inner and outer coffins, wrapped in three layers of animal hides and embroidered silks. After vast amounts of precious stones and jade are collected for the tomb, they also include spears, swords, bronze vessels <term>original: "tings" (鼎); ancient three-legged ritual cauldrons used to symbolize status and power.</term>, pots, and ice containers. They add thousands of decorated reins and yokes, along with carriages, horses, and even dancing girls to be buried with them. Furthermore, they insist on building a massive tunnel to reach the burial chamber, which is located under a mound as high as a natural hill. This practice interferes with the people’s daily work and wastes the nation's wealth to a staggering degree. How can this possibly be justified?

Therefore, Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: I have already stated that if adopting this doctrine and practicing the principle of elaborate funerals and extended mourning could actually enrich the poor, increase a small population, remove danger, and bring order to social chaos, then such things would indeed be considered generous, righteous, and the duty of a devoted son.

Those who give counsel could not help but encourage such practices. Trying to prevent large states from attacking small ones through these methods is impossible. Furthermore, seeking blessings from God and the spirits through these practices only brings calamity.

When we look back and examine the ways of **Yao, Shun, Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu** <note>These are the legendary and historical "Sage-Kings" of Chinese antiquity, cited by Mozi as the ultimate models of virtuous leadership.</note>, we find that their behavior was the exact opposite of these elaborate customs. Therefore, judging from these examples, elaborate funerals and extended mourning were not the way of the sage-kings.

In the state of **Yüeh** <note>Yüeh (Yue) was an ancient state in eastern China, often associated with "barbarian" customs by the central Chinese states.</note> there was the tribe of K’ai Shu. Among them, it was the custom to dismember and devour the first-born son after birth, and this was said to be **propitious** <term>propitious: Favorable or likely to bring good luck.</term> for his younger brothers. When a father died, the mother was carried away and abandoned; the reason given was that one should not live with the "wife of a ghost." The officials established this as a government regulation, and the people accepted it as a normal part of life. They practiced it continually and followed it without questioning. Was it then the good and the right way? No, this is really because habit makes things convenient and custom carries approval. 

South of the state of **Ch’u** there was a cannibal tribe. ¹ When parents died, the flesh was scraped off the bodies and thrown away, while the bones were buried. By following this custom, one was considered a **filial son** <term>filial son: A child who demonstrates the essential virtue of "Xiao," or deep respect, obedience, and care for one's parents and ancestors.</term>. 

West of the state of **Ch’in** there was the tribe of Yi Ch’ü. When their parents died, they were burned on a bonfire. It was said that through the smoke, they ascended to the "golden clouds." In this way, one became a filial son. The officials turned this into government regulations, and the people regarded it as normal. They practiced it continually and followed it without questioning. Is it then the good and the right way? No, this is really because habit makes things convenient and custom carries approval.

Now, the practices of these three tribes are too heartless, and those of the gentlemen of the **Middle Kingdom** <note>original: "Middle Kingdom"; the literal translation of "Zhongguo," referring to the central Chinese states that considered themselves the height of civilization.</note> are too elaborate. Since one extreme is too heartless and the other is too elaborate, there should be balanced rules for funerals and burials. Even regarding clothing and food, which are the necessities...

<summary>Mozi argues that what people consider "proper" funeral customs—whether cannibalism, abandonment, or elaborate burial—is usually determined by local habit rather than true righteousness. <header>THE WORKS OF MOZI</header>

...of life, there are rules. How, then, can there be no rules regarding funerals and burials, which are the final necessities of death? Outlining his rules for funerals and burials, **Mozi** <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: The coffin shall be three inches thick—just enough to hold the decaying bones. There shall be three shrouds—just enough to cover the decaying flesh. The grave shall be dug neither so deep as to reach groundwater, nor so shallow as to allow the smell of decay to rise. The burial mound shall be just high enough to be identified by the mourners. 

One may weep on the way to and from the burial, but upon returning home, people should focus on earning their livelihood. Sacrifices to ancestors must not be neglected, as they express a person’s **filial piety** <term>filial piety: (Xiao) The fundamental Confucian and Mohist virtue of respect, obedience, and care for one's parents and ancestors.</term> toward their parents. In this way, Mozi’s rules neglect the needs of neither the dead nor the living.

Therefore, Mozi said: If the gentlemen of the world truly wish to practice righteousness and benevolence <note>original: "magnanimity"</note>, and seek to be superior men—desiring to follow the path of the **sage-kings** <term>sage-kings: The idealized, virtuous rulers of China's golden age (such as Yao, Shun, and Yu) whom ancient philosophers cited as perfect moral examples.</term> on one hand and to secure blessings for the people on the other—they cannot afford to ignore the principle of **Simplicity in Funerals** in their governance.

<summary>Mozi outlines specific, modest requirements for burials—including coffin thickness and mourning periods—arguing that true righteousness balances respect for the dead with the economic needs of the living.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Simplicity in Funerals, burial customs, filial piety, sage-kings, coffin specifications, mourning rituals</keywords>

--- Page 149 ---
<meta>This page marks the beginning of Book VII, moving from Mozi's practical instructions on funerals to his theological and political arguments regarding the "Will of Heaven."</meta>

# BOOK VII

## CHAPTER XXVI

### THE WILL OF HEAVEN (PART I)

**Mozi** <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: The **gentlemen of the world** <term>gentlemen of the world: A term Mozi often uses to criticize the conventional elite or the "learned" men of his time who lack true moral insight.</term> only understand small matters but not things of great importance. How do we know this? We know it from how a person behaves in their family. If someone should offend the head of the family <note>original: "patriarch"</note>, there are still the houses of the neighbors where they can seek shelter. Yet, parents, brothers, and friends all keep reminding them to be obedient and careful. For how can one offend the head of the family and still live there? 

This is not only true regarding behavior in the family, but also in the state. If someone should offend the ruler <note>original: "lord"</note> of the state, there are still neighboring states where they may flee. Yet, parents, brothers, and friends all keep reminding them to be obedient and careful. For how can one offend the ruler of the state and still live in it? 

In these cases, there are still shelters to flee to, yet people still give such constant advice. Should there not be even more warnings in a case where there is nowhere to flee? As the saying goes: "If you sin in broad daylight, where can you flee?" Truly, there is nowhere to flee. For **Heaven** <term>Heaven: (Tian) In Mohism, Heaven is not just a place or a physical sky, but a supreme, conscious moral power that rewards righteousness and punishes evil.</term> clearly sees everything, even if it occurs in the woods, valleys, or lonely caves where no man is present. But, contrary to our expectations, the gentlemen of the world do not remind each other to be careful regarding Heaven. This is how we know the gentlemen of the world understand only small matters and not things of great importance.

<summary>Mozi argues that people are foolishly more afraid of offending family heads or political rulers than they are of offending Heaven, even though Heaven's sight is inescapable.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Will of Heaven, social conduct, morality, divine omniscience, patriarch, ruler, Heaven (Tian)</keywords>

--- Page 150 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for "The Will of Heaven," establishing that Heaven's will is the ultimate moral standard and that righteousness is the source of social order and prosperity.</meta>

Now, what does Heaven desire and what does it hate <note>original: "abominate"</note>? Heaven desires righteousness and hates unrighteousness. Therefore, if I lead the people of the world to practice righteousness, I will be doing what Heaven desires. When I do what Heaven desires, Heaven will also do what I desire. 

Now, what do I desire and what do I hate? I desire blessings and rewards <note>original: "emoluments"</note>, and I hate calamities and misfortunes. When I do not do what Heaven desires, Heaven will not do what I desire either. In that case, I would be leading the people into calamities and misfortunes. 

But how do we know Heaven desires righteousness and hates unrighteousness? Because with righteousness, the world lives, and without it, the world dies; with it, the world becomes wealthy, and without it, the world becomes poor; with it, the world becomes orderly, and without it, the world becomes chaotic. And Heaven wants the world to live and dislikes to have it die <note>This idea may be seen as the Chinese and humanistic equivalent of the Western classical assumption that existence is better than non-existence <note>original: "being is better than non-being"</note>.</note>, wants it to be wealthy and dislikes to have it be poor, and wants it to be orderly and dislikes to have it be disorderly. Therefore, we know Heaven desires righteousness and hates unrighteousness.

Furthermore, righteousness is the standard <term>standard: (fa) In Mohist philosophy, this refers to a legal or moral model used to guide behavior and governance, much like a compass or square is used in carpentry.</term>. A standard is not something given by subordinates to a superior, but by a superior to subordinates. Therefore, while the common people should work diligently <note>original: "spare no pains at work"</note>, they may not establish the standard themselves. It is the scholars who provide them with the standard. While the scholars should work diligently, they also may not establish the standard themselves. It is the ministers and secretaries who provide them with the standard.

<summary>Mozi argues that Heaven’s will is defined by righteousness, which brings life, wealth, and order to the world. He establishes a social hierarchy where moral standards flow downward from superiors to subordinates.</summary>
<keywords>Heaven's Will, righteousness, moral standard, social hierarchy, prosperity vs. chaos, Mohist ethics</keywords>

--- Page 151 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for "The Will of Heaven," establishing that Heaven is the ultimate source of the moral and legal standards (fa) that govern human society, from the common people up to the emperor.</meta>

-># WILL OF HEAVEN (I)<-

While the ministers and secretaries should work with great diligence, they cannot set the standard at their own will. The high dukes and feudal lords provide the standard for them. While the high dukes and the feudal lords should work with great diligence, they cannot set the standard at their own will either. The emperor provides the standard for them. The emperor may not set the standard at his own will either; Heaven provides the standard for him. 

The gentlemen of the world clearly understand that the emperor gives the standard to the high dukes, the feudal lords, the scholars, and the common people. However, the people do not well understand that Heaven gives the standard to the emperor. Therefore, the ancient sage-kings of the Three Dynasties <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties (c. 2070–256 BCE), regarded by Mozi as a golden age of wisdom.</note>—Yü, T’ang, Wen, and Wu—wished to make it clear to the people that Heaven gives the standard to the emperor. They raised oxen and sheep on grass, and pigs and dogs on grain, and prepared clean offerings of cakes and wine to sacrifice to God on High <note>original: "Shang Di," the supreme deity in ancient Chinese religion.</note> and the spirits, invoking Heaven’s blessing. But I have never heard of Heaven invoking the emperor for a blessing. Therefore, I know that Heaven provides the standard to the emperor.

The emperor is the most honorable person in the world and the wealthiest person in the world. Therefore, even the most honored and wealthy must obey the will of Heaven. Those who obey the will of Heaven—by practicing universal love <note>The central Mohist doctrine of "Jian'ai," which argues for caring for all people equally without partiality.</note> and benefiting others—will receive rewards. Those who oppose the will of Heaven—by being partial <term>partial: (bie) Favoring one's own family, friends, or state over others; the opposite of universal love.</term>, unfriendly, and harmful to others—will incur punishment. Now, who were those who obeyed the will of Heaven and were rewarded, and who were those who opposed the will of Heaven and were punished?

Motse <note>original: "Motse" (Mozi), the founder of Mohism.</note> said: The ancient sage-kings of the Three Dynasties...

<summary>Mozi establishes a political and moral hierarchy where Heaven is the ultimate authority over the emperor, just as the emperor is over the people. He argues that the standard for governance is set by Heaven and is defined by universal love and the benefit of others.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Will of Heaven, Three Dynasties, Universal Love, Sage-kings, Shang Di, Moral Standard, Political Hierarchy</keywords>

--- Page 152 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for "The Will of Heaven," specifically contrasting the legendary sage-kings of old—who were rewarded for following Heaven’s will—with the historical "wicked kings" who were punished for opposing it.</meta>

->## THE WORKS OF MOTSE<-

Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu <note>These are the idealized sage-kings of the Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties.</note> were those who obeyed the will of Heaven and received a reward. And the wicked kings of the Three Dynasties—Chieh, Chow, Yu, and Li <note>Infamous tyrants in Chinese history whose reigns ended in disaster and the fall of their houses.</note>—were those who opposed the will of Heaven and incurred punishment. How did Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu obtain their reward?

Motse <note>Mozi (c. 470–391 BCE), the philosopher and founder of Mohism.</note> said: In the highest realm, they revered Heaven; in the middle realm, they worshipped the spirits; and in the lower realm, they loved the people. Consequently, the will of Heaven proclaimed: "All those whom I love, these kings love also; and all those whom I benefit, these kings benefit also. Their love for humanity is all-embracing, and the benefit they bring to people is most significant." And so, they were raised to the honor of being "Sons of Heaven" <note>A title for the Emperor of China, implying a divine mandate to rule.</note> and were enriched with the inheritance of the empire. They were succeeded by descendants for ten thousand <note>A common Chinese figure of speech meaning a very large number or many generations.</note> generations to continue spreading their righteousness throughout the world. And people praise them to this day, calling them righteous sage-kings.

How did Chieh, Chow, Yu, and Li incur their punishment?

Motse said: In the highest realm, they blasphemed against Heaven; in the middle realm, they blasphemed against the spirits; and in the realm below, they oppressed the people. Consequently, the will of Heaven proclaimed: "They turn away from and hate those whom I love, and they oppress those whom I want to benefit. Their hatred of humanity is without limit, and their oppression of people is most severe." And so, they were not permitted to finish their natural lives <note>Meaning they died prematurely or by unnatural, often violent, means.</note> or to have their houses survive for even a single generation. And people condemn them to this day, calling them wicked kings.

<summary>This page contrasts the ancient sage-kings, who were rewarded with long-lasting dynasties for loving the people and honoring Heaven, with the wicked tyrants who were punished with destruction for their oppression and blasphemy.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Will of Heaven, Sage-kings, Yu, Tang, Wen, Wu, Tyrants, Chieh, Chow, Reward and Punishment, Mandate of Heaven</keywords>

--- Page 153 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument regarding "The Will of Heaven," specifically transitioning from the historical examples of sage-kings and tyrants to a logical proof of Heaven's universal love and the benefits of following a righteous standard.</meta>

->## THE WORKS OF MOTSE<-

# THE WILL OF HEAVEN (I)

How do we know Heaven loves the people? Because it teaches them all. How do we know it teaches them all? Because it claims them all. How do we know it claims them all? Because it accepts sacrifices from them all. How do we know it accepts sacrifices from everyone? Because within the "four seas" <note>An ancient Chinese idiom referring to the entire world known to them.</note>, all who live on grains <note>In ancient times, the cultivation and consumption of grain were seen as the primary marks of a civilized, agricultural society.</note> feed oxen and sheep with grass, and dogs and pigs with grain, and prepare clean cakes and wine to offer as sacrifice to the God on High <note>original: *Shangdi*, the supreme deity or celestial ruler in ancient Chinese religion.</note> and the spirits. Since Heaven claims all people as its own, why would it not love them? Moreover, as I have said, for the murder of a single innocent individual, there will be one calamity <note>A disaster or divine punishment.</note>. Who is it that murders the innocent? It is man. Who is it that sends down the calamity? It is Heaven. If Heaven were thought of as not loving the people, why would it send down calamities for the murder of one person by another? Therefore, I know Heaven loves the people.

To obey the will of Heaven is to accept righteousness as the standard. To oppose the will of Heaven is to accept force as the standard. Now, what will the standard of righteousness achieve?

Motse <note>Mozi (c. 470–391 BCE), the philosopher and founder of Mohism.</note> said: He who rules a large state does not attack small states; he who rules a powerful household does not harass small houses. The strong do not plunder the weak. The honored do not look down on the lowly. The clever do not deceive the simple. This is beneficial to Heaven above, beneficial to the spirits in the middle realm, and beneficial to the people below. Being beneficial to these three, it is beneficial to all. Therefore, the highest reputation is attributed to such a man, and he is called a sage-king <note>An idealized ruler who possesses both supreme wisdom and political authority.</note>.

<summary>Motse argues that Heaven's love for humanity is proven by its universal provision and its punishment of those who murder the innocent. He concludes that a righteous ruler must follow Heaven’s will by protecting the weak and benefiting all levels of existence.</summary>

<keywords>Heaven, Motse, righteousness, sage-king, sacrifice, calamity, God on High, Shangdi, four seas, Mohism, universal love</keywords>

--- Page 154 ---
<meta>This page continues the contrast between the "standard of righteousness" and the "standard of force," concluding the section on the "wicked king" and introducing Motse’s metaphor of the craftsman's tools as a way to measure moral truth.</meta>

->THE WORKS OF MOTSE<-

The standard of force is the opposite of this. It contradicts righteousness in its words and opposes it in its actions, much like two horses galloping in opposite directions. When the ruler of a large state uses force as his standard, he attacks smaller states; when the head of a powerful household uses force, he harasses <note>original: "molests," used here in the older sense of pestering, disturbing, or attacking.</note> smaller families. The strong rob the weak, the high-ranking look down on the humble, and the cunning deceive the simple. This behavior is not beneficial to Heaven above, to the spirits in the middle realm <note>The spiritual world existing between the heavens and the human world.</note>, or to the people below. Since it fails to benefit any of these three, it is beneficial to no one. Consequently, such a person is given the worst reputation in the world and is called a wicked king.

Motse <note>Mozi (c. 470–391 BCE), the philosopher and founder of Mohism.</note> said: To me, the will of Heaven is like the compass used by a wheelwright or the square used by a carpenter. The wheelwright and the carpenter use their square and compass to measure all objects, whether square or circular. They accept those that fit the tools as correct and reject those that do not fit as incorrect. 

The writings of the "gentlemen" of the world today are so numerous they would overflow a cart, and their various doctrines are too many to count. They try to convince feudal lords on one hand and scholars on the other. However, they are far from achieving true benevolence <note>original: "magnanimity," likely translating the Chinese concept of *ren* (human-heartedness).</note> and righteousness. How do we know this? We know because I possess the most reliable standard in the world with which to measure them.

<summary>Motse argues that ruling by force harms all levels of existence and identifies the "Will of Heaven" as a practical moral tool, similar to a craftsman's square or compass, used to judge the many conflicting doctrines of his time.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Will of Heaven, Ethics, Political Philosophy, Standard of Force, Moral Compass, Ancient China, Righteousness</keywords>

# CHAPTER XXVII

## THE WILL OF HEAVEN (PART II)

->THE WORKS OF MOZI<-

Mozi <note>Mozi (c. 470–391 BCE), the philosopher and founder of Mohism, also known as Motse.</note> said: Those gentlemen in the world who want to practice benevolence <note>original: "magnanimity," likely translating the Chinese concept of *ren* (human-heartedness).</note> and righteousness <note>original: "righteousness," translating the Chinese concept of *yi* (moral duty or justice).</note> must examine the origin of righteousness. Since we want to examine the origin of righteousness, where then does it originate?

Mozi said: Righteousness does not originate with the foolish and lowly, but with the honorable and wise. How do we know it does not originate with the dull and lowly, but with the honorable and wise? Because righteousness is the standard. How do we know righteousness is the standard? Because with righteousness the world will be orderly, and without it the world will be in chaos <note>original: "disorderly."</note>. Therefore, righteousness is known to be the standard. Since the uneducated and the lowly cannot create the standard, and only the wise and honorable can, I know that righteousness does not come from the foolish and lowly, but from the honorable and wise.

Now, who is truly honorable and who is wise? Heaven is honorable; Heaven is wise. Therefore, righteousness must originate with Heaven. People in the world might say: "We can clearly see that the Emperor <note>original: "emperor" or *Tianzi* (Son of Heaven).</note> is more honorable than the feudal lords, and that the feudal lords are more honorable than the ministers. But we do not see that Heaven is more honorable and wise than the Emperor."

Mozi said: I know Heaven is more honorable and wise than the Emperor for one specific reason: When the Emperor practices virtue, Heaven rewards him. When the Emperor does evil, Heaven punishes him. When there are diseases and calamities <note>Natural disasters or social misfortunes believed to be signs of Heaven's displeasure.</note>, the Emperor...

<summary>Mozi argues that righteousness serves as the essential standard for social order and must originate from the highest possible authority: Heaven. He asserts that Heaven is superior to the Emperor because it holds the power to reward or punish the ruler's conduct.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Will of Heaven, righteousness, social order, hierarchy, Emperor, divine reward and punishment</keywords>

And this is not the only evidence. We also learn this from the books of the ancient kings, which teach us the vast and inexpressible <note>original: "ineffable."</note> Way <note>original: "Tao."</note> of Heaven. It says: “Brilliant and insightful <note>original: "perspicacious."</note> Heaven on High, who enlightens and watches over the earth below!” <note>These are the two opening lines of the ode “Hsiao Ming” in the *Shih Ching* (Book of Odes). Is there anyone more honorable and wise than Heaven? No, Heaven is truly the most honorable and wise. Therefore, righteousness surely comes from Heaven. And so Mozi <note>original: "Motse."</note> said: If the gentlemen of the world truly desire to follow the Way and benefit the people, they must not disobey the will of Heaven, which is the origin of benevolence <note>original: "magnanimity," likely translating the Chinese concept of *ren* (human-heartedness).</note> and righteousness.

On the other hand, it desires people to use their energy to work for one another, and for those who know the Way to teach each other,

<header>WILL OF HEAVEN (II)</header>

This has been taking place from ancient times to the present.

<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI</header>

...Heaven loves the whole world universally. Everything is provided <note>See above <note>original: "Supra"</note>, p. 145, note 1.</note> for the good of humanity. The work of Heaven extends even to the smallest details enjoyed by man. Such benefits may indeed be called substantial, yet people offer no service in return. They do not even realize that this is ungenerous <note>original: "unmagnanimous"</note>. This is why I say the "gentlemen" of the world understand only trivialities, but not things of true importance.

Yet this does not exhaust the reasons by which I know Heaven loves humanity dearly. It is said that the murder of an innocent person will bring down a calamity. Who is the innocent? A human being is. From whom does the punishment <note>original: "visitation"</note> come? It comes from Heaven. If Heaven did not love the people dearly, why would Heaven send down a punishment upon the person who murders the innocent? Thus, I know Heaven loves humanity dearly.

This is still not all by which I know Heaven loves humanity dearly. There are those who love and benefit the people, obeying the will of Heaven and obtaining rewards from Heaven. There are also those who hate and oppress the people, opposing the will of Heaven and incurring punishment from Heaven. Who are those who love—

2. Compare this theme of Mozi's with the following from Seneca, *On Benefits* <note>original Latin title: *De Beneficiis*</note>, iv, 6: "We can be grateful to a friend for a few acres of land or a small sum of money; and yet for the freedom and mastery of the whole earth, and for the great benefits of our existence—such as life, health, and reason—we consider ourselves under no obligation. If a man grants us a house that is elegantly decorated with paintings, statues, gold leaf, and marble, we treat it as a significant matter; yet it is vulnerable to a gust of wind, the spark of a candle, and a hundred other accidents that could reduce it to dust. Is it then nothing to sleep under the canopy of heaven, where the entire earth is our place of rest and the glory of the stars is our spectacle?" <note>Lucius Annaeus Seneca (4 BCE–65 CE) was a Roman Stoic philosopher who, like Mozi, argued that humans often overlook the immense natural gifts provided by the divine.</note>

<header>WILL OF HEAVEN (II)</header>

...the people and benefit the people, obey the will of Heaven, and obtain rewards from Heaven? They are the ancient sage-kings of the Three Dynasties <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties (c. 2070–256 BC)</note>: Yao, Shun, Yü, T’ang, Wen, and Wu. What did Yao, Shun, Yü, T’ang, Wen, and Wu do? They committed themselves to universality rather than partiality in their love. Loving everyone universally, they did not use their large states to attack small states, nor did they use their large houses to harass small houses. The strong did not plunder the weak, the many did not oppress the few, the clever did not deceive the ignorant, and the noble did not look down upon the humble. Such a government was pleasing to Heaven above, to the spirits in the middle realm, and to the people below. Being helpful to these three, it was helpful to all. This was "Heavenly Virtue." The most excellent titles in the world were collected and given to them; they were called magnanimous, righteous, lovers of humanity, benefactors of the people, obedient to the will of Heaven, and rewarded by Heaven.

In addition to this, their deeds are recorded on bamboo and silk <note>The primary writing materials in ancient China before the invention of paper</note>, carved in metal and stone, and engraved on ceremonial bowls and cups to be passed down to future generations. Why was this done? It was to distinguish those who loved and benefited the people, obeyed the will of Heaven, and obtained rewards from Heaven. Thus, the ode titled "Huang Yi" says:

> “God said to King Wen, ‘I cherish your wise virtue. It was not displayed with loud noise or grand gestures. It was not changed after you gained the empire. How instructively and naturally you follow the plan of Heaven!’” <gloss>1</gloss>

These events are also recorded on bamboo and silk, carved into metal and stone, and engraved on plates and cups to be passed down to future generations. Why was this done? He would not worship God <note>original: "Shang Di"; the Supreme Deity in ancient Chinese thought</note> and pushed away the ancestors and spirits, failing to offer them sacrifices. He said, 'Fortune is with me,' and he neglected and betrayed his duty. Heaven then abandoned him and withdrew its protection." Heaven deserted King Zhou and withdrew its support because he opposed the will of Heaven. Therefore, we are able to know who those people were that hated and oppressed the people, opposed the will of Heaven, and incurred punishment from Heaven.

Therefore, the will of Heaven is like the compass to a <term>wheelwright</term> <note>a person who builds or repairs wooden wheels</note> and the square to a carpenter. A wheelwright tests the roundness of every object in the world with his compass, saying: "That which fits my compass is circular. That which does not is not circular." Therefore, whether an object is truly circular or not is easily known, because the standard of roundness has been established. The carpenter also tests the squareness of every object in the...

<summary>This page cites historical records and the "Great Declaration" to show how King Zhou's defiance of Heaven led to his downfall, eventually establishing that the Will of Heaven serves as an objective moral standard, much like a craftsman's tools.</summary>

--- Page 164 ---
<meta>This page continues the analogy comparing the "Will of Heaven" to a craftsman's tools. It concludes that Heaven's will serves as the ultimate objective standard for judging the morality of individuals and the quality of government.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>150</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

The carpenter tests every object in the world with his square, saying: “That which matches my square is square; that which does not is not square.” Therefore, whether any object is truly square or not is easily known. Why is this? Because the standard of squareness has been established.

Similarly, Motse <note>the philosopher Mozi (c. 470 – c. 391 BC), founder of Moism</note> uses the will of Heaven to measure the rule and government of the lords in the empire on one hand, and the doctrines and teachings of the masses in the empire on the other. If conduct is found to align with the will of Heaven, it is called good conduct; if it opposes the will of Heaven, it is called bad conduct. If a teaching aligns with the will of Heaven, it is called a good teaching; if it opposes the will of Heaven, it is called a bad teaching. And if a government aligns with the will of Heaven, it is called a good government; if it opposes the will of Heaven, it is called a bad government.

With this as the model and with this as the standard, whether the lords and the ministers are <term>magnanimous</term> <note>original: "magnanimous"; in Moist philosophy, this refers to being benevolent and practicing "universal love" (*jian'ai*)</note> or not can be measured as easily as one distinguishes between black and white.

Therefore Motse said: If the rulers and the gentlemen of the world truly desire to follow the right way and benefit the people, they need only obey the will of Heaven, which is the origin of magnanimity and righteousness. Obedience to the will of Heaven is the standard of righteousness.

<summary>Motse explains that just as a carpenter uses a square to identify a perfect shape, the "Will of Heaven" provides a clear, objective standard to judge whether conduct, teachings, and governments are good or bad.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, Mozi, Will of Heaven, righteousness, magnanimity, benevolence, moral standards, governance, ethics</keywords>

# CHAPTER XXVIII

## THE WILL OF HEAVEN (PART III)

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"; the philosopher Mozi (c. 470 – c. 391 BC), founder of Mohism</note> said: What is the reason for the disorder in the world? It is because the <term>gentlemen</term> <note>referring to the scholars and ruling elite of the time</note> of the world all understand trivialities but not things of importance. How do we know they understand trivialities but not things of importance? Because they do not understand the will of Heaven. How do we know they do not understand the will of Heaven? By observing conduct within the family. 

If a man commits a wrongdoing <note>original: "misdemeanour"</note> in the family, he still has other families in which to seek shelter. Yet, a father reminds his son, and an elder brother reminds his younger brother, saying: “Be obedient; be careful in your conduct in the family. If one is not obedient and careful in conduct in the family, how can he live in the state?” 

If a man commits a wrongdoing in the state, he still has other states in which to seek shelter. Yet a father reminds his son and an elder brother reminds his younger brother, saying: “Be obedient. Be careful. One cannot live in a state and be disobedient and careless.” 

Now all men live in the world and under Heaven. When a man sins against Heaven, there is nowhere to seek shelter. But people do not think of warning each other. Thus, I know that they do not understand things of importance. And Mozi said: Be obedient. Be careful. Be sure to do what Heaven desires and avoid what Heaven detests <note>original: "abominates"</note>.

Now, what does Heaven desire and what does Heaven detest? Heaven desires righteousness and detests

<summary>Mozi argues that social disorder stems from a failure to prioritize the Will of Heaven, noting the irony that people fear the consequences of local laws but ignore the inescapable judgment of the divine.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Will of Heaven, righteousness, social order, moral accountability, Mohism</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that righteousness is the ultimate standard for social order, flowing downward from Heaven through a strict hierarchy of authority.</meta>
