<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI <note>original: "MOTSE"</note></header>

unrighteousness. How do we know this? Because righteousness is the standard. How do we know righteousness is the standard? Because with righteousness the world will be orderly; without it, the world will be in chaos <note>original: "disorderly"</note>. Therefore, I know that righteousness is the standard. 

Now, a standard is never established by a subordinate for a superior; it is always established by the superior for the subordinate. Therefore, the common people may not create the standard as they please. There are the scholars to provide them with the standard. The scholars may not create the standard as they please. There are the ministers to provide them with the standard. The ministers may not create the standard as they please. There are the feudal lords to provide them with the standard. The feudal lords may not create the standard as they please. There is the high duke to provide them with the standard. The high duke may not create the standard as they please. There is the emperor <note>The "Son of Heaven," the supreme secular ruler</note> to provide him with the standard. The emperor may not create the standard as he pleases. There is Heaven to provide him with the standard. 

The <term>gentlemen</term> <note>referring to the educated ruling class</note> of the world all understand that the emperor provides the standard for the world, but they do not understand that Heaven provides the standard for the emperor. Explaining this, the sages said: "When the emperor has done good, Heaven rewards him. When the emperor has committed wrong, Heaven punishes him. When the emperor is unjust in rewards and punishments and is not wise in hearing lawsuits, Heaven strikes him with disease and disasters, and the frost and dew will come at the wrong times." The emperor will then be forced to feed grass to the oxen and sheep and grain to the dogs and pigs, and prepare pure cakes and wine to pray and call upon Heaven for a blessing. I have yet to hear of Heaven praying or calling upon the emperor for...

<summary>Mozi explains that righteousness is a universal standard that descends from Heaven through every level of the social hierarchy, ultimately holding even the emperor accountable to divine judgment.</summary>

blessing. Thus, I can say that Heaven is more honorable and wise than the emperor. Therefore, righteousness does not originate from the ignorant and humble, but from the honorable and wise. Who is honorable? Heaven is honorable. Who is wise? Heaven is wise. And so, righteousness assuredly comes from Heaven. Therefore, the <term>gentlemen</term> <note>Referring to the *junzi*, the educated and morally aspiring ruling class</note> of the world who desire to practice righteousness must obey the will of Heaven.

What is the will of Heaven that we must obey? It is to love all the people in the world universally. How do we know it is to love all the people in the world universally? Because Heaven accepts sacrifices from everyone. How do we know Heaven accepts sacrifices from everyone? Because from ancient times to the present day, there is no country, however distant or isolated, that does not raise oxen and sheep, dogs and pigs on grass and grain, and prepare pure cakes and wine to worship God <note>original: "God"; likely referring to *Shangdi*, the "High Lord" or supreme deity in early Chinese thought</note>, the hills and rivers, and the spirits. So we know Heaven accepts sacrifices from all. Since Heaven accepts sacrifices from everyone, it must love them all. 

Take the lords of Chu and Yue <note>original: "Ch'u and Yüeh"; two powerful states in ancient China during the Zhou dynasty</note> for instance. The lord of Chu accepts offerings from everyone within the four borders of Chu; therefore, he loves the people of Chu. And the lord of Yue accepts offerings from everyone within the four borders of Yue; therefore, he loves the people of Yue. Now, Heaven accepts offerings from the whole world, and so I know Heaven loves all the people in the world.

That Heaven loves all the people of the world is proved not only by this. In all the countries in the world, and among all the people who live on grain, the murder of one innocent individual brings down one calamity. Now who is it that...

<summary>Mozi argues that because Heaven is the ultimate source of wisdom and righteousness, its will—which is universal love for all humanity—must be obeyed. He proves this love by noting that Heaven accepts sacrifices from all nations and punishes the murder of the innocent.</summary>

...murders the innocent? It is man. Who is it that sends down disasters? It is Heaven. If Heaven did not truly love the people, why would Heaven send down calamities in response to the murder of the innocent?

Furthermore, Heaven loves the people dearly; Heaven loves the people universally. And this can be known. How do we know Heaven loves the people? Because it certainly rewards the good and punishes the evil. How do we know that the virtuous Heaven rewards the good and punishes the evil? I know this from the examples of the <term>sage-kings</term> <note>Idealized rulers of Chinese antiquity who embodied perfect virtue and wisdom</note> of the <term>Three Dynasties</term> <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties (c. 2070–256 BCE)</note>. In ancient times, the sage-kings of the Three Dynasties—Yao, Shun, Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu—loved the world universally and acted to benefit everyone. They redirected the desires of the people and led them to worship God <note>original: "God"; likely referring to *Shangdi*, the "High Lord" or supreme deity in early Chinese thought</note>, the hills and rivers, and the spirits. Heaven was pleased because they loved what Heaven loved and benefited those whom Heaven wished to benefit. Therefore, Heaven bestowed rewards upon them: it placed them on the throne, crowned them as emperors, established them as the moral standard for others to follow, and called them sage-kings. Here we have proof of the reward given to the good.

In ancient times, the wicked kings of the Three Dynasties—Jie, Zhou, You, and Li <note>original: "Chieh, Chow, Yu, and Li"; infamous tyrants in Chinese history whose reigns led to the collapse of their respective eras</note>—hated everyone in the world and proceeded to oppress them. They redirected the desires of the people and led them to speak disrespectfully of God, the hills and rivers, and the spirits. Heaven was offended because they hated those whom Heaven loved and oppressed those whom Heaven wished to benefit. Consequently, Heaven decreed punishments upon them, causing fathers and sons to be separated, their empires to be dismantled, their states to be lost to them, and capital punishment to fall upon them...

<summary>This page argues that Heaven's universal love is proven by its historical pattern of rewarding virtuous sage-kings and punishing oppressive tyrants. It uses the legendary figures of the Three Dynasties to show that righteousness aligns with the Will of Heaven.</summary>

upon them. Thereupon, the multitudes of the world condemned them, and this condemnation has lasted for ten thousand generations <note>original: "ten thousand generations"; a Chinese idiom meaning for all of eternity</note>, as people still call them wicked kings. Here we have the proof of the punishment of evil.

Those people of the world who desire to act with righteousness have only to obey the will of Heaven. To obey the will of Heaven is to be **universal** in one's love, while to oppose the will of Heaven is to be **partial** <note>The core of Mozi’s philosophy: "Universal Love" (*Jian'ai*) means caring for everyone equally, while "Partiality" (*Bie*) means favoring oneself, one's family, or one's own state.</note>. According to the doctrine of universality, righteousness is the standard; in the doctrine of partiality, force is the basis of government. 

What is it like to have righteousness as the basis of government? Under such a rule:
* The large state will not attack the small.
* The strong will not plunder the weak.
* The many will not oppress the few.
* The clever will not deceive the ignorant.
* The honored <note>those of high social rank or status</note> will not look down upon the humble.
* The rich will not mock the poor.
* And the young will not rob the old. 

Furthermore, the states in the empire will not ruin each other with water, fire, poison, or weapons. Such a regime will be beneficial to Heaven above, to the spirits in the middle sphere <note>In ancient Chinese cosmology, spirits were often thought to inhabit the space between the high heavens and the human world</note>, and to the people below. Being beneficial to these three, it is beneficial to all. This is called the **virtue of Heaven**. Whoever practices this is a sage—magnanimous, gracious, righteous, loyal, affectionate, and filial. All such honorable titles in the world will be gathered and attributed to him. Why is this? Because such conduct is in accordance with the will of Heaven.

Now, what is it like to have force as the basis of government? In such a system:
* The large will attack the small.
* The strong will plunder the weak.
* The many will oppress the few.
* The clever will take advantage of the ignorant.
* The honored will look down upon...

...the humble, the rich will mock the poor, and the young will rob the old. The states within the empire will ruin each other with water, fire, poison, and weapons. Because it is not helpful to these three, it is not helpful to anyone. This is called being an "enemy of Heaven." He who practices this is a bad man—not generous <note>original: "magnanimous"</note>, not gracious, and unrighteous. He is disloyal, cold-hearted, and unfilial <note>original: "unfilial"; failing in the fundamental duty of respect and care for one's parents and ancestors</note>. All such evil names in the world are gathered together and attributed to him. Why is this? It is because such conduct is in opposition to the will of Heaven.

Hence, Motse established the <term>will of Heaven</term> as his standard, just as a wheelwright uses his compasses and a carpenter uses his square as their standards. The wheelwright with his compasses and the carpenter with his square can judge the roundness and the squareness of objects. Similarly, with the <term>will of Heaven</term> as the standard, Motse can tell that the "gentlemen" <note>Referring here to the ruling elite or those who consider themselves socially superior</note> of the world are far from true righteousness.

How do we know the gentlemen of the world are far from righteousness? We know because the lords in the large states compete by saying: "Being a large state, if I do not attack the small states, in what way am I great?" Therefore, they mobilized their warriors and soldiers, and organized their boat and chariot forces to attack some innocent state. They broke through its borders, cut down its crops, felled its trees, tore down its inner and outer city walls, and filled up its moats and ditches. They burned its ancestral temples and seized and killed its sacrificial animals. Of the people, the strong were killed, while the weak were brought back in chains and ropes. The men were turned into servants, grooms, and prisoners. The

<summary>Motse argues that rulers who ignore the "will of Heaven" bring about social chaos and violent warfare, using the craftsman's tools as an analogy for how Heaven's will serves as the ultimate standard for righteousness.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Mozi's argument against the hypocrisy of rulers who celebrate the theft of entire states through war while punishing small-scale theft, illustrating how this leads to a cycle of perpetual violence.</meta>

<page-num>157</page-num>
<header>WILL OF HEAVEN (III)</header>

...women were forced to serve as wine-pourers. Yet, the warring lord did not even realize that this is unkind <note>original: "unmagnanimous"</note> and unjust <note>original: "unrighteous"</note>. He announced to the neighboring lords: "I have attacked a state, defeated an army, and killed many generals." And the neighboring lords also did not realize that this is unkind and unjust; instead, they sent envoys with furs and silk to offer their congratulations. Thus, the warring lords were twice as ignorant of the fact that they were being unkind and unjust. They recorded these events on bamboo and silk <note>original: "bamboos and silk"; materials used for writing before the invention of paper</note> and kept them in the archives so that their descendants would imitate their royal ancestors, saying: "Why don't we open the archives and learn from the achievements of our ancestors?" Then they would certainly not learn: "This was the proper way to rule under the state of Wu," but would instead learn: "I have attacked states, overturned armies, and killed many of their generals." Because the warring lords do not understand this to be unkind and unjust, and neighboring lords do not understand it either, attacks and assaults continue from generation to generation without end.

What do I mean when I say people do not understand important matters but understand trifles? Suppose someone entered another person's orchard or garden and took their peaches, plums <note>original: "prunes"</note>, melons, and ginger. He will be punished by the authorities <note>original: "the superior"</note> when caught and condemned by the public when they hear of it. Why? Because he did not work for the fruit but instead took it and claimed what was not his. How much more is this true for someone who jumps over another person's fence and mistreats the children of another; or for someone who breaks into another's storehouse and carries away...

<summary>Mozi critiques rulers for celebrating military conquest as a glorious achievement to be recorded for posterity, highlighting the hypocrisy of punishing petty theft while ignoring the much larger injustice of aggressive war.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, pacifism, social justice, military conquest, hypocrisy, righteousness, bamboo and silk, archives</keywords>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI</header>

...the other’s gold, jade, silk, and cloth; of him who breaks into another’s livestock pen <note>original: "fold"</note> and steals the other’s oxen and horses; and of him who kills an innocent person? In the governments of the lords of today, everyone—from the one who kills an innocent person to the one who jumps over another’s fence and harms <note>original: "maltreats"</note> the other’s children, who digs into another’s warehouse and carries away his gold, jade, silk, and cloth, who breaks into another’s pen and steals his oxen and horses, and who enters another’s orchard and garden and takes his peaches and plums <note>original: "prunes"</note>, melons, and ginger—all these are punished exactly the same as they would be even in the governments of Yao, Shun, Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu <note>These are the legendary "Sage Kings" of Chinese antiquity, regarded by Mozi as the ultimate standards of virtue and good governance.</note>. Now the lords and chiefs in the world all attack and take over <note>original: "absorb"</note> other states. This is a thousand and ten thousand times worse than killing one innocent individual; a thousand and ten thousand times worse than jumping over another’s fence and harming his children or digging into another’s storehouse and carrying away his gold, jade, silk, and cloth; a thousand and ten thousand times worse than breaking into another’s pen and stealing his oxen and horses, or entering another’s orchard and garden and taking his peaches and plums, melons, and ginger. Yet, they claim it to be just <note>original: "righteous"</note>.

Mozi said: This is meant to confuse us. And is this at all different from confusion in the distinctions between black and white, and sweet and bitter? Suppose there is a man who, upon being shown a little blackness, says it is black, but upon being shown much blackness, says it is white. He will have to admit that his sight is confused and that he cannot tell the difference between black and white. Suppose a man, when served a little of something bitter, says it is bitter, but...

<summary>Mozi argues that rulers are hypocritical for punishing petty crimes while praising the much greater crime of offensive warfare, comparing this moral blindness to a man who cannot distinguish colors or tastes.</summary>

<header>WILL OF HEAVEN (III)</header>
<page-num>159</page-num>

...when served with something very bitter, says it is sweet. Then he would have to admit that his taste is impaired and that he cannot tell the difference between sweet and bitter. In the governments of the current lords, the murderer of an individual is imprisoned by the state. This <note>The major part of this sentence is lost in the original text.</note>... But the murderer of many men from neighboring states is upheld as righteous. How is this different from confusing the distinction between black and white, or sweet and bitter?

Therefore, Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> established the Will of Heaven to be the standard. It was not only Mozi who established the Will of Heaven as the standard; it is also the theme of an ode in the "Greater Odes" <note>original: "Ta Ya." This is the title of a section of the *Shih Ching* or *Book of Odes*, a classic collection of ancient Chinese poetry.</note> among the books of the ancient kings: "God said to King Wen, 'I cherish your intelligent virtue. It was not proclaimed with much noise or grand gestures. It was not changed after you gained the empire. You were instinctively and naturally submissive to the plan of God.'" <note>See page 147, note 1.</note> This is to proclaim that King Wen used the Will of Heaven as his standard and was submissive to God’s plan. If the leaders <note>original: "gentlemen"</note> of the world really desire to practice benevolence <note>original: "magnanimity"</note> and righteousness and be superior men—seeking to follow the way of the sage kings on one hand and to bring blessings to the people on the other—they must not neglect to understand the Will of Heaven. The Will of Heaven is truly the standard of righteousness.

3 See page 147, note 1.

# BOOK VIII

## CHAPTER XXXI

### ON GHOSTS (III)

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: With the passing of the sage-kings of the Three Dynasties <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties (c. 2070–256 BCE), which Mozi viewed as the golden age of moral rule.</note>, the world lost its righteousness and the feudal lords began to believe that "might makes right." Superiors and subordinates are no longer gracious and loyal; father and son, and elder and younger brother, are no longer affectionate, filial <note>original: "filial"; meaning the duty and respect a child owes their parents.</note>, brotherly, respectful, virtuous, and kind. The rulers do not attend diligently to government, and the artisans do not attend earnestly to their work. The people practice immorality and wickedness and become rebellious. Thieves and bandits—armed with weapons, poison, water, and fire—ambush innocent travelers on the highways and bypaths, robbing them of their carts and horses, coats and furs, to enrich themselves. All these problems began with the passing of the sage-kings. And so the world falls into chaos.

Now, what is the reason for this confusion? It is entirely because of the doubt regarding the existence of ghosts and spirits, and the ignorance of their ability to reward virtue and punish vice. If all the people in the world believed that the spirits are able to reward virtue and punish vice, how could the world be in chaos? Those who deny the existence of spirits proclaim: "Of course there are no spirits." From morning until evening, they teach this doctrine to the people of the empire. They bewilder the people, causing them all to doubt the existence of ghosts and spirits. In...

<summary>Mozi argues that the collapse of social order and the rise of crime are direct results of people no longer believing that ghosts and spirits reward the good and punish the wicked.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Three Dynasties, ghosts and spirits, moral decay, social order, divine retribution, skepticism</keywords>

--- Page 175 ---
<meta>This page continues Mozi's argument that social order depends on belief in the supernatural. He transitions from describing the chaos of a godless world to proposing an empirical method for proving that ghosts and spirits actually exist.</meta>

# ON GHOSTS (III)

...in this way, the world becomes disorderly. Therefore, Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: If the rulers and the gentlemen of the world really desire to bring benefits to the world and remove its disasters, they must understand whether ghosts and spirits exist or not.

Since we must understand whether ghosts and spirits exist or not, how can we find out? Mozi said: The way to find out whether anything exists or not is to rely on the testimony of the ears and eyes of the masses. If some have heard it or some have seen it, then we have to say it exists. If no one has heard it and no one has seen it, then we have to say it does not exist. So, then, why not go to some village or some district and inquire? If from ancient times to the present, and since the beginning of humanity, there are people who have seen the forms of ghosts and spirits and heard their voices, how can we say that they do not exist? If none have heard them and none have seen them, then how can we say they do? 

But those who deny the existence of the spirits say: “Many in the world have heard and seen something of ghosts and spirits. Since their testimonies vary, who should be accepted as really having heard and seen them?” Mozi said: Since we are to rely on what many have jointly seen and what many have jointly heard, the case of Du Bo <note>original: "Tu Po"</note> is to be accepted.¹

King Xuan <note>original: "Hsuan"</note> of Zhou (r. 827–783 BCE) put his minister Du Bo to death even though he was innocent. Du Bo remarked: “The king puts me to death while I am innocent. If a person loses his consciousness after death, then that is the end of it. But if a person has consciousness after death, then within three years I will make the king know it.”

---

¹ In the following paragraphs, Mozi cites a number of cases to support the existence of spirits. Most of the persons and occasions mentioned are historical, but the details seem to have come from the folk tales and legends current during his time.

<summary>Mozi proposes an empirical test for the existence of ghosts based on the collective testimony of the people's senses. He begins to cite the historical story of Du Bo, an innocent minister who vowed to haunt the king who executed him.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, ghosts and spirits, empiricism, Du Bo, King Xuan of Zhou, testimony, historical evidence</keywords>

--- Page 176 ---
<meta>This page continues the story of the ghost of Du Bo (Tu Po) seeking revenge on King Xuan (Hsuan) of Zhou, serving as Mozi's primary historical evidence for the existence of the supernatural.</meta>

...consciousness after death, then that is the end of it. But if I still retain my consciousness after death, I will make the king aware of this within three years.”

In three years, King Xuan assembled the feudal lords at Pu Tian <note>original: "P’u T’ien"</note>. There were several hundred chariots. Attendants numbered in the thousands and the crowds covered the fields. At noon, Du Bo <note>original: "Tu Po"</note> appeared in a plain chariot drawn by a white horse, wearing red clothing and a red hat, holding a red bow and carrying red arrows. He pursued King Xuan and shot him while he was in his chariot. The arrow pierced his heart and broke his back. He fell and died face down. At the time, all the people of Zhou <note>original: "Chou"</note> who were there saw it, and those far away heard of it, and it was recorded in the *Spring and Autumn* <note>original: "Spring and Autumn" was then not a proper name, referring to the title of the annals of Lu written by Confucius, but a common name for any state annals.</note> of Zhou. Rulers used this story to instruct their ministers, and fathers used it to warn their sons, saying: “Be careful; be respectful. All who kill the innocent are quickly and severely struck by misfortune and punished by the ghosts and spirits like this.” Judging from what is recorded here, how can we doubt that ghosts and spirits exist?

The records in this book are not the only proof. Formerly, Lord Mu of Qin <note>The original text says Lord Mu of Cheng, but the incident historically belongs to Lord Mu of Qin (r. 659–621 BCE).</note> was once in the temple at noon. A spirit entered and descended. He had the face of a man but the body of a bird. His clothing was plain and dark, and his appearance was dignified. Seeing him, Lord Mu became afraid and started to run away. The spirit said: “Do not be afraid. God <note>The original term here refers to the High God, *Shang Di* or *Tian*.</note> treasures your intelligent virtue, and has authorized me to prolong your life by nineteen years, and has ordained that your state shall be prosperous and your descendants...

<summary>Mozi cites the historical account of the ghost of Du Bo assassinating King Xuan as empirical evidence that spirits exist and execute justice. He begins a second historical example involving Lord Mu of Qin's encounter with a bird-bodied spirit.</summary>

# ON GHOSTS (III)

...to be many and not to lose the state of Qin <note>original: "Ch’in"</note>.” Lord Mu saluted him repeatedly and bowed, saying: “May I ask your name, my god?” He answered: “I am Kou Mang.” If we are to accept what Lord Mu of Qin saw personally as reliable, then how can we doubt that spirits and ghosts exist?

This is not the only record <note>original: "this book" refers to the historical records of the state of Qin</note> that proves it to be so. Long ago, Lord Chien of Yen <note>reigned approximately 504–493 BCE</note> put his minister Chuang Tse Yi to death even though he was innocent. Chuang Tse Yi remarked: “The lord puts me to death though I am innocent. If a person loses their consciousness after death, then that is the end of it. But if I still retain my consciousness after death, I shall make the Lord aware of this within three years.” 

In one year’s time, the Lord of Yen <note>original: "Yen" refers to the ruler as the representative of the state</note> was traveling to Tsu <note>a location for religious sacrifice</note>. These ceremonies were occasions for large gatherings of men and women. At noon, Lord Chien was riding on the road to Tsu. The ghost of Chuang Tse Yi appeared carrying a red staff; he struck the lord and knocked him down. At the time, all the people of Yen who were present saw it, and all those who were far away heard of it. It was recorded in the *Spring and Autumn of Yen* <note>the official historical annals of the state of Yen</note>. The feudal lords spread the news around and remarked: “The misfortunes and punishments from the spirits and ghosts upon those who kill the innocent are swift and severe.” Judging from the records in this book, how can we doubt that spirits and ghosts exist?

Nor is this the only book that proves it. In ancient times, during the reign of Lord Wen of Sung, whose personal name was Pao <note>reigned 610–589 BCE</note>, there was a master of ceremonies named Kuan Ku. While he was working in the temple...

A wizard <gloss>1</gloss> <note>The original term 祝子 (zhù zǐ) refers to a ritual official who acts as a medium for the spirits.</note> carrying a cane appeared and said to him: “Kuan Ku, why don’t the jades and stones measure up to the standard? Why are the cakes and wine unclean, the sacrificial animals <note>original: "victims"</note> imperfect and not fat, and the sacrifices not performed in the proper season? Did you do this, or did Pao do this?” 

Kuan Ku answered: “Pao is still small and in his swaddling clothes. What does he have to do with this? This was all done by the official in charge, Kuan Ku.” Thereupon the wizard lifted his cane and struck him, knocking him down onto the altar. At the time, all those people who were present saw it, and those who were far away heard of it. And it was recorded in the *Spring and Autumn Annals of Sung*. The feudal lords circulated the news and remarked: “The punishment from spirits and ghosts upon those who are not reverent in performing sacrifices is swift and severe!” Judging from the records of this book, how can we doubt that spirits and ghosts exist?

The record in this book is not the only thing that proves it to be so. In the past, Lord Chuang of Qi <note>reigned 794–731 BCE; original: "Ch’i"</note> had two ministers, Wang Li Kuo and Chung Li Chiao, who were engaged in a lawsuit. For three years, no judgment could be reached. The Lord of Qi thought of putting both of them to death, but he was afraid to kill the innocent; he thought of acquitting both of them, but he was afraid to let the guilty go free. 

So he had them provide a lamb and swear an oath on the altar of Qi. The two men agreed to take the oath of blood. The throat of the lamb was cut and its blood was sprinkled on the altar. The testimony of Wang Li Kuo was read all the way through. But before even half of the case of Chung Li Chiao was read, the sacrificed lamb stood up and butted him, breaking his leg and knocking him down on...

...the altar. At the time, all the people of Qi <note>original: "Ch'i"</note> who were present saw it, and those far away heard of it. It was recorded in the *Spring and Autumn Annals of Qi*. The feudal lords circulated the news around and remarked: "How speedy and severe is the punishment from spirits and ghosts to those who take an oath in bad faith!" Judging from the records in this book, how can we doubt that spirits and ghosts exist?

Therefore Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: One must not act disrespectfully even in woods, valleys, or solitary caves where no human is present. Spirits and ghosts are watching everywhere.

Those who deny the existence of spirits ask: "Are the senses of hearing and sight of the common people <note>original: "the multitude"</note> sufficient to resolve such a doubt? How can people strive to be learned gentlemen while they continue to trust the senses of hearing and sight of the common people?"

Mozi said: If the senses of hearing and sight of the common people are thought to be untrustworthy, we may ask: are men like the Sage-Kings of the Three Dynasties—Yao, Shun, Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu—trustworthy? Of course, regarding this, anyone of reasonable mind <note>original: "above the mediocre"</note> will say that such men as the ancient Sage-Kings of the Three Dynasties <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties</note>—Yao, Shun, Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu—are indeed trustworthy. If the ancient Sage-Kings of the Three Dynasties are trustworthy, we may review some of their deeds.

In ancient times, having captured Yin <note>The Shang dynasty</note> and punished Zhou <note>original: "Chow"; the last, tyrannical ruler of the Shang dynasty</note>, King Wu permitted the feudal lords to participate in the worship of the ancestors of Yin. Those more closely related were to participate in the temple sacrifices, and those less closely related were to participate in the outdoor sacrifices. It follows that King Wu must have believed there were spirits and ghosts; otherwise, why would he, after capturing Yin...

<summary>Mozi concludes a historical anecdote about divine punishment in the state of Qi and argues that if one trusts the wisdom of the ancient Sage-Kings, one must also accept their belief in the existence of spirits.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, Qi, Spring and Autumn Annals, Sage-Kings, Three Dynasties, King Wu, spirits and ghosts, ritual sacrifice</keywords>

--- Page 180 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument for the existence of ghosts and spirits by citing the religious practices of King Wu and other ancient Sage-Kings, demonstrating that they prioritized spiritual duties over civil administration.</meta>

<page-num>166</page-num>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...and punishing King Zhou <note>original: "Chow"; the last ruler of the Shang dynasty</note>, he allowed the feudal lords to share in the worship. If there were no spirits and ghosts, why did King Wu assign these duties of worship?

Not only does the deed of King Wu prove this to be true. When the ancient Sage-Kings distributed rewards, it was always done before their ancestors. When they carried out punishments, it was always done before the altar of the soil. Why are rewards distributed before the ancestors? It is to demonstrate their fairness. Why are punishments carried out before the altar? It is to demonstrate their justice.

Not only does the record in that book prove this to be true. On the day when the ancient Sage-Kings of the Three Dynasties—Yu, Xia, Shang, and Zhou <note>The "Three Dynasties" usually refers to the Xia, Shang, and Zhou. "Yu" here refers to the era of the legendary Emperor Shun and the Xia founder, Yu the Great.</note>—first established their empire and built their capitals, they always chose the central altar as the site for the ancestral temple. They would pick out the most lush and elegant trees to plant in the temple of agriculture. They would select the elders of the country who were the most affectionate, filial, virtuous, and kind to serve as masters of ceremonies. They would choose the sacrificial animals <note>original: "victims"</note> from among the six domestic animals <note>The horse, ox, sheep, pig, dog, and fowl</note> based on their fatness, perfection, and the color of their wool. The jades and stones used were required to be of appropriate material and exact measurement. And the sacrificial cakes and wine were to be prepared with the most fragrant and golden grain; the quality of these offerings would vary based on the abundance of the year’s harvest. 

This shows that in the government of the ancient Sage-Kings, spirits and ghosts had priority over the people. Before the government offices and courts were even fully established, the sacrificial vessels and robes had to be completed. 

<summary>Mozi argues that the existence of spirits is proven by the religious devotion of the ancient Sage-Kings, who prioritized ancestral worship and sacrificial preparations over the construction of government buildings.</summary>

<keywords>King Wu, King Zhou, Sage-Kings, Three Dynasties, ancestor worship, sacrifice, justice, religious ritual, Mozi</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument for the existence of ghosts and spirits by citing the sacrificial preparations of the ancient Sage-Kings and their efforts to record these beliefs for future generations.</meta>

<page-num>167</page-num>

<header>ON GHOSTS (III) 167</header>

...all were stored in the storehouse, the masters and attendants of ceremonies were all installed in court, and the sacrificial animals <note>original: "victims"</note> were kept apart from the original flock. Since the government of the ancient Sage-Kings was organized in this way, the ancient Sage-Kings must have believed in the existence of spirits and ghosts.

Their own interest in the welfare of ghosts and spirits was deep. Yet they were afraid their descendants might not understand it. Therefore, they recorded it on bamboo and silk to leave to them. Fearing that these might rot and disappear so that their descendants might not learn of it, they engraved it on ritual vessels and bowls <note>original: "plates and cups"</note> and carved it into metals and stones. They also feared that their descendants might not be reverent and obtain blessings; thus, in the books of the ancient kings and the records of sages, testimonies to the existence of ghosts and spirits appear time and again, even on a single foot of silk or a single page in the books. Why was this? Because the Sage-Kings were interested in it. Those who deny the existence of spirits are opposing the interests of the Sage-Kings, and such is not the way of the superior person <note>original: "superior man"; likely a translation of the term *junzi*, referring to a person of high moral character</note>.

Those who deny the existence of spirits might say:
> "Among the books of the ancient kings, not a foot of silk or a single page is found which repeatedly testifies to the existence of ghosts and spirits. If so, where are these testimonies?"

Motse <note>also known as Mozi</note> replied: They are found, for instance, in the "Daya" <note>original: "Ta Ya"; the "Greater Odes" section of the *Classic of Poetry*</note> of the books of Zhou. The "Daya" says:
> "The rule of King Wen over the people pleased Heaven. Although Zhou is an old country, it has received a new commission <note>the 'Mandate of Heaven'</note> from Heaven. Zhou does not appear showy. The commission from God does not appear to be predictable <note>original: "seasonable"; meaning the Mandate does not follow a simple or fixed schedule</note>. King Wen reached high..."

<summary>Motse argues that the ancient Sage-Kings' elaborate sacrificial systems and their efforts to record their beliefs on durable materials prove their conviction in the spirit world. He begins to refute skeptics by citing the "Daya" section of the Classic of Poetry as historical evidence.</summary>

<page-num>168</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...high and low, he was on the left and the right of God <note>original: "God" here likely refers to Shangdi, the supreme celestial deity in ancient Chinese religion</note>. How active was King Wen! He practiced his wise virtue without ceasing.”¹ If ghosts and spirits do not exist, then how could King Wen be “on the left and right of God” since he was already dead? Here we have evidence of ghosts in the Book of Zhou <note>original: Chou; the dynasty founded by King Wen's family</note>.

If there was evidence only in the books of Zhou and none in those of Shang <note>the dynasty preceding the Zhou</note>, it still might not be reliable. But we find the following among the books of Shang: “Oh! In ancient times, before the Xia <note>original: Hsia; the dynasty preceding the Shang</note> was visited by misfortune, none of the various animals, insects, or even birds strayed from their proper course. As for those with human faces, who would dare to be divided in heart? Even the ghosts and spirits of the hills and rivers did not dare to be rebellious.”² If one is respectful and sincere, one can maintain harmony in the world and stability on the earth below. Now, it was to assist Yu <note>original: Yü; the legendary founder of the Xia dynasty</note> that the ghosts and spirits of the hills and rivers did not dare to rebel. Here we have evidence of ghosts in the Book of Shang.

If there was evidence of ghosts only in the books of Shang and none in those of Xia, it would still be unreliable. But we have the “Speech at Kan”³ among the books of Xia, which says: “In the middle of the war at Kan, the Emperor called—"

¹ This quotation is from the ode “Wen Wang” <note>King Wen</note> in the “Daya” <note>Greater Odes</note> section of the *Shih Ching* <note>The Classic of Poetry</note>. It is a song of praise for the life of King Wen. James Legge’s translation is found in volume 4, pages 427–428.

² The quotation is from the “Instructions of Yi” <note>original: Instructions of E</note> in the *Shu Ching* <note>The Classic of History</note>. However, the two texts differ significantly in their wording. For comparison, see Legge, volume 3, pages 193–194.

³ The text says “Speech of Yu.” Some ancient writers seem to use both titles for the same work. In the current text of the *Shu Ching*, the essay is called “Speech at Kan.”

<page-num>169</page-num>
### ON GHOSTS (III)

...the six generals <note>original: "associates"; refers to the commanders of the "six armies" of the Emperor</note> to receive instructions at the headquarters. 

He said: "The Prince of Hu <note>a rebellious vassal who refused to submit to the Emperor</note> has violated the Five Elements <note>the fundamental cosmic forces of wood, fire, earth, metal, and water</note> and neglected the three calendars <note>archaic systems for tracking time and seasons</note>. Heaven has decreed the end of his life!" And he continued: "At noon I shall struggle with the Prince of Hu for the fate of the day. But, mark my words, you ministers and people: it is not because I covet their land and treasures. I am only carrying out this punishment in obedience to Heaven. If you on the left wing do not do your part on the left, you will be disobeying my orders; if you on the right wing do not do your part on the right, you will be disobeying my orders; if you charioteers do not manage your horses according to my instructions, you will be disobeying my orders. Rewards will be distributed in the presence of my ancestors, and punishments will be carried out before the altar <note>original: "altar"; specifically the Altar of the Soil, where spirits of the land were worshipped</note>." ¹ 

Why were rewards distributed before the ancestors? To demonstrate their fairness. Why were punishments meted out before the altar? To demonstrate their justice. Because the ancient sage-kings believed they must reward virtue and punish vice in the sight of ghosts and spirits, they distributed rewards before the ancestors and carried out punishments before the altar. And here we have evidence of ghosts in the books of the Xia <note>original: Hsia; the earliest of the three ancient dynasties</note>.

Now, first in the books of the Xia, and next in the books of the Shang and Zhou, evidence for the existence of ghosts and spirits appears again and again. What is the reason for this? It is because the sage-kings were concerned with them. Judging from the records in these books, how can we doubt that there are ghosts and spirits?

¹ This is almost the entire "Speech at Kan" <note>original: "Speech at Kan"; a famous address found in the *Book of Documents*</note>. For the standard version in the *Shu Jing* <note>original: *Shu Ching*, the Classic of History</note>, see Legge, volume 3, pages 152–155.

<summary>Mozi concludes his appeal to historical records by citing the Xia dynasty's "Speech at Kan," arguing that the practice of rewarding and punishing before ancestral spirits proves the ancient sage-kings believed in ghosts.</summary>

-><header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header><-

In ancient times, on the auspicious day of *Ting Mao* <note>Ting Mao (丁卯) is a specific day in the traditional Chinese calendar</note>, the Zhou <note>original: "Chou"; the dynasty that followed the Shang</note> offered sacrifices of thanksgiving to the Earth, the spirits of the Four Quarters, and their ancestors. They did this to extend their lives. If ghosts and spirits did not exist, how could their lives have been extended?

Motse said: Regarding the fact that ghosts and spirits can reward virtue and punish wickedness—if this could be proclaimed to the entire country and all its people, it would truly be a source of order for the nation and a blessing to the people. The corruption of officials in their public duties and the immorality between men and women would all be witnessed by ghosts and spirits. The crimes of those who use weapons, poisons, water, or fire to ambush innocent travelers—robbing them of their carts, horses, and clothing to enrich themselves—would also be seen by ghosts and spirits. 

Consequently, officials would not dare to be corrupt in their offices; they would not withhold rewards from the virtuous or refuse to punish the wicked. Those among the people who commit crimes and cruelties—ambushing innocent travelers with weapons, poisons, water, or fire to rob them of their carts, horses, and clothing for their own gain—all of these people would exist no more. The world would then have order. Truly, the intelligence of ghosts and spirits cannot be challenged. Even in isolated caves, large ponds, deep woods, and remote valleys, the ghosts and spirits are watching. The punishments of ghosts and spirits cannot be evaded. Neither wealth, large numbers, courage, strength, nor heavy armor and...

# ON GHOSTS (III)

...even with strong armor and sharp weapons, the punishment of ghosts and spirits will frustrate them.

If you doubt this, look at the story of the ancient King Jie <note>original: Chieh</note> of the Xia <note>original: Hsia</note> dynasty. He was an emperor of great honor and possessed the wealth of the entire empire. Yet he cursed Heaven, blasphemed against the spirits above, and brought destruction upon the masses below.¹ Consequently, Heaven commissioned King Tang <note>original: T'ang</note> to carry out a righteous punishment. With ninety chariots <note>original: "nine"; but historical context suggests ninety</note>,² Tang arranged his troops into the Bird Formation and the Wild Goose March. He ascended the Ta Tsan <note>likely the name of a mountain pass</note>³ and scattered the forces of the Xia, entering their land. There he captured T’uei Yi Ta Hsi.

Now, King Jie of the Xia was an emperor of great honor and possessed the wealth of the entire empire. In his service was a man of immense daring and strength named T’uei Yi Ta Hsi, who had once torn a live buffalo apart. He could kill a person with just a flick of his finger, and the number of people he killed reached a million; their bodies were cast into the lakes and valleys <note>original: mountains</note>. Yet, despite all this, Jie could not escape the punishment of the ghosts and spirits. This is why I say that even wealth and large numbers, daring and strength, strong armor and sharp weapons cannot withstand the punishment of ghosts and spirits.

This was not an isolated incident. In ancient times, King Zhou <note>original: Chow</note> of the Yin <note>also known as the Shang dynasty</note> was also an emperor of great honor and possessed the wealth of the entire empire. He cursed Heaven and blasphemed against the...

<page-num>173</page-num>

# ON GHOSTS (III)

In the dispensing of punishments by ghosts and spirits, no man is too great to be punished.

Those who deny the existence of spirits argue: "If a person does not work for the benefit of their parents, but instead works toward their ruin, could such a person still be considered a filial son?" <note>The skeptics are likely arguing that focusing on spirits instead of living parents is a distraction from true filial piety.</note>

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> explained: Throughout history, ghosts and spirits can be divided into three categories: the spirits of Heaven, the spirits of the hills and rivers, and the ghosts of deceased men. It is true that some sons die before their fathers, and younger brothers before their older brothers. However, as the common saying goes: "He who is born first, dies first." Therefore, those who die first would usually be the mother if not the father, or the older sister if not the older brother.

In any case, we should reverently prepare clean cakes and wine for sacrifice. If ghosts and spirits do exist, then the sacrifice serves to provide food and drink to one’s deceased father, mother, older sisters, and older brothers. Is this not a great blessing? Even if ghosts and spirits did not exist, it might seem like a waste of materials to provide cakes and wine. However, this wealth is not simply being thrown into a ditch or a gully. The relatives of the clan and friends from the village and district can still eat and drink these offerings. Thus, even if there were truly no ghosts or spirits, a sacrifice still serves as a social gathering where participants can enjoy themselves and build friendships with their neighbors.

Those who maintain that there are no ghosts say: "Naturally, there are no ghosts or spirits; therefore, I should not spend my wealth on sacrificial cakes, wine, or animals <note>original: victims</note>. It is not that I am stingy with my wealth regarding these offerings, but rather that I do not see what I can accomplish with them." This view is opposed—

<summary>Mozi defends the practice of sacrifice by arguing that it either honors deceased ancestors or serves as a valuable social ritual for the living, countering skeptics who view it as a waste of resources.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, ghosts, spirits, sacrifice, filial piety, social cohesion, ritual, ancestors</keywords>

--- Page 188 ---
<meta>This page concludes Mozi’s defense of belief in ghosts and spirits, arguing that even if they did not exist, the communal act of sacrifice remains socially beneficial.</meta>

<page-num>174</page-num>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI</header>

...contrary to the records of the sage-kings of old and opposed to the practices of filial sons <note>Children who demonstrate deep respect and care for their parents and ancestors.</note> among the common people. Yet, these skeptics claim to be "superior men" <note>original: "superior men" (Junzi), a term for the ethical and social elite.</note> throughout the empire. This is certainly no way to be a superior man. 

But Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: "For me, offering a sacrifice is not like throwing resources into a ditch or a gully. Instead, it serves to honor the spirits above and provides an opportunity to gather for a feast, enjoy ourselves, and build friendships with our neighbors below. Furthermore, if spirits truly exist, I am serving my deceased father, mother, and brothers with food. Is this not a great blessing for the world?"

Therefore, Mozi said: "If the rulers and the gentlemen of the world truly desire to bring benefits to the world and eliminate its disasters, they must believe in and teach the existence of ghosts and spirits. This is the way of the sage-kings."

<summary>Mozi concludes his argument by asserting that belief in spirits is both historically traditional and socially beneficial, fostering community cohesion even if the spirits themselves were not real. He urges leaders to promote this belief to bring order and prosperity to the empire.</summary>

# CHAPTER XXXII

## CONDEMNATION OF MUSIC (I)

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: The policy of the benevolent <note>original: magnanimous</note> person seeks to promote what brings benefits to the world and eliminate what causes harm. If any practice, when established as a law, is beneficial to the people, it will be carried out; if it is not beneficial, it will not be done. Moreover, those who are benevolent in their concern for the world do not focus on doing things that merely delight the eyes, please the ears, satisfy the sense of taste, or provide comfort to the body. When these luxuries deprive the people of their means of obtaining food and clothing, the benevolent person would not pursue them. 

Therefore, the reason why Mozi condemns music is not because the sounds of the great bell, the sounding drum, the *qin* <note>original: ch'in; a Chinese horizontal psaltery of five to seven strings</note> and the *se* <note>original: she; a horizontal psaltery of twenty-five strings</note>, and the *yu* <note>original: yü; a large hand organ of thirty-six reed pipes</note> and the *sheng* <note>a small hand organ of seventeen bamboo pipes</note> are not pleasant. It is not because the carvings and ornaments are not delightful, or because the fried and roasted meats of grain-fed and grass-fed animals are not delicious. Nor is it because high towers, grand pavilions, and quiet villas are not comfortable. 

Although the body knows they are comfortable, the mouth knows they are delicious, the eyes know they are beautiful, and the ears know they are pleasing, these things are found to be inconsistent with the deeds of the sage-kings <note>The legendary ideal rulers of ancient China, such as Yao and Shun, whom Mozi holds as moral benchmarks.</note> of antiquity and do not contribute to the benefit of the people today. And so, Mozi proclaims: To promote music is wrong.

<summary>Mozi introduces his argument against music, stating that while it is aesthetically pleasing, it is a wasteful expenditure that diverts resources away from the basic needs of the people and contradicts the examples of the ancient sage-kings.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, Condemnation of Music, benevolence, utility, sage-kings, qin, se, yu, sheng, social welfare</keywords>

--- Page 190 ---
<meta>This page continues Mozi’s critique of music, specifically focusing on the economic burden placed on the common people by the state's production of expensive musical instruments.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>176</page-num>
->THE WORKS OF MOZI <note>original: MOTSE</note><-

Currently, rulers treat the production of musical instruments as a major undertaking of the state. These items cannot be produced as easily as evaporating water to get salt or digging into the earth for clay. Inevitably, heavy taxes must be collected from the people to produce the sounds of the big bell, the sounding drum, the *qin* <note>original: ch'in; a seven-stringed zither</note> and the *se* <note>original: she; a twenty-five-stringed zither</note>, and the *yu* <note>original: yü; a large reed pipe organ</note> and the *sheng* <note>a smaller reed pipe organ</note>. 

The ancient sage-kings <note>The idealized legendary rulers of China's past</note> did, indeed, collect heavy taxes from the people to build boats and vehicles. But when these were completed and the people asked, "What use do we have for these?" the answer was: "Boats are to be used on the water and vehicles on land, so that the gentlemen can rest their feet and the laborers can rest their shoulders and backs." Thus, the people contributed their money and did not dare to grumble about it. This was because the boats and vehicles contributed to the actual benefit of the people. 

If musical instruments also contributed to the benefit of the people, even I would not dare to condemn them. If musical instruments were as useful as the boats and carts of the sage-kings, I would not condemn them.

There are three things that the people worry about:
1. That the hungry cannot be fed.
2. That the cold cannot be clothed.
3. That the tired cannot get rest.

These three are the great worries of the people. Now, suppose we strike the big bell, beat the sounding drum, play the *qin* and the *se*, and blow the *yu* and the *sheng*—can the materials for food and clothing then be procured for the people? Even I do not think this is possible. 

Furthermore, every large state now attacks small states, and every large house molests small houses. The strong plunder the weak, the many oppress the few, the clever deceive the stupid, and the honored disdain the humble.

<summary>Mozi argues that state-funded music is a wasteful use of tax money because, unlike boats and carts, musical instruments do not solve the people's primary needs for food, clothing, and rest.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, utility, taxation, musical instruments, sage-kings, social welfare, qin, se, yu, sheng</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Mozi’s argument against the state’s obsession with music, contrasting the uselessness of musical instruments with the social chaos and economic hardship of the people.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>177</page-num>

### CONDEMNATION OF MUSIC (I)

And bandits and thieves rise up all together and cannot be suppressed. But can the chaos in the world be put in order by striking a large bell, beating a sounding drum, playing the *qin* and the *se* <note>original: ch'in and she; types of zithers</note>, and blowing the *yu* and the *sheng* <note>original: yü and sheng; reed pipe organs</note>? Even I do not think it is possible. Therefore Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: Levying heavy taxes on the people to construct the large bell, the sounding drum, the *qin*, the *se*, the *yu*, and the *sheng* is not at all helpful in the effort to secure benefits for the world and eliminate its calamities. Therefore Mozi said: To have music is wrong.

When the rulers look down from a high tower or a grand pavilion, a bell looks just like an inverted *ding*. <note>original: ting; a tripod-shaped sacrificial vessel</note> If it is not struck, where would the pleasure come from? Therefore, it must be struck. To strike it, of course, the elderly and the young would not be employed. Their eyes and ears are not sharp, their arms are not strong, and they cannot produce a harmonious tone with varied expression. So, those in the prime of life must be employed because their eyes and ears are sharp, their limbs are strong, and their voices are harmonious and expressive. If men are employed in this way, it interferes with their plowing and planting. If women are employed, it interferes with their weaving and spinning. Rulers today indulge in music and deprive the people of their means of clothing and food to such an extent! Therefore Mozi said: To have music is wrong.

Now, even when the large bell, the sounding drum, the *qin*, the *se*, the *yu*, and the *sheng* are provided, it is still no pleasure for the lords to listen to the playing alone. Therefore, they must enjoy it with either the common people or...

<summary>Mozi argues that music is a wasteful indulgence that fails to solve social disorder, drains public resources through heavy taxation, and diverts the most productive workers from essential tasks like farming and weaving.</summary>

--- Page 192 ---
<meta>This page continues Mozi's argument against the indulgence in music by the ruling class. He emphasizes that the performance and enjoyment of music distract both the governing elite and the working class from their essential duties, leading to a lack of resources and social instability.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>178</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...the gentlemen. If the music is performed with the gentlemen, it will interfere with their attendance to government. If it is performed with the common people, it will interfere with their work. Now the rulers indulge in music and deprive the people of so many of their opportunities to produce food and clothing! Therefore Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: It is wrong to have music.

Formerly, Lord Kang of Qi <note>original: K’ang of Ch’i; ruler of the State of Qi from 404–379 B.C.</note> loved music and dancing. The dancers were not permitted to wear garments of coarse cloth or to eat husks and bran <note>coarse, low-quality remains of milled grain</note>. For if their food and drink are not refined, their appearance and complexion will not be pleasing. And if their clothing is not elegant, their bodies and movements will not be delightful. Therefore, their food had to consist of fine grain and meat, and their clothing had to be made of silk and embroidery. They did not produce any materials for clothing or food themselves but lived off the labor of others at all times. Hence Mozi said: Now the lords take to music and deprive the people of so many of their opportunities to produce food and clothing! Therefore Mozi proclaimed: To have music is wrong.

Also, humans are different from birds, beasts, and insects. The birds, beasts, and insects have their feathers and fur for coats, their hooves and claws for sandals and shoes, and water and grass for drink and food. Therefore, the males do not sow seeds or plant trees, and the females do not weave or spin, yet their food and clothing are provided for them. Now, human beings are different from these. Those who exert themselves will live. Those who do not exert themselves cannot live. When the gentlemen do not attend to government diligently, the administration of justice <note>original: jurisdiction</note> will fall into chaos. When the common men do not attend to their work, the supply of basic needs will not be sufficient.

<summary>Mozi argues that music is a wasteful distraction that prevents rulers from governing and commoners from producing food and clothing. He contrasts the necessity of human labor for survival with the natural provisions given to animals.</summary>

<meta>This page continues Mozi’s argument that music is a wasteful distraction. Having previously argued that music provides no practical benefit for the people's needs, he now illustrates how it actively interferes with the specific duties of every social class, from the high-ranking rulers to the laboring farmers and weavers.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>179</page-num>
<header>CONDEMNATION OF MUSIC (I)</header>

If the leaders of the world doubt my words, let us list the various responsibilities in the world and see the harm that music causes them. 

It is the duty of rulers to arrive at court early and stay late to hear legal cases and manage the government. It is the duty of the officials <note>original: "gentlemen" (shi); the educated class of administrators and ministers</note> to exert their physical energy and use their full wisdom to manage the inner court and collect taxes from customs checkpoints <note>original: "passes"; mountain passes or border gates where duties were collected</note>, markets, and resources from the mountains, woods, waters, and fields to fill the granaries and the treasury. It is the duty of farmers to set out early and return late to sow seeds and plant trees, producing large quantities of grain <note>original: "soy beans and millet"; these are used here to represent grains and staple crops in general</note>. It is the duty of women to rise at dawn and retire at night to weave and spin in order to produce plenty of silk, linen, and cloth. 

Now, if rulers love and listen to music, they will not be able to arrive at court early or stay late to hear lawsuits and manage the government. As a result, the country will fall into chaos and the state will be in danger. 

If the officials love and listen to music, they will not be able to exert their physical energy or use their wisdom to manage the court and collect taxes from the checkpoints, markets, and natural resources to fill the granaries and the treasury. Consequently, the granaries and the treasury will not be filled. 

If the farmers love and listen to music, they will not be able to set out early and return late to sow seeds, plant trees, and produce a large quantity of grain. Consequently, the grain will...

<summary>Mozi argues that music is a destructive distraction because it prevents rulers, officials, farmers, and weavers from performing the essential labors required to keep a state stable and prosperous.</summary>

--- Page 194 ---
<meta>This page continues the argument that music distracts from essential labor, focusing on how it prevents women from producing textiles and why ancient texts condemned it as a "sorcerer's fashion" that leads to the downfall of kingdoms.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>180</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...not be sufficient. If women love and listen to music, they will not be able to rise at dawn and go to bed late to weave, spin, and produce large quantities of silk, flax linen, and cloth. Consequently, there will not be enough cloth and linen. If you ask what it is that interferes with rulers attending to the government and common people attending to their work, the answer must be: music. Therefore, Motse said: To indulge in music is wrong.

How do we know this is true? It is found in the "Code of Punishment of Tang" <note>original: "T’ang"; referring to the legendary founder of the Shang dynasty</note> among the books of the ancient kings. This text proclaims: "Constant dancing in the palace is called the 'sorcerer’s pleasure' <note>original: "witch's pleasure"</note>." ¹ As for the punishment: a high-ranking official <note>original: "gentleman" (shi)</note> will be fined six hundred and forty ² pieces of silk, while a common man will be allowed to go free. It also says: "Ah! How much dancing there is! His words are well known. God <note>original: "Shangdi"; the text uses "God" to translate this Chinese concept of a supreme deity</note> does not bless him; therefore, the nine provinces <note>original: "districts"</note> ³ are lost to him. God does not favor him; therefore, He sent various curses upon him. His family must also be destroyed." ⁴ The reason the nine provinces were lost to King Jie of Xia <note>original: "Chieh of Hsia"; the last ruler of the Xia dynasty, traditionally portrayed as a tyrant</note> was his obsession with elaborate music.

---

¹ There is no book titled "Code of Punishment of Tang" in the *Book of Documents* <note>original: *Shu Ching*</note>. A passage similar to this quote is found in the "Instructions of Yi" <note>original: "Instructions of E"</note>. According to James Legge <note>a famous 19th-century translator of Chinese classics</note>, volume 3, page 196, it reads: "He established punishments for officials and warned those in authority, saying: 'If you dare to have constant dancing in your palaces and drunken singing in your chambers, that is called the sorcerer's fashion.'"
² The accuracy of this number is uncertain.
³ At that time, the Empire was divided into nine administrative regions or provinces.
⁴ Compare Legge, volume 3, page 198, "Instructions of Yi" in the *Book of Documents*. The two versions differ significantly in both wording and meaning. Scholars generally believe that the version in *Motse* is more likely to be the original text of the *Book of Documents*.

<page-num>181</page-num>
<header>CONDEMNATION OF MUSIC (I)</header>

Quoting the *Wu Kuan*¹ <note>an ancient Chinese text that is no longer extant</note>: "Qi <note>original: "Ch'i"</note>² then abandoned himself to lust and music. He drank and ate in inappropriate places. *Clang, clang, boom, boom* <note>original: "Ding ding, dong dong"</note> went the woodwinds and percussion instruments in harmony. He indulged in drinking and ate in inappropriate places. The dancing continued with great brilliance. It reached the hearing of Heaven, and Heaven was not pleased." Thus, it was not pleasing to Heaven above and not beneficial to the people below.

Therefore, Motse <note>also known as Mozi, the philosopher and founder of Mohism</note> said: If the leaders <note>original: "gentlemen"</note> really desire to bring benefits to the world and eliminate its disasters, they have no choice but to prohibit such a thing as music.

---

¹ The *Wu Kuan* is a book that no longer exists.
² Qi <note>original: "Ch'i"</note> was the son of Yu <note>original: "Yü"</note> and the second emperor of the Xia <note>original: "Hsia"</note> Dynasty.

<vocab>Wu Kuan, Qi, Motse, music, Heaven, Yu, Xia Dynasty</vocab>

Motse concludes that leaders must prohibit music to ensure the world’s benefit and avoid disaster.</summary>

# BOOK IX

## CHAPTER XXXV

### ANTI-FATALISM (I)

Motse <note>also known as Mozi, the 5th-century BCE Chinese philosopher</note> said: At present, when governing their states, all rulers desire to have wealthy countries, large populations, and orderly administrations. But instead of wealth, they find poverty; instead of an increase in population, they see a decrease; and instead of order, they find chaos. In other words, they lose what they like and get what they dislike. What is the reason for this? Motse said: It is due to the large number of fatalists <note>those who believe that all events are predetermined and inevitable</note> among the people.

The fatalists say: “When fate decrees that a man shall be wealthy, he will be wealthy. When it decrees poverty, he will be poor. When it decrees a large population, the population will be large; and when it decrees a small population, it will be small. If order is decreed, there will be order; if chaos, there will be chaos. If fate decrees old age, there will be old age; if an untimely death, there will be an untimely death. Even if a man sets himself against his fate, what is the use?” By preaching this doctrine, rulers in high positions are encouraged to be lazy, and the common people below are kept away from their work. Therefore, the fatalists are unmagnanimous <note>original: "unmagnanimous"; here meaning lacking in benevolence or social responsibility</note>. Their doctrines must be clearly examined.

Now, how is this doctrine to be examined? Motse said: Some standard of judgment <note>Mozi refers to this as the "Three Tests" or "Three Criteria" for determining the truth of a theory</note> must be established. To expound a doctrine without regard to a standard is like trying to determine the directions of sunrise and sunset on a revolving... <note>The text likely refers to a revolving potter's wheel, suggesting that without a fixed point of reference, judgment is impossible.</note>

<vocab>Motse, anti-fatalism, fate, rulers, population, standard of judgment, unmagnanimous</vocab>

<summary>Motse argues that belief in fatalism is the root cause of poverty and social disorder because it discourages both rulers and subjects from working hard. He proposes that all doctrines must be tested against a objective "standard of judgment" to prove their validity.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, Mozi, fatalism, determinism, governance, economic prosperity, social order, ethics, Three Tests of Mozi</keywords>

### ANTI-FATALISM (I)

...established. For a doctrine without a standard is like trying to determine the direction of sunrise and sunset on a moving potter’s wheel. By such means, the distinction between right and wrong, or benefit and harm, cannot be known. Therefore, there must be three tests. What are these three tests? Motse said: its basis, its verifiability, and its applicability. 

1. **How is it to be based?** It should be based on the deeds of the ancient sage-kings. 
2. **How is it to be verified?** It is to be verified by the actual senses of hearing and sight of the common people. 
3. **How is it to be applied?** It is to be applied by adopting it in government and observing whether it benefits the country and the people. 

This is what is meant by the three tests of every doctrine.

Some of the "gentlemen of the world" <note>the intellectual elite or rival scholars of the time</note> assume that fate exists. Now let us examine the deeds of the sage-kings. In ancient times, the confusion produced by Chieh <note>the tyrannical last ruler of the Xia dynasty</note> was replaced by the orderly government of T’ang <note>the virtuous founder of the Shang dynasty</note>. Likewise, the chaos caused by Chow <note>King Zhou, the last ruler of the Shang dynasty, famous for his cruelty</note> was turned into order by King Wu <note>founder of the Zhou dynasty</note>. The times did not alter and the people did not change, yet under Chieh and Chow the world was chaotic, while under T’ang and Wu it was orderly. Can it then be said that there is such a thing as fate?

But these gentlemen of the world still assume that fate exists. Now let us look at some of the writings of the early kings. The writings of the early kings that were issued to the whole country and distributed among the people were the laws <note>original character: 憲 (xiàn), meaning laws or constitutions</note>. Did any of the laws of the early kings ever say: "Blessing cannot be invoked and disaster cannot be avoided; reverence will do no good and cruelty will do no harm"? 

Furthermore, the standards according to which lawsuits were tried and punishments were meted out were the codes of punishment <note>original character: 刑 (xíng), meaning penal codes or criminal law</note>. Did any of the codes of punishment of the early kings say: "Blessing cannot be...

# THE WORKS OF MOTSE

"...can blessings be invoked and disaster avoided? Will reverence do any good, and will cruelty do any harm?" The inspiration used to organize armies and command soldiers to advance or retreat came from the declarations <note>original: 誓 (shì); these were solemn oaths or proclamations made by rulers before military campaigns</note>. Did any of the declarations of the early kings ever say: "Blessing cannot be invoked and disaster cannot be avoided; reverence will do no good and cruelty will do no harm"? 

Motse said: I have not listed all the good books in the world completely. Since they cannot all be listed, I limit myself to the most prominent ones—namely, the three <note>The original text says "five" (五), but this is likely a copyist's error for "three" (三). These three refer to the laws, the codes of punishment, and the royal declarations mentioned previously</note> types of records mentioned above. No matter how hard we try, we cannot find any belief in the doctrine of fatalism in them. Therefore, shouldn't this doctrine be abandoned?

To adopt the doctrine of the fatalists is to overthrow righteousness in the world. Overthrowing righteousness establishes the idea of "fate," which acts as a temptation to the people to be negligent. To offer people such a temptation is to destroy them. 

Now, why is it that we desire righteousness to be found in our leaders? It is because when the righteous are in authority, the world will have order. God <note>original: Shangdi, the High God</note>, the spirits of the hills and rivers, and the ancestral spirits will have their chief sacrificer to honor them, and the people will receive great blessings as a result. How do we know this? 

Motse said: In ancient times, T’ang <note>the virtuous founder of the Shang dynasty</note> was given a small territory at Po. Even making allowance for irregular boundary lines, his land only amounted to about a hundred li <term>li: a traditional Chinese unit of distance, roughly 1/3 of a mile or 500 meters</term> square. He worked with the people based on universal love and mutual benefit, and he shared his abundance with them. And he led his people to...

<summary>Motse argues that historical military declarations and royal records never mention fate, but instead emphasize personal responsibility. He concludes that fatalism destroys social righteousness and contrasts it with the virtuous, prosperous rule of King T’ang.</summary>

# ANTI-FATALISM (I)

...respected Heaven and worshipped the spirits. Because of this, Heaven and the spirits blessed him with wealth, the feudal lords became his friends, the people loved him, and virtuous men flocked to him. Within a single generation, he ruled over the entire empire and led the feudal lords.

Again, in ancient times, King Wen <note>The founder of the Zhou Dynasty, celebrated by Motse as a model of virtuous leadership</note> was appointed to the state of Qizhou <note>original: Ch'i Chou</note>. Even allowing for the irregular boundary lines, his land amounted to only about a hundred square *li* <note>original: li; a traditional Chinese unit of distance, roughly one-third of a mile or 500 meters</note>. He worked with his people toward universal love and mutual benefit. Consequently, those near him enjoyed his government, and those far away yielded to his moral influence. All who heard of King Wen rose up and hurried to join him. Even the simple-minded, the arrogant, and those with physical disabilities stayed where they were and lamented: "Why does King Wen's land not reach this place? Would I not then also be a subject of King Wen?" 

Because of this, Heaven and the spirits enriched him, the feudal lords became his friends, the people loved him, and the virtuous came to him. Within a single generation, he ruled over the entire empire and led the feudal lords. As we have said: when the righteous are in authority, the world will have order. The High God <note>original: Shangdi</note>, the spirits of the hills and rivers, and the ancestral spirits will have their chief sacrificer <note>The ruler’s primary duty was to perform rituals to maintain harmony between the human and spirit realms</note>, and the people will receive great benefits as a result. This is how we know it to be true.

The ancient sage-kings promulgated laws and issued orders to serve as standards for reward and punishment, both to encourage the virtuous and to hinder the wicked. As a result, the people were devoted to their parents at home and respectful to the elders in their villages and districts. They observed proper etiquette in their behavior, practiced moderation in their public and private lives, and maintained decency...

<summary>Motse describes how King Wen and other sage-kings achieved imperial power through virtuous governance and "universal love" rather than fate. He argues that their success proves that rewards and social order come from human effort and righteous leadership.</summary>

...between men and women. When they were assigned to look after the court, they did not steal; when they were assigned to defend a city, they did not stage an insurrection. If their lord met with death, they would remain loyal even unto death <note>original: commit suicide; in this context, it refers to the ultimate act of devotion to a leader</note>, and if the lord was banished, they would follow him into exile. These are the behaviors that a ruler rewards and the people applaud. 

Now, the fatalists say: "Whoever is rewarded by the ruler is simply destined to be rewarded. It is not because of his virtue that he is rewarded." Under these conditions, the people would not show filial piety <note>A core Chinese virtue involving respect and care for one's parents and ancestors</note> to their parents at home, nor would they be respectful to the elders in their village or district. They would not observe proper conduct <note>original: propriety</note>, practice moderation in their daily lives, or maintain decency between men and women. 

Furthermore, if they were assigned to look after the court, they would steal; if they were assigned to defend a city, they would stage an insurrection. If their lord met with death, they would not remain loyal, and if the lord were banished, they would not accompany him. These are the actions that a ruler will punish and the people will condemn. 

Yet the fatalists say: "Whoever is punished by the ruler is simply destined to be punished. It is not because of his vice that he is punished." By believing in this, the ruler would not be righteous, the minister would not be loyal, the father would not be affectionate, the son would not be filial, the elder brother would not be kind, and the younger brother would not be respectful. This unnatural devotion to the doctrine of fate is responsible for harmful ideas and is the path of the wicked.

Now, how do we know that fatalism is the way of the wicked? In ancient times, people who lived in misery indulged in excess food and drink and were lazy in their work. Consequently, their...

...food and clothing became scarce, and the danger of hunger and cold drew near. They did not admit, "I was foolish and irresponsible and did not work hard."
