They failed to follow the examples of their ancestors, and so they lost their countries and ruined their states. They did not realize they should have confessed, "I am foolish and irresponsible and was not diligent in governing." Instead, they would say, "It is merely my destiny to lose it." 

The "Announcement of Chung Hui" <note>Chung Hui was a minister to King T'ang, the founder of the Shang Dynasty.</note> says: "I have heard that the ruler of the Hsia dynasty issued orders while pretending they were the fate ordained by Heaven. "The Great Declaration" says: "King Chow became arrogant and refused to worship God. He cast aside his ancestors and the spirits, failing to offer them sacrifices. He said, 'Good fortune is with my people,' and so he neglected and betrayed his duty. Heaven therefore abandoned him and withdrew its protection." [2] This describes how King Wu proved that King Chow's <note>Chow, or King Zhou, was the last ruler of the Shang Dynasty, known for his cruelty.</note> belief in fate was wrong.

If the doctrine of the fatalist were put into practice, those in high positions would not attend to the government, and those in lower positions would not attend to their work. If the rulers do not attend to the government, the legal system and administration will fall into chaos. If the common people do not attend to their work, wealth will not be sufficient. Then, there will not be the means...

It is a great disaster <note>original: calamity</note> for the world.

# CHAPTER XXXVI

## ANTI-FATALISM (II)

Motse <note>Mozi (c. 470–391 BC), the philosopher who founded Mohism, which emphasized universal love and practical logic.</note> said: To make any statement or to put forward any doctrine, one must first establish a standard of judgment. To debate without such a standard is like trying to determine the directions of sunrise and sunset on a spinning potter's wheel. Even the most skillful artisans could not get accurate results that way. Since it is difficult to distinguish between truth and error in the world, we must apply three tests. What are these three tests? They are the test of its **basis**, the test of its **verifiability**, and the test of its **applicability**.

To test the **basis** of a doctrine, we examine it against the will of Heaven and the spirits, and the historical deeds of the ancient sage-kings <note>The legendary virtuous rulers of China's past, such as Yao, Shun, and Yu, whom Mozi viewed as moral exemplars.</note>. To test its **verifiability**, we consult the historical records <note>original: "books"</note> of these early kings. As for its **applicability**, it is tested by observing its practical use in the administration of justice and the functioning of government. These, then, are the three tests of any doctrine.

Among the scholars <note>original: "gentlemen"</note> of today, some believe in fate, while others do not. My ability to judge whether fate exists depends on the sensory evidence <note>original: "sense testimony"</note> of the masses. If people have actually heard it or seen it, I will say fate exists. If no one has heard it and no one has seen it, I shall say fate does not exist. Why don't we inquire into the sensory evidence of the people? From ancient times to the present, since the beginning of the human race, has anyone ever seen such a thing as "fate," or heard the sound of "fate"? Of course, no one has. If the common people are considered too uneducated <note>original: "stupid"</note> for their hearing and sight to be reliable, then why not inquire into the records of the elite?

<summary>Motse establishes a logical framework called the "three tests" to evaluate the truth of any idea, arguing that fate cannot be proven because there is no empirical evidence of its existence.</summary>
<keywords>Motse, Mozi, logic, Three Tests, fatalism, empiricism, sage-kings, governance, Chinese philosophy</keywords>

--- Page 204 ---
<meta>This page continues Motse’s argument against fatalism. Having established that there is no sensory evidence (seeing or hearing) for fate among the common people, he now applies his "three tests" to the historical records of rulers and the practical outcomes of governance.</meta>

<page-num>190</page-num>

...the recorded statements of the feudal lords? But from antiquity to the present, since the beginning of humanity, has anyone ever heard the sound of fate or seen such a thing as fate? Of course, no one has. 

Again, why don't we inquire into the deeds of the sage-kings? The ancient kings promoted filial sons and encouraged them to continue serving their parents; they respected the virtuous and the gentle, encouraging them to continue doing good. They issued their orders to instruct the people and ensured that rewards and punishments were fair to encourage the good and obstruct the evil. In this way, confusion could be reduced to order, and danger could be converted to peace. 

If anyone doubts this, let us remember: in ancient times, the confusion caused by Chieh <note>Jie (c. 1728–1675 BC), the legendary last ruler of the Xia dynasty, traditionally portrayed as a tyrant.</note> was restored to order by T’ang <note>Tang of Shang, who overthrew Jie to found the Shang Dynasty.</note>, and the chaos of Chow <note>King Zhou (or Di Xin), the last ruler of the Shang Dynasty, also known for his cruelty.</note> was restored by King Wu <note>The first ruler of the Zhou Dynasty.</note>. Now, the times did not change and the people did not alter. Yet, when the ruler changed the regime, the subjects modified their conduct. Under T’ang and Wu, the world was orderly, but under Chieh and Chow, it was disorderly. Therefore, whether there is peace or danger, order or disorder, depends entirely on the government of the ruler <note>original: "the superior"</note>. How can it be said that everything happens according to fate? Thus, assertions that fate exists are completely false.

The fatalists tell us: "This doctrine was not invented by us in these later generations. Such a doctrine has existed and been handed down since the Three Dynasties <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties (c. 2070–256 BC).</note>. Why do you, sir, now oppose it?" 

In answer, Motse asked: Did this fatalistic doctrine come from the sages and good men of the Three Dynasties, or from the wicked and the vicious men of those times? How can we find this out? In the beginning, the secretaries and ministers were careful in...

# ANTI-FATALISM (II)

...well-spoken and intelligent in their conduct. They were able to persuade their rulers above and instruct the people below. Thus, they received rewards from their rulers and praise from the people. The fame of those secretaries and ministers has lasted to the present day. The whole world remarks:

> "This is the result of effort <note>original: "endeavour"</note>."

And they will never say: <note>The first part of the following sentence seems to be missing in the original text.</note>

> "I see fate at work there."

On the other hand, the wicked kings of the Three Dynasties <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties, whose final rulers are traditionally cited as examples of tyranny and moral decay.</note> did not control the lust of their ears and eyes and did not restrain the passions of their hearts. When they went out, they indulged in racing, hunting, and trapping. When they stayed indoors, they reveled in wine and music. They neglected the government of the country and the people; instead, they spent their time on many things that were useless. They oppressed the people, causing their subjects to lose affection for their superiors. As a result, the country became depleted and lacked a future, and the rulers themselves faced punishment and disaster. But they would not confess and say:

> "I am foolish, arrogant <note>original: "insolent"</note>, and poor at administering the government."

Instead, they would say:

> "It is simply my fate to perish."

Even the common people who suffered during the Three Dynasties acted this way. In private <note>original: "Within"; referring to the home or family life.</note>, they could not serve their parents well; in public <note>original: "without"; referring to civil or social duty.</note>, they could not serve their ruler well. They disliked courtesy and thrift <note>original: "frugality"</note> but preferred lack of restraint <note>original: "licence"</note> and ease. They overindulged in food and drink and were lazy. Their supply of food and clothing became insufficient, and they placed themselves in danger of hunger and cold. They would not confess:

> "I am foolish and arrogant, and I was not hardworking <note>original: "diligent"</note>..."

<summary>Motse argues that success is the result of personal effort while failure—whether by kings or commoners—is blamed on 'fate' to avoid personal responsibility.</summary>

at work." But they would say, "It is simply my fate to be poor." Such, then, were also the miserable people of the Three Dynasties <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties.</note>.

Fatalism has been disguised <note>original: "glossed over"</note> and taught to the ignorant <note>original: "stupid"</note> people. This was a great concern to the sage-kings, and they recorded their opposition to it on bamboo and silk, and engraved it in metal and stone. Among the books of the early kings, "The Announcement of Chung Hui" says:

This shows how King Jie of Xia believed in fate and how both T'ang <note>The founder of the Shang dynasty.</note> and Chung Hui thought he was wrong. Among the books of early kings, "The Great Declaration" says:

He cast aside his ancestors and spirits without offering them sacrifices. He said, 'Fortune is with my people,' and he neglected and betrayed his duty. Heaven then deserted him and withdrew its protection."<sup>2</sup> 

This shows how Chow believed in fate and how King Wu <note>The founder of the Zhou dynasty who overthrew King Zhou.</note> proclaimed it to be wrong in "The Great Declaration." 

Again, "The Three Dynasties and Hundred States"<sup>3</sup> says: "Do not place too much faith in the fate of Heaven." So, "The Three Dynasties and Hundred States" also says there is no fate. Similarly, "Shao Kung"<sup>3</sup> discredits the belief in fate. It says: 

> "Assuredly there is no fate in Heaven. Let us not teach false doctrines. One's destiny does..."

<sup>1</sup> See page 187, and note 1 on that same page.

<sup>3</sup> Both "The Three Dynasties and Hundred States" and "Shao Kung" appear to be essays from the *Shu Ching* <note>The *Classic of History*, one of the Five Classics of Chinese literature.</note> that are now lost.

<summary>Motse cites historical texts and the actions of ancient sage-kings to prove that fatalism is a false doctrine used by corrupt rulers like King Jie and King Zhou to justify their failures. He argues that the concept of "fate" was officially condemned in early Chinese records such as the Shu Ching.</summary>

<header>ANTI-FATALISM (II)</header>

"...does not come from Heaven, but is shaped by one's own self." It is also stated in the *Classic of Poetry* <note>original: "the odes"</note> and the historical records of the Shang and Xia <note>original: "Hsia"</note> dynasties: "The concept of fate originated with wicked kings."

Therefore, if the leaders <note>original: "gentlemen"; referring to those in positions of social and political responsibility</note> of the world truly wish to distinguish between right and wrong, or benefit and harm, the idea of "Heavenly fate" must be vigorously rejected. To believe in fate is the greatest disaster for the world. For this reason, Motse <note>The philosopher Mozi, who lived during the 5th century BCE and founded Mohism.</note> argued against it.

<meta>This page begins the third part of Motse's argument against fatalism. It introduces his famous "Three Tests" (also known as the Three Gnomons or Three Criteria) for evaluating any doctrine: its historical basis, its verifiability by common experience, and its practical utility in government.</meta>

# CHAPTER XXXVII

## ANTI-FATALISM (III)

Motse <note>The philosopher Mozi (c. 470–391 BCE), founder of Mohism.</note> said: In order to explain a doctrine, one must establish a standard of judgment. To explain a doctrine without a standard is like trying to determine the directions of sunrise and sunset on a spinning potter’s wheel. I believe that even obvious distinctions, such as the directions of sunrise and sunset, cannot be determined that way. Therefore, every doctrine must pass three tests. 

What are these three tests? They are the test of its basis, the test of its verifiability, and the test of its applicability. 

1.  **How is it to be based?** It must be based on the deeds of the early sage-kings <note>The legendary righteous rulers of Chinese antiquity.</note>. 
2.  **How is it to be verified?** It must be verified by the testimony of the ears and eyes of the common people. 
3.  **How is it to be applied?** It is to be applied by being adopted into the government and observing its effects on the people. 

These are what we call the "Three Tests."

When the ancient sage-kings of the Three Dynasties—Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu <note>Yu founded the Xia dynasty; Tang founded the Shang; Wen and Wu founded the Zhou.</note>—ruled, they said: “We must promote filial sons and encourage them to serve their parents; we must honor virtuous and good men and instruct them in doing good.” In this way, they administered the government and issued instructions, rewarded the good and punished the evil. It appeared that, in this way, the confusion in the world could be brought to order, and the dangers facing the state could be transformed into safety. 

If this is doubted, let us remember: In ancient times, the disorder caused by Jie <note>original: "Chieh"; the last, tyrannical ruler of the Xia dynasty</note> was restored to order by Tang, and the disorder of Zhou <note>original: "Chow"; the last, tyrannical ruler of the Shang dynasty</note> was reduced to...

<summary>Motse introduces his "Three Tests" for validating any belief: its historical foundation, its evidence from the senses of the people, and its practical benefit to the state. He argues that ancient sage-kings achieved order not through fate, but by rewarding virtue and punishing evil.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Anti-Fatalism, Three Tests, Sage-kings, Three Dynasties, Yu, Tang, Wen, Wu, political philosophy, epistemology</keywords>

--- Page 209 ---
<meta>This page continues Chapter XXXVII, "Anti-Fatalism (III)." It completes the argument that social order is a result of human governance rather than predestined fate, using the historical examples of virtuous sage-kings versus wicked tyrants.</meta>

...were brought into order by King Wu <note>The founder of the Zhou Dynasty, traditionally portrayed as a model of virtue.</note>. During that time, the era did not change, nor did the nature of the people alter. Yet, when the leader changed the government, the subjects modified their behavior. Under the tyrants Chieh <note>The last ruler of the Xia dynasty, synonymous with cruelty.</note> and Chow <note>The last ruler of the Shang dynasty, often paired with Chieh as a symbol of decadence.</note>, the world was chaotic; but under the sage-kings Tang and Wu, it became orderly. 

The fact that the world became orderly was due to the efforts of Tang and Wu. The fact that the world was chaotic was due to the sins of Chieh and Chow. Judging from this, safety and danger, or order and chaos, all depend on how the leader conducts the government. How, then, can it be said that fate exists? 

In ancient times, when Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu ruled the empire, they said: “We must feed the hungry, clothe the cold, give rest to the weary, and bring peace to those who are disturbed.” Thus, their good reputations were heard all over the world. Can this be attributed to fate? No, it was actually due to their efforts. The virtuous and kind people of today respect virtue and pursue the methods and means to benefit the world. Consequently, they are rewarded by the rulers above and praised by the people below, and their good names are heard all over the world. Can this be attributed to fate? This is also due to their own efforts.

Now, were those who believed in fate the sages of the Three Dynasties <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties.</note>, or were they the wicked rulers of those times? Judging by the nature of this doctrine, it could not have been the sages of the Three Dynasties who believed it; it must have been the wicked who believed in fate. The ancient wicked kings of the Three Dynasties—Chieh, Chow, Yu, and Li—were honored as emperors and possessed the wealth of the entire world. Yet they could not control the desires of their ears and eyes, but instead gave free rein to their passions. When they went out, they would race horses, hunt, and trap animals. When they stayed indoors, they reveled in wine and music. They...

<summary>Motse argues that the shift from chaos to order in Chinese history proves that human effort and governance, not fate, determine the fate of a nation. He contrasts the diligent efforts of sage-kings like Tang and Wu with the self-indulgent reigns of tyrants like Chieh and Chow.</summary>

# THE WORKS OF MOTSE

They did not attend to the government of the country and the people, but instead did much that was of no use. They oppressed and violated the people, and as a result, they lost their ancestral temple <note>original: "ancestral temple"; a metaphor for losing the empire or the right to rule, as the temple was the most sacred site a dynasty possessed.</note>. They would not confess: “I am arrogant and stupid. I did not attend to government matters diligently.” Instead, they would say: “It is simply my fate that I lost it.” Even the arrogant people of the Three Dynasties <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties.</note> were like this. They could not serve their parents and their lord well. They greatly hated politeness and frugality, preferring indulgence and ease. They indulged in eating and drinking and were lazy at work. Their means of obtaining clothing and food became insufficient, and they faced the danger of hunger and cold. They would not confess: “I am stupid and arrogant. I am not diligent in my work.” Instead, they also said: “It is simply my fate that I am poor.” Thus, the arrogant people of the Three Dynasties also believed in fate.

The ancient wicked kings originated this belief, and the miserable people practiced it. It shook the convictions of the masses and converted the foolish. This was already a matter of great concern to the ancient sage-kings. They recorded their warnings on bamboo and silk, carved them into metal and stone, and engraved them on dishes and cups to be handed down to their descendants. In what books are these teachings found? “Exalting Virtue” <note>original: "Tsung Teh"; an essay no longer found in the modern version of the *Classic of History* (*Shu Jing*).</note> from the time of Yu says: “When promises are not fulfilled, even a subject of Heaven will not be protected. When one has invited disaster, Heaven will visit him with its curse. When one is not careful about one’s conduct, how can fate...

<summary>Motse argues that belief in fate is a dishonest excuse used by failed rulers and lazy individuals to avoid responsibility for their own poor choices and lack of effort. He cites lost ancient texts to show that the sage-kings warned against such thinking.</summary>

...of Heaven protect him?" "The Announcement of Zhong Hui" <note>original: "Chung Hui"; a famous minister to King Tang, the founder of the Shang dynasty.</note> says: "I have heard that the King of Xia issued orders, falsely claiming they were the decree of Heaven. God was displeased and destroyed his armies."^1 He treated things that did not exist as if they did, and therefore his claims were called a "pretension." If he had been declaring something that truly existed, how could it be called a pretension? In ancient times, Jie <note>The last, tyrannical ruler of the Xia dynasty.</note> believed in fate and acted accordingly. King Tang <note>The virtuous founder of the Shang dynasty who overthrew Jie.</note> showed this to be wrong through "The Announcement of Zhong Hui."

"The Great Declaration" <note>A chapter in the *Classic of History* recording the speeches of King Wu.</note> says: "Therefore the Prince Regent Fa^2 said: 'Ah, my lords, Heaven blesses the virtuous. Its way is clear. We do not need to look far for an example. It is found in the King of Yin <note>King Zhou, the last ruler of the Shang (Yin) dynasty, known for his cruelty.</note>. He claimed that every man has his own fixed fate, that worship should not be practiced, that sacrifices were useless, and that wickedness could do no harm. God withdrew his blessing, and the nine provinces <note>original: "districts"; the traditional divisions of ancient China.</note> are now lost to him. God is not pleased and is bringing him to ruin. This is why our Zhou dynasty has been given the empire by the Great God.' "^3 

This shows that King Zhou believed in fate and acted accordingly, and King Wu refuted him in "The Great Declaration." So, why not examine the records of the Yu, Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties? You will see that all of them held that there is no such thing as fate. How else would you account for these historical facts?

And Motse said: In explaining a doctrine or developing a system, the gentlemen of the world should not do it just to exercise their voices and tongues or to practice moving their lips. It...

<meta>This page continues Mozi’s argument against fatalism by illustrating how different social classes—rulers, officials, farmers, and weavers—rely on their own hard work rather than "fate" to ensure the prosperity and order of society. # THE WORKS OF MOTSE

...must aim at being applied to the government of the country, the district, and the people. Today, rulers go to court early and retire late; they spend the whole day hearing lawsuits, attending to matters of state, and administering justice, not daring to be negligent. Why do they do this? They believe that diligence brings order, while negligence leads to chaos; diligence produces safety, while negligence leads to danger. Therefore, they do not dare to be negligent. 

The ministers and secretaries exhaust their physical energy and stretch their intellectual wisdom—working internally to manage the court and externally to collect taxes from trade routes, markets, and resources from the mountains, forests, ponds, and fields to fill the treasury—and they dare not be negligent. Why do they do this? They believe that diligence earns honor, while negligence brings dishonor; diligence produces glory, while negligence results in disgrace. Therefore, they do not dare to be negligent. 

The farmers head out at daybreak and return at dusk, diligently sowing seeds and planting trees to produce a bounty of soybeans and millet, and they dare not be negligent. Why do they do this? They believe that diligence leads to wealth, while negligence leads to poverty; diligence produces plenty, while negligence results in famine. Therefore, they do not dare to be negligent. 

The women rise at dawn and retire late at night, diligently weaving and spinning to produce much silk, linen <note>original: "flax linen"</note>, and cloth, and they dare not be negligent. Why do they do this? They believe diligence produces wealth and negligence leads to poverty; diligence provides warmth and negligence results in cold. Therefore, they do not dare to be negligent.

Now, if these people were to believe in fate and act accordingly, the rulers would become negligent in hearing lawsuits and managing the government; the ministers and secretaries would be...

<summary>Mozi argues that the success of society depends on the active diligence of all classes, from rulers to farmers. He warns that a belief in fate would lead to universal negligence, causing the collapse of government, the economy, and basic survival.</summary>

<keywords>Anti-Fatalism, diligence, negligence, social roles, government, labor, soybeans, Mozi, ancient Chinese philosophy</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the argument that fatalism leads to social and economic collapse by undermining the motivation for hard work. It concludes with a call for leaders to reject the doctrine as a tool of failed rulers.</meta>

## ANTI-FATALISM (III)

...they would be negligent in attending to court; the farmers would be negligent in sowing seeds and planting trees; the women would be negligent in weaving and spinning. When the rulers are negligent in hearing lawsuits and attending to government, and the ministers and secretaries are negligent in attending to court, then I believe the world will fall into chaos. When the farmers are negligent in sowing seeds and planting trees, and the women are negligent in weaving and spinning, then in my opinion, the supply of clothing and food for the world will be insufficient.

As for the result of applying the doctrine of fatalism to the government of the empire: if one attempts to worship Heaven and the spirits above while holding this belief, Heaven and the spirits will not be pleased. If one tries to nurture the people below with it, they will not be benefited, but will instead be demoralized and become impossible to employ. Internally, the national defense will not be strong, and externally, military attacks will not be victorious. The reason why the wicked kings of the Three Dynasties—Chieh, Chow, Yu, and Li <note>The "Three Dynasties" refers to the Xia, Shang, and Zhou. Chieh (Jie) was the last king of Xia; Chow (Zhou) the last of Shang; Yu and Li were notorious kings of the Western Zhou. All are traditionally cited as examples of moral failure leading to political ruin.</note>—lost their countries and ruined their states was precisely because of this doctrine.

Therefore, Motse <note>original: "Motse," also known as Mozi</note> said: If the gentlemen of the world truly desire to procure benefits for the world and destroy its calamities, they must vigorously refute the doctrine of fatalism. For fatalism was an invention of wicked kings and a practice of miserable men. It was not a doctrine of the magnanimous <note>original: "magnanimous," referring to the Confucian/Mohist ideal of benevolence or *Ren*</note>. Therefore, those who practice magnanimity and righteousness must examine it and vigorously refute it.

<vocab>Fatalism, Motse, Three Dynasties, Chieh, Chow, Yu, Li, magnanimity, righteousness</vocab>

<summary>Mozi concludes that fatalism leads to economic scarcity, government corruption, and military weakness, ultimately causing the downfall of civilizations as seen in the examples of failed ancient kings. He calls on all righteous people to actively oppose the doctrine of fate to ensure the world's benefit.</summary>
<keywords>Anti-Fatalism, Mozi, political philosophy, social order, Chieh, Chow, Yu, Li, Three Dynasties, economic stability, magnanimity</keywords>

# CHAPTER XXXIX

## ANTI-CONFUCIANISM (II) <note>original: This chapter exists in two records, but the first is lost. The style of this chapter differs from other books in the collection, and it contains historical errors, suggesting it was written at a much later date. While it likely reflects Mozi’s general ideas, it was probably put into this exaggerated form by a later follower.</note>

The Confucian says: Love among relatives should depend upon the closeness of the relationship, and honor shown to the virtuous should be ranked by degree. <note>original: This Confucian tenet is found in *The Doctrine of the Mean* (中庸): "The decreasing measures of the love due to relatives, and the steps in the honor due to the worthy, are produced by the principle of propriety."</note> This is to advocate for discrimination between near and distant relatives, and between those who are prestigious and those who are humble. 

However, according to his code of propriety <note>original: "li," referring to the traditional rites, ceremonies, and social norms of Confucianism</note>: 
*   Mourning for the death of a parent should last three years.
*   For a wife or the eldest son, three years.
*   For an uncle, a brother, or other sons, one year.
*   For a near relative, five months. 

If these periods are based on the closeness of the relationship, then mourning for the closest relative should obviously be the longest, and for more distant relatives, it should be shorter. By this logic, the wife and the eldest son are treated as being as close as the parents. 

If, instead, the periods are based on the degrees of respect due to each person, then it means the wife and the eldest son are respected as much as the parents, while uncles and brothers are placed on the same level as the other sons. What could be more inconsistent <note>original: "perversity"</note> than this!

When a parent dies, the Confucian first lets the body lie there without

<header>ANTI-CONFUCIANISM (II)</header>

...dressing him for burial. He climbs on the roof, looks into the well, reaches into the rat holes, and searches in the washing basins to look for the dead man. <note>original: These ceremonies are not found in the *Li Chi* (The Book of Rites).</note> If one assumes that the man still exists, this procedure is certainly foolish. If he does not exist, then this insistent search is the height of hypocrisy.

When a Confucian takes a wife, <note>original: This description is based on the "Ceremony of the Scholar's Wedding" in the *Yi Li* (The Book of Etiquette and Ceremonial).</note> he must escort her in person, dressed in ceremonial robes as if he were a servant. He drives the carriage himself, as if he were waiting on a revered parent. In this way, the dignity and solemnity of the marriage ceremony are made to equal those of sacrifice and worship. The hierarchy of high and low is turned upside down. Parents are disobeyed; they are brought down to the same level as the wife, and the wife is exalted to a position that interferes with the service owed to the parents. Can such conduct truly be called filial? 

The Confucian tells us: "A wife is taken to share in continuing the worship and sacrifice to the ancestors, and the son will eventually attend to the ancestral temple; therefore, they are highly regarded." 

We answer him: This is a false justification. Consider this: his brothers attend to the ancestral temple for dozens of years, yet when they die, he will mourn for them for only one year. The brothers' wives also help continue the worship and sacrifice of his ancestors, yet there is no mourning period observed upon their deaths at all. Therefore, it is clear that the three-year mourning period for the death of a wife or an eldest son is not actually because of their service to the ancestral temple or their role in continuing sacrifices. In reality, to show such partiality to one's wife and son is already quite misguided. Yet the Confucianist pretends it is for the sake of the...

Furthermore, the Confucianist clings tenaciously to the dogma of <term>fate</term> and argues:

> “Old age or an early death, poverty or wealth, safety or danger, order or chaos are destined by the <term>fate</term> of <term>Heaven</term> and cannot be modified. Failure or success, reward or punishment, luck or adversity, are all settled; human wisdom and power can do nothing about them.”

When various officials believe this, they will neglect their individual duties. When the common people believe this, they will neglect their work. Lax government leads to disorder, and inefficient agriculture leads to poverty. And poverty is the root of disorder and insurrections. Yet the <term>Confucianists</term> take this teaching about <term>fate</term> to be the <term>Tao</term> <note>original: Tao; the "Way" or fundamental path of moral truth</note> and the guiding principle of life. This serves only to destroy the people of the <term>Empire</term>.

Moreover, the <term>Confucianist</term> justifies elaborate <term>ceremonials</term> and music to make people extravagant; he extends the period of <term>mourning</term> and fakes grief to deceive his parents. He introduces the concept of <term>fate</term>, causes poverty, and lives in idleness. He overthrows fundamental principles and avoids work, remaining lazy and proud. Self-indulgent in eating and drinking and too lazy to labor, he often suffers from hunger and cold and is in danger of freezing or starving, without the ability to prevent them. He behaves like a beggar; he grasps food like a hamster, stares at things like a male goat, and jumps up like a wild boar. Gentlemen all laugh at him. He becomes angry and exclaims:

> “What does an undisciplined man know about a good <term>Confucianist</term>?”

<header>ANTI-CONFUCIANISM (II)</header>

...he attends funerals. <note>The text continues from the previous page's description of the Confucianist's supposedly greedy behavior.</note> He brings along all his sons and grandsons so they can stuff themselves with food and drink. Managing just a few funerals provides him with enough to live on. He relies on other people’s wealth for his own riches and uses their property to maintain his own status. When a death occurs in a wealthy family, he is overjoyed, for it presents an opportunity for him to acquire clothing and food.

The Confucianist claims: “The <term>superior man</term> <note>original: junzi; the ideal moral person or "gentleman" in the Confucian tradition</note> must use ancient patterns of speech and wear ancient styles of dress before he can be truly virtuous.” We respond to him: What you call ancient speech and dress were all modern once. When the ancients first used that speech and wore those clothes, they would not have been "superior men" by your standards. Do you mean to say, then, that one must wear the clothes of a common person and speak the speech of an ordinary person before he can become virtuous?

Again, the Confucianist says: “The superior man follows the old ways but does not create innovations.” <note>original: quoting the Analects 7:1; "A transmitter and not a maker, believing in and loving the ancients..."</note> We respond to him: In ancient times, Yi invented the bow, Yü invented armor, Hsi Chung invented vehicles, and Ch’iao Ch’ui invented boats. Would he then say that the tanners, armor-makers, and carpenters of today are all superior men, whereas Yi,

<note>1. In the Chinese system of beliefs, there is no separate class of priests. The entire nation was represented by the Emperor at periodic sacrifices to Heaven, Earth, and other spirits. The accusation made here against the Confucianist seems to be a mocking caricature of this practice.</note>

<meta>This page continues Mozi’s critique of the Confucianists, specifically addressing the contradiction in their refusal to innovate and their impractical, ritualistic approach to warfare.</meta>

<page-num>204</page-num>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

Were Yü, Hsi Chung, and Ch'iao Ts'ui <note>Yü is credited with flood control; Hsi Chung with inventing the chariot; Ch'iao Ts'ui (also known as Ch'iao Ch'ui) was a legendary craftsman.</note> all ordinary men? Moreover, some of those whom the Confucianist follows must have been inventors themselves. Therefore, his instructions are, after all, merely the ways of men who were once "ordinary" or "modern" in their own time.

Again, the Confucianist says: "When the <term>superior man</term> <note>original: junzi; the ideal moral person or "gentleman" in the Confucian tradition</note> is victorious, he does not pursue a fleeing enemy. When the enemy is cornered <note>original: "kept at bay"</note>, he does not shoot. When the enemy retreats, he will even help them push their carts." 

We answer him: If you are referring to the truly benevolent, they would have no reason for such conflict. Benevolent people remind each other of the principles of right and wrong, and of what should be accepted and what should be rejected. He who has no just cause follows him who has one. He who lacks knowledge follows him who possesses it. When a person runs out of arguments, he would acknowledge defeat; when he sees what is good, he would be converted to it. How can there be any physical strife in such a case? 

If the contestants are both wicked, even if the victor does not pursue the fleeing enemy, does not shoot the enemy when they are cornered, and helps push the enemy's carts during their retreat—even if he does all these things—he still cannot be called a superior man. 

On the other hand, suppose a <term>sage</term> <note>A perfectly wise and virtuous ruler who acts for the benefit of all.</note> starts out to remove a great evil <note>original: "destroy a curse"</note> on behalf of the world. He raises an army to punish a wicked and cruel state. Suppose that when he is victorious, he follows the Confucian way and commands his army: "Do not pursue the fleeing enemy. Do not shoot when the enemy is cornered. Help them push their carts when they retreat." If he does this, the wicked men will be set free, and the evil plague on the world will not be removed. This is to harm the common people <note>original: "parents of the multitudes"; a term implying that the people are the foundation of society</note> and to greatly ruin the world. Nothing can be more unrighteous!

<summary>Mozi argues that the Confucian refusal to innovate is illogical and that their chivalrous rules of warfare are impractical because they allow wicked rulers to escape and continue harming the people.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Confucianism, warfare, superior man, invention, ethics, justice, sage-kings</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Mozi’s critique of the Confucianists, specifically addressing the contradiction in their refusal to innovate and their impractical, ritualistic approach to warfare. It transitions into a critique of the Confucian ideal of the "superior man" as a passive figure who only speaks when prompted.</meta>

<page-num>205</page-num>

<header>ANTI-CONFUCIANISM (II)</header>

Again, the Confucianist says: “The <term>superior man</term> <note>original: junzi; the ideal moral person or "gentleman" in the Confucian tradition</note> is like a bell. It will sound when it is struck, but it will remain silent when it is not struck.” 

We answer him: A <term>magnanimous</term> <note>original: ren; often translated as "benevolent" or "humanitarian"</note> person, in serving his superior, should be loyal; and in serving his parents, he should be filial. When the superior demonstrates excellence, the minister should adore him; when there is a fault, the minister should give counsel. This is the proper way for a minister to act. Now, if a person only "sounds when struck" and remains silent when not struck, then he will hide his knowledge and withhold his efforts, waiting to be questioned before he answers. Even if a great advantage for his lord or parents is at stake, he will not speak up without being asked. Even if a massive invasion or insurrection is approaching, or a conspiracy is afoot, and no one knows it but him, he still will not speak up in the presence of his lord and parents without being questioned. What a criminal, producing such confusion! Such a man will not be loyal as a minister, filial as a son, respectful in serving an elder brother, or gentle in treating the people.

When a benefit is in sight, the only fear should be that one's counsel may arrive too late. Yet, the Confucianist says that when the ruler starts something that is not beneficial, one should simply fold his hands high on the breast, look down, and utter with difficulty: “This I have not learned.” In an emergency, he says one should simply withdraw and set out on a long journey. <note>The original text of these last two sentences is very corrupted, but this interpretation is considered correct in principle.</note> However, every principle, doctrine, and standard of benevolence and <term>righteousness</term> <note>original: yi; moral duty or justice</note> is intended to be used on a large scale to rule men and on a small scale to hold office; they are meant to be used widely to exercise a universal influence and, narrowly, to cultivate one’s own person. What is not righteous should...

<summary>Mozi critiques the Confucian ideal of the "passive" gentleman, arguing that waiting to be questioned before giving advice is a betrayal of loyalty and filial duty, especially during times of crisis or potential benefit.</summary>

<keywords>Confucianism, Mozi, Superior Man, Junzi, Loyalty, Filial Piety, Righteousness, Benevolence, Political Ethics, Counsel</keywords>

...should not be tolerated; whatever is not according to principle should not be practiced. One should endeavor to secure benefits for the empire, both directly and indirectly, while avoiding anything that brings no profit. Such is the way of the <term>superior man</term> <note>original: junzi; the ideal ethical gentleman</note>. But what we hear of the conduct of Kong Mo <note>original: K’ung Mo; the author uses 'Mo'—meaning 'somebody'—instead of Confucius's personal name out of a mix of traditional taboo and perhaps a lack of reverence</note> is diametrically opposed to this.

Lord Jing <note>original: Lord Ching</note> of Qi <note>original: Ch’i</note> asked Yanzi <note>original: Yentse; a famous advisor in the state of Qi</note>: “What kind of a man is Confucius?” Yanzi did not answer. The Lord repeated the question, and still there was no answer. Lord Jing said: “Many have told me about Kong Mo, and they all said he was a virtuous man. Now that I am asking you about him, why don’t you answer?” 

Yanzi replied: “Ying <note>original: Ying; Yanzi's personal name</note> is not wise and cannot know virtuous men. Yet, Ying has heard that a virtuous man must be someone who, upon entering a state, will endeavor to bring about friendly relations between the ruler and the ministers and resolve the grudges between superiors and subordinates. 

This man, Confucius, once visited the state of Jing <note>original: Ching; a name for the state of Chu</note>. He heard of the secret plans of Duke Bai <note>original: Duke Po</note> and told them to Shi Qi <note>original: Shih Ch’i</note>. As a result, the ruler almost perished and Duke Bai was executed. <note>Note: This historical incident involving Duke Bai's rebellion actually occurred long after Yanzi’s death; the author of the text has likely misattributed the story or used an inaccurate historical source to discredit Confucius.</note> 

Ying has also heard that the virtuous man does not gain the confidence of the superior through flattery, nor does he gain the confidence of subordinates through threats. If his counsel is listened to by the ruler, it will benefit the people; if his instructions are followed by the subordinates, they will...

<header>ANTI-CONFUCIANISM (II)</header>

...benefit the superior. His speech is clear and easy to understand, and his conduct is simple and easy to follow. His righteous conduct enlightens the people, and his thoughtful counsel convinces the lord and his ministers. Now, this man Confucius conspired with rebels using elaborate plans and committed wicked acts through devious plots. To persuade subordinates to plot against their superiors and to tell ministers to assassinate their lord is not the behavior of a virtuous man. To enter a country and join with its traitors is not the way of the righteous. To urge those known to be disloyal to revolt does not fit the path of the <term>magnanimous</term> <note>original: magnanimous; meaning great-souled or noble</note>. Plotting against someone from a distance and condemning them behind their back—since his conduct does not enlighten the people and his counsel does not convince the lord—I, your servant Ying <note>Yanzi's personal name</note>, do not see how Confucius is any different from Duke Bai <note>original: Duke Po; a rebel in the state of Chu who led a failed coup</note>. This is why I did not answer you." 

Lord Jing <note>original: Ching</note> said: "Oh! I have truly benefited from this. If it were not for you, I would never have realized that Kong Mo <note>Confucius</note> is the same kind of man as Duke Bai."

Kong Mo visited the state of Qi <note>original: Ch’i</note> and met with Lord Jing. Lord Jing was pleased and was planning to assign the district of Ni Xi <gloss>1</gloss> to him. He told Yanzi <note>original: Yentse</note> about this plan. 

Yanzi said: "Please do not do this. A scholar of his school would sit idly and take things easy; therefore, he cannot be used to teach subordinates. He likes music and will corrupt the people; therefore, he cannot be trusted to govern. He believes in fate and will neglect his duty; therefore, he cannot be given a public office. He places too much emphasis on mourning and makes a display of grief; therefore, he cannot be made to take care of—

1 Ni Xi <note>original: Ni Hsi</note> is the name of a place, probably a district.

...the people. He is formal in his dress and affected in his manners; therefore, he cannot lead the masses. Kong Mo <note>original: K’ung Mo; a name used for Confucius in this text</note> dresses elaborately and wears ornaments to mislead the people. He promotes music and dancing to attract the multitudes, performs complex ceremonies of ascending and descending the steps, and practices the etiquette of "rushing and soaring" <note>original: "rushing and soaring"; a ceremonial movement where the arms are held to imitate the wings of a bird</note> to dazzle the commoners. 

Despite all his extensive learning, he cannot create a plan for the world; for all his laborious thought, he cannot help the people. A whole lifetime is not enough to master his learning; a grown man cannot keep up with his ceremonies; and even the wealthy cannot afford his music. He elaborates and decorates his improper ways just to keep the feudal lords busy; he provides excessive sounds and music to corrupt the people. His principles cannot instruct the world, and his learning cannot lead the masses. Now you, my lord, intend to commission him to change the customs of Qi <note>original: Ch’i</note>. This is truly not the way to lead a country and encourage the people." 

The Lord said, "This is well said."

At this, the Lord gave him valuable gifts but withheld the official appointment; he received him with respect but did not inquire into his teaching. Kong Mo became angry—angry with Lord Jing and Yanzi <note>original: Yentse</note>. Then he returned to the state of Lu. Not long after, Qi intended to attack Lu. He remarked to...

Tse Kung <note>original: Tse Kung, or Zigong; one of the seventy-two most famous disciples of Confucius. "Ts'e" is his personal name.</note> said: "Oh, Tse! Now is the time to perform the great deed." Thereupon, Confucius sent Tse Kung to the state of Qi <note>original: Ch’i</note> and, through an introduction by Huizi <note>original: Huitse</note> of the South City, he met with Tian Chang <note>original: T’ien Ch’ang; a general of Qi</note>. 

Tse Kung persuaded Tian Chang to attack the state of Wu instead of the state of Lu. He also told the generals Kao Kuo and Pao Yen <note>original: Kao Kuo Pao Yen</note> not to interfere with Tian Chang's rebellion. Then he went on and convinced the state of Yue <note>original: Yüeh</note> to attack Wu. For three years, both the states of Qi and Wu were threatened with ruin. The number of people killed totaled hundreds of thousands. And this was the revenge of Kong Mo <note>original: K’ung Mo; a name used for Confucius in this text. Historical records do not support this specific charge of instigating war for revenge</note>.

Kong Mo was once the Chief Justice of the state of Lu. However, he abandoned the cause of his lord and entered the service of Ji Sun <note>original: Chi Sun; the head of a powerful family in Lu</note>. Ji Sun was the Chancellor of Lu but betrayed his duty and fled. <note>Modern historians generally consider the charge that Confucius assisted a traitor to be unfounded.</note> As Ji Sun was trying to force open the gate against the guards, Kong Mo lifted the heavy gate-beam for him.

Once, Kong Mo was in great distress between the states of Cai <note>original: Ts'ai</note> and Chen <note>original: Ch'en</note>, having only vegetable soup without even any rice to eat. <note>This famous incident of starvation is mentioned in the Analects, Book XV.</note> After ten days of this, Tse Lu <note>original: Tse Lu, or Zilu; a disciple of Confucius known for his courage and directness</note> cooked a pig for him. Kong Mo did not ask where the meat had come from, and he ate it. Tse Lu...

...stole a person's robe and traded it for wine. Kong Mo <note>original: K'ung Mo; the name used for Confucius in this text</note> did not ask where the wine came from, and he drank it. But when Duke Ai <note>original: Lord Ai</note> received Confucius, Confucius would not sit on a mat that was not perfectly straight, nor would he eat meat that was not cut properly. <note>These details also appear in the *Analects*, Book 10, as rules of conduct for Confucius. When he was starving, he was not careful about how he stayed alive, but when his belly was full, he acted hypocritically to appear refined. What could be greater foolishness, corruption, villainy, and pretension than this!

Kong Mo was relaxing with his disciples. He remarked: "When Emperor Shun saw Gusou <note>original: Ku Sou; Gusou was the blind father of the legendary Emperor Shun.</note> he felt uneasy. The empire at that time must have been in danger. And was not Tan, the Duke of Zhou, lacking in generosity? However, it is highly unlikely that Confucius actually said them.</note> This shows Kong Mo’s conduct and his state of mind.

His followers and disciples all imitated him: Zigong <note>original: Tse Kung</note> and Jilu <note>original: Chi Lu; Jilu is another name for Zilu.</note> assisted Kong Li <note>Kong Li was a rebel in the state of Wei.</note> and committed high treason against the state of Wei. # ANTI-CONFUCIANISM (II)

Fei Kan <note>original: Fei Kan; likely referring to Bi Xi, a governor who rebelled against the state of Jin. Confucius was famously criticized by Mohists for being willing to visit him</note> was entrusted with the city of Zhongmou <note>original: Chung Mou</note> and then declared his independence. Qidiao Kai <note>original: Ch'i Tiao; a prominent disciple of Confucius</note> had a ferocious appearance. Nothing can be more <note>A word or phrase of a derogatory nature seems to have been lost from the original text here.</note> . . . than this! 

Of course, when disciples and pupils follow a teacher, they will promote his doctrines and imitate his conduct. The only difference is that they are not as powerful or as clever as the master himself. Now, since this was the actual conduct of Kong Mo <note>the name used for Confucius in this text</note>, it is only natural that Confucian scholars should be viewed with suspicion.

<summary>This page concludes a Mohist argument claiming that the rebellious actions and intimidating character of Confucius's disciples prove that his teachings and personal conduct were fundamentally corrupt.</summary>

# BOOK XI
## CHAPTER XLVI
### KENG CHU <note>original: Keng Chu; Keng Chu or Keng Chutse was a disciple of Mozi</note>

Mozi was angry with Keng Chutse. Keng Chutse said, “Am I not any better than others?” Mozi said, “Suppose I am starting out for Mount Taihang <note>original: T’ai Hang; a mountain range in modern-day Henan Province</note>, and a horse and an ox are to pull my cart. Which of them would you urge forward?” Keng Chutse said, “I would urge the horse.” Mozi asked, “Why urge the horse?” Keng Chutse said, “Because the horse is capable of better speed.” Mozi said, “I also think you are capable of better things <note>Mozi uses this analogy to explain that he criticizes Keng Chutse because he has higher expectations for him than for others</note>.”

Wu Matse <note>original: Wu Matse; possibly Wu Ma Qi, a disciple of Confucius, or his son</note> questioned Mozi: “Who are wiser: the ghosts and spirits, or the sages?” Mozi said: “The ghosts and spirits are wiser than the sages by as much as those with sharp hearing and keen sight surpass the deaf and the blind. In ancient times, Emperor Qi <note>original: Ch’i; the second emperor of the Xia (Hsia) Dynasty and son of Yu. While this story of the nine tripods is usually attributed to Yu, Mozi credits it to Qi</note> of the Xia Dynasty commissioned Fei Lien to mine minerals in the mountains and rivers and to cast ceremonial tripods <note>original: tings (鼎); ancient three or four-legged bronze vessels used for sacrifices</note> at Kunwu. He ordered Yi to sacrifice a pheasant to consult the tortoise shell oracle <note>original: "invoke the tortoise"; referring to the practice of divination using tortoise shells</note> of Bo Ruo <note>original: Po Jo; likely a place name, though interpretations vary</note>, saying: ‘Let the tripods, when completed, have four legs. Let them be able to cook automatically, without fire, and to hide themselves without being [moved or seen]...’”

<summary>This page introduces Chapter 46 with an anecdote about Mozi motivating his disciple Keng Chu and a discussion on the superior wisdom of spirits compared to human sages, illustrated by an ancient legend of the Xia Dynasty.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, Keng Chu, Mount Taihang, Wu Matse, ghosts and spirits, Emperor Qi, Xia Dynasty, tings, ceremonial tripods, divination</keywords>

### KENG CHU

[...be easily] lifted, and move themselves without being carried. In this way, they may be used for the sacrifice at Kunwu <note>original: K'un Wu; a legendary mountain famous for its copper, where the first tripods were said to be cast</note>. May our offering be accepted!" 

Then the oracle was interpreted as saying: "I have accepted the offering. Abundant are the white clouds: one to the south, one to the north, one to the west, one to the east. When the nine tripods <note>original: nine tings; large bronze vessels that symbolized the right to rule China</note> have been completed, they shall be handed down to three dynasties <note>original: empires</note>. When the emperor of Xia <note>original: Hsia</note> loses them, the people of Yin <note>original: Yin; also known as the Shang dynasty</note> will possess them; when the people of Yin lose them, the people of Zhou <note>original: Chou</note> will possess them." 

Now, the transfer of power from the emperor of Xia to the Yin and Zhou dynasties took many centuries. Even if a sage planned in consultation with his excellent ministers and superior assistants, could he foresee what would happen after many centuries? Yet the ghosts and spirits can. Therefore, we say that the ghosts and spirits are wiser than the sages, just as those with sharp hearing and keen sight are more capable than the deaf and blind.

Zhi Tuyu <note>original: Chih T'u Yü</note> and Xian Zeshi <note>original: Hsien Tse Shih</note> <margin>Zhi Tuyu and Xian Zeshi were two of Mozi's disciples.</margin> asked Mozi: "What is the greatest righteousness in conduct?" 

Mozi said: It is like the building of a wall. Let those who can lay the bricks, lay the bricks; let those who can fill in the mortar, fill in the mortar; and let those who can carry the materials, carry the materials. Then the wall can be completed. Practicing righteousness is just like this. Let those who can debate, debate; let those who can explain the doctrines, explain the doctrines; and let those who can govern, govern. Then righteousness is achieved.

Wu Maqi <note>original: Wu Matse; likely Wu Ma Qi, a contemporary of Mozi and a follower of Confucianism</note> said to Mozi: "Even though you practice universal love <note>Mozi’s core philosophy of "Jian'ai," or impartial care for all people</note>, the world cannot be said to benefit from it; even though I do not...

"...by loving universally, the world cannot be said to be harmed. Since neither of us has achieved anything yet, why do you praise yourself and criticize me?" 

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> answered: "Suppose a great fire is burning. One person is fetching water to extinguish it, and another is holding fuel to feed it. Neither of them has yet achieved their goal, but which one do you value more?" 

Wu Maqi <note>original: Wu Matse</note> answered that he approved of the intention of the person who fetches water and disapproved of the intention of the person who holds fuel. 

Mozi said: "In the same manner, I approve of my intention and disapprove of yours."

Mozi had recommended Geng Zhuzi <note>original: Keng Chutse</note> for a position in the state of Chu <note>original: Ch'u</note>. Some other pupils went to visit him there. They were given only three *sheng* <note>A sheng (升) was a unit of volume. In Mozi's time, it was much smaller than a modern pint, perhaps only 1/5 or 1/10 as large.</note> of grain for each meal and were not generously entertained. The pupils returned and reported this to Mozi, saying: "Geng Zhuzi has not benefited from serving Chu. When we visited him, we were given only three *sheng* at each meal and were not treated with hospitality." 

Mozi said: "You cannot be sure of his situation yet." 

Shortly after, Geng Zhuzi sent Mozi ten *chin* <note>A chin (jin/金) was a unit of weight; ten chin of silver was a substantial sum of money.</note> of silver, saying: "Your humble student, who does not even deserve to live <note>original: "who dare not die"; a traditional expression of extreme humility, suggesting the speaker feels they have been so unfilial or failing in duty that they deserve death.</note>, sends herewith ten *chin*, which I hope you will use." 

Mozi said: "Indeed, as I said, we could not have known."

Wu Maqi said to Mozi: "Despite all the righteous work you do, people do not help you and spirits <note>original: ghosts</note> do not bless you. Yet you keep on doing it. You must be out of your mind." Mozi...

# GENG ZHU <note>original: KENG CHU; Geng Zhuzi was a disciple of Mozi.</note>

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: "Suppose you have two employees. One of them works when he sees you but will not work when he does not see you. The other one works whether he sees you or not. Which of the two would you value?" Wu Maqi <note>original: Wu Matse</note> said that he would value the one who worked whether he saw him or not. Mozi then said: "Then you are valuing someone who is crazy <note>original: demented. Mozi is likely using Wu Maqi's own logic—which argued for limited, self-interested love—to show that Wu Maqi's preference for an unobserved worker is actually a preference for the kind of universal, internal duty Mozi advocates.</note>."

A pupil of Zi Xia <note>original: Tse Hsia; a prominent disciple of Confucius</note> asked Mozi whether there could be any competition or struggle among superior men <note>term: superior men; the *junzi*, or moral elite in Chinese philosophy</note>. Mozi said: "Superior men do not struggle." The pupil of Zi Xia said: "There is struggle even among dogs and pigs; how can there be no struggle among men?" Mozi said: "What a shame! Sage-kings like Tang and Wu <note>King Tang of Shang and King Wu of Zhou</note> are praised with words, but you compare human conduct to that of dogs and pigs. What a shame!"

Wu Maqi criticized Mozi, saying: "To ignore your contemporaries and praise the early kings is to praise rotten bones. It is like a carpenter who knows only about decaying lumber but not the living tree." Mozi said: "The world today lives because of the instructions left by those early kings. To praise the early kings is to praise the source of life for the world. Not to praise what should be praised is not benevolent <note>original: magnanimous</note>."

Mozi said: "The Jade of He <note>original: Ho; a legendary gemstone of great value</note>, the Pearl of Marquis Sui <note>original: Duke Sui</note>, and the nine tripods <note>original: tings; ancient bronze vessels symbolizing the authority of the throne</note>—these are what the feudal lords value as excellent treasures. But can they enrich the country, increase the population, bring order to the government, and keep the state safe? Of course they cannot. Excellent treasures are to be valued for their usefulness <note>original: efficacy</note>. Now, since the Jade of He, the Pearl of Marquis Sui, and the nine tripods cannot benefit people, then they...

<summary>Mozi defends the value of the ancient sage-kings and argues that true "treasures" should be measured by their utility to the state and the people rather than their rarity.</summary>

<page-num>216</page-num>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI <note>original: MOTSE</note></header>

...are not the world's most excellent treasures. On the other hand, if righteousness is employed in the government of the state, the population will increase, the government will be orderly, and the state will be secure. Excellent treasures are valued for their effectiveness <note>original: efficacy</note>. Since righteousness benefits people, then righteousness is the world's most excellent treasure.

The Lord of She <note>original: Lord Tse Kao of Sheh</note> asked Zhongni <note>original: Chung Ni, the courtesy name of Confucius</note> about government, saying, "What is a good governor like?" Zhongni answered him that a good governor will attract those who are far away and renew old friendships. <note>original: This event is also recorded in the *Analects* (Legge, vol. i, p. 269): "The Duke of She asked about government. The Master said: 'Good government occurs when those who are near are made happy, and those who are far off are attracted.'"</note> Mozi heard about this and commented: "The Lord of She did not ask the right question, and Zhongni did not give the right answer. Did the Lord of She not already understand that being a good governor means attracting those far away and renewing old friendships? The real question was *how* to achieve this. The answer only told the inquirer what he already understood, but failed to tell him what he did not know. Therefore, I say the Lord of She did not ask the right question, and Zhongni did not give the right answer."

Mozi said to Prince Wen of Luyang <note>original: Lu Yang; Prince Wen was a prince of the State of Chu, and Luyang was the principality assigned to him</note>: "Large states attacking small states is like boys playing 'horse.' When boys play horse, they merely exhaust their own..."

<summary>Mozi argues that righteousness is the most valuable tool for governance because of its practical benefits and critiques Confucius for providing superficial political advice that lacks practical instructions.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Confucius, Zhongni, Lord of She, Prince Wen of Luyang, righteousness, governance, war, Analects</keywords>

## ->KENG CHU <note>Geng Zhu</note><-

...feet. Now, when a large state attacks a small state, the farmers of the attacked states cannot cultivate the fields and the women cannot weave. They must go to the defense. Similarly, the farmers of the invading states cannot cultivate the fields, and the women cannot weave either. They must take part in the attack. Therefore, I say that large states attacking small states is like boys playing horse.

Mozi said: Doctrines that can be put into practice <note>original: translated into conduct</note> should be taught often. Doctrines that cannot be put into practice should not be taught often. To speak frequently about what cannot be carried out is merely to wear out one’s mouth.

Mozi sent Guan Qin Ao <note>original: Kuan Ch’in Ao</note> to recommend Kao Shizi <note>original: Kao Shihtse</note> to the state of Wei. The lord of Wei gave him a generous salary <note>original: heavy emoluments</note> and ranked him among the ministers. Kao Shizi attended court three times and offered all his counsel, but none of it was implemented. So he left for the state of Qi <note>original: Ch’i</note>, where he met Mozi and said: “On your recommendation, the lord of Wei gave me a high salary and ranked me among the ministers. I went to court three times and gave all my advice, but none of it was carried out. So I resigned. Wouldn’t the lord of Wei think I was crazy <note>original: demented</note>?” Mozi replied: “If you left because it was in accordance with the Way <note>original: Tao 道; the significant word "Way" is perhaps best understood here as the right path</note>, what does it matter even if you are suspected of being crazy? In ancient times, Duke Zhou was displeased with Uncle Guan <note>original: Kuan; Guan was the uncle of the reigning Emperor Cheng and the brother of Duke Zhou</note> and resigned from...”

<header>JING ZHU <note>original: Keng Chu</header>

This is like asking for a reward for deserting late in a defeated army.

Gong Mengzi <note>original: Kung Mengtse, a Confucian</note> said: "The superior man <note>original: *chun-tzu*; the ideal ethical person</note> does not create anything new, but only transmits the wisdom of the past."$^1$ Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: "Not at all. The most inferior <note>original: unsuperior</note> men neither transmit the good of old nor create any good for the present. Less inferior men do not transmit the good of old, but they will produce what good they possess merely for the sake of receiving praise. Now, to transmit without creating is no different from creating without transmitting. It seems to me that whatever good exists from the past, one should transmit it; and whatever good can be made for the present, one should establish it, so that the amount of good may increase all the more."

Wu Mazi <note>original: Wu Matse</note> told Mozi: "I disagree with you. I cannot love universally. I love the people of Zou <note>original: Tsou</note> better than the people of Chu <note>original: Ch'u</note>, the people of Lu better than the people of Zou, the people of my own district better than the people of Lu, the members of my family better than the people of my district, my parents better than the other members of my family, and myself better than my parents. This is because of their nearness to me. When I am beaten, I feel pain. When they are beaten, the pain does not reach me. Why should I resist what does not cause me pain, rather than resist what does cause me pain? Therefore, I would rather have them killed to benefit me than have myself killed to benefit them." Mozi said: "Is this view of yours to be kept secret, or told to others?" Wu Mazi replied: "Why should I keep my opinion to myself? Of course I shall tell it to others." Mozi said: "Then if one person is pleased—"

If ten people are pleased with you, there will be ten people who will desire to kill you to benefit themselves. If the people of the whole world are pleased with you, the whole world will desire to kill you to benefit themselves.

So, then, those who are pleased with you desire to kill you, and those who are not pleased with you also desire to kill you. This is to say: what comes out of your mouth is what kills your body. 

Mozi continued: "Then where, after all, does the benefit of your doctrine lie? To teach what is not profitable is merely to waste your breath <note>original: "wear out one's mouth"</note>."

Mozi said to Prince Wen of Lu Yang: "Suppose there is a man who has such an abundance of sheep, oxen, and other livestock <note>original: "grass-fed and grain-fed animals"</note> that he cannot eat all that the cooks prepare for him. Yet, when he sees a man baking cakes, he looks surprised and steals them, saying, 'Let me eat them.' Now, is this due to an unsatisfied appetite, or is he suffering from a 'stealing disease' <note>original: "kleptomania"</note>?" 

Prince Wen of Lu Yang replied that the man must be suffering from a stealing disease.

# GENG ZHU <note>original: KENG CHU; this is the title of Chapter 46 of the Mozi</note>

...to thousands of *mu* <term>mu: a Chinese unit of land measurement, roughly 1/6th of an acre</term> and is more than sufficient for cultivation. Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: "When Ji Sun Shao and Meng Bo Chang <note>original: Chi Sun Shao and Meng Po Ch'ang</note> were in power in Lu, they could not trust each other. So they took an oath at the altar, saying, 'May we be harmonious!' This is like closing one's eyes and praying at the altar, 'May I be able to see everything!' Is this not nonsense?"

Mozi said to Luo Hua Li <note>original: Lo Hua Li</note>: "I have heard you are brave." <note>original note: There is a pun in the Chinese text of this paragraph that is impossible to bring out in English. When Mozi said to the man, "I have heard you are brave," the Chinese words used (*zi hao yong*) also mean "I have heard you admire bravery."</note>

Luo Hua Li replied, "Yes. When I hear there is a brave man somewhere, I always go and kill him." 

Mozi said, "The whole world promotes what it likes and destroys what it hates. But when you hear of a brave man somewhere, you must go and kill him. This is not admiration for bravery, but hatred for it."

<summary>Mozi uses logical analogies to criticize the expansionist greed of large states and the hypocrisy of leaders who swear to harmony while lacking trust. He also exposes the contradiction in a man who claims to love bravery yet seeks to kill anyone else who is brave.</summary>

<page-num>222</page-num>

# BOOK XII

## CHAPTER XLVII

### ESTEEM FOR RIGHTEOUSNESS <note>original: GUI YI; This chapter focuses on the intrinsic value of righteousness (*yi*) as the highest human priority.</note>

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: Among all things in existence, none is more valuable than righteousness. Suppose we say to someone: "We will give you a hat and shoes on the condition that you let us cut off your hands and feet." Would he agree to this? Of course he would not. Why? Simply because hats and shoes are not as valuable as hands and feet. 

Again, if we say, "We will give you the whole world on the condition that you let us kill you," would he agree to this? Of course he would not. Why? Simply because the world is not as valuable as one's own life <note>original: "one's person"</note>. Yet, people have struggled against one another for the sake of a single principle. This shows that righteousness is even more valuable than one’s own life. Therefore, we say that among all things, none is more valuable than righteousness.

On his way from Lu to Qi <note>original: Ch’i; a powerful state in ancient China</note>, Mozi met an old friend who said to him: "Nowadays, no one in the world practices any righteousness. You are merely inflicting pain on yourself by trying to practice it. You would be better off giving it up." 

Mozi replied: Suppose a man has ten sons. Only one works on the farm while the other nine stay at home. In that case, the son who farms must work all the more vigorously. Why? Because many people are eating while few are working. Since no one in the world currently practices righteousness, you should be encouraging me all the more. Why do you try to stop me?

<vocab>Mozi <note>original: Motse</note>, righteousness <note>original: *yi*</note>, Lu, Qi <note>original: Ch’i</note>, principle, farm</vocab>

<summary>Mozi argues that righteousness is the most valuable possession, surpassing even one's life or the entire world, and uses the analogy of a lone farmer to explain why he must work harder when others are idle.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, righteousness, ethics, Lu, Qi, sacrifice, duty, social responsibility</keywords>

<page-num>223</page-num>

# ESTEEM FOR RIGHTEOUSNESS

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> traveled south to the state of Chu <note>original: Ch'u</note> to see Lord Hui of Chu. Lord Hui refused to see him, using the excuse that he was old, and instead had Mu Ho receive him. Mozi spoke with Mu Ho, and Mu Ho was greatly pleased. He said to Mozi: "Your ideas may be quite good. But our Lord is a great ruler of the empire. Might he not refuse to employ them simply because they come from a man of humble status?" 

Mozi replied: "As long as ideas are useful, they are like good medicines, which are often derived from the roots of herbs. Yet even the Emperor takes them to cure his illness. Does he refuse to take them just because they are only the roots of a herb? Consider how a farmer pays his taxes to his superior. With these resources, the superior prepares wine and cakes to offer as sacrifices to God, ghosts, and spirits. Do these divine beings refuse to accept the offerings because they come from a humble farmer? 

Therefore, even a man of humble status can be compared to the farmer, or at least to medicine. Is he of less value than the roots of a herb? Furthermore, has my Lord not heard the story of King Tang <note>original: T'ang; the legendary founder of the Shang Dynasty</note>? Long ago, Tang was going to visit Yi Yin <note>a famous sage and minister of low original status</note> and had a son from the house of Peng <note>original: P'eng</note> serve as his driver. 

On the way, the son of Peng asked where the king was going. Tang told him that he was going to see Yi Yin. The son of Peng said: 'Yi Yin is merely a humble man of the world. If you want to see him, just send for him and he will feel quite honored to come.' 

Tang replied: 'This is something you do not understand. If there were a medicine here that, when taken, would sharpen the ears and brighten the eyes, I would be delighted and strive to take it. Now, Yi Yin is to me like a skilled physician and an effective medicine. Yet you do not think I should go to see him. This means you do not want to see me become a better person.' Thereupon, he dismissed the son of Peng from his service."

<vocab>Mozi, Chu, Lord Hui, Mu Ho, King Tang, Yi Yin, Peng, righteousness, humble status</vocab>

<summary>Mozi argues that the value of an idea or a person should be judged by their utility and righteousness rather than their social standing, using the analogies of herbal medicine and King Tang's respect for the sage Yi Yin.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, meritocracy, social status, King Tang, Yi Yin, Chu state, ancient Chinese philosophy, righteousness, wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 238 ---
<meta>This page concludes the anecdote about King Tang's humility and transitions into a series of Mozi's teachings regarding the practical application of ethics. He establishes criteria for beneficial conduct based on historical precedent and the cultivation of personal character.</meta>

<page-num>224</page-num>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI <note>original: Motse</note></header>

...and did not let him drive anymore. They did not resume their journey until the son of Peng <note>original: P'eng</note> became respectful.

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: Every word and every action that is beneficial to Heaven, the spirits, and the people must be carried out. Every word and every action that is harmful to Heaven, the spirits, and the people must be abandoned. Every word and every action that is in harmony with the sage-kings of the Three Dynasties—Yao, Shun, Yu <note>original: Yü</note>, Tang <note>original: T'ang</note>, Wen, and Wu—is to be carried out. Every word and every action that agrees with the wicked kings of the Three Dynasties—Chieh, Zhou <note>original: Chow</note>, Yu, and Li—is to be abandoned.

Mozi said: If a principle can improve a person's conduct, explain <note>original: expound</note> it thoroughly. If a principle cannot improve conduct, do not speak of it much. To speak at length about what cannot change behavior is merely to exhaust yourself for no reason <note>original: "wear out one's mouth"</note>.

Mozi said: The six distractions <note>original: "peculiarities"</note> must be removed. When silent, one should be reflecting; when talking, one should be instructing; when acting, one should be achieving something. When a person employs these three habits alternately, he will become a sage. Emotions like pleasure, anger, joy, sorrow, love, and hate are to be set aside, and replaced by magnanimity and righteousness. When a person's hands, feet, mouth, nose, ears, and eyes are all used for the sake of righteousness, then he will surely be a sage.

Mozi said to a few of his disciples: "Even if you cannot fully achieve perfect righteousness, you must not abandon the Way. It is just like a carpenter: he must not blame his measuring line <note>original: "the line"</note> just because he cannot saw the lumber perfectly straight."

Mozi said: Just as the "gentlemen" of the world are not butchers of dogs and pigs and would refuse to do such work if asked, they are also often not capable of being ministers...

<summary>Mozi outlines the criteria for ethical speech and action, emphasizing their benefit to society and alignment with the lives of ancient sage-kings. He stresses that true righteousness requires practical achievement and the disciplined removal of emotional distractions.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, Three Dynasties, Sage-kings, Righteousness, Ethics, Conduct, Self-discipline, Practicality</keywords>
