<meta>This page continues Mozi’s critique of the ruling class, moving from a discussion of sage-like behavior to the hypocrisy of "gentlemen" who can define virtue but cannot practice it.</meta>

<page-num>225</page-num>

# ESTEEM FOR RIGHTEOUSNESS

...in a state, they would accept it when asked to be such. Isn’t this perverse?

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: The blind say that what is bright is white, and what is dark is black. Even the keen-sighted cannot change this definition. But if we were to mix up black and white objects and ask the blind to sort them, they could not do it. Therefore, when I say the blind do not know white from black, it is not because of how they define the words, but because of how they perform in the process of selection.

Now, the way the "gentlemen" <note>original: "gentlemen of the world"; likely referring to the *Junzi* or the social elite</note> of the world define magnanimity <note>original: "magnanimity" often translates the Chinese concept of *Ren*, meaning benevolence or humaneness</note> is so standard that even the sage-kings Yu <note>original: Yü</note> and Tang <note>original: T'ang</note> could not change the definition. But when we mix magnanimous conduct with unmagnanimous conduct and let the gentlemen of the world choose between them, they do not know which is which. So, the reason I say the gentlemen of the world do not know magnanimity does not lie in how they define it either; it also lies in their failure in the process of selection.

Mozi said: The gentlemen of today treat their own characters <note>original: "handle their persons"</note> with even less care than a merchant would treat a bale of cloth. When a merchant handles a bale of cloth, he would not dare sell it without careful judgment; he will surely select a good one. But the gentlemen of today treat themselves quite differently. They carry out whatever they happen to desire. In severe cases, they are legally punished; even in less severe cases, they are met with public condemnation. Therefore, these gentlemen are even less careful in managing their own lives than a merchant is in handling a bale of cloth.

Mozi said: The gentlemen of our time desire to achieve righteousness. Yet, when we try to help them in the cultivation of their personality, they become resentful.

<sig>Q</sig>

<summary>Mozi argues that elite "gentlemen" understand the abstract definitions of virtue like magnanimity but fail to recognize or choose virtuous actions in practice. He further critiques them for being less disciplined with their own character than a merchant is with his merchandise.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, selection vs. definition, magnanimity (Ren), righteousness, self-cultivation, merchant analogy, hypocrisy</keywords>

<language>English</language>
<page-num>226</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI <note>original: MOTSE</note></header>
<page-type>text</page-type>

This is like wanting a wall to be finished, yet getting angry when someone helps you build it. Isn’t this irrational?

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: The ancient sage-kings wanted their teachings to be passed down to future generations. Therefore, they recorded them on bamboo and silk and engraved them in metal and stone to leave for their descendants so they could follow them. Today, the ways of the early kings are known, but they are not put into practice. This is breaking the tradition of the early kings.

Mozi brought many books in the storage compartments of his carriage on his journey south as an envoy to the state of Wei. Hsien Tangzi <note>original: Hsien T’angtse</note> saw them and was surprised. He asked: “Sir, you instructed Gong Shangguo <gloss>¹</gloss> <note>original: Kung Shang Kuo</note> to only consider whether an action is right or wrong and nothing more. Now, you are bringing many books with you. What use are they?” 

Mozi said: In ancient times, the Duke of Zhou <note>original: Duke Tan of Chou; a famous statesman of the early Zhou dynasty</note> read one hundred pages every morning and received seventy scholars every evening. Because of this, his achievements as an advisor to the king have lasted to this day. I have no master above me to serve, nor any farm below to manage. How could I dare to neglect these books? I have heard that although different paths may lead to the same goal, they are presented with many variations. Furthermore, common people do not know how to properly weigh the importance of what they hear. This is why there are so many books. Once someone has reviewed these ideas and thought deeply about them, they understand the core principles that lead to that same goal. At that point, they no longer need to be taught by books. Why should you be so surprised?

<summary>Mozi defends the importance of books and historical records as tools for understanding universal principles, arguing that while the "goal" of truth is one, the many paths to it require study for those who have not yet mastered the essentials.</summary>

<meta>This page continues the collection of anecdotes and teachings known as "Esteem for Righteousness," where Mozi advises officials and critiques the misplaced priorities of the ruling class.</meta>

<page-num>227</page-num>

# ESTEEM FOR RIGHTEOUSNESS

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said to Gongliang Huanzi <gloss>¹</gloss> <note>original: Kung Liang Huantse</note>: Wei is a small state situated between the larger states of Qi <note>original: Ch'i</note> and Jin <note>original: Chin</note>. <gloss>²</gloss> It is like a poor family living in the midst of wealthy families. If a poor family tries to imitate the rich by being extravagant in their clothing and food, their ruin is guaranteed. 

Now, we find in your household hundreds of decorated carriages, hundreds of horses fed on fine grain, and several hundred women dressed in finery and embroidery. If the money spent on decorating those carriages, feeding those horses, and buying those embroidered clothes were instead used to maintain soldiers, you could support more than a thousand men. In an emergency, several hundred of them could be stationed in the front <note>original: van</note> and several hundred in the rear. Which would provide more security: doing this, or letting those several hundred women hold the front and the rear lines? I believe that keeping these women is not nearly as secure as maintaining a military force.

Mozi had recommended someone for a government office in Wei. After a time, the man left his post and returned. Mozi asked him why he had come back. 

He answered: "In council, my opinions were not taken seriously. Furthermore, though I was promised a thousand *pen* <gloss>³</gloss> <note>original: p'en</note>, I was given only five hundred. Therefore, I left." 

Mozi asked: "Suppose you had been given more than a thousand *pen*; would you still have left?" 

The man answered, "No." 

Mozi said: "Then your reason for leaving was not because your advice was ignored. It was simply because the salary was too small."

Mozi said: The so-called "gentlemen" of the world have even less regard for a righteous man than they do for a common grain carrier.

---

¹ <note>Gongliang Huanzi was a minister of the state of Wei.</note>
² <note>See the sketch map included in the book *Mozi, the Neglected Rival of Confucius*.</note>
³ <gloss>A *pen* <note>original: 盆</note> is a unit of dry measure equal to thirteen Chinese bushels, or *dou* <note>original: 斗</note>. At this time, it was standard practice to collect taxes and pay government salaries in grain rather than coin.</gloss>

<summary>Mozi advises a minister of Wei against luxury, arguing that resources spent on extravagance should be redirected to national defense, and critiques an official for prioritizing his salary over his principles.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Wei, Gongliang Huanzi, military preparedness, extravagance, government salaries, righteousness, pen (unit of measure)</keywords>

--- Page 242 ---
<meta>This page continues the "Esteem for Righteousness" section, contrasting the practical calculations of merchants with the supposed moral duty of the elite, and concludes with an anecdote about Mozi's encounter with a superstitious fortune teller.</meta>

<page-num>228</page-num>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI <note>original: MOTSE</note></header>

If a grain carrier was resting by the roadside and was unable to stand back up, the gentlemen <note>original: gentlemen; referring to the *junzi* or social elite</note> would surely help him rise upon seeing him, whether he was old or young, honorable or humble. Why? Because it is the right thing to do. But when a gentleman who practices righteousness urges these same people to follow the way of the ancient kings, they are not only unwilling to carry it out, but they will even trample upon those teachings. Thus, the gentlemen of the world have even less regard for a righteous man than they do for a common grain carrier.

Mozi said: Merchants travel everywhere to do business, and their profits are doubled and multiplied. They persist despite the difficulties at the mountain passes and bridges, and the dangers posed by highwaymen and robbers. Now, the gentlemen of the elite can sit down and teach righteousness. For them, there are no difficulties at the passes and bridges, nor any dangers from highwaymen and robbers. Their gain should not only be doubled and multiplied, but should become incalculable. Yet, they refuse to do it. Thus, these gentlemen are not as discerning as merchants when it comes to calculating benefits.

Mozi was traveling North to the state of Qi <note>original: Ch'i</note> and met a fortune teller on the way. The fortune teller told him: "God <note>original: God; likely referring to *Di*, a celestial deity or spirit</note> is killing the black dragon in the North today. Now, your complexion is dark. You must not go North." Mozi did not listen to him and continued North. At the Zi River <note>original: Tse River</note>, he found he could proceed no further and returned. The fortune teller said: "I told you that you must not go North." Mozi replied: "People from the South, of course, cannot go North across the river, but neither can those from the North come South. Moreover, there are people with dark complexions, but there are also those with fair complexions. Why is it that neither group can proceed?" <note>Mozi argues that the physical barrier of the river, rather than the fortune teller's omen regarding skin color and dragons, is the true reason for the delay.</note>

<vocab>Mozi, Qi, Zi River, righteousness, merchants, fortune teller, black dragon</vocab>

<summary>Mozi critiques the elite for valuing physical labor over moral leadership and for lacking the practical foresight of merchants. He also uses logic to debunk a fortune teller's superstition regarding a journey North.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, righteousness, social elite, merchants, Qi, Zi River, superstition, ancient kings, ethics vs. practicality</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Mozi’s debate with a fortune teller who tried to discourage him from traveling North based on superstitious omens. Mozi uses the fortune teller's own logic to demonstrate its absurdity, arguing that if such omens were followed, all human activity would cease.</meta>

<page-num>229</page-num>

<header>ESTEEM FOR RIGHTEOUSNESS</header>

Furthermore, God <note>original: God; likely referring to *Di* or *Tian* (Heaven), the supreme celestial power</note> kills the blue dragon on the days of Jia <note>original: Chia</note> and Yi <note>original: I</note> in the East, the red dragon on the days of Bing <note>original: Ping</note> and Ding <note>original: Ting</note> in the South, the white dragon on the days of Geng and Xin <note>original: Hsin</note> in the West, and the black dragon on the days of Ren and Gui <note>original: Kuei</note> in the North. According to your logic, then, all the travelers in the world would be forbidden from moving, their plans would be obstructed, and the world would be left empty. Your idea should not be adopted.

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: "My principle is sufficient. To abandon my principle and rely on your brand of thinking is like throwing away the harvest just to pick up stray grains. To try to refute my principle with your own is like throwing an egg against a boulder. All the eggs in the world could be exhausted, and yet the boulder would remain unharmed."

---

¹ These characters are members of the Chinese Sexagenary Cycle <note>A traditional 60-day or 60-year cycle used in China for timekeeping</note>. Briefly, the Cycle consists of two series of characters. The Heavenly Stems number ten and the Earthly Branches number twelve <note>The original text reverses these numbers: there are 10 Stems and 12 Branches</note>. As 60 is the least common multiple of 12 and 10, we have the Cycle of Sixty. In ancient times, such as Mozi’s era, it was used to designate days. Later, it was used for both months and years, and especially the latter. For a more detailed treatment of this point, see James Legge: “On Chinese Chronology,” a paper read before the Victoria Institute, 1892.

² In the previous sentence, eight of the ten Heavenly Stems <note>The original text mistakenly calls these "Earthly Branches"</note> are mentioned. Pi Yuan <note>A Qing dynasty scholar and editor of Mozi</note> added “and the yellow dragon on the days of Wu and Ji in the middle” to his edition of the text. This addition seems plausible because it completes the set of five legendary dragons <note>representing the five elements and directions</note>. With this addition, the statement includes all the days of the cycle, making the argument even more logical: if every day is dangerous, no one could ever act.

<summary>Mozi refutes a fortune teller's superstition by showing that if one avoided travel based on celestial omens, all movement in the world would stop. He concludes by asserting that his rational principles are as indestructible as a boulder compared to the "eggs" of superstitious logic.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, Chinese Sexagenary Cycle, Heavenly Stems, Earthly Branches, Superstition vs. Reason, Five Dragons, Pi Yuan, James Legge</keywords>

# CHAPTER 48 <note>original: XLVIII. This chapter primarily contains further criticisms of Confucianism. The arguments are presented as a dialogue and are less formal and systematic than other chapters.</note>
## GONGMENG <note>original: Kung Meng; likely referring to Gongmengzi, a follower of the Confucian school</note>

Gongmengzi <note>original: Kung Mengtse</note> said to Mozi <note>original: Motse</note>: "A gentleman should wait with his hands folded across his chest. He should speak only when someone asks him a question, and remain silent when no one asks. He is like a bell; it makes a sound only when it is struck, and it remains silent when it is not."

Mozi replied: "Your idea involves three different situations, but you only understand one of them; therefore, you do not truly understand what you are talking about. If a ruler is committing acts of violence within the state, going directly to warn him would be seen as insolence <term>insolence: rude or disrespectful behavior toward a superior</term>. Attempting to warn him through his advisors would be called meddling in the council’s affairs. These are the specific cases where a gentleman might hesitate to speak.

However, if a ruler, in his administration, encounters a crisis in the state that is like a loaded machine ready to fire... <note>The original text is incomplete here</note> the gentleman must give a warning. The benefit to the ruler... <note>The original text is incomplete here</note> In such cases, he should offer counsel even if he has not been asked for it. 

Furthermore, suppose a ruler embarks on an unrighteous and extreme project. If he has clever military strategies and uses them to attack innocent states just to expand his territory, collect more taxes, and gather wealth—and if this path leads him to humiliation, as such wars benefit neither the victor nor the defeated but are harmful to both—in such a case..." <note>The text on this page ends mid-sentence</note>

<summary>In this chapter, Mozi debates Gongmengzi regarding the Confucian principle of remaining silent until spoken to. Mozi argues that a true gentleman must proactively advise a ruler to prevent unrighteous wars and administrative disasters, even if not invited to do so.</summary>
<keywords>Gongmengzi, Mozi, Confucianism, ethics, political advice, military schemes, righteous governance</keywords>

<meta>This page continues the debate between Mozi and the Confucian scholar Gongmengzi regarding the proactive role of a teacher or advisor. Mozi points out the hypocrisy in Gongmengzi's "silent bell" philosophy and argues that, unlike physical beauty, moral goodness must be actively promoted to be recognized in a chaotic world.</meta>

# GONGMENG <note>original: KUNG MENG</note>

...in that case, the gentleman must respond with counsel even though he is not asked. Furthermore, according to what you have said, the gentleman is to fold his arms across his chest and wait. He will speak when consulted; he will not speak when not consulted. He is like a bell; it sounds when struck, but does not sound when not struck. Now, no one had asked you anything, and yet you spoke. Is this what you call "sounding without being struck"? Is this what you call being ungentlemanly?

Gongmengzi <note>original: Kung Mengtse</note> said to Mozi <note>original: Motse</note>: "How is it possible for the people to be ignorant of what is really good? For instance, when a talented fortune teller remains at home and does not travel, he will have grain in abundance; when a beautiful maiden remains at home and does not travel, people will compete to win her hand. On the other hand, if she were to go out and try to sell herself, no one would want her. Now you go about, trying to persuade everyone—why all this fuss?"

Mozi said: In the present world of chaos, there are many who seek beautiful maidens. So, even if they stay at home, most people would seek them out. But those who seek goodness are few. Without intelligent persuasion, people will not understand it. Furthermore, suppose there are two people who are skilled at fortune telling. One travels about to tell people’s fortunes, and the other remains at home and does not go out. Which of these two will have more grain? 

Gongmengzi said that the one who travels about and tells people’s fortunes will have more grain. 

Mozi said: It is the same with magnanimity <term>magnanimity: here used to translate 'ren' (benevolence or humaneness), a core Confucian and Mohist virtue</term> and righteousness. He who travels about and urges the people to do good also has more merit. Why, then, should we not travel about and urge the people?

Gongmengzi, wearing a ceremonial hat and carrying the... <note>The text ends mid-sentence as he is described in his formal Confucian attire.</note>

<summary>Mozi refutes Gongmengzi’s argument for passive leadership by pointing out that Gongmengzi himself speaks uninvited and by arguing that moral goodness, unlike physical beauty, requires active promotion in a chaotic society.</summary>

# THE WORKS OF MOZI <note>original: MOTSE</note>

...carrying an official's tablet <note>original: *hu* 笏. A tablet held by officials during audiences with the ruler to record notes or symbolize rank. It is similar in shape and function to the *gui* 珪 stone tablet mentioned on page 111.</note> and wearing the cloak of a scholar, Gongmengzi <note>original: Kung Mengtse</note> came to see Mozi and asked: "Does a gentleman <term>Junzi: the Confucian ideal of a 'superior person' or 'gentleman'</term> put on the appropriate attire before acting? Or does he perform his duties first and only then consider his attire?"

Mozi said: Right action does not depend on attire.

Gongmengzi asked, "How can we know this is true?"

Mozi said: In the past, Duke Huan of Qi <note>original: Lord Huan of Ch'i (reigned 685–643 B.C.)</note>, wearing a high hat and a wide sash, with a gold sword and a wooden shield, governed his state—and his state was well-ordered. Duke Wen of Jin <note>original: Lord Wen of Chin (reigned 636–628 B.C.; text cites 780–746 B.C.)</note>, wearing garments of coarse cloth and a sheepskin cloak, with his sword in a leather belt, governed his state—and his state was well-ordered. King Zhuang of Chu <note>original: Lord Chuang of Ch'u (reigned 613–591 B.C.; text cites 671–626 B.C.)</note>, wearing a flashy hat with a tassel along with a red garment and a large gown, governed his state—and his state was well-ordered. King Goujian of Yue <note>original: Lord Kou Chien of Yüeh (reigned 496–465 B.C.)</note> had his hair cut short <note>In ancient China, cutting one's hair was often considered a barbaric practice or a punishment, as most people let their hair grow and tied it on top of their heads.</note> and his body tattooed, yet he governed his state—and his state was well-ordered. 

Now, these four rulers differed in their attire, but they were the same in their actions. Therefore, I know that right action does not depend on attire.

Gongmengzi said, "That is a fine point. I have heard that it is unlucky to keep one's goodness hidden. So, let me go and put away this tablet, change my hat, and then come back to see you. Would that be acceptable?"

Mozi said: Please, just tell me your business. If you feel you must put away the tablet and change your hat before you can even speak with me, then in your case, action *does* depend on attire.

Gongmengzi said, "The gentleman must follow the ways of the ancients..."

<summary>Mozi argues against the Confucian emphasis on ritual dress, using historical examples of successful rulers with different styles of attire to prove that virtuous leadership is based on action rather than appearance.</summary>

# GONGMENGZI <note>original: KUNG MENG</note>

...in his attire and in his speech before he can be considered magnanimous <term>Magnanimous: possessing "Ren," the Confucian virtue of benevolence or humaneness</term>."

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: In ancient times, Minister Fei Zhong <note>original: Fei Chung</note> of Emperor Zhou <note>original: Chow</note> of the Shang dynasty was a terror to the world, while the Earl of Qi <note>original: Baron Chi</note> and the Earl of Wei <note>original: Baron Wei</note> were the sages of the world. Now, these men all spoke the same dialect, yet the latter were virtuous and the former was wicked. Later, the Duke of Zhou <note>original: Duke Tan of Chou</note> was the sage of the world, and Uncle Guan <note>original: Uncle Kuan</note> was the villain of the world. These two wore the same attire <margin>Guan and the Duke of Zhou were brothers and were both uncles of Emperor Cheng. Naturally, they wore the same royal clothing.</margin>, yet the Duke was virtuous and Guan was wicked. Therefore, virtue evidently does not depend on wearing ancient attire or using archaic speech. Moreover, you are following only the customs of the Zhou dynasty and not those of the earlier Xia <note>original: Hsia</note> dynasty. Your idea of "antiquity" does not go back far enough.

Gongmengzi <note>original: Kung Mengtse</note> said to Mozi: "In ancient times, when assigning ranks, the sage-kings <margin>The term "sage-king" here refers to an impartial judge of merit who selects the most capable leaders for the people.</margin> crowned the most wise as emperor, and appointed the others as ministers and secretaries. Now, Confucius had an extensive knowledge of poetry and history, a clear understanding of rituals and music <margin>These subjects—Poetry, History, Rituals (Ceremonials), and Music—were the core departments of the Confucian Classics.</margin>, and a deep insight into many things. If it were up to a sage-king to choose, why shouldn't Confucius be made emperor?"

Mozi said: A wise man should revere Heaven and worship the spirits, love the people, and be frugal with expenses. Combining these qualities is what constitutes true wisdom. Now, you claim that because Confucius had an extensive knowledge of poetry and history, understood rituals and music, and had insight into many things, he should therefore be made emperor.

<summary>Mozi argues that virtue is internal rather than a product of ancient dress or speech, citing historical figures who dressed alike but acted differently. He then questions Gongmengzi's claim that Confucius's academic knowledge alone should qualify him for the throne.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, Gongmengzi, Confucius, virtue, attire, sage-kings, Zhou Dynasty, Xia Dynasty, rituals, wisdom</keywords>

--- Page 248 ---
<meta>This page continues Mozi’s critique of the Confucian scholar Gongmengzi. Mozi is responding to Gongmengzi’s claim that Confucius possessed great knowledge, arguing that knowledge without practical application or moral foundation is meaningless.</meta>

This is like estimating one’s wealth by counting the number of notches on a contract. <note>original: "This is like estimating one’s wealth by counting the number of notches." In ancient China, loan contracts were carved into bamboo strips which were then split; the creditor and debtor each kept one half. Mozi refers to a story of a foolish man who found a discarded half of a contract and believed the notches represented wealth he actually possessed.</note>

Gongmengzi <note>original: Kung Mengtse</note> said: "Poverty or wealth, old age or an untimely death—all are determined by Heaven and cannot be changed." Yet he also said: "The superior man must learn." Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> replied: To believe in fatalism <term>Fatalism: the belief that all events are predetermined and inevitable</term> while also teaching people to learn is like telling a man to cover his hair while at the same time removing his hat.

Gongmengzi said to Mozi: "There is only righteousness and unrighteousness; there is no such thing as good or bad omens <note>original: propitiousness or unpropitiousness</note>." 

Mozi replied: The ancient sage-kings all regarded the ghosts and spirits as intelligent beings who control calamity and blessings. They believed in good and bad omens, and because of this, the government was well-administered and the country was secure. From the time of the wicked kings Jie <note>original: Chieh</note> and Zhou <note>original: Chow</note> onward, however, rulers regarded the ghosts and spirits as unintelligent and unable to control calamity or blessings. They claimed there were no such things as good or bad omens, and as a result, the government became disorderly and the country fell into danger. Therefore, the "Jizi" <note>original: Chitse; likely a lost chapter of the *Shujing* or Book of Documents named after the sage Jizi/Baron Chi</note>—a book of the ancient kings—says, "Pride brings calamity." This means that evil acts will be punished and good acts will be rewarded.

Mozi said to Gongmengzi: According to your ceremonial rules,

# KUNG MENG

“For the death of the ruler, the parents, the wife, and the first-born son, there shall be mourning for three years. For the elder uncle, younger uncle, elder brother, younger brother, and first cousins within the family, five months. And for the aunt, the sister, the maternal uncle, and the nephew on the sister’s side, there will be mourning of several months for each. Many also use the intervals between periods of mourning to read the *Three Hundred Poems* <note>original: "Three Hundred Poems"; this refers to the *Classic of Poetry* (*Shijing*), a foundational collection of Chinese poems and songs.</note> according to their rhymes, to play them on stringed instruments, <note>original: "ch'in and she"; these are the *qin* and *se*, ancient Chinese plucked zithers. See page 175.</note> to sing them, and to dance to them. <note>The author notes that in ancient China, poetry, music, and dance were treated as a single integrated art form, similar to how classical compositions might be interpreted through dance in the West.</note> If your advice should be followed, when can the gentleman attend to government, or the common man attend to his work?”

Gongmengzi <note>original: Kung Mengtse</note> said: “When the country is in chaos, it should be put in order; when it is in order, rituals <note>original: ceremonials</note> and music may be practiced. When the country is poor, work should be attended to; when it is rich, rituals and music may be practiced.”

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: A country may be orderly, but it is only because it is being well-governed that it remains so. As soon as good administration is abandoned, order disappears. A country may be rich, but it is only because work is being attended to that it remains rich. As soon as work is abandoned, wealth disappears. Therefore, even though a country is orderly, it is necessary to encourage unceasing attention to administration. Now, you say that when the country is in order, rituals and music may be practiced, but that one should only put it in order when it becomes disorderly. This is like waiting to dig a well until someone is already choking <note>In this context, "choking" likely refers to someone gasping for water due to extreme thirst; the analogy implies that ritual is a luxury that distracts from the constant effort needed to maintain a state.</note>

<summary>Mozi critiques the lengthy Confucian mourning rites and musical performances, arguing that they distract from the essential work of governing and production. He rejects the idea that rituals should only be paused during times of crisis, noting that stability requires constant effort.</summary>

<keywords>Gongmengzi, Mozi, mourning rites, Book of Odes, qin, se, governance, ritual vs. utility, labor, social order</keywords>

...and is like seeking a physician when someone is already dead. In ancient times, the wicked kings of the Three Dynasties <note>The Three Dynasties are the Xia, Shang, and Zhou.</note>—Jie, Zhou, You, and Li <note>original: "Chieh, Chow, Yu, and Li"; these were historical rulers cited in Chinese philosophy as archetypes of tyranny and moral failure.</note>—indulged in music and did not consider their people. Therefore, they were executed and brought disaster to their empire. All of this resulted from following these ideas.

Gongmengzi <note>original: Kung Mengtse</note> said that there were no ghosts or spirits; yet, he also said that the gentleman must learn the rituals of sacrifice and worship. Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: "To hold that there are no spirits while learning sacrificial rituals is like learning the customs of hospitality when there is no guest, or making fishing nets when there are no fish."

Gongmengzi said to Mozi: “You think mourning for three years is wrong. However, your practice of mourning for only three days is also wrong.” Mozi replied: "You uphold mourning for three years and condemn mourning for three days. This is like a naked person condemning someone who merely lifted up their hem as being indecent."

Gongmengzi asked Mozi whether it constitutes wisdom when one person knows something better than another. Mozi answered: "A fool may know some specific thing better than someone else. But can that fool truly be called wise?"

Gongmengzi said: “I mourn for three years to imitate the affection that a son shows to his parents.” Mozi said: "But does a baby have the intelligence to love only its parents? Why, then, does it keep on crying when the parents are unavailable?¹ It is really the extreme—"

<meta>This page continues Mozi’s critique of Confucianism, specifically addressing the circular reasoning used to justify music and four core principles Mozi believes lead to social collapse.</meta>

# GONGMENGZI <note>original: KUNG MENG</note>

...degree of foolishness. Thus, is the intelligence of the Confucianists any higher than that of a baby?

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> asked a Confucianist why the Confucianists practiced music. He replied, "Music is practiced for music’s sake." Mozi said: You have not yet answered me. Suppose I asked, "Why build houses?" and you answered, "It is to keep off the cold in winter and the heat in summer, and to separate men from women." Then you would have told me the reason for building houses. Now I am asking why you practice music. And you answer that music is practiced for music’s sake. This is like asking: "Why build houses?" and being told: "Houses are built for the sake of houses."

Mozi said to Chengzi <note>original: Ch’engtse</note>¹: In the teachings of the Confucianists, there are four principles sufficient to ruin the empire: 

1.  The Confucianists hold that **Heaven is not intelligent** and that **ghosts are inanimate**. Heaven and the spirits are thus displeased. This is enough to ruin the world. 
2.  Again, they practice **elaborate funerals and extended mourning**. They use several inner and outer coffins and many layers of shrouds. The funeral procession looks like a household move. Crying and weeping last for three years. The mourners become so weak they cannot stand up without support and cannot walk without a cane. Their ears cannot hear and their eyes cannot see. This is enough to ruin the world. 
3.  And they **play stringed instruments, dance, sing**, and practice songs and music. This is enough to ruin the empire. 
4.  And, finally, they **believe in fate** <term>fatalism</term>—supposing that poverty or wealth, old age or untimely death, order or chaos, and security or danger are all predetermined and cannot be altered. 

When those in authority apply this belief,

¹ Chengzi <note>original: Ch’engtse</note> or Cheng Fan <note>original: Ch’eng Fan</note> is another Confucianist.

<page-type>text</page-type>

->THE WORKS OF MOZI <note>original: MOTSE</note><-

...of course, they will not attend to government affairs, and those below will not attend to their work. Again, this is enough to ruin the world. 

Chengzi <note>original: Ch'engtse</note> said, "Sir, you are accusing the Confucianists of too much." 

Mozi said: If the Confucianists held nothing like these four principles and yet I said they did, then it would be a false accusation. But now that the Confucianists *do* hold these four principles and I say so, then it is not an accusation but a statement of fact <note>original: information</note>.

Chengzi had nothing more to say and went out. Mozi called him back. After he was seated, Mozi continued: "What you, sir, have just said is not without fault. For according to what you have said, there would be no praise for Yu or blame for Jie and Zhou." <note>Yu was a legendary sage king; Jie and Zhou were the stereotypical "last wicked kings" of the Xia and Shang dynasties.</note> 

Mozi replied: Not at all. You are only cleverly criticizing me according to traditional notions. When an attack is heavy, the defense must be strong. When an attack is light, the defense must be light. To criticize me according to traditional notions is similar to trying to kill a moth with a thill <term>thill: the heavy wooden shaft connecting a horse to a cart</term>.

In a discussion with Chengzi, Mozi cited Confucius. Chengzi inquired why, since he condemned Confucianism, he cited Confucius. 

Mozi said: This citation refers to what is right and cannot be altered. When a bird becomes aware of the danger of heat and of drought, it flies high. When a fish becomes aware of the danger of heat and of drought, it swims deep <note>original: low</note>. In such circumstances, even the deliberations of Yu and Tang <note>King Tang, the virtuous founder of the Shang dynasty</note> cannot differ from this instinct. The bird and the fish may be said to be unintelligent. Yet, in some instances, even Yu and Tang would follow them. Should I never cite Confucius when he is right?

A man visited Mozi's school. He was physically healthy...

<summary>Mozi defends his criticism of Confucianism as factual rather than slanderous and explains that he cites Confucius because even a flawed philosopher can express universal truths.</summary>

<header>GONG MENG <note>original: KUNG MENG</note></header>

...physically strong and mentally brilliant. Desiring to have him in his school, Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> told him to come and study, promising that he would make him an official. Persuaded by such an attractive promise, the man came to study. After a year, he demanded a position from Mozi. Mozi said: "I have not yet made you an official. But haven't you heard the story from the state of Lu? 

There were five brothers in Lu whose father passed away. The eldest son loved wine and refused to oversee the funeral. The four younger brothers said to him, 'If you conduct the funeral for us, we will buy wine for you.' Persuaded by this attractive promise, he buried his father. After the burial, he demanded the wine from his four brothers. The four brothers told him, 'We will not give you any wine. You were burying your father, and we were burying ours. Was he only our father? If you had not buried him, people would have laughed at you; therefore, we urged you to do it.' 

Now, you have done what is right, and I have done what is right. Is this only for my benefit <note>original: is it only my righteousness?</note>? If you do not learn, people will laugh at you. Therefore, I simply urged you to learn."

A man visited Mozi’s school. Mozi said: "Why not come and study?" The man replied, "No one in my family is educated." Mozi said: "That does not matter. Does a person who loves beauty say, 'None of my family loves it, therefore I will not'? Or does a person who desires wealth and honor say, 'None of my family desires them, therefore I will not'? Now, in the love of beauty and the desire for wealth and honor, a person moves forward regardless of others. And righteousness is the greatest thing in the world. Why should someone only follow what others have done when it comes to doing what is right?"

A man visited Mozi’s school and said to him: "Sir, you teach that the ghosts and spirits are intelligent and can..."

<summary>Mozi uses parables to argue that education and righteousness should be pursued for their own sake and personal benefit, rather than just for external rewards or family tradition.</summary>

...bring calamity or blessing to people. They will enrich the good and harm the evil. Now, I have served you for a long time. Yet no blessing has come. Is it possible that your teaching is not entirely correct, and that the spirits <note>original: ghosts and spirits</note> are not intelligent? Otherwise, why don't I receive any blessing?"

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: "Even though you have not received any blessing, how does that invalidate my teaching, and how does that make the spirits unintelligent?"

The man replied that he did not know. 

Mozi continued: "Suppose there is a man ten times as virtuous as you are; can you praise him ten times while you praise yourself only once?" 

He answered that he could not. 

"Now, suppose there is a man a hundred times as virtuous as you are; can you, during your whole life, praise him and not praise yourself even once?" 

He answered that he could not. 

Mozi said: "He who hides <note>original: obscured</note> the virtues of even one person is guilty. Now, you have hidden the virtues of so many. You must be very guilty indeed. With what reason can you expect a blessing?"

***

Mozi was sick. Tieh Pi came and asked: "Sir, you have taught that the spirits are intelligent and are in control of calamity and blessing. They reward the good and punish the evil. Now, you are a sage. How can you be sick? Is it possible that your teaching was not entirely correct, and that the spirits are, after all, unintelligent?" 

Mozi said: "Though I am sick, how does it follow that the spirits are unintelligent? There are many ways by which a person can contract diseases. Some are affected by the climate, and some by fatigue. If there are a hundred gates to a house and only one of them is closed, why shouldn't a burglar still be able to get in?" <note>Mozi uses the analogy of a house with many gates to explain that while spiritual protection might close one "gate" against misfortune, physical causes like exhaustion or weather remain open "gates" through which illness can enter.</note>

# GONG MENG <note>original: KUNG MENG</note>

Some of the pupils asked to learn archery from Mozi <note>original: Motse</note>. Mozi said: "It is impossible. A wise person should measure the extent of their energy and plan their career accordingly. Even a soldier cannot fight a battle and help someone else at the same time. You are not soldiers. How, then, can you expect to be both accomplished scholars and accomplished archers?"

Some of the pupils reported to Mozi that Gaozi <note>original: Kaotse</note> claimed Mozi teaches righteousness but acts wickedly, and they urged Mozi to condemn Gaozi. Mozi said: "That would not be right. For him to praise my teaching while criticizing my conduct is still better than total indifference. Suppose someone declares that I, Mozi <note>original: Ti</note>, am not at all benevolent <note>original: unmagnanimous</note>, yet acknowledges that I honor Heaven, worship the spirits, and love all people—this is still better than if he ignored me entirely. Gaozi was quite specific in his statements; he does not criticize me for teaching benevolence and righteousness. Therefore, criticism from Gaozi is still better than indifference."

Some of the pupils reported to Mozi that Gaozi was enthusiastic about practicing benevolence <note>original: magnanimity</note>. Mozi remarked: "That may not be the case at all. Gaozi practices benevolence in the same way as a man who stands on his tiptoes to appear tall or puffs himself out to appear broad. It cannot last long."

---

<note>
1. There is a book in the *Mencius* <note>original: 孟 子</note> titled "Gaozi" (Legge, vol. ii, bk. vi, pp. 270-324). The Gaozi mentioned there argued that human nature is morally neutral, leading to a famous controversy with Mencius, who taught that human nature is inherently good. He is said to have once been a pupil of Mencius. If this is true, the Gaozi mentioned here by Mozi must be a different person. However, considering that Mozi probably died around 390 B.C. and Mencius was born in 372 B.C., it is possible for the same person to have spoken with both Mozi and Mencius if they lived to a great age.
</note>

<note>
2. Here Mozi is referring to himself by his personal name, Zhai <note>original: Ti</note>.
</note>

Gaozi <note>original: Kaotse</note> said to Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> that he was capable of administering the country and the government. Mozi replied: "To govern is to put into practice what one teaches. Currently, you do not behave according to your own teachings; this means that you are in revolt against yourself. If you are unable to govern yourself, how can you possibly govern the country? Your own conduct will only throw it into chaos."

# BOOK XIII

## CHAPTER XLIX

### LU'S QUESTION

The Lord of Lu asked Mozi <note>original: Motse</note>: "I am afraid that the state of Qi <note>original: Ch'i</note> will attack me. Is there any remedy to prevent this?"

Mozi said: "Yes. The sage-kings of the Three Dynasties <note>The Xia, Shang, and Zhou dynasties</note>—Yu, Tang, Wen, and Wu—were originally feudal lords of states that were only a hundred *li* square <note>A "li" is a Chinese unit of distance, roughly one-third of a mile; a hundred li square was a relatively small territory.</note>. Yet, by enlisting the loyal and practicing righteousness, they won the empire. Meanwhile, the wicked kings of the Three Dynasties—Jie, Zhou, You, and Li—lost the empire by alienating the loyal and practicing wickedness. I wish your Lordship would show reverence toward Heaven and the spirits above, and love and benefit the people below. Prepare generous gifts of furs and money, use humble speech to befriend all the neighboring lords, and lead your state to serve Qi. Aside from this, truly nothing else can be done."

When Qi was preparing to attack Lu, Mozi said to Xiang Ziniu <note>Xiang Ziniu (Hsiang Tse Niu) was a general of the state of Qi.</note>: "For Qi to attack Lu is a great injustice. In the past, the Lord of Wu attacked the state of Yue to the east and forced Lord Goujian of Yue to take refuge on Mount Kuaiji <note>Kuaiji (Kuei Chi) is the name of a mountain.</note>. He attacked the state of Chu to the west and captured Lord Zhao at Sui. To the north, he attacked Qi and brought Guozi <note>Guozi (Kuotse) was a general of Qi, also known as Guo Shu. However, the original Chinese characters *guo zi* (國子) could also mean "the Prince of the state." The scholar Alfred Forke, for instance, used the latter translation.</note> back to Wu as a captive. But then the other feudal lords took their revenge, and his own people... 

<summary>Mozi advises the Lord of Lu to use religious devotion, diplomacy, and humility to avoid an invasion by the state of Qi, while warning a Qi general that aggressive expansionism historically leads to a state's downfall.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Lord of Lu, Qi, diplomacy, sage-kings, Three Dynasties, pacifism, statecraft, Xiang Ziniu, military ethics</keywords>

--- Page 258 ---
<meta>This page continues Mozi’s arguments against aggressive warfare, illustrating the downfall of historical rulers who ignored the suffering of their people. It transitions from the fall of the Lord of Wu and Zhi Bo to a philosophical dialogue with the Grand Lord of Qi about the moral consequences of violence.</meta>

<page-num>244</page-num>

...complained of the hardship and would no longer follow orders. Consequently, the state was destroyed and the Lord of Wu was executed.

Formerly, Zhi Bo <note>original: Chih Po; a powerful official of the Jin state whose greed led to his downfall in 453 BCE</note> attacked both the house of Fan and the house of Zhonghang <note>original: Chung Hsing</note>, and absorbed all the land of the three Jin states <note>The states of Zhao, Wei, and Han, which emerged from the partition of the Jin state</note>. The feudal lords then took vengeance; his people complained of the hardship and would no longer follow orders. Consequently, the state was destroyed and he was executed. Therefore, an attack by a large state on a small state results in injury to both, and the consequences of the wrongdoing will always return to the large state.

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> saw the Grand Lord of Qi <note>original: Ch'i</note> and said: "Suppose there is a sword here. When it is tested on a man's neck, it cuts through it swiftly. Can it be said to be sharp?" 

The Grand Lord said, "It is sharp." 

Mozi said: "When it is tested on the necks of several men, it cuts through them swiftly. Can it be said to be sharp?" 

The Grand Lord said, "It is sharp." 

Mozi said: "Certainly, the sword is proved to be sharp, but who will bear the curse of the deed?" <note>In the original Chinese text, there is a play on the word *li* (利). It means both "sharp" and "benefit" or "profit." Mozi is using this double meaning to contrast the "sharpness" of a weapon with the lack of "benefit" or moral "profit" for the person using it.</note> 

The Grand Lord said, "The sword reaped the benefit <note>original: li</note>, but he who uses it will be visited by the curse for the act." 

Mozi continued: "Now, to capture a state, ruin an army, and destroy the people—who will be visited by the curse for these acts?" 

The Grand Lord looked down and then up, deliberated, and said: "I shall be visited with the curse for these acts."

Prince Wen of Luyang <note>original: Lu Yang</note> was going to attack the state of Zheng <note>original: Cheng</note>. Mozi heard of it and tried to stop him, saying: "Suppose that within the borders of Luyang, the large cities should attack the..."

<summary>Mozi uses historical examples and a Socratic-style dialogue about a "sharp" sword to convince the Grand Lord of Qi and Prince Wen of Luyang that aggressive warfare brings moral ruin and ultimate failure to the aggressor.</summary>

# LU'S QUESTION

“...small cities and the large clans <note>original: "houses"; referring to the noble families or estates within a state</note> attack the small clans, killing the people and carrying away the oxen and horses, dogs and pigs, cloth and silk, and grain and valuables. What would you say to that?”

Prince Wen of Lu Yang replied: “Within the borders of Lu Yang, everyone is my subject. Now, should the large cities attack the small cities and the large clans attack the small clans, carrying away their valuables, I would punish them severely.”

Mozi <note>original: Motse</note> said: “Now, Heaven possesses the whole world just as your Lordship possesses your state. But you are raising an army to attack the state of Cheng. Shouldn’t punishment from Heaven come to you?”

Prince Wen of Lu Yang said: “Why should you, sir, prevent me from attacking Cheng? I attack Cheng in accordance with the Will of Heaven. For three generations, the people of Cheng have murdered their lords <gloss>1</gloss>. Heaven has been visiting them with punishment, causing them to be unsuccessful in their harvests for three years. I am only helping Heaven to carry out the punishment.”

Mozi said: “The people of Cheng have murdered their lords for three generations. Heaven has been visiting them with punishment, causing them to be unsuccessful in their harvests for three years. The punishment of Heaven is sufficient. Yet, you are raising an army to attack Cheng, proclaiming, ‘My attack on Cheng is in accordance with the Will of Heaven.’ Suppose there is a man whose son is strong but arrogant. Because of this, the father punished him with a rod <note>original: "ferule"; a flat ruler or stick used for punishing children</note>. But the neighbor’s father struck the boy with a heavy club, saying, ‘It is in...’ ”

---

1 “Father” here refers to the lord. Among the Five Relationships <note>The cardinal social hierarchies in ancient Chinese philosophy: ruler/subject, father/son, husband/wife, elder/younger brother, and friend/friend</note>, the relationship between the lord and the minister comes first and that between the father and the son second.

<summary>Mozi challenges Prince Wen of Lu Yang’s plan to invade the state of Cheng, arguing that the Prince has no divine mandate to "help" Heaven punish a state that is already suffering from famine and internal strife.</summary>

# THE WORKS OF MOZI

"...accordance with his father’s will that I strike him.” Isn’t this perverse behavior?

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said to Prince Wen of Lu Yang: If a lord attacked neighboring states, killed their people, and carried away their oxen, horses, grain, and valuables, that lord might still record these deeds on bamboo and silk <note>Ancient Chinese writing materials used before the invention of paper</note> and engrave them on metal and stone. He might even write them as maxims on a bell or a <term>ting</term> <note>An ancient Chinese three-legged bronze cauldron used for ritual offerings and symbolizing political power</note> to hand down to his descendants, saying: “None possess as much as I.” 

Now, consider the unscrupulous common man who also attacks neighboring homes, kills the residents, and takes their dogs, pigs, food, and clothing. He would also like to record these deeds on bamboo and silk and write them as maxims on his vessels and dishes to hand down to his descendants, saying: “None possesses as much as I.” Is this permissible? 

Prince Wen of Lu Yang said: “According to what you have said, then what the world takes for granted may not be right after all.”

Mozi said to Prince Wen of Lu Yang: The gentlemen of the world know only trifles, but not things of importance. If a man steals a dog or a pig, they call him wicked. But if a man steals a state or a city, it is regarded as righteous. This is similar to calling something white when one sees a little bit of white, but calling it black when one sees a great deal of white. This is what is meant when we say the gentlemen of the world know only trifles and not things of importance.

Prince Wen of Lu Yang said to Mozi: “There is a cannibal tribe to the south of Ch’u. When the firstborn son is born—

---

The same tribe was mentioned in another connection on page 133. This point needs to be made because in the Chinese text, the name given there is *Yan* <note>original: 炎</note> and not *Dan* <note>original: 啖</note> as it is given here, and it is liable to be misread. Evidently, they refer to the same tribe.

<summary>Mozi uses analogies to expose the hypocrisy of rulers who view large-scale conquest as glorious while condemning petty theft as criminal. He challenges Prince Wen to rethink the morality of aggressive warfare through the lens of universal standards.</summary>

<page-num>247</page-num>

# LU'S QUESTION

"...they cut him apart and eat him. This is said to bring good fortune to his younger brothers. If he tastes delicious, he is offered to the chief, and if the chief is pleased, the father is rewarded. Is this not a wicked custom?” 

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: “Customs in China are just as bad. How is killing a father and rewarding the son any different from eating a son and rewarding the father? If compassion <note>original: "magnanimity"</note> and righteousness are not practiced, why should we condemn the barbarians for eating their sons?”

When a favorite concubine of the Lord of Lu died, someone in the state wrote a funeral tribute <note>original: "obituary"</note> for her. The Lord of Lu was pleased with it and hired the writer. Mozi heard of this and remarked: “A funeral tribute is meant only to describe the aspirations of the deceased. Hiring a man simply because his tribute is pleasing is like trying to make a wildcat pull a carriage.” <note>Mozi means the man’s skill in writing does not mean he has the strength or character to serve in government.</note>

Prince Wen of Lu Yang asked Mozi: “Suppose someone was recommended as a loyal minister. He would bow down when I commanded him to bow; he would bend back when I commanded him to bend back. While standing there, he would remain silent, and when called upon, he would answer. Can such a man be called loyal?” 

Mozi said: “To bow when permitted and to bend back when permitted—this is nothing more than a shadow. To remain silent when left alone and to answer when called upon—this is nothing more than an echo. What would Your Lordship gain from an echo or a shadow? According to my understanding of a loyal minister, when the ruler <note>original: "superior"</note> is at fault, the minister should wait for the right moment and give a warning. If he has a good idea, he should offer counsel to the ruler without seeking public credit <note>original: "without revealing it to the world"</note>. He should correct mistakes and lead others toward goodness; he should identify with the ruler and not ally himself with...”

<summary>Mozi critiques the arbitrary nature of "righteousness" in China versus barbarian lands and defines a truly loyal minister as someone who actively corrects their ruler rather than merely acting as a passive "shadow" or "echo."</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, Lord of Lu, Prince Wen of Lu Yang, loyalty, righteousness, funeral tribute, barbarian customs, shadow and echo</keywords>

<meta>This page continues Mozi’s discussion on the role of a loyal minister, arguing that a true minister redirects credit to the ruler and takes blame upon himself. <header>THE WORKS OF MOZI</header>

...subordinates. This way, goodness and excellence are attributed to the ruler <note>original: "superior"</note>, while complaints and grudges are directed toward the subordinates. In this arrangement, ease and happiness belong to the ruler, while trouble and worry fall to the ministers. *This is what I call a loyal minister.*

The Lord of Lu consulted Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note>, saying: "I have two sons. One enjoys learning <note>scholarship and study</note> and the other enjoys distributing property to the people. Which one should be crowned Prince?" Mozi said: "We cannot tell just from these actions. It may be that they behave this way only for the sake of praise and reward. A fisherman's bait is not intended to feed the fish; trapping a mouse with worms is not done out of love for the mouse. I wish Your Lordship would observe both their intentions and the actual results <note>original: "consequences"</note> of their actions."

There was a man in Lu who sent his son to study with Mozi. The son died in a battle. The father blamed Mozi for it. Mozi said: "You wanted to have your son trained. Now he has completed his training and died in battle, and yet you have become resentful <note>original: "sore"</note>. This is like trying to sell something, and then becoming angry when it is actually sold. Isn't this strange?"

Among the country people living south of Lu, there was a man named Wu Lü. Making pottery in the winter and farming in the summer, he compared himself to Shun <note>Shun was a legendary sage-king of ancient China, famous for his humility and for working as a farmer and potter before being chosen as emperor.</note>. Mozi heard of him and went to visit him. Wu Lü told Mozi: "Righteousness is simply righteousness. Why all these many words <note>original: "verbosity"</note>?" Mozi asked him: "Now, does what you call righteousness have the power to serve other people and produce wealth to be shared among the people?" Wu Lü replied that it did. Mozi continued: "I have thought deeply about this matter. I have considered becoming a farmer to feed the people in..."

<summary>Mozi defines a loyal minister as one who protects the ruler's reputation and cautions the Lord of Lu to judge his sons by their motives rather than their outward charity. He also defends the practical application of his teachings against a self-styled sage who questions the need for complex philosophy.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, Lord of Lu, Wu Lü, Shun, righteousness, loyalty, education, intentions vs. consequences</keywords>

--- Page 263 ---
<meta>This page continues Mozi’s debate with the rustic philosopher Wu Lü, who believes that only those who perform manual labor, like farming and weaving, are truly virtuous.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<header>LU’S QUESTION</header>
<page-num>249</page-num>

...the world. If that could be successful, I would become one. But when a farmer’s produce is divided among the world, each person cannot get even one <term>sheng</term> <note>A traditional Chinese unit of volume, roughly equal to one liter.</note> of grain. Even if they could obtain that much, it clearly cannot feed all the hungry in the world. I have thought of becoming a weaver and clothing all the people in the world. If that could be successful, I would become one. But when a weaver’s goods are divided among the world, each person cannot get even a foot of cloth. Even if they could obtain that much, it clearly cannot keep everyone who is cold in the world warm. I have thought of putting on armor and carrying a weapon to come to the feudal lord’s rescue. If that could be successful, I would become a soldier. Now, it is evident that a single soldier cannot hold out against a regular army. 

I concluded that none of these is as good as familiarizing myself with the <term>Tao</term> <note>The Way; the fundamental path of moral and social truth.</note> of the ancient sage-kings, discovering their principles, and understanding the words of the sages to be clear about their expressions. With these, I can persuade the rulers, the common people, and the travelers <note>original: "pedestrians"</note>. When the rulers adopt my principles, their states will be orderly. When the common people and the travelers adopt my principles, their conduct will be regulated. Therefore, I believe that even though I do not plow and feed the hungry or weave and clothe the cold, I have greater merit than those who plow and feed, or weave and clothe. Therefore, I think my merit is greater than that of those who plow and weave, even though I do not perform those tasks myself.

Wu Lü <note>A contemporary of Mozi who advocated for a philosophy of agrarian self-sufficiency.</note> kept on saying, “Righteousness is just righteousness. Why all this wordiness <note>original: "verbosity"</note>?” Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> continued: “Suppose the world does not know how to plow. Who has...”

<summary>Mozi argues that his intellectual work of teaching the "Way" of ancient kings is more beneficial to society than individual manual labor or military service because it creates systemic order. He defends his "wordiness" against Wu Lü’s preference for simple, unstated righteousness.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, Wu Lü, Tao, division of labor, merit, righteousness, social order, sage-kings</keywords>

--- Page 264 ---
<meta>This page continues Mozi’s debate with the philosopher Wu Lü about the value of teaching versus physical labor, and then transitions to an account of Mozi being offered a high position and land in the state of Yue.</meta>

<language>English</language>
<page-type>text</page-type>
<page-num>250</page-num>
<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

"Who deserves more credit <note>original: "merit"</note>: the man who teaches people to plow, or the man who does not teach others but simply plows himself?"

Wu Lü answered that he who teaches others to plow deserves more credit.

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: "In the attack of an unrighteous state, who deserves more credit: the person who beats the drum and urges the soldiers to fight on, or the person who does not beat the drum to urge the soldiers on, but only fights himself?"

Wu Lü said that he who beats the drum and urges the soldiers to fight on deserves more credit.

Mozi continued: "Now, the common people and the travelers <note>original: "pedestrians"</note> in the world know little about righteousness. Naturally, those who teach them righteousness deserve more credit too. Why don’t you agree in this case? Would my own righteousness not be advanced if I can encourage them to act with righteousness?"

After Mozi had visited Kung Shang Kuo, Kung Shang Kuo recommended him to the Lord of Yüeh <note>Yue; a major state in ancient China during the Spring and Autumn and Warring States periods.</note>. The Lord of Yüeh was greatly pleased and said to Kung Shang Kuo: “Sir, if you can persuade Mozi to come to Yüeh and instruct me, I shall offer him five hundred square <term>li</term> <note>A traditional Chinese unit of distance; a "li" is roughly 500 meters. A territory of five hundred li square would be a massive grant of land, effectively making Mozi a high-ranking feudal lord.</note> of the land located in the former state of Wu.”

Kung Shang Kuo promised to try, and fifty wagons were made ready to go to the state of Lu and welcome Mozi. Kung Shang Kuo told him: “When I tried to persuade the Lord of Yüeh using your principles, he was quite pleased. He told me that if I could induce you to come to Yüeh and instruct him, he would offer you five hundred square li of the land in the former state of Wu.”

Mozi said to Kung Shang Kuo: "As you observe it, what is the intention of the Lord of Yüeh? If the Lord of Yüeh will listen to my words and adopt my way..."

<summary>Mozi concludes his argument that teaching righteousness is more valuable than individual labor and receives a lucrative offer of land from the Lord of Yue in exchange for his teachings.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Wu Lü, Kung Shang Kuo, Lord of Yue, righteousness, merit, state of Wu, state of Lu, li, teaching vs. doing</keywords>

<header>LU'S QUESTION</header>

"I will come, asking only for food according to the capacity of my stomach and clothing according to the size <note>original: "stature"</note> of my body. I will serve simply as one of the ministers. What is the use of a land grant <note>original: "commission"; referring to the 500 square li offered on the previous page</note>? If I were to sell my integrity, I could just as well do it here in the central states <note>The original text uses "China," referring to the "Middle Kingdom" states like Lu or Song. At the time, Yue was considered a peripheral, "barbarian" state on the southern frontier.</note>. Why, then, should I go out to Yue?" <margin>Yue was on the southern border of the central Chinese states. Its people were also considered to be of "barbarian" origin. See the *Sketch Map* appended in *Motse, the Neglected Rival of Confucius*.</margin>

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> was visiting Wei Yue <note>Wei Yue was a disciple of Mozi.</note>. The latter asked: "Now that you have seen the scholars <note>original: "gentlemen"</note> from every direction <note>original: "four quarters"</note>, what would you say is the most urgent task?"

Mozi replied: "Upon entering a country, one should identify what it lacks <note>original: "locate the need"</note> and work on that. If the country is in chaos <note>original: "upset in confusion"</note>, teach them the doctrines of **Honoring the Worthy** <note>original: "Exaltation of the Virtuous"</note> and **Identifying with the Superior**. If the country is in poverty, teach them **Frugality in Expenditure** <note>original: "Economy of Expenditures"</note> and **Simplicity in Funerals**. If the country is indulging in music and wine, teach them the **Condemnation of Music** and **Anti-fatalism**. If the country is disrespectful <note>original: "insolent"</note> and lacks proper etiquette <note>original: "without propriety"</note>, teach them to reverence Heaven and worship the spirits. <note>This refers to the two doctrines expounded in the chapters "The Will of Heaven" and "On Ghosts."</note> If the country is engaged in conquest and oppression, teach them **Universal Love** and the **Condemnation of Offensive War**. Therefore we say, one should identify the need and work on that."

Mozi had recommended Ts'ao Kungtse to the state of Song <note>original: "Sung"</note>. He

<summary>Mozi refuses a lavish land grant from the Lord of Yue, insisting that his primary goal is the adoption of his teachings rather than personal wealth. He also explains his "situational" approach to philosophy, where a teacher must apply specific Mohist doctrines based on a nation's particular social or political failings.</summary>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI</header>

...returned after three years and met with Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note>, saying: "When I first came to your school, I had to wear short jackets and eat vegetable soup. Even this I could not have in the evening if I had eaten it in the morning. And I had nothing to offer in sacrifice to the ghosts and spirits. Now, thanks to you, my family has become more prosperous. I have been able to respectfully offer sacrifices and worship the ghosts and spirits at home. Yet several members of my household have died, the six domestic animals <note>The six animals traditionally raised by Chinese households: the horse, ox, goat, pig, dog, and fowl.</note> do not breed, and I myself have been troubled with illnesses. I doubt if your Way <note>original: "way"</note> is meant to be followed after all." 

Mozi said: "This is not fair. What the ghosts and spirits desire from humanity is this: that when someone holds high rank and receives a large salary <note>original: "emolument"</note>, they should step down in favor of the worthy <note>original: "virtuous"</note>; and that when someone possesses great wealth, they should share it with the poor. How could the ghosts and spirits merely desire to take food and drink? <note>original footnote: "This refers to material offerings."</note> 

Now, although you held a high rank and received a large salary, you did not give up your position in favor of the worthy. This was your first step toward bad fortune. Though you possessed great wealth, you did not share it with the poor. This was your second step toward misfortune. Now you serve the ghosts and spirits merely by offering them sacrifices, and yet you wonder why these illnesses have come. This is like shutting only one out of a hundred gates and wondering how the thieves entered. How can you pray to the ghosts and spirits for blessings in this way?"

The Master of Sacrifice <note>An official responsible for overseeing religious rituals and state offerings.</note> of Lu offered one pig and asked for a hundred blessings. Upon hearing of it, Mozi said: "This cannot be done. To give others little but to expect much from them would make them wary <note>original: "afraid"</note> of receiving gifts. Now

<summary>Mozi rebukes a student and a government official for believing that ritual sacrifices can replace moral duties like sharing wealth and promoting the worthy. He argues that the divine cannot be bribed with food and that true misfortune stems from ethical failures rather than a lack of ritual offerings.</summary>

<keywords>Mozi, ritual sacrifice, ghosts and spirits, wealth redistribution, meritocracy, Lu, ethics vs ritual, religious offerings</keywords>

<header>LU'S QUESTION</header>

...only one pig is offered, yet a hundred blessings are requested from the ghosts and spirits. They <note>The spirits</note> would be quite intimidated if someone offered a sacrifice of oxen and sheep <note>as the petitioner would likely demand even greater, impossible rewards in return</note>. In ancient times, when the sage-kings worshipped the ghosts and spirits, they simply offered them sacrifice and that was all. One would be better off remaining poor than trying to become rich by offering a pig in sacrifice and asking for a hundred blessings.

Peng Qing Shengzi <note>original: "P'eng Ch'ing Shengtse," likely a disciple of Mozi</note> said: "The past can be known, but the future cannot." 

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: "Suppose your parents met with misfortune a hundred *li* <note>A Chinese unit of distance, roughly one-third of a mile or 500 meters.</note> away, and you had only a single day to reach them. If they could be reached in time, they would live; if not, they would die. Here you have a choice: a sturdy wagon and an excellent horse, or a broken horse and a cart with square wheels. Which would you take?" 

He replied that the excellent horse and the sturdy wagon would obviously make for a faster journey. 

Mozi said: "Then how can you say the future is not knowable?" <note>The original Chinese text is somewhat ambiguous here, but the implication is that choosing the faster horse demonstrates a purposeful expectation and "knowledge" of a future goal.</note>

Meng Shan <note>A follower of Mozi</note> praised Prince Ze Lü <note>original: "Tse Lü"</note>, saying: "Formerly, during the uprising of Bo Gong <note>original: "Po Kung"</note>, Prince Ze Lü was held captive. With axes held against his waist and spears pointed at his heart, Bo Gong told him to either become the Lord <note>the ruler</note> and live, or refuse and die. Prince Ze Lü said to him, 'What an insult to me. You have killed my parents and now you tempt me with the state of Chu <note>original: "Ch'u"</note>. If it is not righteous, I would not even take the whole empire, let alone the state of Chu.' Thus he refused." <note>According to the *Zuo Zhuan* (The Commentary of Zuo), an ancient Chinese narrative history, the Prince lost his life for this refusal.</note>

<summary>Mozi critiques the greed inherent in small sacrifices for large rewards, argues that the future is plan-able and thus "knowable," and recounts the martyrdom of Prince Ze Lü for the sake of righteousness.</summary>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOZI</header>

“Wasn’t Prince Ze Lü <note>original: "Tse Lü"</note> magnanimous?” 

Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said: “What he did was indeed difficult, but it could hardly be called magnanimous. If he thought the Lord had strayed from the Way <note>original: "Tao"</note>, why did he not accept the offer of the throne and take over the government himself? If he thought Bo Gong <note>original: "Po Kung"</note> was unrighteous, why did he not also accept the lordship, execute Bo Gong, and then return the lordship to the rightful Lord? Therefore, I say what he did was indeed difficult, but it was hardly magnanimous.”

Mozi sent Sheng Zhuo <note>original: "Sheng Cho"</note>¹ to serve Xiang Ziniu <note>original: "Hsiang Tse Niu"</note>. Xiang Ziniu invaded the state of Lu three times, and Sheng Zhuo accompanied him every time. Hearing of this, Mozi sent Gao Sunzi <note>original: "Kao Suntse"</note> to call him back, saying: “I sent Zhuo there in order to cure pride and restrain insolence. Now, Zhuo receives a high salary and flatters his master. His master invaded Lu three times and he was with him every time. This is like trying to urge a horse forward while pulling back on its martingale <note>a strap on a horse's harness used to control its head</note>.² I have heard that to preach righteousness but not practice it is a deliberate commitment of wrong. It is not that Zhuo is ignorant. It is a case of his salary <term>emolument</term> triumphing over his sense of righteousness.”

In the past, the people of Chu <note>original: "Ch'u"</note> and the people of Yue <note>original: "Yüeh"</note> fought a battle on the Yangtze River <note>original: "the River"</note>.³ The people of Chu had the current with them as they advanced, but were against the current when they retreated. When victory was in sight, they advanced. But when defeat faced them, they found it very difficult to retreat. On the contrary, the people of Yue advanced upstream but retreated downstream. When victory was in sight, they would—

---

¹ Sheng Zhuo, a follower of Mozi.
² This is a metaphor suggesting that instead of encouraging the man to advance in virtue, his behavior actually halts his progress.
³ The Yangtze River is often simply called "the River" in ancient Chinese texts.

<header>LU'S QUESTION</header>

...advance. And when defeat was confronting them, they could easily retreat.¹

With this advantage, the people of Yue <note>original: "Yüeh"</note> greatly defeated the people of Chu <note>original: "Ch’u"</note>. Gongshu Zi <note>original: "Kung Shutse"</note>² came south from the state of Lu to Chu and began making implements for naval warfare, which consisted of grappling hooks and rams. When the enemy was retreating, they used the hooks to pull them back. And when the enemy was advancing, they employed the rams to push them away. The weapons were made to match the length of these hooks and rams. The weapons of Chu were thus all standardized, while those of Yue were not. With this technical advantage, the people of Chu greatly defeated the people of Yue. 

Gongshu Zi was proud of his cleverness and asked Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note>: “I have these implements for grappling and ramming in my warships. Do you have such devices in your doctrine of righteousness?” 

Mozi said: The grappling and ramming devices in my righteousness are more excellent than your tools in the warships. In my system, I pull with love and push with respect. If you do not pull with love, there can be no intimacy. If you do not push with respect, there will be rapid disrespect <note>original: "desecration"; here meaning a violation of social or personal boundaries</note>. And disrespect without real intimacy will soon end in separation. Therefore, mutual love and mutual respect lead to true mutual benefit. Now, you pull people in to stop their retreat, but they will also pull you in to stop your retreat. You push people back to stop their advance, but they will also push you back to stop your advance. This mutual pulling and pushing...

¹ For the geographical situation of these two states, consult the Sketch Map appended in *Motse, the Neglected Rival of Confucius*.  
² Gongshu Ban <note>original: "Kung Shu Pan"</note> is a mechanical inventor of great renown from that time <note>traditionally known as Lu Ban, the patron saint of Chinese builders and contractors</note>.

<summary>Mozi uses the inventor Gongshu Zi’s naval grappling hooks and rams as a metaphor to argue that "love" and "respect" are more effective social tools than physical weapons.</summary>

<header>THE WORKS OF MOTSE</header>

...only mutual injury. Therefore, the principles of pulling and pushing within my concept of righteousness are superior to the tools of pulling and pushing used on your warships.

Gongshu Zi <note>original: "Kung Shutse"</note> constructed a bird out of bamboo and wood, and when it was finished, he flew it. It stayed in the air for three days.<sup>1</sup> Gongshu Zi was proud of his supreme skill. Mozi <note>original: "Motse"</note> said to him: "Your achievement in building a bird does not compare to that of a carpenter making a linchpin <note>a pin passed through the end of an axle to keep a wheel in place</note>. In a short time, a carpenter can carve a three-inch piece of wood, yet it can support a load of fifty *shih* <note>a unit of weight; see footnote 2</note>. For any achievement to be considered skillful, it must benefit humanity; anything that is not beneficial is considered clumsy."

Gongshu Zi confessed to Mozi: "Before I met you, I wanted to conquer the state of Song <note>original: "Sung"</note>. Since meeting you, even if Song were offered to me, I would not accept it if doing so were unrighteous." Mozi said: "Before you met me, you wanted to take Song. Since meeting me, even if Song were offered to you, you would not take it if it were unrighteous. This means I have essentially given you Song. If you dedicate yourself to practicing righteousness, I will give you the whole world."<sup>3</sup>

---

<sup>1</sup> This short passage has sometimes been cited as a historical record of the invention of a flying machine by the Chinese.
<sup>2</sup> One *shih* <note>original: "石"</note> equals 120 *jin* <note>original: "chin 斤"</note>; the latter is equal to 1 1/3 lbs. <note>Therefore, 50 shih is approximately 8,000 lbs.</note>
<sup>3</sup> This paragraph would more properly be placed at the end of the next chapter. In any case, the reference is to that event.

<summary>Mozi argues that technical skill is only valuable if it serves a useful purpose, comparing a decorative mechanical bird unfavorably to a functional wagon linchpin. He successfully convinces the inventor Gongshu Zi to abandon his plans to invade the state of Song on ethical grounds.</summary>
<keywords>Mozi, Gongshu Zi, Song State, utility vs. skill, technology ethics, righteousness, pacifism, mechanical bird</keywords>
